### H-1 / `jWuI7tcEvGKNrRHhPxhvQ`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS of Staking via Malicious Service Multisig Upgrade (Gas Griefing)

```md
## [H-1]. Permanent DoS of Staking via Malicious Service Multisig Upgrade (Gas Griefing)

## id: jWuI7tcEvGKNrRHhPxhvQ

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
StakingBase.checkpoint

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This finding is a variant of AU-S4980: even if a service initially stakes with a legitimate proxy, the proxy’s delegated logic can be changed (or was malicious from the start) to make nonce retrieval extremely gas-expensive or non-terminating. Because StakingBase forwards essentially all gas to `activityChecker.staticcall` and does not bound downstream gas usage, a single malicious service can cause `checkpoint()` to OOG/revert consistently, which in turn blocks `unstake()`/`stake()`/`checkpointAndClaim()`. No existing guard in StakingBase prevents this class of gas griefing (no gas caps, no per-service isolation), so the staking contract can be frozen, locking NFTs and halting rewards.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `checkpoint` function iterates over all staked services to calculate rewards. It calls `_calculateStakingRewards`, which triggers `activityChecker.staticcall` to query the service's multisig nonce. The `activityChecker` typically forwards gas to the target multisig. Since the `multisig` is user-provided and likely a Gnosis Safe Proxy (verified by codehash in `_stake`), a malicious service owner can exploit the upgradeability of Gnosis Safes. By upgrading their Safe's implementation (Master Copy) to a malicious contract that infinite loops or consumes excessive gas in its `nonce` function, the attacker can force the `activityChecker` call to consume all transaction gas. As `StakingBase` does not limit the gas forwarded to `activityChecker` (and `staticcall` forwards all available gas by default), the `checkpoint` transaction will inevitably revert due to Out of Gas. Since `stake`, `unstake`, and `claim` operations all require a successful `checkpoint` execution, the entire staking contract becomes permanently frozen.

## Impact
Permanent Denial of Service. All funds (rewards and staked NFTs) are locked in the contract as no state transitions can occur.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker registers a valid service with a standard Gnosis Safe Proxy.
2. Attacker stakes the service in `StakingBase`.
3. Attacker uses the Safe's `execTransaction` to upgrade the Safe's master copy to a malicious contract that contains an infinite loop in its fallback or `nonce` function.
4. Any user attempts to call `checkpoint` (or `stake`/`unstake`/`claim` which call it).
5. `checkpoint` loops through services and reaches the attacker's service.
6. It calls `activityChecker.getMultisigNonces(attackerMultisig)`.
7. `activityChecker` calls `attackerMultisig.nonce()`.
8. The call consumes all gas.
9. The transaction reverts OOG.

## Proof of Code
/* Mock test demonstrating gas griefing impact */
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/staking/StakingBase.sol";

contract MockStakingDoS is StakingBase {
    function _transfer(address, uint256) internal override {}
    function initialize(StakingParams memory params) external { _initialize(params); }
    function addService(uint256 id, address multisig) external {
        mapServiceInfo[id].tsStart = block.timestamp;
        mapServiceInfo[id].multisig = multisig;
        setServiceIds.push(id);
    }
}

contract MockActivityChecker {
    function getMultisigNonces(address multisig) external view returns (uint256[] memory) {
        // Simulate calling the multisig which burns gas
        (bool s, ) = multisig.staticcall("");
        // In reality, this burns all gas and reverts the top level tx if not handled
        // Here we just consume gas to simulate
        uint256 start = gasleft();
        while (start - gasleft() < 100000) {} 
        return new uint256[](1);
    }
    function isRatioPass(uint256[] memory, uint256[] memory, uint256) external pure returns (bool) { return true; }
}

contract GasGriefer {
    fallback() external { while(true) {} }
}

contract DoSTest is Test {
    MockStakingDoS staking;
    GasGriefer griefer;
    MockActivityChecker checker;

    function setUp() public {
        staking = new MockStakingDoS();
        griefer = new GasGriefer();
        checker = new MockActivityChecker();
        
        StakingBase.StakingParams memory params;
        params.metadataHash = bytes32(uint256(1));
        params.maxNumServices = 10;
        params.rewardsPerSecond = 1;
        params.livenessPeriod = 1;
        params.timeForEmissions = 100;
        params.numAgentInstances = 1;
        params.minNumStakingPeriods = 1;
        params.maxNumInactivityPeriods = 1;
        params.minStakingDeposit = 2;
        params.serviceRegistry = address(0x1);
        params.activityChecker = address(checker);
        staking.initialize(params);
    }

    function testDoS() public {
        staking.addService(1, address(griefer));
        // This mimics the logic in checkpoint -> _checkRatioPass
        // The internal call will burn gas. 
        // Note: Full OOG simulation requires mainnet forking or precise gas limit setup in Foundry
        // but this demonstrates the vector: calling an unchecked external contract in a loop.
        
        // To properly verify, we'd wrap this in a call with limited gas and assert failure
        // but checkpoint() does not limit gas.
        
        vm.expectRevert(); // Reverts due to OOG (simulated here or via explicit revert in mock if we want)
        staking.checkpoint();
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Enforce a gas limit on the `activityChecker` staticcall in `_checkRatioPass` to prevent a single service from consuming the entire block gas limit. Alternatively, ensure the `ActivityChecker` implementation strictly limits the gas forwarded to the multisig.
```

### M-2 / `6VIQqFECY8GRDFYyOO5Mb`
- Finding title: DoS with USDT and other non-compliant ERC20s due to incompatible IToken interface
- Report lines: 1417-1472
```md
## [M-2]. DoS with USDT and other non-compliant ERC20s due to incompatible IToken interface

## id: 6VIQqFECY8GRDFYyOO5Mb

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
NonStandardERC20Behavior

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
LiquidityManagerETH.approve

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This issue concretely affects USDT itself (approve() returns no data and requires allowance reset-to-zero), so it does not depend on a non-standard token other than USDT.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `LiquidityManagerETH` and `LiquidityManagerCore` contracts interact with ERC20 tokens using the `IToken` interface, which defines `approve` as `function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) external returns (bool);`. However, major tokens like USDT do not follow the ERC20 standard strictly and do not return a boolean value on approval. 

When `LiquidityManager` calls `IToken(token).approve(...)` on USDT, the call will revert because Solidity 0.8.x enforces strict return data size checks. Since USDT returns no data (void), the decoding of the `bool` return value fails. This renders the protocol incompatible with USDT, which is a primary stablecoin for liquidity provisioning.

Additionally, USDT requires resetting allowance to 0 before setting a new non-zero value. The current implementation calls `approve` with the desired amount directly. If a previous transaction left a dust allowance (e.g. `mint` used slightly less than approved), subsequent approvals will revert.

## Impact
Functional DoS. The protocol cannot manage liquidity or perform buybacks for pools involving USDT, which violates the requirement to support standard and common non-standard tokens (like USDT as specified in scope).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `LiquidityManagerETH`.
2. Create a MockUSDT contract that mimics USDT behavior (no return value on `approve`).
3. Call `convertToV3` or `checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2` passing the MockUSDT address.
4. The transaction reverts with `EvmError: Revert` or decoding error due to missing return data.

## Proof of Code
function testUSDTApproveReverts() public {
    // Mock USDT with no return value
    MockUSDT usdt = new MockUSDT();
    address[] memory tokens = new address[](2);
    tokens[0] = address(usdt);
    tokens[1] = address(weth);
    
    // Attempt to convert or interact
    vm.expectRevert(); // Decodes failure
    liquidityManager.convertToV3(tokens, ...);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeTransferLib.safeApprove` (if available and robust) or implement a safe approval helper that uses a low-level call to ignore return data size if necessary. Ensure allowance is reset to 0 before setting a new value to support USDT's race condition protection.
```

### M-3 / `cHTZfN14l5r_MIayF8Ra2`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS / Fund Lock due to immutable bridge address and lack of rescue mechanism
- Report lines: 1473-1545
```md
## [M-3]. Permanent DoS / Fund Lock due to immutable bridge address and lack of rescue mechanism

## id: cHTZfN14l5r_MIayF8Ra2

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
Bridge2Burner.relayToL1Burner

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Code path exists: Bridge2BurnerGnosis.relayToL1Burner() uses immutable l2TokenRelayer and is the only method that can move OLAS out (no owner, no sweep/rescue). If the relayer reverts (paused bridge / deprecated proxy / blacklisting), relayToL1Burner always reverts at relayTokens, leaving funds stuck. This is reproducible today by deploying with a relayer mock that always reverts; the lock is permanent because the relayer cannot be changed and there is no alternative withdrawal route. No in-contract safeguard mitigates this dependency risk. Although bridge failure is an external condition, it can occur at any time (including already-paused bridges), so this is not purely speculative. Impact is permanent loss of all OLAS held by the contract at time of outage (and any deposited thereafter), which is objectively High.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `Bridge2Burner` contract stores the `l2TokenRelayer` address as an `immutable` variable set during deployment. There is no functionality to update this address or to withdraw `olas` tokens via a governance-controlled mechanism. If the external bridge contract (Gnosis Omnibridge) is paused, deprecated, or migrated to a new address, the `relayToL1Burner` function—which is the only way to move funds—will permanently fail (revert). This results in all current and future OLAS tokens held by the contract being irretrievably locked.

## Impact
Complete and permanent loss of assets held by the contract in the event of bridge migration or failure.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `Bridge2BurnerGnosis` with a valid bridge address.
2. Simulate a bridge upgrade where the old bridge contract at `l2TokenRelayer` is disabled (reverts all calls).
3. Any call to `relayToL1Burner` now reverts.
4. Since `l2TokenRelayer` is immutable and there is no `rescueFunds` function, the OLAS tokens are stuck forever.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {Bridge2BurnerGnosis} from "contracts/utils/Bridge2BurnerGnosis.sol";

contract MockToken {
    function balanceOf(address) external pure returns (uint256) { return 200 ether; }
    function approve(address, uint256) external returns (bool) { return true; }
}

contract BrokenBridge {
    function relayTokens(address, address, uint256) external {
        revert("Bridge Deprecated");
    }
}

contract DosTest is Test {
    Bridge2BurnerGnosis bridge;
    MockToken token;
    BrokenBridge relayer;

    function setUp() public {
        token = new MockToken();
        relayer = new BrokenBridge();
        bridge = new Bridge2BurnerGnosis(address(token), address(relayer));
    }

    function testFundsStuck() public {
        // Bridge failure blocks the only exit path
        vm.expectRevert("Bridge Deprecated");
        bridge.relayToL1Burner();
        // No other function exists to recover funds
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Implement a `rescueFunds(address token, uint256 amount)` function callable only by the contract owner (governance) to allow recovery of assets in case of bridge obsolescence.
```

### H-4 / `DL1JhZIFCapJ9HHQq5mgo`
- Finding title: Token-Secured Services bypass Slashing mechanism due to decoupled bond accounting
- Report lines: 1546-1613
```md
## [H-4]. Token-Secured Services bypass Slashing mechanism due to decoupled bond accounting

## id: DL1JhZIFCapJ9HHQq5mgo

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Exploit Type
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Location
ServiceManager.create

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Same root cause as the other slashing-bypass report: ServiceManager stores real bond amounts only in TokenUtility while forcing ServiceRegistry’s bond accounting to `BOND_WRAPPER = 1`. With slashing happening in ServiceRegistry and no slashing API exposed by TokenUtility, the token stake cannot be reduced in response to slashing. Therefore an operator can recover essentially all token bond via `unbondTokenRefund` even after being slashed on the registry side, undermining crypto-economic security.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `ServiceManager` enables services to use ERC20 tokens for agent bonding. To integrate with the `ServiceRegistry` (which natively handles ETH bonds), the Manager sets the bond requirement in the Registry to a nominal 1 wei (`BOND_WRAPPER`) while storing the actual token bond requirement in the `ServiceRegistryTokenUtility`. 

However, the slashing mechanism resides in the `ServiceRegistry` and operates on the bond value stored there (1 wei). There is no logic in `ServiceManager` or the `ServiceRegistryTokenUtility` interface to handle slashing of the token bond. The `ServiceRegistry` slashes the operator's ETH bond (max 1 wei), but the token bond in the Utility remains untouched. 

When `unbond` is called, `TokenUtility.unbondTokenRefund` is invoked, which refunds the full token amount since it is unaware of the slashing event. This renders the slashing penalty ineffective for token-secured services, breaking the protocol's economic security model.

## Impact
Malicious operators in token-secured services can only be slashed 1 wei (dust) instead of their full bond, allowing them to misbehave without significant financial penalty.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy a Service using `ServiceManager` with an ERC20 token bond (e.g., 1000 OLAS).
2. Operator registers via `registerAgents`, depositing 1000 OLAS into `TokenUtility` and 1 wei ETH into `ServiceRegistry`.
3. Operator misbehaves, and the Service Multisig calls `ServiceRegistry.slash(operator, 1 wei)`.
4. `ServiceRegistry` burns the 1 wei bond.
5. Operator calls `ServiceManager.unbond()`.
6. `TokenUtility.unbondTokenRefund` is called and returns the full 1000 OLAS (as it has no slash logic).
7. Operator exits with 1000 OLAS - 1 wei.

## Proof of Code
function testSlashingBypass() public {
    // Setup: Create service with Token Bond
    uint256 serviceId = serviceManager.create(owner, address(token), configHash, agentIds, agentParams, threshold);
    
    // Operator registers
    vm.prank(operator);
    token.approve(address(tokenUtility), 1000 ether);
    vm.prank(operator);
    serviceManager.registerAgents{value: 1}(serviceId, agents, agentIds);

    // Simulate Slashing in Registry (mock call or state change)
    // registry.slash(operator, serviceId, 1);
    
    // Unbond
    vm.prank(operator);
    serviceManager.unbond(serviceId);
    
    // Assert full refund despite slash
    assertEq(token.balanceOf(operator), 1000 ether);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Implement a slashing interface in `ServiceRegistryTokenUtility` and ensure `ServiceManager` or `ServiceRegistry` can trigger it. Alternatively, `ServiceManager` should facilitate the slashing process by reducing the refundable token amount when slashing occurs.
```

### H-5 / `d_xbCK4coBPU3zLTfnKAV`
- Finding title: Drained service slashed funds are permanently locked in Treasury due to missing accounting update

```md
## [H-5]. Drained service slashed funds are permanently locked in Treasury due to missing accounting update

## id: d_xbCK4coBPU3zLTfnKAV

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Exploit Type
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Location
Treasury.drainServiceSlashedFunds

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Although owner-triggered, the lock happens even under intended/benign use of drainServiceSlashedFunds (accounting is objectively wrong), matching the 'admin can brick/fail while following spec' caveat; it is not merely an admin mistake.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresAdminRole


## Description
The `Treasury.drainServiceSlashedFunds()` function calls `IServiceRegistry(serviceRegistry).drain()` to pull slashed ETH funds into the Treasury. While the contract's ETH balance increases, the function fails to update the `ETHOwned` state variable (or `ETHFromServices`). The `withdraw` function strictly checks `ETHOwned` before allowing withdrawals. Consequently, any funds pulled via `drainServiceSlashedFunds` are effectively unaccounted for and cannot be withdrawn by the owner or used by the protocol, leading to permanent loss of funds.

## Impact
Permanent loss of drained funds (ETH) as they become locked in the Treasury contract.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Admin calls `drainServiceSlashedFunds()`. 2. `ServiceRegistry.drain()` sends 10 ETH to Treasury. 3. Treasury balance increases by 10 ETH, but `ETHOwned` remains unchanged. 4. Admin tries to `withdraw` the 10 ETH. 5. Contract reverts with `LowerThan` error because `amountOwned` (based on `ETHOwned`) is insufficient.

## Proof of Code
function testDrainLocksFunds() public {
        uint256 amount = 1 ether;
        // Mock registry drain
        vm.mockCall(address(tokenomics), abi.encodeWithSelector(ITokenomics.serviceRegistry.selector), abi.encode(address(registry)));
        vm.mockCall(address(registry), abi.encodeWithSelector(IServiceRegistry.slashedFunds.selector), abi.encode(amount));
        vm.mockCall(address(registry), abi.encodeWithSelector(IServiceRegistry.drain.selector), abi.encode(amount));
        vm.deal(address(registry), amount);
        // Simulate transfer logic of drain
        vm.prank(address(registry));
        payable(address(treasury)).transfer(amount);
        
        uint256 preOwned = treasury.ETHOwned();
        vm.prank(owner);
        treasury.drainServiceSlashedFunds();
        
        assertEq(treasury.ETHOwned(), preOwned); // Not updated
        assertEq(address(treasury).balance, preOwned + amount);
        
        vm.prank(owner);
        vm.expectRevert();
        treasury.withdraw(owner, preOwned + amount, address(0xEeeeeEeeeEeEeeEeEeEeeEEEeeeeEeeeeeeeEEeE));
    }

## Suggested Mitigation
Update `ETHOwned` in `drainServiceSlashedFunds`: `ETHOwned += uint96(amount);`.
```

### M-6 / `I0qedlcD_ewvQf5AkEez-`
- Finding title: Missing Deadline in Operator Signatures
- Report lines: 1674-1724
```md
## [M-6]. Missing Deadline in Operator Signatures

## id: I0qedlcD_ewvQf5AkEez-

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
PermitDeadlineBypass

## Exploit Type
PermitDeadlineBypass

## Location
ServiceManager.getRegisterAgentsHash

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: `getRegisterAgentsHash()` and `getUnbondHash()` commit only to (operator, serviceOwner, serviceId, agentsData, nonce) and contain no timestamp. Therefore signatures do not naturally expire. Since operators also lack an on-chain nonce-bump/revoke mechanism, a signature can remain usable for an arbitrarily long time. This matches the report and is reproducible: a service owner can submit the same operator signature months later as long as the corresponding nonce for (operator, serviceId) has not been consumed by a prior signature-based execution.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `OperatorSignedHashes` implementation uses EIP-712 signatures for `unbond` and `registerAgents` but does not include a `deadline` parameter in the struct hash. This allows signatures to be valid indefinitely. A Service Owner can withhold a signature provided by an operator and execute it at a later time when conditions (market value, service configuration, or operator intent) have changed, potentially to the detriment of the operator.

## Impact
Replay of stale intent; griefing of operators who cannot cancel off-chain signatures.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Operator signs `RegisterAgents`.
2. Operator changes mind, but cannot invalidate the signature on-chain without registering (consuming slot) or incrementing nonce (requires executing an action).
3. 1 year later, Service Owner submits the signature, forcing Operator to bond/register against their current will.

## Proof of Code
function testNoDeadline() public {
    // 1. Generate signature
    bytes memory sig = _signRegisterAgents(operatorKey, serviceId, instances, ids, 0);
    
    // 2. Warp time 10 years
    vm.warp(block.timestamp + 3650 days);
    
    // 3. Execute succeeds
    vm.prank(owner);
    serviceManager.registerAgentsWithSignature(operator, serviceId, instances, ids, sig);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add a `deadline` parameter to the `RegisterAgents` and `Unbond` structs and verify `block.timestamp <= deadline` in `_verifySignedHash` or the calling function.
```

### H-7 / `f1WVAhp-n1jMWcGo_hpiA`
- Finding title: UniswapPriceOracle.getPrice returns inverted price (Quote/Base vs Base/Quote)
- Report lines: 1725-1778
```md
## [H-7]. UniswapPriceOracle.getPrice returns inverted price (Quote/Base vs Base/Quote)

## id: f1WVAhp-n1jMWcGo_hpiA

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Oracle

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
UniswapPriceOracle.getPrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The code path exists in UniswapPriceOracle.getPrice(). It sets balanceIn = reserves[direction] (the configured “second token”) and balanceOut = reserves[(direction+1)%2] (the other token, intended to be OLAS), then returns (balanceOut * 1e18) / balanceIn. This is OLAS-per-secondToken, i.e., the inverse of “OLAS price in second token” as commonly understood (secondToken-per-OLAS) and also inconsistent with validatePrice’s comment (“TWAP for OLAS in terms of native token”). No other scaling/inversion is applied elsewhere, so the returned value is indeed inverted relative to the typical intended quote. There are no safeguards (no explicit unit naming, no dual-return, no inversion parameter). It is exploitable whenever downstream code assumes the return is secondToken/OLAS; the impact can be severe mispricing in swaps/liquidity actions. Likelihood is Occasional because it depends on integrators actually consuming getPrice under the assumed denomination.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `getPrice` function is documented to return the "current OLAS token price". In standard financial terms, this means the value of OLAS denominated in the second token (e.g., USDC per OLAS). However, the implementation calculates `balanceOut` (OLAS) divided by `balanceIn` (SecondToken), effectively returning the amount of OLAS per 1 unit of SecondToken (the price of the SecondToken in OLAS).

Additionally, `validatePrice` fetches the Uniswap V2 cumulative price corresponding to the pair's price (Reserve1/Reserve0 or vice versa). Due to the `direction` logic, `validatePrice` selects the cumulative variable that tracks the 'Price of OLAS', while `getPrice` returns the 'Price of SecondToken'. While the `validatePrice` calculation cancels out this mismatch due to the tautology bug (Finding 1), the `getPrice` function returns the mathematical inverse of the expected price. Integrators expecting the price of OLAS will receive `1 / Price`, leading to massive valuation errors (e.g., if OLAS is $10 and USDC is $1, it returns 0.1 instead of 10).

## Impact
Integrators (like LiquidityManager) relying on `getPrice` to value OLAS will use a grossly incorrect price (the inverse), potentially selling assets at a fraction of their value or minting excessive tokens.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy Oracle with OLAS and USDC pair.
2. Assume Reserves: 100 OLAS, 1000 USDC. Real Price of OLAS = 10 USDC.
3. Call `getPrice()`.
4. `direction` logic selects `balanceIn` = 1000 (USDC), `balanceOut` = 100 (OLAS).
5. Returns `(100 * 1e18) / 1000` = `0.1 * 1e18`.
6. Return value implies 1 OLAS = 0.1 USDC, which is 100x lower than reality.

## Proof of Code
function testInvertedPrice() public {
    uint256 reserveOLAS = 100e18;
    uint256 reserveUSDC = 1000e18;
    // Logic from getPrice
    uint256 price = (reserveOLAS * 1e18) / reserveUSDC;
    // Expected: 10e18 (10 USDC per OLAS)
    // Actual: 0.1e18
    assertEq(price, 0.1e18);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Invert the calculation in `getPrice`. Set `balanceIn` to OLAS reserves and `balanceOut` to SecondToken reserves (or swap the division order) to return `SecondToken / OLAS`.
```

### H-8 / `n1A5RFZkv7pfuahxMDoXy`
- Finding title: Contract non-functional due to missing setters for allowlist configuration

```md
## [H-8]. Contract non-functional due to missing setters for allowlist configuration

## id: n1A5RFZkv7pfuahxMDoXy

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
DoS / Initialization Missing

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
VerifyData.N/A

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: VerifyData defines mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds, and _verifyData requires it to be true for the packed (target|selector|chainId) key. In the provided code (VerifyData, VerifyBridgedData, ProcessBridgedDataGnosis) there is no constructor initialization and no external/admin setter to ever write this mapping. Therefore the mapping stays all-false, so any call reaching _verifyData reverts NotAuthorized, bricking processBridgeData for all real payloads. This is immediate, unconditional DoS with no mitigation besides redeploying/adding a setter.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `ProcessBridgedDataGnosis` contract inherits from `VerifyBridgedData` and `VerifyData`. The `VerifyData` contract defines the `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds` mapping which is critical for the authorization logic in `_verifyData`. However, the provided code for `VerifyData` (and the inheriting contracts) does not contain any functions to set or update this mapping (e.g., `setAuthorizedTarget`). Since the default value for all keys in a mapping is `false`, and there is no mechanism to change it, every call to `processBridgeData` will eventually fail at `_verifyData` with `NotAuthorized`. This renders the contract permanently unusable for its intended purpose.

## Impact
The contract is functionally bricked; it cannot verify any data successfully, causing all dependent governance actions to fail.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `ProcessBridgedDataGnosis`.
2. Attempt to call `processBridgeData` with any valid payload.
3. The execution flow reaches `_verifyData`.
4. `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds` lookup returns `false` (default).
5. Reverts with `NotAuthorized`.
6. Observe there is no function in the contract ABI to update the map.

## Proof of Code
contract TestMissingSetters { function test() public { /* Inspection of source shows no setters */ } }

## Suggested Mitigation
Add an authenticated setter function (e.g., `onlyOwner` or `onlyTimelock`) to `VerifyData` or `ProcessBridgedDataGnosis` to allow configuring `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds`.
```

### H-9 / `xiIyanPs6cnpJX0o9weoy`
- Finding title: DoS on USDT interactions due to incompatible ERC20 interface in approve calls
- Report lines: 1823-1866
```md
## [H-9]. DoS on USDT interactions due to incompatible ERC20 interface in approve calls

## id: xiIyanPs6cnpJX0o9weoy

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
NonStandardERC20Behavior

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
LiquidityManagerETH._checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The failure mode matches USDT behavior directly (no return data on approve), and USDT is in-scope; it does not require a different non-standard token.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The contract forces a return value check on `approve()` calls by defining the `IToken` interface with `function approve(...) external returns (bool);`. While compliant with ERC20, widely used tokens like USDT (Tether) do not return a boolean, violating the standard but being de facto standard. When `LiquidityManagerETH` calls `approve` on USDT (e.g., in `convertToV3`, `changeRanges`, or `increaseLiquidity`), the transaction will revert because the EVM expects 32 bytes of return data but receives 0. This bricks all functionality for USDT pools.

## Impact
The protocol cannot support USDT, a primary stablecoin, causing Denial of Service for any USDT-paired pools.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `LiquidityManagerETH`. 2. Attempt to call `convertToV3` or `increaseLiquidity` with USDT as one of the tokens. 3. The `IToken(USDT).approve(...)` call executes. 4. USDT returns void (no data). 5. The Solidity runtime attempts to decode a `bool` from the return data, fails, and reverts the transaction.

## Proof of Code
contract MockUSDT { mapping(address => mapping(address => uint256)) public allowance; function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) external { allowance[msg.sender][spender] = amount; } } 
 // In a Foundry test: 
 function testUSDTApproveReverts() public { MockUSDT usdt = new MockUSDT(); LiquidityManagerETH lm = new LiquidityManagerETH(address(0), address(0), address(0), address(0), 10, address(0), address(0)); 
 vm.expectRevert(); 
 // This mimics the internal call structure 
 IToken(address(usdt)).approve(address(1), 100); }

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeTransferLib.safeApprove` or use `address(token).call(...)` to handle non-compliant return values. Since `SafeTransferLib` is already imported, use `SafeTransferLib.safeApprove` (if available in the specific version) or implement a safe approval helper that ignores return data size if the call succeeded.
```

## [M-10]. DoS with USDT due to incorrect approve interface

## id: NPHnqoRRjrDXL__imdksF

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UncheckedReturn

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
LiquidityManagerETH.convertToV3

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The incompatibility is specifically triggered by USDT-style behavior (no return value on approve), and USDT is explicitly in-scope; it does not require some other exotic non-standard token.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The contracts `LiquidityManagerETH` and `BuyBackBurnerUniswap` use the standard `IERC20` interface which expects `approve` to return a `bool`. 
- In `LiquidityManagerETH.convertToV3` and `increaseLiquidity`, `IToken(tokens[i]).approve(...)` is called.
- In `BuyBackBurnerUniswap._performSwap`, `IERC20(token).approve(...)` is called.

Tokens like USDT (which is explicitly in-scope) do not return a boolean value on `approve`. This causes the transaction to revert due to a function signature/return data size mismatch when compiled with modern Solidity versions, effectively bricking the protocol for USDT pools and buybacks.

## Impact
Inability to manage liquidity or perform buybacks for USDT and other non-compliant ERC20 tokens.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `LiquidityManagerETH` managing an OLAS/USDT pool.
2. Call `increaseLiquidity` providing USDT.
3. The contract calls `IToken(usdt).approve(...)`.
4. The call reverts because USDT's `approve` implementation returns `void`, but the interface expects `bool`.

## Proof of Code
function testUSDTDoS() public {
    // Mock USDT behavior (no return)
    deployMockUSDT();
    // Attempt call
    vm.expectRevert(); // EvmError: Return data length mismatch
    liquidityManager.increaseLiquidity(tokens, fee, burnRate);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeTransferLib.safeApprove` (which is already imported in `BuyBackBurner`, though not used for approval in the Uniswap implementation) or perform a low-level call to `approve` and ignore the return data size if necessary.
```

### M-11 / `WFQStm95ZAXZro6Pm_23W`
- Finding title: Infinite signature validity and missing revocation in `ServiceManager`
- Report lines: 1919-1962
```md
## [M-11]. Infinite signature validity and missing revocation in `ServiceManager`

## id: WFQStm95ZAXZro6Pm_23W

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
PermitDeadlineBypass

## Exploit Type
PermitDeadlineBypass

## Location
ServiceManager.unbondWithSignature

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The signed data for `Unbond` and `RegisterAgents` contains no deadline/expiry (see `UNBOND_TYPE_HASH` and `REGISTER_AGENTS_TYPE_HASH`), so signatures are valid indefinitely until the specific nonce is consumed. Additionally, `operatorApproveHash()` only sets approvals to `true` and there is no revoke/unapprove function. Operators also cannot invalidate pending signatures by incrementing nonces via an operator-only method; nonces only increment when the service owner executes `*_WithSignature`. A malicious/changed-intent service owner can therefore execute stale authorizations much later, forcing registration attempts (and token deposits if allowance remains) or forcing unbonding against operator preference.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The EIP-712 signatures used in `unbondWithSignature` and `registerAgentsWithSignature` do not include a deadline or expiry timestamp. Furthermore, the `OperatorSignedHashes` contract (inherited by `ServiceManager`) allows operators to approve hashes via `operatorApproveHash` (enabling `v=5` signatures) but provides no function to revoke these approvals. As a result, once an operator signs or approves a message, the service owner can execute it at any time in the future, even if the operator's intent or the operating conditions have changed. This exposes the operator to indefinite liability.

## Impact
Operators cannot cancel stale or compromised authorizations, potentially leading to forced bonding/unbonding against their current will.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Operator signs a `RegisterAgents` message for a service. 2. Conditions change (e.g., bond requirement increases or market crashes), and Operator decides not to participate. 3. Operator cannot revoke the signature (no nonce increment function available for direct calls) and cannot revoke approval if pre-approved. 4. Service Owner executes the signature months later, locking the Operator's funds.

## Proof of Code
function testInfiniteValidity() public {
    // 1. Operator signs
    // 2. Time passes (vm.warp)
    // 3. Signature still valid
}

## Suggested Mitigation
1. Add a `deadline` parameter to the `unbond` and `registerAgents` structs and verify `block.timestamp <= deadline` in `_verifySignedHash` or the calling function.
2. Add a `revokeOperatorHash(bytes32 hash)` function to `OperatorSignedHashes` to allow setting `mapOperatorApprovedHashes` to `false`.
```

### H-12 / `fmYSYKHqWM3K2OSAbkZwq`
- Finding title: DoS in ProcessBridgedDataGnosis due to unsafe assembly casting in VerifyBridgedData
- Report lines: 1963-2037
```md
## [H-12]. DoS in ProcessBridgedDataGnosis due to unsafe assembly casting in VerifyBridgedData

## id: fmYSYKHqWM3K2OSAbkZwq

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Unsafe assembly type casting

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataGnosis.processBridgeData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This is the same core DoS as JKukh0TEDERpyoA6wyy8M/tBnx5DC_Z7o0XTMSac8Ze: VerifyBridgedData reads `payloadLength` with `mload` over a word containing (target suffix + value + length) and does not mask to 32 bits, so payloadLength is polluted by upper bytes. Using this polluted value in `new bytes(payloadLength)` causes OOG/memory reverts for ordinary targets, breaking verification.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData` function uses inline assembly to unpack a packed byte stream containing `(address target, uint96 value, uint32 payloadLength, bytes payload)`. When extracting `payloadLength`, the code performs an `mload` from an offset that aligns the 4-byte length at the least significant bits of the loaded 32-byte word. However, the assembly block does not mask the loaded value to 32 bits. 

The loaded word at that position contains `[Target Suffix (16 bytes)] [Value (12 bytes)] [Length (4 bytes)]`. Because the `Target Suffix` and `Value` are present in the upper 224 bits of the word, and `target` addresses are rarely zero in their lower bytes, the `uint32 payloadLength` variable is assigned a dirty 256-bit value (a very large integer). 

Subsequently, the code attempts to allocate memory using `bytes memory payload = new bytes(payloadLength);`. Since `payloadLength` is interpreted as a massive integer, this allocation exceeds the gas limit or memory bounds, causing the transaction to revert. This affects all bridged messages where the target address has non-zero bytes in indices [4..19], effectively causing a Denial of Service for the governance bridge.

## Impact
The bridge verification logic consistently reverts for valid messages, blocking cross-chain governance actions and preventing the Gnosis Safe Guard from authorizing legitimate transactions.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Construct a valid packed payload for the bridge: `abi.encodePacked(address(0x1234...1234), uint96(0), uint32(4), bytes4(0xdeadbeef))`. 
2. Call `processBridgeData` with this payload properly wrapped in the L1/L2 selectors.
3. The verifier will unpack the `target`. The assembly `mload` for `payloadLength` will read a word containing part of the target address in the high bits.
4. `payloadLength` becomes a huge number (e.g., `0x1234...00000004`).
5. `new bytes(payloadLength)` reverts due to out-of-gas/memory.
6. The transaction fails.

## Proof of Code
contract VulnerabilityTest is Test {
    VerifyBridgedDataHarness verifier;
    function setUp() public {
        verifier = new VerifyBridgedDataHarness();
    }
    function testDos() public {
        // Construct packed L2 message: Target(20) | Value(12) | Len(4) | Payload
        // Target has non-zero bytes in 4..19 range to trigger dirty bits
        address target = address(uint160(uint256(keccak256("target"))));
        bytes memory l2Msg = abi.encodePacked(target, uint96(0), uint32(4), bytes4(0x11223344));
        
        // Wrap in AMB selectors
        bytes memory mediatorPayload = abi.encodePacked(bytes4(keccak256("processMessageFromForeign(bytes)")), l2Msg);
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(
            bytes4(keccak256("requireToPassMessage(address,bytes,uint256)")),
            abi.encode(address(0xBAD), abi.encode(mediatorPayload), uint256(0))
        );
        
        // Expect revert due to OOG/Memory
        vm.expectRevert(); 
        verifier.processBridgeData(data, address(0xBAD), 100);
    }
}

contract VerifyBridgedDataHarness is ProcessBridgedDataGnosis {
    // Helper to access internal
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Mask the loaded value to 32 bits in the assembly block:
`payloadLength := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xffffffff)`
```

### M-13 / `gxRokS-Fs7ExSdykZqntv`
- Finding title: Incompatible ERC20 interface causes DoS and stuck funds for USDT on Mainnet

```md
## [M-13]. Incompatible ERC20 interface causes DoS and stuck funds for USDT on Mainnet

## id: gxRokS-Fs7ExSdykZqntv

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer.buyBack / transfer

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: USDT on mainnet is itself a void-return token for approve/transfer; the issue does not require a non-standard token other than USDT.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurnerBalancer` contract uses the `IERC20` interface which defines `approve` and `transfer` as returning a `bool`. However, USDT on Ethereum Mainnet does not conform to the ERC20 standard's return value requirement (it returns `void`). 

When `buyBack` is called for USDT (V2 flow uses `approve`) or when `transfer` is called to sweep USDT to the treasury (uses `transfer`), the calls will revert due to ABI decoding errors (missing return data). This results in a Denial of Service for USDT operations and can lead to permanently stuck USDT funds in the contract.

## Impact
Funds (USDT) sent to the contract on Mainnet cannot be swept or used for buybacks, resulting in effective loss of funds.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `BuyBackBurnerBalancer` on a fork of Mainnet or a testnet.
2. Send USDT to the contract.
3. Call `transfer(address(usdt))`.
4. The transaction reverts because `IERC20(usdt).transfer` expects a boolean return value, but USDT implementation returns nothing.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {BuyBackBurnerBalancer} from "contracts/BuyBackBurnerBalancer.sol";

contract MockUSDT {
    // Mimic Mainnet USDT: No return value
    function approve(address, uint256) external {}
    function transfer(address, uint256) external {}
    function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint256) { return 1000; }
}

contract USDTDosTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerBalancer bbb;
    MockUSDT usdt;

    function setUp() public {
        bbb = new BuyBackBurnerBalancer(address(0x1), address(0x2), address(0x3), address(0x4));
        usdt = new MockUSDT();
    }

    function testUSDTRevert() public {
        // Give contract USDT
        // Try to sweep
        vm.expectRevert(); // Reverts due to decoding error
        bbb.transfer(address(usdt));
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use OpenZeppelin's `SafeERC20` library or Solmate's `SafeTransferLib` to handle non-compliant tokens like USDT safely.
```

### M-14 / `ieN07-aeT52ezbToLzBpo`
- Finding title: `changeRanges` causes persistent DoS and misroutes funds for single-sided positions
- Report lines: 2107-2170
```md
## [M-14]. `changeRanges` causes persistent DoS and misroutes funds for single-sided positions

## id: ieN07-aeT52ezbToLzBpo

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.changeRanges

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: In LiquidityManagerCore.changeRanges(), after _decreaseLiquidity() and _collectFees(), minting a new position is skipped unless both amounts are >0. If the burned position is effectively single-sided (common for out-of-range UniV3 positions, and fees may not compensate), the function does not mint and also does not clear mapPoolAddressPositionIds[pool]. It then calls _manageUtilityAmounts(MAX_BPS,false), sweeping whatever was withdrawn to treasury. The mapping still points to the now-zero-liquidity position; subsequent changeRanges()/decreaseLiquidity() will revert in _decreaseLiquidity() due to liquidity==0. This is a reproducible broken state requiring manual recovery steps.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
In `changeRanges`, the contract decreases liquidity and collects tokens. It then attempts to mint a new position using `_calculateTicksAndMintPosition`. However, the minting logic is guarded by `if (amounts[0] > 0 && amounts[1] > 0)`. If the current position is out-of-range such that withdrawal yields only one token (e.g., `amounts[0] > 0` but `amounts[1] == 0`), the minting block is skipped. 

The function then proceeds to sweep the withdrawn funds to the treasury via `_manageUtilityAmounts`. Crucially, it does **not** clear `mapPoolAddressPositionIds[pool]`, which still points to the (now empty) position ID. Subsequent calls to `decreaseLiquidity` or `changeRanges` will revert because the position has 0 liquidity. The funds intended for rebalancing are moved to the treasury, and the contract is left in a broken state for that pool requiring manual refunding and `increaseLiquidity` to fix.

## Impact
Operational DoS of the `LiquidityManager` for the affected pool. Funds intended for rebalancing are effectively closed out to the treasury instead of redeployed, breaking the rebalancing logic.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Pool price moves significantly, placing the protocol's position entirely out of range. 
2. Owner calls `changeRanges` to rebalance. 
3. `_decreaseLiquidity` and `_collectFees` return tokens, but one amount is 0 (due to being out of range). 
4. The `if` condition for minting fails. 
5. The single-sided funds are swept to treasury. 
6. The transaction succeeds, but `mapPoolAddressPositionIds` still links to the empty position. 
7. Owner calls `changeRanges` again (or `decreaseLiquidity`). It reverts with `ZeroValue` because the position is empty. 
8. The pool management is bricked until manual intervention.

## Proof of Code
function testChangeRangesDoS() public {
    // Setup out of range position
    movePriceOutOfRange(pool);
    
    // Call changeRanges
    vm.prank(owner);
    liquidityManager.changeRanges(tokens, feeTier, shifts, false);
    
    // Assert mapping still exists
    uint256 pid = liquidityManager.mapPoolAddressPositionIds(pool);
    assertGt(pid, 0);
    
    // Assert next call fails
    vm.expectRevert(ZeroValue.selector);
    vm.prank(owner);
    liquidityManager.changeRanges(tokens, feeTier, shifts, false);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In `changeRanges`, if the minting condition is not met, explicitly clear the mapping `mapPoolAddressPositionIds[pool] = 0` to reflect that the position is closed. Ideally, implement swapping logic or allow single-sided minting if appropriate.
```

### H-15 / `YR9XLGUR-OjmKgTXmj0Zd`
- Finding title: Critical DoS in BridgeMessenger due to unsafe assembly bitmasking in `_processData`
- Report lines: 2171-2255
```md
## [H-15]. Critical DoS in BridgeMessenger due to unsafe assembly bitmasking in `_processData`

## id: YR9XLGUR-OjmKgTXmj0Zd

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
OptimismMessenger._processData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This finding describes the same core defect as eUVQ3Y: _processData decodes packed fields with mload but does not mask, so uint96 value and uint32 payloadLength retain contaminated high bits. For any non-zero target, value becomes astronomically large and immediately fails the balance check, reverting every legitimate bridged governance transaction. Additionally, payloadLength can also become huge (tainted by preceding bytes), making new bytes(payloadLength) revert due to memory expansion, further confirming DoS. No existing safeguards (no masking, no abi.decode usage) mitigate this. The bug exists in the exact provided BridgeMessenger code and is directly reachable via OptimismMessenger.processMessageFromSource.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `_processData` function in `BridgeMessenger.sol` (inherited by `OptimismMessenger`) uses inline assembly to unpack `target` (20 bytes), `value` (12 bytes), and `payloadLength` (4 bytes) from the `data` buffer. However, the `mload` instruction loads full 32-byte words, and the code fails to apply bitwise masks to isolate the specific fields. 

Because the data is packed sequentially (`[target][value][payloadLength]...`), the 32-byte word loaded for `value` contains the `target` address in its most significant bits. Since Solidity 0.8+ does not automatically clean dirty bits from inline assembly assignments, the `value` variable holds a massive integer (approx `target << 96`). This causes the check `if (value > address(this).balance)` to incorrectly fail and revert with `InsufficientBalance` for any valid message with a non-zero target. Additionally, `payloadLength` is also tainted with high-order bits from `target` and `value`, which causes `new bytes(payloadLength)` to attempt an impossibly large memory allocation, also reverting. This defect permanently bricks the governance bridge.

## Impact
Complete loss of cross-chain governance functionality. The L1 Timelock cannot execute any transactions on L2, effectively permanently freezing the L2 protocol state.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `OptimismMessenger`. 
2. As the authorized L2 CrossDomainMessenger, call `processMessageFromSource` with a valid packed payload (e.g., `target` = non-zero address, `value` = 0, `payloadLength` = 0). 
3. The `_processData` function parses `value`. Due to lack of masking, `value` includes the `target` address in the high bits, making it a huge number. 
4. The check `value > address(this).balance` evaluates to true. 
5. The transaction reverts with `InsufficientBalance`, blocking the governance action.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/bridges/OptimismMessenger.sol";

contract MockCDM {
    function xDomainMessageSender() external pure returns (address) {
        return address(0x456);
    }
}

contract OptimismMessengerTest is Test {
    OptimismMessenger messenger;
    MockCDM cdm;
    address governor = address(0x456);

    function setUp() public {
        cdm = new MockCDM();
        messenger = new OptimismMessenger(address(cdm), governor);
        vm.deal(address(messenger), 100 ether);
    }

    function test_DoS_ParsingBug() public {
        // Construct valid data: target=address(0x123), value=0, len=0
        address target = address(0x123);
        uint96 value = 0;
        uint32 pLen = 0;
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(target, value, pLen);
        
        // Expect revert because 'value' is read as dirty (huge) number
        vm.prank(address(cdm));
        // We expect InsufficientBalance because value (dirty) > balance
        // The huge value provided in error is approx 0x123 << 96
        vm.expectRevert(abi.encodeWithSelector(IBridgeErrors.InsufficientBalance.selector, uint256(uint160(target)) << 96, 100 ether));
        messenger.processMessageFromSource(data);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Apply explicit bitwise masking in the assembly block to clean the loaded values:

```solidity
target := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF)
value := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF)
payloadLength := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xFFFFFFFF)
```
```

### M-16 / `qXm3skKOM1buPaPPeXVrA`
- Finding title: DoS of Service Redeployment via malicious Operator in unbond
- Report lines: 2256-2306
```md
## [M-16]. DoS of Service Redeployment via malicious Operator in unbond

## id: qXm3skKOM1buPaPPeXVrA

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
PullorPushPaymentbugs

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
ServiceRegistry.unbond

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: ServiceRegistry.unbond() does a push-payment refund via (bool result,) = operator.call{value: refund}("") and reverts on failure (TransferFailed). If an operator is a contract that reverts on receiving ETH, unbond() always reverts, so service.numAgentInstances is not decremented and the service remains stuck in TerminatedBonded (unbond is the only transition path to PreRegistration when bonded instances exist). No alternative refund path or pull-balance exists. This blocks redeployment/updates for that Service NFT. Requires a malicious (or incompatible) operator address, which is realistic for permissionless registration.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `unbond` function in `ServiceRegistry.sol` transfers ETH bonds back to the operator using a push payment pattern: `(bool result, ) = operator.call{value: refund}("");`. It explicitly checks the return value and reverts if the transfer fails (`if (!result) { revert TransferFailed(...); }`). 

A malicious operator (or a contract that cannot receive ETH) can cause this transfer to fail by reverting in their receive/fallback function. Since `unbond` is the only mechanism to decrement `service.numAgentInstances` and eventually transition the service state from `TerminatedBonded` to `PreRegistration`, blocking `unbond` effectively jams the service in the `TerminatedBonded` state. This prevents the Service Owner from ever redeploying or resetting the service.

## Impact
The service becomes permanently stuck in `TerminatedBonded` state, preventing redeployment/reuse.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Operator registers an agent instance using a smart contract that reverts on ETH receipt. 
2. Service Owner terminates the service via `terminate()`. State becomes `TerminatedBonded`. 
3. Service Manager (or Owner) attempts to call `unbond(operator, serviceId)` to clear the instance slots. 
4. The `operator.call{value: refund}` fails and `unbond` reverts. 
5. The service cannot transition to `PreRegistration`.

## Proof of Code
contract RevertingOperator {
    receive() external payable { revert("No ETH"); }
}
// In test:
// deploy Service, register RevertingOperator.
// terminate Service.
// call unbond -> reverts.

## Suggested Mitigation
Use a Pull payment pattern (allow operators to withdraw separately) or do not revert on transfer failure (instead moving funds to a 'drainable' balance or the `drainer`).
```

### M-17 / `i-gmsNtIwpOl7nzzrZ3QV`
- Finding title: Missing transaction expiration check in liquidity management functions
- Report lines: 2307-2358
```md
## [M-17]. Missing transaction expiration check in liquidity management functions

## id: i-gmsNtIwpOl7nzzrZ3QV

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.increaseLiquidity

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: _increaseLiquidity() and _decreaseLiquidity() set Uniswap V3 PositionManager deadlines to block.timestamp. Because this value is evaluated at execution time (not at signing/submission time), the deadline check becomes meaningless (block.timestamp <= block.timestamp). This removes any time-bounded execution protection and allows inclusion much later than the owner intended. There is no other time-based safeguard in LiquidityManagerCore to constrain execution timing.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
Functions like `increaseLiquidity`, `decreaseLiquidity`, and internal helpers pass `block.timestamp` as the `deadline` to `IPositionManagerV3`. This effectively disables the expiration check, meaning a transaction stuck in the mempool can be executed much later than intended, potentially under very different market conditions/prices.

## Impact
Liquidity operations can be executed at stale prices if transactions are delayed, leading to unpredictable execution and potential loss of value for the DAO.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. DAO submits a transaction to `increaseLiquidity`.
2. Network congestion delays inclusion.
3. Market moves significantly.
4. Transaction is included days later.
5. Since `deadline` is `block.timestamp`, it passes the check in Uniswap V3, executing the operation at a time/price the DAO did not intend.

## Proof of Code
function testNoDeadline() public {
    // Snapshot state
    vm.warp(1000);
    // Manager calls increaseLiquidity with deadline = block.timestamp
    // ...
    // Even if we warp to 1 year later, the call inside the contract uses current block.timestamp
    vm.warp(31536000 + 1000);
    // Call succeeds because deadline argument becomes 'now'
    // Expected: Should allow user to pass a fixed deadline
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add a `deadline` parameter to external functions (`convertToV3`, `increaseLiquidity`, etc.) and pass it to the underlying `IPositionManagerV3` calls instead of using `block.timestamp`.
```

### M-18 / `2keXmUkz03Q3OUWgVbuNJ`
- Finding title: NeighborhoodScanner chooses wrong optimization mode for unbalanced amounts causing capital inefficiency
- Report lines: 2359-2412
```md
## [M-18]. NeighborhoodScanner chooses wrong optimization mode for unbalanced amounts causing capital inefficiency

## id: 2keXmUkz03Q3OUWgVbuNJ

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Optimization Logic Error

## Exploit Type
IncentiveMisalignmentOrGameTheory

## Location
NeighborhoodScanner._chooseMode

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: _chooseMode returns (V0 >= V1) and _scanNeighborhood uses that to select pickHiMaxUtil when token0 value dominates. pickHiMaxUtil explicitly sets liquidity using amounts[1] via getLiquidityForAmount1(sa, sqrtP, amounts[1]) (token1-limited), so when token1 is scarce and token0 abundant, liquidity is capped small and cannot consume most of token0 even if hi is widened to MAX_TICK. Given the contract’s explicit utilization metric (utilization1e18) and neighborhood search trying to maximize utilization, choosing the token1-limited path when token1 is the scarce side is inconsistent with the stated goal and can be reproduced with highly unbalanced inputs. There is no in-code mitigation beyond a very local neighborhood search (+/-4 spacing), which cannot correct a globally wrong branch choice.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_chooseMode` function determines whether to fix the lower tick and optimize the upper (`pickHiMaxUtil`) or vice versa (`pickLoMaxUtil`) based on the relative value of token balances (`V0` vs `V1`). The current logic returns `true` (selecting `pickHiMaxUtil`) when `V0 >= V1`. `pickHiMaxUtil` fixes the lower tick `lo`, which constrains liquidity `L` based on `amount1` (`V1`). If `V0` is large (abundant) and `V1` is small (scarce), `L` is constrained by the scarce asset to a small value. Consequently, even with the widest possible upper tick, the position cannot utilize the abundant `amount0`. To maximize utilization, the protocol should use `pickLoMaxUtil` when `V0` is abundant, letting `V0` determine a large `L` and narrowing the lower tick `lo` to fit the scarce `V1`. The inverted logic consistently forces the inefficient strategy.

## Impact
High capital inefficiency for unbalanced liquidity provisions (e.g., single-sided or rebalancing strategies), leaving the majority of funds unutilized.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Caller has `100 ETH` (token0) and `1 USDC` (token1). `sqrtP` corresponds to 2000 USDC/ETH.
2. `V0` (100 ETH) >> `V1` (1 USDC). `_chooseMode` returns `true` (optimizeHi).
3. Calls `pickHiMaxUtil`, fixing `lo`. Liquidity `L` is calculated from 1 USDC over `[lo, P]`, resulting in a tiny `L`.
4. Scanner finds best `hi` for tiny `L`. Even at max range, `amount0` consumed is tiny.
5. Result: 1 USDC used, ~0.001 ETH used, 99.999 ETH idle. Utilization ~0%.
6. Correct logic (`pickLoMaxUtil`) would fix `hi`, set `L` based on 100 ETH, and shrink `lo` close to `P` to fit 1 USDC, achieving ~100% utilization.

## Proof of Code
function testWrongMode() public { 
    // Setup unbalanced amounts: lots of token0, dust token1
    uint256[] memory amounts = new uint256[](2);
    amounts[0] = 100 ether; amounts[1] = 1000; // tiny amount1
    uint160 sqrtP = TickMath.getSqrtRatioAtTick(0); // Price 1.0
    // _chooseMode logic check
    // V0 = 100 ether, V1 = 1000. V0 > V1.
    // Returns true -> pickHiMaxUtil.
    // pickHiMaxUtil fixes lo. Liquidity is limited by amount1 (1000). 
    // L is tiny. amount0 usage is tiny. 100 ether wasted.
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Invert the condition in `_chooseMode` or swap the branches in `_scanNeighborhood`. If `V0 >= V1`, use `pickLoMaxUtil` (optimize `lo`). If `V1 > V0`, use `pickHiMaxUtil` (optimize `hi`).
```

### H-19 / `GHMSoOJ2HCch-nPtHNErS`
- Finding title: Missing configuration setters for `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds` renders verification unusable
- Report lines: 2413-2451
```md
## [H-19]. Missing configuration setters for `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds` renders verification unusable

## id: GHMSoOJ2HCch-nPtHNErS

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccessControlOrAuthByPass

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataGnosis.N/A

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Same root cause as n1A5RFZkv7pfuahxMDoXy: VerifyData contains the allowlist mapping and _verifyData enforces it, but the shown contracts expose no mechanism to populate it (no setter, no constructor writes). Since mappings default to false, _verifyData always reverts NotAuthorized, making ProcessBridgedDataGnosis unusable for its intended verification role.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The abstract contract `VerifyData` defines the `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds` mapping which is critical for the authorization logic in `_verifyData`. However, the provided codebase (including the concrete `ProcessBridgedDataGnosis` implementation) lacks any function to set or update this mapping (e.g., a `setAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds` function protected by `onlyOwner`). As the mapping defaults to all false/empty and cannot be populated, every call to `processBridgeData` will invariably revert with `NotAuthorized` (unless the target is the zero address which reverts earlier, or the key collides with 0 which is impossible). This renders the contract functionally useless for its intended purpose of verifying bridged data.

## Impact
The contract is permanently bricked; it will reject all valid bridge messages, causing a Denial of Service for any governance flow relying on this verifier.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `ProcessBridgedDataGnosis`. 2. Attempt to verify any valid payload using `processBridgeData`. 3. The call calls `_verifyData`, which checks `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds`. 4. Since the map is empty and there is no function to write to it, the check fails and reverts `NotAuthorized`. 5. Observe there are no external/public functions in the contract to modify the mapping.

## Proof of Code
contract MissingSetterTest is Test { ProcessBridgedDataGnosis verifier; function setUp() public { verifier = new ProcessBridgedDataGnosis(); } function testVerifierIsBricked() public { bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(bytes4(verifier.REQUIRE_TO_PASS_MESSAGE()), bytes32(0), bytes32(uint256(32)), bytes32(uint256(32)), uint256(0)); vm.expectRevert(); verifier.processBridgeData(data, address(0x1), 1); } }

## Suggested Mitigation
Introduce an authenticated setter function (e.g., `setAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds`) in `VerifyData` or `ProcessBridgedDataGnosis`, secured by an `onlyOwner` modifier, to allow the governance/admin to populate the allowlist.
```

### M-20 / `jYEv6OQfADRnQlFLeHda6`
- Finding title: Incompatibility with USDT due to strict ERC20 interface return check causing DOS
- Report lines: 2452-2531
```md
## [M-20]. Incompatibility with USDT due to strict ERC20 interface return check causing DOS

## id: jYEv6OQfADRnQlFLeHda6

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UncheckedERC20Return

## Exploit Type
UncheckedERC20Return

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer._performSwap / transfer

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The issue is triggered by USDT itself (and other void-return ERC20s). Saying it requires a non-standard token 'other than USDT' is incorrect.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The contract defines the `IERC20` interface with `approve` and `transfer` functions returning `bool`. 
```solidity
interface IERC20 {
    function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) external returns (bool);
    // ...
}
```
It uses this interface to approve the Balancer/SwapRouter and to sweep tokens.
```solidity
IERC20(secondToken).approve(balVault, secondTokenAmount);
```
USDT and other older tokens do not return a boolean value (return void). In Solidity 0.8+, calling a function that returns less data than declared in the interface causes the transaction to revert. Since `buyBack` is intended to work with various `secondToken`s accumulated by the protocol, this renders the buyback functionality broken for USDT.

## Impact
Permanent Denial of Service for buybacks involving USDT or other non-compliant tokens. Funds in USDT cannot be used for buybacks or swept to the treasury.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `BuyBackBurnerBalancer`.
2. Configure a USDT-like token (no return value on approve) as `secondToken`.
3. Call `buyBack(usdt, amount)`.
4. The transaction reverts due to Solidity's return data size check, making it impossible to process USDT.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.30;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/BuyBackBurnerBalancer.sol";

contract MockUSDT {
    function approve(address, uint256) external { /* no return */ }
    function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint256) { return 1000; }
    function transfer(address, uint256) external { /* no return */ }
}

contract BuyBackUSDTTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerBalancer buyBackBurner;
    MockUSDT usdt;

    function setUp() public {
        usdt = new MockUSDT();
        // ... setup burner ...
        buyBackBurner = new BuyBackBurnerBalancer(address(0x1), address(0x2), address(0x3), address(0x4));
    }

    function testUSDTRevert() public {
        // This will revert because interface expects bool but MockUSDT returns void
        vm.expectRevert(); 
        // Note: Revert reason is empty/decoding error in sol 0.8
        IERC20(address(usdt)).approve(address(0x4), 100);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use OpenZeppelin's `SafeERC20` library or `solmate`'s `SafeTransferLib` for `approve` and `transfer` calls to handle non-compliant tokens correctly.
```

### M-21 / `DWT9RExdut73r_2ONB5ET`
- Finding title: Lack of rescue mechanism locks funds if bridge is deprecated or paused
