```md
## [M-32]. Oracle validation accepts stale data allowing slippage attacks

## id: BkFnpC_Ic5A85ISATni2R

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Oracle

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
BalancerPriceOracle.validatePrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This is the same missing-staleness-bound issue as wlN5B2Dg…: validatePrice never rejects snapshots that are very old. As a result, the slippage bounds are enforced relative to an old timeWeightedAverage. If the protocol relies on validatePrice as a safety gate, long oracle inactivity can weaken safety, because an attacker only needs to align the pool spot price with the stale baseline to pass. There is no existing safeguard such as require(elapsedTime <= MAX_DELAY) or forced/keeperless refresh logic.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `validatePrice` function compares the current spot price against the oracle's time-weighted average. However, it does not check if the oracle has been updated recently. It projects the last known `averagePrice` over the `elapsedTime` since the last update. If `updatePrice` has not been called for a long period (e.g., due to low activity or the `maxSlippage` check reverting during volatility), the oracle's `averagePrice` will be stale. `validatePrice` will effectively validate the spot price against this ancient price. 

Attackers can exploit this by manipulating the spot price to match the stale oracle price (which may differ significantly from the true market price) and passing the validation check.

## Impact
Protocol mechanisms relying on `validatePrice` for slippage protection or safety checks can be bypassed. Users or the protocol may transact at off-market prices validated by the stale oracle, leading to fund loss.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. `updatePrice` is called at T=0. Price is 100. 
2. Time passes (T=1 year). No updates occur. True market price drops to 50.
3. Attacker wants to sell assets to the protocol (or bypass slippage) at price 100. 
4. Attacker manipulates the Balancer pool spot price to 100 (matching the stale oracle). 
5. Attacker calls `validatePrice`. The function calculates the projected average (which converges to the old 100) and returns `true`. 
6. The protocol accepts the trade at 100, overpaying by 2x.

## Proof of Code
function testStaleOracle() public {
    // Init at 100
    vault.setBalances(100e18, 1e18);
    oracle.updatePrice();
    
    // Warp 1 year without updates
    vm.warp(block.timestamp + 365 days);
    
    // Real market price crashes to 10. 
    // But we manipulate spot to 100 to pass validation.
    vault.setBalances(100e18, 1e18);
    
    // Validate against stale oracle
    bool valid = oracle.validatePrice(1); // 1% slippage
    assertTrue(valid);
    // The validation passes despite the oracle being 1 year old.
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add a staleness check in `validatePrice`: `require(block.timestamp - snapshot.lastUpdated <= MAX_ORACLE_DELAY, "Oracle stale");`. Alternatively, require `updatePrice` to be called successfully within a recent window.
```

### M-33 / `PX8ZC_JmvVcf3Uy9DNxlL`
- Finding title: Permissionless `transfer` enables DoS of Buyback mechanism
- Report lines: 3484-3566
```md
## [M-33]. Permissionless `transfer` enables DoS of Buyback mechanism

## id: PX8ZC_JmvVcf3Uy9DNxlL

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccessControl

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer.transfer

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The inherited BuyBackBurner.transfer function is permissionless and can move the entire balance of any ERC20 held by the burner to the treasury (or bridge2Burner for OLAS). This allows any third party to front-run/monitor and sweep the intended buyback input token before a keeper’s buyBack transaction, causing buyBack to execute with zero balance and revert (ZeroValue) or become ineffective. This is a liveness/DoS vector against the burn mechanism, not a direct theft vector.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurnerBalancer` contract includes a `transfer` function that allows any external caller to sweep any ERC20 token held by the contract to the `treasury` (for non-OLAS tokens). This function is permissionless (`external`) and has no access controls.

The intended purpose of the contract is to accumulate tokens and use them to `buyBack` OLAS for burning. However, because `transfer` is permissionless, an attacker can continuously front-run any `buyBack` attempts or simply monitor the contract and sweep funds to the treasury as soon as they arrive. This effectively permanently disables the buyback and burn mechanism, diverting all funds to the treasury instead of reducing OLAS supply as intended by the protocol economics.

## Impact
Functional DoS of the protocol's deflationary mechanism. Tokens intended for buy-and-burn are diverted to the treasury, disrupting the tokenomics model.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. `BuyBackBurnerBalancer` receives `1,000 USDC` intended for buyback.
2. A keeper attempts to call `buyBack(USDC, 1000, ...)`.
3. An attacker front-runs this transaction with `transfer(USDC)`.
4. The `transfer` function executes, sending `1,000 USDC` to the `treasury` address.
5. The keeper's `buyBack` transaction reverts (or processes 0 amount) because the contract's USDC balance is now 0.
6. The buyback never occurs.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/utils/BuyBackBurnerBalancer.sol";

contract MockERC20 {
    mapping(address => uint256) public balanceOf;
    function transfer(address to, uint256 amount) external returns (bool) {
        balanceOf[msg.sender] -= amount;
        balanceOf[to] += amount;
        return true;
    }
    function mint(address to, uint256 amount) external { balanceOf[to] += amount; }
}

contract BuyBackBurnerDoS is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerBalancer public bbb;
    MockERC20 public token;
    address public treasury = address(0xbeef);
    
    function setUp() public {
        token = new MockERC20();
        // Minimal setup
        bbb = new BuyBackBurnerBalancer(address(0x1), address(0x2), treasury, address(0x4));
    }

    function test_PermissionlessTransferDoS() public {
        token.mint(address(bbb), 1000 ether);
        
        // Attacker calls transfer
        vm.prank(address(0xbad));
        bbb.transfer(address(token));
        
        // Balance is gone to treasury
        assertEq(token.balanceOf(address(bbb)), 0);
        assertEq(token.balanceOf(treasury), 1000 ether);
        
        // Buyback is effectively blocked (no funds)
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Restrict the `transfer` function to `onlyOwner` or implement a whitelist of callers/keepers allowed to manage the funds.
```

### M-34 / `XevzWIrGg2g2q_NaF8y6s`
- Finding title: DoS and Stuck Funds due to Incompatibility with Non-Compliant ERC20 Tokens (e.g., USDT)
- Report lines: 3567-3637
```md
## [M-34]. DoS and Stuck Funds due to Incompatibility with Non-Compliant ERC20 Tokens (e.g., USDT)

## id: XevzWIrGg2g2q_NaF8y6s

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer.transfer

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: USDT is a primary example of the affected class (void-return approve/transfer). The downgrade reason that it requires a non-standard token other than USDT is incorrect.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurner` and `BuyBackBurnerBalancer` contracts define the `IERC20` interface for `transfer` and `approve` as returning a `bool`. However, widely used tokens like USDT do not return a value (void). In Solidity 0.8.x, calling a function that expects a return value when none is provided results in a transaction revert due to ABI decoding failure. 

This affects:
1. `_performSwap`: Calls `approve` on the second token. If the second token is USDT, the buyback flow reverts.
2. `transfer`: Calls `transfer` on any token to sweep fees. If the token is USDT, the sweep fails.

Since the contract is designed to collect fees in various tokens, receiving USDT is highly probable. These funds will be permanently stuck as the `transfer` function will always revert.

## Impact
Funds in non-compliant ERC20 tokens (like USDT) collected as fees cannot be withdrawn or used for buybacks, resulting in permanent lockup of assets.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy a MockUSDT contract that has `transfer` and `approve` functions returning nothing (void).
2. Send MockUSDT to the `BuyBackBurnerBalancer` contract.
3. Call `transfer(address(mockUSDT))`.
4. The transaction reverts because the contract expects a boolean return value, but the token returns nothing.

## Proof of Code
contract MockUSDT {
    mapping(address => uint256) public balanceOf;
    function mint(address to, uint256 amount) public { balanceOf[to] += amount; }
    function transfer(address to, uint256 amount) public { balanceOf[msg.sender] -= amount; balanceOf[to] += amount; }
    function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) public {}
}

contract TestUSDTDoS is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerBalancer burner;
    MockUSDT usdt;

    function setUp() public {
        // Deploy burner with mocks
        burner = new BuyBackBurnerBalancer(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(4));
        usdt = new MockUSDT();
    }

    function testTransferReverts() public {
        usdt.mint(address(burner), 1000 ether);
        // This should revert due to ABI decoding error
        vm.expectRevert(); 
        burner.transfer(address(usdt));
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use OpenZeppelin's `SafeERC20` library or Solmate's `SafeTransferLib` for all ERC20 interactions. These libraries handle non-standard return values correctly.
```

### H-35 / `S2LFOwZ-p9PsjXrPOxjYH`
- Finding title: Sybil Attack on `IDF` Calculation via `numNewOwners` Manipulation
- Report lines: 3638-3711
```md
## [H-35]. Sybil Attack on `IDF` Calculation via `numNewOwners` Manipulation

## id: S2LFOwZ-p9PsjXrPOxjYH

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Oracle

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
Tokenomics._trackServiceDonations

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Tokenomics._trackServiceDonations increments `numNewOwners` when previously unseen units are first used and their owners are previously unseen (`mapNewUnits` + `mapNewOwners`). This can be Sybil-manipulated by creating many new units held by many fresh addresses and including them in a donated service. `_calculateIDF` directly increases with `numNewOwners` and is capped by `epsilonRate` (default 10%), so manipulation is bounded but can still deterministically push IDF to the cap (e.g., ~10 new owners suffices under default params). There is no anti-Sybil safeguard or stake/identity requirement. Economic extraction depends on how BondCalculator applies IDF, but protocol docs indicate bond payouts depend on IDF.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `Tokenomics.sol` contract calculates the Inverse Discount Factor (`IDF`) based on the `numNewOwners` metric, which tracks the number of new unique owners of components/agents in services receiving donations. The `_calculateIDF` function rewards a higher number of new owners with a higher `IDF` (up to a cap `epsilonRate`). 

However, `numNewOwners` is vulnerable to a Sybil attack. An attacker can cheaply generate many new addresses, transfer component/agent NFTs to them, and make minimum donations (0.065 ETH) to a service containing these units. `_trackServiceDonations` increments `numNewOwners` for each fresh address found. 

By artificially inflating `numNewOwners`, the attacker can maximize the `IDF` for the next epoch. If the `IDF` is used by the `BondCalculator` to determine bond payouts (where higher `IDF` implies a higher payout/discount), the attacker can extract value from the protocol by buying bonds at a manipulated rate in the subsequent epoch.

## Impact
Manipulation of the protocol's economic parameter (`IDF`) leading to value extraction via bonds. An attacker can boost the IDF by up to 10% (epsilonRate) with low cost.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker prepares N fresh addresses.
2. Attacker sets up a Service and registers N agent instances, assigning ownership of each agent NFT to one of the fresh addresses.
3. Attacker calls `Treasury.depositServiceDonationsETH` with the minimum required ETH (e.g., 0.065 ETH) targeting this service.
4. `Tokenomics` registers N new owners, incrementing `numNewOwners`.
5. `fKD` increases, pushing `IDF` towards its cap (`1.0 + epsilonRate`).
6. Attacker calls `checkpoint` to finalize the epoch.
7. In the new epoch, `IDF` is elevated. Attacker buys bonds using `Depository.deposit`, receiving an inflated amount of OLAS due to the manipulated `IDF`.

## Proof of Code
function testSybilIDF() public {
    // Assume setup with tokenomics, treasury, serviceRegistry, etc.
    // And a deployed service with ID 1
    
    uint256 minDonation = 0.065 ether;
    uint256[] memory serviceIds = new uint256[](1);
    serviceIds[0] = 1;
    uint256[] memory amounts = new uint256[](1);
    amounts[0] = minDonation;

    // Create Sybil owners
    address sybil1 = address(0x101);
    address sybil2 = address(0x102);
    
    // Transfer unit NFTs to Sybils (mocking registry ownership change)
    // assume componentRegistry.ownerOf(unit1) == sybil1
    // assume componentRegistry.ownerOf(unit2) == sybil2
    
    // Donate
    vm.deal(address(this), minDonation);
    treasury.depositServiceDonationsETH{value: minDonation}(serviceIds, amounts);
    
    // Check numNewOwners in Tokenomics
    (,,,uint32 numNewOwners,,,) = tokenomics.mapEpochTokenomics(tokenomics.epochCounter()).epochPoint;
    assertGt(numNewOwners, 0);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Remove `numNewOwners` from the `IDF` calculation or implement strict identity/stake requirements that cannot be Sybil-attacked (e.g., require `veOLAS` locking for new owners to be counted).
```

### H-36 / `AU-S4980Tn3m3C4j4sExO`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS of Staking Contract and Locked Funds via Gas Griefing in `checkpoint()`
- Report lines: 3712-3874
```md
## [H-36]. Permanent DoS of Staking Contract and Locked Funds via Gas Griefing in `checkpoint()`

## id: AU-S4980Tn3m3C4j4sExO

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
GasGriefBlockLimit

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
StakingBase._checkRatioPass

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Code path exists: `_checkRatioPass()` performs `activityChecker.staticcall(activityData)` with no gas cap, so the call can consume almost all provided gas. If the activityChecker’s logic ends up calling into a service multisig that burns gas (e.g., via a malicious delegate implementation behind a proxy), the outer `checkpoint()` loop may be left with too little gas (EIP-150 63/64 rule), causing the whole transaction to OOG and revert. Since `_stake()` and `_unstake()` both call `checkpoint()`, a permanently gas-griefing service can freeze staking operations and lock all staked NFTs. There is no mitigation in StakingBase (no per-call gas limit, no try/catch with bounded gas). This is a direct permissionless DoS with High impact.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `checkpoint()` function iterates over all staked services to calculate rewards and update liveness. Inside the loop, it calls `_checkRatioPass`, which triggers a `staticcall` to the `activityChecker` contract. The `activityChecker` is expected to query the service's multisig contract (e.g., via `nonce()`) to verify activity. 

However, `StakingBase` does not enforce a gas limit on the `staticcall` to `activityChecker`. By default, `staticcall` forwards all available gas (leaving only 1/64th to the caller upon OOG failure). A malicious user can deploy a service with a Gnosis Safe Proxy that points to a malicious Singleton implementation containing an infinite loop or excessive gas consumption in its `nonce()` function. When `checkpoint()` is called, this service consumes nearly all the transaction gas. The remaining 1/64th gas is insufficient to complete the iteration over the remaining services, causing the transaction to revert with Out-of-Gas.

Crucially, `_unstake()` calls `checkpoint()`. Therefore, if `checkpoint()` is permanently DoS'd by a single malicious service, no user can unstake their Service NFTs. The assets (NFTs) become permanently locked in the contract.

## Impact
All staked Service NFTs are permanently locked (users cannot unstake). Rewards distribution is halted. The contract becomes non-functional.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker deploys a `MaliciousSingleton` contract with a `nonce()` function that loops until gas is near zero.
2. Attacker deploys a standard Gnosis Safe Proxy pointing to `MaliciousSingleton`. The Proxy's codehash matches the allowed `proxyHash` in `StakingBase`.
3. Attacker registers a service in `ServiceRegistry` using this proxy as the multisig.
4. Attacker stakes the service in `StakingBase` (checking `proxyHash` passes).
5. Attacker (or anyone) calls `checkpoint()` or attempts to `unstake` any service.
6. The transaction processes the malicious service, burns all gas in `_checkRatioPass`, and reverts the entire transaction when the remaining 1/64th gas is insufficient to finish the loop.

## Proof of Code
pragma solidity ^0.8.30;

import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {StakingBase} from "contracts/staking/StakingBase.sol";

// Minimal concrete implementation for testing
contract ConcreteStaking is StakingBase {
    function _transfer(address, uint256) internal override {}
    function _checkTokenStakingDeposit(uint256, uint256, uint32[] memory) internal view override {}
    function _getRewardReceiversAndAmounts(uint256, address, address, uint256, uint256) internal pure override returns (address[] memory r, uint256[] memory a) {
        r = new address[](1); a = new uint256[](1);
    }
}

interface IServiceRegistry {
    struct Service {
        uint96 securityDeposit;
        address multisig;
        bytes32 configHash;
        uint32 threshold;
        uint32 maxNumAgentInstances;
        uint32 numAgentInstances;
        uint8 state;
        uint32[] agentIds;
    }
    function getService(uint256) external view returns (Service memory);
    function safeTransferFrom(address, address, uint256) external;
}

contract MaliciousMultisig {
    function nonce() external view returns (uint256) {
        uint256 i = 0;
        // Burn gas loop
        while (gasleft() > 5000) {
            i++;
        }
        return i;
    }
}

contract MockActivityChecker {
    function getMultisigNonces(address multisig) external view returns (uint256[] memory) {
        // Calls the multisig, vulnerable to gas griefing
        (bool s, ) = multisig.staticcall(abi.encodeWithSignature("nonce()"));
        require(s, "Check failed");
        return new uint256[](1);
    }
    function isRatioPass(uint256[] memory, uint256[] memory, uint256) external view returns (bool) { return true; }
}

contract DoSTest is Test {
    ConcreteStaking staking;
    address registry = makeAddr("registry");
    MockActivityChecker checker;
    MaliciousMultisig badMultisig;
    
    function setUp() public {
        staking = new ConcreteStaking();
        checker = new MockActivityChecker();
        badMultisig = new MaliciousMultisig();
        
        // Initialize
        uint256[] memory ids = new uint256[](0);
        StakingBase.StakingParams memory params = StakingBase.StakingParams({
            metadataHash: bytes32("meta"),
            maxNumServices: 10,
            rewardsPerSecond: 1e18,
            minStakingDeposit: 10,
            minNumStakingPeriods: 1,
            maxNumInactivityPeriods: 10,
            livenessPeriod: 100,
            timeForEmissions: 1000,
            numAgentInstances: 1,
            agentIds: ids,
            threshold: 0,
            configHash: bytes32(0),
            proxyHash: address(badMultisig).codehash, // Assume attacker uses valid proxy hash logic
            serviceRegistry: registry,
            activityChecker: address(checker)
        });
        
        // Access internal init via harness or just trust setup for PoC logic
        // For this PoC, we assume initialization works or use a harness. 
        // Here we simulate the state setup directly for brevity/compilability.
    }

    // Mock Service Registry behavior
    function testDoS() public {
        // Since we can't easily init the abstract contract via external call without a harness,
        // we will demonstrate the OOG in the loop logic directly if we could.
        // Instead, valid PoC structure for the finding:
        
        // 1. Deploy malicious multisig
        // 2. Call checker
        uint256 startGas = gasleft();
        try checker.getMultisigNonces(address(badMultisig)) {
            fail();
        } catch {
            // Expected to fail or consume all gas
        }
        uint256 used = startGas - gasleft();
        // In a loop of 10 services, this gas consumption triggers OOG for the whole tx
        assertTrue(used > 100000, "Gas should be burnt");
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Enforce a strict gas limit when calling the activity checker in `_checkRatioPass`. 

```solidity
// StakingBase.sol

function _checkRatioPass(...) internal view returns (...) {
    // ...
    // Limit gas to prevent griefing (e.g., 100k gas)
    (bool success, bytes memory returnData) = activityChecker.staticcall{gas: 100000}(activityData);
    // ...
}
```
```

### H-37 / `3ABkc0NWeaoHFjJ8E_Z6e`
- Finding title: Bypass of Liveness Check via Malicious Multisig Implementation allowing Reward Theft
- Report lines: 3875-3931
```md
## [H-37]. Bypass of Liveness Check via Malicious Multisig Implementation allowing Reward Theft

## id: 3ABkc0NWeaoHFjJ8E_Z6e

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AuthByPass

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
StakingBase._checkRatioPass

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This is substantively the same root issue as GH7: `_stake()` authenticates the multisig by `codehash` of the proxy, which does not bind to the implementation logic that provides `nonce()`/activity data. A proxy can delegate to a malicious implementation (or be upgraded to one) while keeping identical proxy bytecode and thus identical `codehash`, causing `_checkRatioPass()`/ActivityChecker to observe fabricated nonce growth. There is no in-contract allowlist of master copies or immutable binding to a specific singleton, so the liveness mechanism can be bypassed and rewards claimed without real activity. This is permissionless for attacker-controlled services, and the economic impact is High due to reward theft/dilution.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `StakingBase` contract verifies the service multisig by comparing its bytecode hash (`service.multisig.codehash`) against a trusted `proxyHash` (typically a Gnosis Safe Proxy). However, a Proxy's `codehash` only reflects the proxy delegation logic, not the implementation logic (Master Copy) it delegates to. An attacker can deploy a service using the standard Safe Proxy bytecode (passing the `codehash` check) but point it to a malicious implementation that mocks the `nonce` or activity functions. The `ActivityChecker` (which queries the multisig to verify liveness) will receive fake activity data, causing the protocol to payout rewards to the attacker for zero actual activity.

## Impact
Theft of protocol rewards by bypassing the Proof-of-Activity mechanism.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker deploys a malicious contract `FakeMasterCopy` that returns incrementing nonces when queried.
2. Attacker deploys a standard Gnosis Safe Proxy pointing to `FakeMasterCopy`.
3. Attacker registers a service in `ServiceRegistry` using this multisig (assuming Registry allows it or lacks deep validation).
4. Attacker stakes the service in `StakingBase`. The `proxyHash` check passes because the Proxy bytecode is standard.
5. Attacker calls `checkpoint()`. `StakingBase` calls `ActivityChecker`, which calls `multisig.nonce()`.
6. The malicious multisig returns a valid/high nonce.
7. `StakingBase` awards tokens to the attacker.

## Proof of Code
contract MaliciousSafeImpl {
    function nonce() external pure returns (uint256) { return 100; }
}
// In a test file inheriting StakingBase:
function testProxyBypass() public {
    // Setup: deploy malicious safe implementation and standard proxy
    MaliciousSafeImpl impl = new MaliciousSafeImpl();
    // Standard Proxy bytecode creation (mocked for simplicity)
    // Assume 'proxy' has correct codehash but delegates to 'impl'
    // Stake service with 'proxy'
    // Call checkpoint
    // Assert rewards > 0
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Modify `StakingBase` to verify the implementation address of the proxy (e.g. read storage slot 0 for Gnosis Safe) against a whitelist, or rely strictly on `ServiceRegistry` to ensure only safe implementations are deployed (and remove the misleading `codehash` check).
```

### M-38 / `5nGELAxtz7kZLroo4pVAQ`
- Finding title: Burner.burn() lacks idempotency enabling front-running DoS/griefing of keepers
- Report lines: 3932-4023
```md
## [M-38]. Burner.burn() lacks idempotency enabling front-running DoS/griefing of keepers

## id: 5nGELAxtz7kZLroo4pVAQ

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
DoubleExecutionOrReplay

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
Burner.burn

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Code path exists in Burner.burn(): it reads olasBalance and reverts with ZeroValue() when balance == 0. This makes the function non-idempotent. A front-runner can call burn() first, consuming the entire balance, causing the later (keeper) tx to revert deterministically due to the strict zero-balance revert. No on-chain safeguard prevents this (no commit-reveal, no access control, no “return-if-zero” behavior). This is a practical gas-grief/operational DoS against specific callers (keepers/automation), not an asset theft. Impact is Medium because it can disrupt automation reliability and waste keeper gas, potentially delaying burns if keepers give up; likelihood is Occasional because it requires mempool monitoring/MEV but is straightforward.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `burn()` function in `Burner.sol` enforces a strict check `if (olasBalance == 0) { revert ZeroValue(); }` before attempting to burn tokens. This design makes the function non-idempotent, creating a race condition for any external actor (e.g., a keeper, bridge relayer, or automation bot) attempting to trigger the burn. An attacker can observe a pending legitimate `burn()` transaction and front-run it with their own call to `burn()`. The attacker's transaction succeeds, consuming the balance and reducing it to zero. Subsequently, the legitimate transaction attempts to execute, reads a zero balance, and reverts with `ZeroValue`. This vulnerability allows an adversary to consistently grief keepers by forcing their transactions to fail, wasting their gas and potentially disrupting automated maintenance schedules or bridge processing flows. This finding aligns with the 'Adversary inducing permanent ZeroValue reverts' actor described in the audit context.

## Impact
Operational disruption of keepers/relayers and gas griefing via intentional front-running.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. The `Burner` contract holds 100 OLAS tokens.
2. A Keeper (or automated service) submits a transaction `T_Keeper` calling `burn()` to process the funds.
3. An Attacker monitors the mempool, sees `T_Keeper`, and submits `T_Attacker` calling `burn()` with a higher gas tip/priority.
4. `T_Attacker` is included first. It successfully burns the 100 OLAS. The contract balance becomes 0.
5. `T_Keeper` is executed. It reads `olasBalance` as 0.
6. `T_Keeper` hits the `revert ZeroValue();` check and fails.
7. The Keeper pays for the failed transaction execution, and the automation job is marked as failed.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {Burner} from "../contracts/Burner.sol";

// Mock IToken to simulate OLAS behavior
interface IToken {
    function balanceOf(address account) external view returns (uint256);
    function burn(uint256 amount) external;
}

contract MockToken is IToken {
    mapping(address => uint256) public balanceOf;
    function mint(address to, uint256 amount) external {
        balanceOf[to] += amount;
    }
    function burn(uint256 amount) external {
        balanceOf[msg.sender] -= amount;
    }
}

contract BurnerTest is Test {
    Burner burner;
    MockToken token;

    function setUp() public {
        token = new MockToken();
        burner = new Burner(address(token));
    }

    function testGriefingDoS() public {
        // Setup: Burner holds 100 tokens
        token.mint(address(burner), 100 ether);

        // 1. Attacker front-runs the keeper
        vm.prank(address(0xBAD));
        burner.burn();

        // Verify Burner is empty
        assertEq(token.balanceOf(address(burner)), 0);

        // 2. Keeper's transaction executes next
        vm.prank(address(0xKEEP));
        // Expect revert due to non-idempotent ZeroValue check
        // ZeroValue is a custom error defined in Burner.sol: error ZeroValue();
        // The selector is keccak256("ZeroValue()") which is 0x98f6d333
        vm.expectRevert(bytes4(keccak256("ZeroValue()")));
        burner.burn();
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Modify `burn()` to be idempotent. Instead of reverting when `olasBalance == 0`, the function should simply `return;` (or emit an event with 0 amount if strictly necessary, though returning is cheaper).
```

### M-39 / `hs3plRfLeuMzmF3VRbaVP`
- Finding title: Denial of Service and Gas Griefing on burn() due to Strict Zero-Value Revert
- Report lines: 4024-4127
```md
## [M-39]. Denial of Service and Gas Griefing on burn() due to Strict Zero-Value Revert

## id: hs3plRfLeuMzmF3VRbaVP

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
DoS

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
Burner.burn

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Same concrete issue as 5nGELAxtz7kZLroo4pVAQ: burn() reverts on zero balance, so a front-run burn makes subsequent burns revert and wastes the follower’s gas. This is fully supported by the code (strict ZeroValue revert) and is reproducible with two sequential calls where the first drains the balance. There is no existing mitigation in the function. Objective impact remains Medium (gas griefing/automation disruption) with Occasional likelihood due to mempool/MEV requirement.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `burn()` function in the `Burner` contract retrieves the contract's OLAS balance and strictly reverts with the custom error `ZeroValue()` if the balance is zero. Since `burn()` is a permissionless function intended to be called by keepers or automation services when funds are bridged or transferred, this creates a race condition exploitable for gas griefing. A malicious actor or a competing MEV bot can front-run a legitimate `burn()` transaction, successfully burning the tokens and reducing the balance to zero. The legitimate transaction then executes, finds the balance to be zero, and reverts. This causes the legitimate caller to waste gas on the failed transaction. This vulnerability aligns with the 'Adversary inducing permanent ZeroValue reverts' actor described in the context, potentially disrupting the reliability of the burning mechanism by disincentivizing keepers.

## Impact
Legitimate keepers waste gas on reverted transactions; discourages automation; potentially delays burning if keepers abandon the task due to unprofitability and frequent reverts.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. The `Burner` contract holds 100 OLAS tokens.
2. A legitimate keeper (Alice) observes the balance and submits a transaction to call `burn()`.
3. An attacker (Bob) or an MEV bot sees Alice's pending transaction and front-runs it by calling `burn()` with a higher gas price.
4. Bob's transaction executes first, burning the 100 tokens. The `Burner` balance becomes 0.
5. Alice's transaction executes. It reads the balance as 0 and reverts with `ZeroValue()`.
6. Alice pays for the transaction execution up to the revert, losing gas funds without achieving the desired outcome.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/Burner.sol";

contract MockToken is IToken {
    mapping(address => uint256) public balances;
    function balanceOf(address account) external view returns (uint256) {
        return balances[account];
    }
    function burn(uint256 amount) external {
        balances[msg.sender] -= amount;
    }
    function mint(address to, uint256 amount) external {
        balances[to] += amount;
    }
}

contract BurnerTest is Test {
    Burner burner;
    MockToken token;

    function setUp() public {
        token = new MockToken();
        burner = new Burner(address(token));
    }

    function testGasGriefing() public {
        // Setup: Burner has 100 tokens
        token.mint(address(burner), 100);

        // Keeper prepares to call burn, but Attacker front-runs
        vm.prank(address(0xbad));
        burner.burn();

        // Keeper calls burn and reverts due to strict check
        vm.expectRevert(ZeroValue.selector);
        vm.prank(address(0xkeeper));
        burner.burn();
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Remove the strict revert on zero balance. Instead, if `olasBalance` is 0, reset the reentrancy lock and return from the function. This makes the function idempotent and prevents transaction failure for the second caller, avoiding the griefing vector.

```solidity
    function burn() external {
        if (_locked > 1) {
            revert ReentrancyGuard();
        }
        _locked = 2;

        uint256 olasBalance = IToken(olas).balanceOf(address(this));

        if (olasBalance == 0) {
            // Fix: Reset lock and return instead of reverting
            _locked = 1;
            return;
        }

        IToken(olas).burn(olasBalance);
        emit Burned(olasBalance);

        _locked = 1;
    }
```
```

### M-40 / `ETxrZVf4diIDrZ_A0Ocrm`
- Finding title: USDT Incompatibility leads to DoS due to non-standard `approve` signature
- Report lines: 4128-4181
```md
## [M-40]. USDT Incompatibility leads to DoS due to non-standard `approve` signature

## id: ETxrZVf4diIDrZ_A0Ocrm

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StandardViolation

## Exploit Type
UncheckedERC20Return

## Location
LiquidityManagerETH._mintV3

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The bug is directly triggered by USDT itself (a major in-scope token), not only by some other non-standard ERC20.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `LiquidityManagerETH` contract (and `LiquidityManagerCore`) interacts with arbitrary tokens passed in the `tokens` array. It uses the `IToken` interface to call `approve`, which expects a `bool` return value (`function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) external returns (bool);`). However, the USDT token (and other non-compliant ERC20s) has an `approve` function that returns `void` (no return value). When compiled with Solidity 0.8+, calling a function that does not return data when the interface expects a return value results in a revert (due to missing return data check). This causes the `convertToV3`, `increaseLiquidity`, and `changeRanges` functions to permanently revert for pairs containing USDT, making the protocol incompatible with one of the most common stablecoins. Additionally, `approve` is not reset to 0 before setting a new value, which causes reverts with USDT if a residual allowance exists.

## Impact
Denial of Service (DoS) for any liquidity operations involving USDT or similar non-standard tokens. The protocol cannot manage USDT liquidity.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `LiquidityManagerETH`. 2. Attempt to call `convertToV3` using USDT and OLAS as the token pair. 3. The function calls `IToken(USDT).approve(...)`. 4. The transaction reverts because USDT does not return a boolean, but the interface expects one.

## Proof of Code
contract USDTMock {
    function approve(address, uint256) external { /* no return */ }
    function balanceOf(address) external view returns(uint) { return 1000; }
}
function testUSDTRevert() public {
    USDTMock usdt = new USDTMock();
    LiquidityManagerETH lm = new LiquidityManagerETH(address(this), address(this), address(this), address(this), 10, address(this), address(this));
    lm.initialize(100);
    address[] memory tokens = new address[](2);
    tokens[0] = address(usdt);
    tokens[1] = address(this);
    // Setup tokens...
    // Calling any function that triggers approve will revert
    // vm.expectRevert(); // EVM revert due to return data size mismatch
    // lm.convertToV3(tokens, ...);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeTransferLib.safeApprove` (if available and handles non-standard tokens) or implement a low-level call wrapper that handles missing return values for `approve`. Also, ensure allowances are reset to 0 before setting a new value to support USDT.
```

### M-41 / `UV7qCn6gj7DptIWlPFczW`
- Finding title: Non-compliant EIP-1271 implementation prevents smart contract operators
- Report lines: 4182-4245
```md
## [M-41]. Non-compliant EIP-1271 implementation prevents smart contract operators

## id: UV7qCn6gj7DptIWlPFczW

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StandardViolation

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
ServiceManager._verifySignedHash

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The EIP-1271 path is clearly non-standard in `_verifySignedHash()`: it enforces `signature.length == 65`, encodes the validator address into `r`, and forwards the same (now structurally constrained) signature bytes to `isValidSignature`. Standard EIP-1271 contract signatures (e.g., Safe’s concatenated signatures) are variable-length and do not expect the validator address embedded into `r`. This makes many contract wallets unusable as `operator` for the signature-based flows, creating a real functionality/DoS issue for smart-contract operators.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `ServiceManager` (via `OperatorSignedHashes._verifySignedHash`) implements a custom and broken verification logic for EIP-1271 signatures (identified by `v == 4`). 

1. It strictly enforces `signature.length == 65`. Standard multisig signatures (e.g., Gnosis Safe) are typically longer (concatenation of owner signatures).
2. It repurposes the `r` component of the signature to hold the validator address: `recOperator = address(uint160(uint256(r)))`.
3. It passes the **corrupted** signature (with `r` overwritten by address) to the validator: `ISignatureValidator(recOperator).isValidSignature(msgHash, signature)`.

This logic forces callers to construct non-standard signatures and prevents standard wallets (which verify the payload against the hash) from validating the signature, as the payload they receive is malformed and does not contain the actual signature data (which was displaced by the address).

## Impact
Prevents smart contract wallets (like Gnosis Safe, which are key actors in the Autonolas ecosystem) from functioning as operators using the signature-based flows (`unbondWithSignature`, `registerAgentsWithSignature`).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. A Gnosis Safe is set up as an operator.
2. The Safe signs a `RegisterAgents` hash. The resulting signature is > 65 bytes (depending on owners).
3. When `ServiceManager.registerAgentsWithSignature` is called, it reverts immediately due to `IncorrectSignatureLength`.
4. Even if length was 65, the caller must set `r` to the Safe address.
5. The Safe receives `isValidSignature(hash, signature)`. It parses `r` from `signature` (which is its own address) instead of the ECDSA `r` value it expects. Verification fails.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/ServiceManager.sol";

contract ServiceManagerTest is Test {
    // ... setup ...
    function testEIP1271Breakage() public {
        // Hypothetical test showing Safe signature failure
        bytes memory safeSignature = new bytes(130); // 2 owners
        // call unbondWithSignature
        // vm.expectRevert(IncorrectSignatureLength.selector);
        // manager.unbondWithSignature(..., safeSignature);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Modify `_verifySignedHash` to:
1. Remove the strict 65-byte length check, or allow variable lengths for `v=4`.
2. Do not encode the operator address in `r`. The `operator` address is already passed as an argument to `_verifySignedHash`. Use that argument directly to call `isValidSignature`.
3. Pass the original, valid signature payload to the validator.
```

### H-42 / `ORP-ijcFzaro-MkAf-IYP`
- Finding title: DoS in bridge verification due to unsafe assembly casting of payloadLength
- Report lines: 4246-4284
```md
## [H-42]. DoS in bridge verification due to unsafe assembly casting of payloadLength

## id: ORP-ijcFzaro-MkAf-IYP

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UnsafeAssembyTypeCasts

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataWormhole._verifyBridgedData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: In VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData(), payloadLength is declared uint32 but assigned inside inline assembly via payloadLength := mload(add(data, i)) without masking. At that offset, mload reads a full 32-byte word containing (high bits) parts of target/value and (low 4 bytes) the intended uint32 length. Solidity does not automatically clear dirty high bits for such assignments from assembly, so payloadLength can become a huge 256-bit value when later used. The subsequent new bytes(payloadLength) will attempt an enormous allocation and revert (OOG/memory error), causing DoS for typical non-zero target addresses. The suggested mitigation (masking to 0xffffffff) is appropriate.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `VerifyBridgedData.sol`, the `_verifyBridgedData` function uses inline assembly to parse a packed byte stream. It reads `payloadLength` (a `uint32` variable) using `mload`, which loads a full 32-byte word from memory. Due to the packed data layout `[Target(20)|Value(12)|Length(4)|Payload...]`, the `mload` at the `Length` offset reads a word containing the preceding 12 bytes of `Value` and 16 bytes of `Target` in its higher-order bits. Since Solidity 0.8+ does not automatically clean dirty higher-order bits for stack variables assigned in inline assembly, `payloadLength` retains these dirty bits. When this dirty `payloadLength` is subsequently used to allocate memory via `new bytes(payloadLength)`, the value is interpreted as a 256-bit integer (which is extremely large if `Target` or `Value` are non-zero). This causes the transaction to revert due to Out-Of-Gas or memory limit violation. As `Target` is a contract address and typically non-zero, this effectively causes a Denial of Service for processing any valid bridge message.

## Impact
Permanent Denial of Service of the cross-chain governance bridge for valid messages, preventing execution of governance actions.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
The attacker (or any user) sends a valid bridge message where the target address has non-zero bytes in the range [4..19] (which is true for almost all addresses). The `_verifyBridgedData` function parses the message. The assembly block loads `payloadLength` with dirty high bits from the target address. The check `payloadLength < SELECTOR_DATA_LENGTH` passes (as the dirty value is huge). The line `bytes memory payload = new bytes(payloadLength);` attempts to allocate ~2^200 bytes of memory, causing the transaction to revert.

## Proof of Code
contract DosTest is Test { ProcessBridgedDataWormhole verifier; function setUp() public { verifier = new ProcessBridgedDataWormhole(); } function testDos() public { address target = address(0x1234567812345678123456781234567812345678); bytes memory inner = abi.encodePacked(target, uint96(0), uint32(4), bytes4(0x11223344)); bytes memory wormholePayload = abi.encode(uint16(1), address(this), inner, uint256(0), uint256(0)); bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(verifier.SEND_MESSAGE(), wormholePayload); if (data.length < 324) { bytes memory padded = new bytes(324); for(uint i=0; i<data.length; i++) padded[i] = data[i]; data = padded; } vm.expectRevert(); verifier.processBridgeData(data, address(this), 1); } }

## Suggested Mitigation
Mask the loaded value in the assembly block to ensure only the lower 32 bits are used: `payloadLength := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xffffffff)`
```
