## [M-43]. Treasury reverts on USDT and non-compliant ERC20 transfers due to incorrect interface return type

## id: z6vQ9C3opqt4uv0tq4ksR

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StandardViolation

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
Treasury.depositTokenForOLAS

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The issue is triggered by USDT itself (a prominent in-scope non-compliant ERC20), so it is not limited to non-standard tokens 'other than USDT'.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `Treasury` contract casts the token address to `IToken` and calls `transfer` or `transferFrom`, expecting a `bool` return value. While standard ERC20 tokens return a boolean, tokens like USDT (and BNB on mainnet) do not return any value. In Solidity 0.8.x, calling a function that expects a return value when none is provided causes the transaction to revert due to a decoding error. This renders `depositTokenForOLAS` and `withdraw` unusable for USDT, which is explicitly in-scope.

## Impact
Protocol cannot support USDT for bonds or treasury reserves. Any USDT sent to the Treasury (e.g. if enabled as a bond token) cannot be withdrawn or used, effectively causing a Denial of Service for that asset integration.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Governance enables USDT (or a MockUSDT with no return value) via `enableToken`. 2. User attempts to create a bond using USDT via `Depository.deposit`. 3. `Depository` calls `Treasury.depositTokenForOLAS`. 4. `Treasury` calls `IToken(usdt).transferFrom(..., amount)`. 5. The call succeeds on the token contract but returns no data. 6. The Solidity runtime in `Treasury` attempts to decode a `bool` from empty data and reverts.

## Proof of Code
function testUSDTRevert() public { 
    // Mock contract simulating USDT (no return value) 
    DeployMockUSDT usdt = new DeployMockUSDT(); 
    treasury.enableToken(address(usdt)); 
    // Approve treasury 
    usdt.approve(address(treasury), 1000); 
    // Expect revert due to missing return value decoding error 
    vm.expectRevert(); 
    treasury.depositTokenForOLAS(address(this), 100, address(usdt), 10); 
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use OpenZeppelin's `SafeERC20` library for `transfer` and `transferFrom` operations, or wrap the low-level `call` to handle tokens that return no data (success if no revert).
```

### M-44 / `jfJXyiSRSCGmfZtSZLA5s`
- Finding title: DoS of Service Deployment via Sentinel Address Registration
- Report lines: 4333-4398
```md
## [M-44]. DoS of Service Deployment via Sentinel Address Registration

## id: jfJXyiSRSCGmfZtSZLA5s

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StandardViolation

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
ServiceRegistry.registerAgents

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: ServiceRegistry.registerAgents() disallows address(0) only indirectly (by checking mapAgentInstanceOperators[agentInstance] == 0) but does not forbid address(0x1). address(0x1) is the Gnosis Safe sentinel and is invalid as an owner. If registered as an agent instance, any deployment path that adds owners to a Safe (e.g., RecoveryModule.create adding owners via addOwnerWithThreshold) will revert inside the Safe. This bricks deployment until the service is terminated and the operator unbonded/cleared, wasting gas and delaying service launch. There is no explicit guard against sentinel.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `ServiceRegistry.registerAgents` function allows any address (except `address(0)` and the operator's address) to be registered as an agent instance. It does not prevent the registration of `address(0x1)`, which is the reserved Sentinel address in Gnosis Safe contracts. 

If a malicious operator registers `address(0x1)` as an agent instance, the subsequent call to `ServiceRegistry.deploy` will invoke `RecoveryModule.create`. The `create` function attempts to add this address to the Safe via `IMultisig.addOwnerWithThreshold(0x1, ...)`. The Gnosis Safe contract will revert this call (Error `GS203`) because `0x1` is not a valid owner address. 

This permanently blocks the deployment of the service as long as `0x1` is a registered agent. The service owner must terminate the service and unbond the operator (refunding them) to clear the invalid agent, causing a Denial of Service and loss of gas/time.

## Impact
Malicious operators can brick service deployment, forcing service owners to terminate and redeploy, wasting gas and deposits.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Service Owner creates a service and activates registration.
2. Malicious Operator calls `registerAgents` with `agentInstances = [address(0x1)]`.
3. Registration completes (`FinishedRegistration`).
4. Service Owner calls `deploy`.
5. `ServiceRegistry` calls `RecoveryModule.create`.
6. `RecoveryModule` calls `IMultisig(safe).addOwnerWithThreshold(address(0x1), 1)`.
7. Safe reverts. Deployment fails.

## Proof of Code
    function test_Sentinel_DoS() public {
        // Setup service
        uint256 serviceId = serviceRegistry.create(owner, configHash, agentIds, agentParams, threshold);
        serviceRegistry.activateRegistration{value: 100}(owner, serviceId);
        
        // Malicious operator registers Sentinel address 0x1
        address[] memory agents = new address[](1);
        agents[0] = address(0x1);
        uint32[] memory ids = new uint32[](1);
        ids[0] = 1;
        
        vm.prank(operator);
        serviceRegistry.registerAgents{value: 100}(operator, serviceId, agents, ids);
        
        // Deploy attempts to create Safe with 0x1 owner -> Reverts
        vm.expectRevert(); // Gnosis Safe reverts GS203
        serviceRegistry.deploy(owner, serviceId, address(recoveryModule), "");
    }

## Suggested Mitigation
In `ServiceRegistry.registerAgents`, add a check: `if (agentInstances[i] == address(0x1)) revert InvalidAgentAddress();`.
```

### H-45 / `8Qy71cN2P1xX-oJ4q9dHh`
- Finding title: Accounting corruption via reentrancy in _withdraw function
- Report lines: 4399-4526
```md
## [H-45]. Accounting corruption via reentrancy in _withdraw function

## id: 8Qy71cN2P1xX-oJ4q9dHh

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
CEIViolation

## Exploit Type
Reentrancy

## Location
StakingBase._withdraw

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Code path exists: `_withdraw()` caches `balance` into `updatedBalance`, performs external `_transfer()` calls in a loop, and only writes `balance = updatedBalance` after the loop. If `_transfer()` can invoke receiver-controlled code (e.g., native ETH transfer to a contract receiver), the receiver can re-enter `claim()` for another service. The inner `_withdraw()` updates `balance` based on the old stored value, but the outer call later overwrites `balance` with its stale cached value, losing deductions and desynchronizing accounting. This likely leads to insolvency/DoS (future withdrawals revert when real funds are insufficient), rather than direct double-withdrawal (rewards are zeroed per-service). There is no reentrancy guard and the CEI pattern is violated, so the bug exists and is practically triggerable in native-transfer variants.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_withdraw` function in `StakingBase` violates the Checks-Effects-Interactions (CEI) pattern. It caches the `balance` state variable into a local `updatedBalance` variable, performs external calls (`_transfer`) inside a loop, and finally updates the `balance` state variable with the cached value *after* the loop. If the specific implementation of `_transfer` passes control to the receiver (e.g., sending native ETH or tokens with callbacks), a malicious receiver can re-enter the contract (e.g., by calling `claim` on a different service ID). 

The inner execution of `claim` -> `_withdraw` will read the stale `balance` from storage, perform its deduction, and update storage. However, when the inner call returns, the outer execution resumes and overwrites the `balance` state variable with its own calculated `updatedBalance`, effectively erasing the deduction made by the inner withdrawal. This permanently inflates the contract's `balance` variable relative to the actual assets held, leading to accounting inconsistencies and potential insolvency/DoS for future withdrawals.

## Impact
Permanent inflation of the `balance` accounting variable leads to a mismatch between tracked and held funds. This causes the contract to believe it has more funds than it does (insolvency), eventually causing valid withdrawals to revert when the physical balance runs out, effectively locking user rewards.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker controls two staked services (S1, S2) with accrued rewards (e.g., 10 ETH each).
2. Contract `balance` is 100 ETH.
3. Attacker calls `claim(S1)`.
4. `_claim` calls `_withdraw`. Local `updatedBalance` is initialized to 100.
5. `_withdraw` loop: subtracts 10 from local `updatedBalance` (now 90) and calls `_transfer(attacker, 10)`.
6. Attacker's `receive()` function triggers and calls `claim(S2)`.
7. Inner `claim(S2)` -> `_withdraw`. Reads `balance` from storage (still 100). Subtracts 10. Calls `_transfer`.
8. Inner `_withdraw` finishes, updating `balance` storage to 90.
9. Execution returns to outer `_withdraw`. It finishes its loop and updates `balance` storage to `updatedBalance` (which is 90).
10. Final State: `balance` is 90, but 20 ETH was transferred. Real balance is 80. The 10 ETH deduction from S2 is overwritten.

## Proof of Code
import {Test} from "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {StakingBase} from "contracts/staking/StakingBase.sol";

// Mock concrete implementation
contract MockStaking is StakingBase {
    constructor() {
        // Initialize minimal params to avoid revert
        // Not strictly needed for this unit test of _withdraw
    }
    // Allow receiving ETH
    receive() external payable {}
    // Concrete transfer for ETH
    function _transfer(address to, uint256 amount) internal override {
        (bool s, ) = to.call{value: amount}("");
        require(s, "Transfer failed");
    }
    // Helpers for test setup
    function setBalance(uint256 b) external { balance = b; }
    function getBalance() external view returns (uint256) { return balance; }
    // Bypass registry/logic for PoC
    function mockSetupService(uint256 id, address owner, uint256 reward) external {
        ServiceInfo storage s = mapServiceInfo[id];
        s.owner = owner;
        s.reward = reward;
        // Use ServiceOwner distribution to avoid registry calls
        s.rewardDistributionInfo = uint256(RewardDistributionType.ServiceOwner);
    }
}

contract Attack is Test {
    MockStaking staking;
    bool reentered = false;
    uint256 public service1 = 1;
    uint256 public service2 = 2;

    function setUp() public {
        staking = new MockStaking();
        // Fund staking contract
        vm.deal(address(staking), 100 ether);
        staking.setBalance(100 ether);
    }

    receive() external payable {
        if (!reentered) {
            reentered = true;
            staking.claim(service2);
        }
    }

    function testReentrancyAccounting() public {
        // Setup attacker owning two services with rewards
        staking.mockSetupService(service1, address(this), 10 ether);
        staking.mockSetupService(service2, address(this), 10 ether);

        // Attack
        staking.claim(service1);

        // Check accounting
        uint256 contractBalanceVar = staking.getBalance();
        uint256 realBalance = address(staking).balance;

        // Expected: 100 - 10 - 10 = 80.
        // Actual Accounting due to bug: 90.
        assertEq(realBalance, 80 ether, "Real balance should be 80");
        assertEq(contractBalanceVar, 90 ether, "Accounting variable should be 90 (Bug)");
        assertFalse(contractBalanceVar == realBalance, "Accounting invariant broken");
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Update the `balance` state variable *before* performing external transfers. 

```solidity
    function _withdraw(address[] memory receivers, uint256[] memory amounts) internal virtual {
        // CEI: Update state before external calls
        for (uint256 i = 0; i < receivers.length; ++i) {
             balance -= amounts[i];
             _transfer(receivers[i], amounts[i]);
             emit Withdraw(receivers[i], amounts[i]);
        }
    }
```
```

### M-46 / `rOPRfnYPpghzgrclcUVHb`
- Finding title: Rewards DoS and Loss via Push Payment Griefing in `_withdraw`
- Report lines: 4527-4596
```md
## [M-46]. Rewards DoS and Loss via Push Payment Griefing in `_withdraw`

## id: rOPRfnYPpghzgrclcUVHb

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
PullorPushPaymentbugs

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
StakingBase._withdraw

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This is not purely user mistake: once a reverting receiver is included in the payout set (e.g., via an operator address that is a reverting contract), the owner can be forced into a rewards-DoS where claim/unstake revert, and the only on-chain escape is forcedUnstake with reward forfeiture. That is a push-payment design weakness / griefing vector by a counterparty, not merely accidental misuse by the victim.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_withdraw` function distributes rewards by pushing funds to a list of receivers (service owner and operators). If any receiver in the list reverts (e.g., a malicious operator contract that reverts on receipt), the entire `_withdraw` transaction fails. This blocks the `claim` and `unstake` functions for the Service Owner.

The only recourse for the Service Owner is to call `forcedUnstake`, which unstakes the service but forfeits all accrued rewards (returning them to the pool). This allows a malicious operator (who can register permissionlessly in the ServiceRegistry) to grief the Service Owner by effectively burning their rewards.

## Impact
Functional Denial of Service on reward claiming. Monetary loss for Service Owners who are forced to forfeit rewards to retrieve their staked Service NFT.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker registers as an operator for a victim Service and sets their operator address to a contract that reverts on `receive()`.
2. Victim (Service Owner) stakes the service.
3. Service accrues rewards.
4. Victim calls `claim()` or `unstake()`.
5. `_withdraw` iterates recipients. When it attempts to transfer to the attacker's operator address, the transfer reverts.
6. The entire transaction reverts. Funds are stuck.
7. Victim calls `forcedUnstake()` to recover the NFT. The rewards are zeroed out and returned to `availableRewards`, resulting in loss of yield for the victim.

## Proof of Code
import {Test} from "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {StakingBase} from "contracts/staking/StakingBase.sol";

contract GriefingTest is Test {
    // Similar setup to ReentrancyTest
    // ...
    // Mock Operator reverts in receive()
    receive() external payable { revert("Grief"); }

    function testGriefing() public {
        // Stake service with this contract as operator
        // ...
        // Claim should fail
        vm.expectRevert("Transfer failed"); // Or panic code
        staking.claim(1);
        
        // Unstake should fail
        vm.expectRevert("Transfer failed");
        staking.unstake(1);
        
        // Forced unstake succeeds but rewards lost
        staking.forcedUnstake(1);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Implement a Pull-Payment mechanism (e.g., `claimReward(receiver)`) where each recipient claims their own share individually, or wrap transfers in `try/catch` blocks (if applicable to the transfer logic) to ensure one failure does not revert the entire distribution.
```

### H-47 / `Ex0d1FyBn1Rl8O4Rple8W`
- Finding title: DoS in bridge verification due to dirty upper bits in assembly memory load causing huge memory allocation
- Report lines: 4597-4693
```md
## [H-47]. DoS in bridge verification due to dirty upper bits in assembly memory load causing huge memory allocation

## id: Ex0d1FyBn1Rl8O4Rple8W

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
DelegatecallLowLevelOps

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataOptimism.processBridgeData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData loads `payloadLength := mload(add(data, i))` where only the lowest 4 bytes are the uint32 length; the remaining 28 bytes overlap non-zero parts of target/value due to the chosen alignment. Solidity does not auto-mask values assigned from inline assembly, even if the destination variable is declared `uint32`. As a result, `payloadLength` typically contains a huge 256-bit number, bypasses the `< 4` check, and then `new bytes(payloadLength)` attempts an enormous allocation and reverts (OOG/memory). This is directly triggerable with any normal packed message (non-zero address/value), so it is a common, permissionless DoS that bricks verification/execution paths relying on this parser.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_verifyBridgedData` function in `VerifyBridgedData.sol` (used by `ProcessBridgedDataOptimism`) uses inline assembly to unpack the bridged payload. Specifically, it reads `payloadLength` (a `uint32`) from the packed byte stream using `mload`. 

```solidity
payloadLength := mload(add(data, i))
```

The `l2Message` payload is packed as `Target (20 bytes) | Value (12 bytes) | Length (4 bytes) | Payload ...`. The assembly logic calculates the offset `i` such that `mload` reads a 32-byte word ending with the 4 bytes of `Length`. However, because `mload` reads the entire 32-byte word and the preceding bytes correspond to `Value` and the tail of `Target`, the loaded word contains significant non-zero dirty bits in the higher order positions (from the address and value). 

Solidity assembly does not automatically clean/mask these higher order bits when assigning to a `uint32` variable. Consequently, `payloadLength` holds a huge value (interpreting the dirty bits as part of the number). When this variable is subsequently used to allocate memory `bytes memory payload = new bytes(payloadLength);`, it attempts to allocate an impossibly large amount of memory, causing the transaction to revert (OOG or Panic). 

This flaw means that any valid bridge message containing a normal target address and value will fail verification, resulting in a permanent Denial of Service for the GuardCM mechanism on Optimism.

## Impact
The Community Multisig cannot execute any governance actions on Optimism or Base via the bridge, as all valid messages will be rejected by the Guard verification logic.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. The L1 governance sends a valid message to L2 via the Optimism Messenger. The message payload is packed correctly (Target, Value, Length, Payload).
2. The L2 GuardCM calls `ProcessBridgedDataOptimism.processBridgeData` to verify the message.
3. Inside `_verifyBridgedData`, the assembly reads `payloadLength`. Due to the packed format, the read word includes the non-zero bytes of `Target` and `Value`.
4. `payloadLength` becomes a huge integer.
5. `new bytes(payloadLength)` fails due to excessive memory request.
6. The transaction reverts, blocking the governance action.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {ProcessBridgedDataOptimism} from "contracts/multisigs/bridge_verifier/ProcessBridgedDataOptimism.sol";

contract ProcessBridgedDataTest is Test {
    ProcessBridgedDataOptimism verifier;
    address mediatorL2 = address(0x1234567890123456789012345678901234567890);
    uint256 chainId = 10;

    function setUp() public {
        verifier = new ProcessBridgedDataOptimism();
    }

    function test_DosDueToDirtyBits() public {
        // Construct a valid l2Message packed: Target (20) | Value (12) | Len (4) | Payload
        // Target must be non-zero to trigger the dirty bits issue (and pass zero-address check)
        address target = address(0x1111111111111111111111111111111111111111);
        uint96 value = 1 ether;
        bytes memory innerPayload = hex"AABBCCDD";
        uint32 len = 4;
        
        bytes memory l2Message = abi.encodePacked(target, value, len, innerPayload);
        
        // Wrap in processMessageFromSource(bytes)
        bytes memory mediatorPayload = abi.encodeWithSelector(
            ProcessBridgedDataOptimism.PROCESS_MESSAGE_FROM_SOURCE.selector,
            l2Message
        );

        // Wrap in sendMessage(address,bytes,uint32)
        bytes memory data = abi.encodeWithSelector(
            ProcessBridgedDataOptimism.SEND_MESSAGE.selector,
            mediatorL2,
            mediatorPayload,
            uint32(1_000_000)
        );

        // The call should revert. If the bug is present, it reverts with Panic/OOG (not NotAuthorized).
        // If we fixed it, it would revert with NotAuthorized (since map is empty).
        // Here we just verify it reverts, confirming the DoS of the path.
        vm.expectRevert(); 
        verifier.processBridgeData(data, mediatorL2, chainId);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Mask the loaded value to ensure only the 32 bits of length are used: 
`payloadLength := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xFFFFFFFF)`
```

### M-48 / `IIebFMIIPalHilbk4t_-E`
- Finding title: Inverted optimization logic in NeighborhoodScanner causes severe capital underutilization
- Report lines: 4694-4772
```md
## [M-48]. Inverted optimization logic in NeighborhoodScanner causes severe capital underutilization

## id: IIebFMIIPalHilbk4t_-E

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
IntegerMath

## Exploit Type
IntegerMath

## Location
NeighborhoodScanner._scanNeighborhood

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This is the same underlying issue as the mode-selection report: _scanNeighborhood calls pickHiMaxUtil when _chooseMode returns true (V0>=V1). pickHiMaxUtil is token1-limited and widens hi, which is a poor strategy when token1 is the scarce side and token0 is abundant, leading to large unused token0 value. The contract itself implements utilization scoring, implying utilization is a target property, and the wrong branch choice can deterministically yield very low utilization for unbalanced inputs. There is no effective safeguard because neighborhood search only explores a very small local grid around the initial branch’s solution.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_scanNeighborhood` function determines which optimization strategy to use based on the relative value of the two token amounts (`V0` vs `V1`) calculated by `_chooseMode`. The goal is to maximize capital utilization by anchoring the range on the abundant asset and adjusting the other side to fit the scarce asset.

However, the logic is inverted. When `V0 >= V1` (Token0 is abundant), `_chooseMode` returns `true`, causing `_scanNeighborhood` to call `pickHiMaxUtil`. `pickHiMaxUtil` fixes the lower tick (`lo`) and calculates liquidity `L` based on `amounts[1]` (the scarce asset). This locks in a low liquidity value. It then expands the upper tick (`hi`) to try and use `amounts[0]`. Because `L` is constrained by the small `amounts[1]`, the utilized `amount0` remains a small fraction of the available `amounts[0]`, even at the maximum possible tick range, leaving the majority of the dominant asset idle.

The correct behavior for `V0 >= V1` would be to call `pickLoMaxUtil`. This function fixes `hi`, calculates `L` based on `amounts[0]` (the abundant asset), and then searches for a `lo` closer to the current price to fit the `amounts[1]` constraint. This approach allows for a much higher `L` (by narrowing the Token1 range), resulting in near 100% utilization of both assets.

## Impact
When integrating protocols (like LiquidityManager) use this scanner with unbalanced token amounts, the resulting liquidity position will systematically underutilize the dominant asset (often utilizing <5% of the total value), leading to significant loss of potential yield and liquidity depth.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Assume current price P=1. Input amounts: 1000 ETH (Token0) and 1 ETH (Token1).
2. `_chooseMode` calculates V0 (1000) > V1 (1). Returns `true` (optimizeHi).
3. `optimizeLiquidityAmounts` calls `pickHiMaxUtil` with `lo` fixed (e.g., at tick corresponding to user input).
4. `pickHiMaxUtil` calculates liquidity `L` based on `amounts[1]` (1 ETH). `L` is small.
5. The function searches for `hi`. Even if `hi` extends to `MAX_TICK`, the Token0 required for small `L` is roughly 1 ETH.
6. Result: 1 ETH of Token0 and 1 ETH of Token1 are used. 999 ETH of Token0 remains idle.
7. If `pickLoMaxUtil` were used, `L` would be based on 1000 ETH, and `lo` would be adjusted closer to P, utilizing all 1000 ETH and 1 ETH.

## Proof of Code
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "contracts/pol/NeighborhoodScanner.sol";

contract ScannerTest is Test {
    NeighborhoodScanner scanner;
    function setUp() public { scanner = new NeighborhoodScanner(); }
    function testSuboptimalUtilization() public {
        uint160 sqrtP = 79228162514264337593543950336; // P=1
        uint256[] memory amounts = new uint256[](2);
        amounts[0] = 100 ether; amounts[1] = 1 ether;
        int24[] memory ticks = new int24[](2);
        ticks[0] = -887200; ticks[1] = 887200;
        (,, uint256[] memory amountsDesired) = scanner.optimizeLiquidityAmounts(sqrtP, ticks, 60, amounts, true);
        // With bug: Utilizes ~1 ether of amounts[0] (1% utilization)
        assertLt(amountsDesired[0], 2 ether);
        // Manually calling the correct function demonstrates high utilization
        (,, uint256[] memory betterAmounts) = scanner.pickLoMaxUtil(60, sqrtP, ticks[1], amounts);
        assertGt(betterAmounts[0], 99 ether);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In `NeighborhoodScanner.sol`, swap the branches in `_scanNeighborhood` to correctly align the optimization strategy with the dominant asset:

```solidity
if (optimizeHi) {
    // V0 >= V1, prioritize Token0 by fixing hi and searching lo
    return pickLoMaxUtil(tickSpacing, sqrtP, ticks[1], amounts);
} else {
    // V1 > V0, prioritize Token1 by fixing lo and searching hi
    return pickHiMaxUtil(tickSpacing, sqrtP, ticks[0], amounts);
}
```
```

### H-49 / `WAhSRtPZNPWBfOsZFzNKM`
- Finding title: DoS and fund loss via permissionless `collectFees` burning/sweeping tokens
- Report lines: 4773-4830
```md
## [H-49]. DoS and fund loss via permissionless `collectFees` burning/sweeping tokens

## id: WAhSRtPZNPWBfOsZFzNKM

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Attack is rational because attacker profits from delay/failure; no code bug needed besides an incentive misalignment

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.collectFees

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: collectFees() is external and permissionless, and after collecting it calls _manageUtilityAmounts(tokens, MAX_BPS, true). _manageUtilityAmounts transfers/burns based on the contract’s total balances of the pair tokens, not only the just-collected fees. Therefore, if the owner/DAO pre-funds the contract with OLAS and the paired token before calling increaseLiquidity/convertToV3 (or any accidental transfer occurs), any third party can call collectFees first and cause OLAS to be burned and the other token swept to treasury, breaking the intended operation and potentially causing permanent OLAS loss. There is no access control or balance-segregation safeguard.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `collectFees` function in `LiquidityManagerCore` is external and permissionless. It calls `_manageUtilityAmounts` with `utilizationRate = MAX_BPS` and `olasBurnOrTransfer = true`. This logic sweeps the entire balance of the configured tokens held by the contract, burning any OLAS and sending the paired token to the Treasury.

The intended workflow for `increaseLiquidity` requires the owner to first transfer tokens to the `LiquidityManager` contract (since `increaseLiquidity` relies on `balanceOf(address(this))`) and then call the function. An attacker can front-run the `increaseLiquidity` transaction (specifically sandwiching the transfer and the call) by calling `collectFees`. This immediately burns the OLAS tokens and moves the paired tokens to the Treasury, leaving the contract with zero balance. The owner's subsequent `increaseLiquidity` call will then fail or execute with zero amounts.

This constitutes a Denial of Service on the liquidity provision mechanism and causes a permanent loss of funds (burned OLAS) or a lock of funds (sent to Treasury requiring governance intervention).

## Impact
Permanent loss of OLAS tokens (burned) and temporary lock of other assets; prevention of liquidity provision.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Owner transfers 1000 OLAS and 1000 USDC to `LiquidityManager` intending to call `increaseLiquidity`. 2. Attacker sees the transfer in the mempool and calls `collectFees` on the relevant pool/tokens. 3. `collectFees` executes `_manageUtilityAmounts`, which burns the 1000 OLAS and transfers 1000 USDC to the Treasury. 4. Owner's `increaseLiquidity` transaction executes, finds 0 balance, and fails/does nothing.

## Proof of Code
function testGriefingCollectFees() public {
    // Setup: Fund contract mimicking owner transfer
    address token0 = address(new MockERC20("T0", "T0", 18));
    address token1 = address(new MockERC20("T1", "T1", 18));
    MockERC20(token0).mint(address(liquidityManager), 1000 ether);
    MockERC20(token1).mint(address(liquidityManager), 1000 ether);

    // Attacker calls collectFees
    vm.prank(attacker);
    // Assuming implementation exposes collectFees or similar public entry
    liquidityManager.collectFees(getTokens(token0, token1), 500);

    // Assert balances are gone
    assertEq(MockERC20(token0).balanceOf(address(liquidityManager)), 0);
    assertEq(MockERC20(token1).balanceOf(address(liquidityManager)), 0);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Restrict `collectFees` to `onlyOwner` or ensure `increaseLiquidity` can pull tokens (via permit or transferFrom) in the same transaction to prevent sandwiching.
```

### M-50 / `EmhDkex7xg1Cjw_uSbJIZ`
- Finding title: Front-running `collectFees` Causes Revert and Blocks Governance/Maintenance Batches
- Report lines: 4831-4944
```md
## [M-50]. Front-running `collectFees` Causes Revert and Blocks Governance/Maintenance Batches

## id: EmhDkex7xg1Cjw_uSbJIZ

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
CheapGriefingOrDosProfit

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.collectFees

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: `LiquidityManagerCore.collectFees()` is permissionless and reverts if both collected amounts are zero (`if (amounts[0]==0 && amounts[1]==0) revert ZeroValue();`). A griefer can front-run a scheduled governance/maintenance transaction that includes `collectFees` by calling `collectFees` first, emptying `tokensOwed` for the position. The scheduled call then reverts on zero, which can fail an entire batched execution (e.g., timelock execution that sequences multiple actions in one transaction). There is no “no-op on zero” behavior, so no built-in mitigation.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `collectFees` function in `LiquidityManagerETH` (inherited from `LiquidityManagerCore`) allows anyone to trigger fee collection for a specific pool. However, the function enforces a strict check: `if (amounts[0] == 0 && amounts[1] == 0) revert ZeroValue();`.

This creates a griefing vector. If the protocol owner (e.g., a DAO via a Timelock or a keeper script) submits a transaction to collect fees (often as part of a larger maintenance batch), an attacker can observe this transaction in the mempool and front-run it by calling `collectFees` themselves. The attacker's call successfully collects the pending fees (which are sent to the Treasury/Burner, providing no direct profit to the attacker aside from griefing). Subsequently, the owner's transaction executes, finds zero fees remaining, and reverts. 

For a DAO, a reverting transaction in a proposal execution flow can cause significant operational delay, requiring a new proposal and voting process.

## Impact
Denial of Service for maintenance operations; disruption of DAO proposals that include fee collection.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Fees accumulate in a Uniswap V3 position managed by `LiquidityManagerETH`.
2. The DAO schedules a proposal that includes a call to `collectFees` for that position.
3. Just before the DAO's transaction is mined, an attacker calls `collectFees` on the same pool.
4. The attacker's transaction succeeds, collecting fees and resetting `tokensOwed` to zero.
5. The DAO's transaction attempts to execute `_collectFees`, which returns `[0, 0]`.
6. The `collectFees` function hits the `revert ZeroValue()` check.
7. The entire DAO transaction (and potentially other batched actions) fails.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {LiquidityManagerCore} from "contracts/pol/LiquidityManagerCore.sol";

contract MockCore is LiquidityManagerCore {
    constructor() LiquidityManagerCore(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(4), 1) {}
    function _burn(uint256) internal override {}
    function _checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2(address[] memory, bytes32) internal override returns (uint256[] memory) {}
    function _feeAmountTickSpacing(int24) internal pure override returns (int24) {}
    function _getV3Pool(address[] memory, int24) internal pure override returns (address) { return address(0x123); }
    function _mintV3(address[] memory, uint256[] memory, uint256[] memory, int24[] memory, int24, uint160) internal override returns (uint256, uint128, uint256[] memory) {}
    
    // Mock internal logic
    uint256 public fees = 100;
    
    // Override internal functions to simulate environment
    function checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(address) public pure returns (uint160) { return 0; }
    function _collectFees(uint256) internal returns (uint256[] memory amounts) {
        amounts = new uint256[](2);
        amounts[0] = fees;
        amounts[1] = fees;
        fees = 0; // Reset fees after collection
    }
    // Expose checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice via a mock in inheritance or just bypass in harness. 
    // But for this PoC, we rely on the logic flow.
}

contract CollectFeesDosTest is Test {
    MockCore lm;

    function setUp() public {
        lm = new MockCore();
        // Mock mapping
        // In a real test we would set mapPoolAddressPositionIds
    }

    function testGriefing() public {
        // 1. Attacker calls collectFees (simulated)
        // Calling the exposed wrapper in MockCore which replicates the vulnerable logic:
        // We simulate the logic directly:
        
        // Owner Tx pending...
        
        // Attacker Front-runs:
        uint256[] memory amounts = lm.expose_collectFees_Logic(); 
        // amounts are [100, 100]
        
        // 2. Owner Tx executes:
        // Fees are now 0.
        try lm.expose_collectFees_Logic() {
            fail();
        } catch Error(string memory reason) {
             assertEq(reason, "ZeroValue");
        }
    }
}

// Extension to MockCore to expose the logic flow of `collectFees`
contract MockCoreExposed is MockCore {
    function expose_collectFees_Logic() external returns (uint256[] memory) {
         // Simulate checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice pass
         // Simulate _collectFees
         uint256[] memory amounts = _collectFees(1);
         if (amounts[0] == 0 && amounts[1] == 0) revert("ZeroValue");
         return amounts;
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Modify `collectFees` to return early or succeed with zero amounts instead of reverting when no fees are collected.
```

### M-51 / `5bKr-eEzh_1TXHanQpoyA`

## [M-51]. Liquidity removal functions susceptible to sandwich attacks due to spot-price based slippage calculation

## id: 5bKr-eEzh_1TXHanQpoyA

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SandwichableOracle

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.decreaseLiquidity

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Both `_decreaseLiquidity` and `_increaseLiquidity` derive min-amount constraints from `slot0` spot price and then apply the `maxSlippage` haircut. This makes the min-amount baseline manipulable in a sandwich (attacker moves spot before execution, mins are computed from that moved spot). The pre-check `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` is intended to mitigate but is implemented in a fail-open/incorrect manner, so it does not reliably constrain price manipulation. Owner-only access does not prevent MEV extraction against predictable maintenance transactions.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
In `LiquidityManagerCore`, functions like `decreaseLiquidity` and `increaseLiquidity` calculate the minimum expected output amounts (`amountMin`) using the current on-chain spot price (`sqrtPriceX96` from `slot0`). Although `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` enforces a 10% deviation limit between spot and TWAP, this still allows the spot price to be manipulated within that 10% window. An attacker can sandwich a legitimate `decreaseLiquidity` transaction by manipulating the price to the edge of the allowed deviation, causing the protocol to withdraw liquidity at an unfavorable ratio/value, which the attacker can then arbitrage.

## Impact
Protocol assets in LP positions can be devalued by up to ~10% during liquidity management operations.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Governance/Owner submits a transaction to call `decreaseLiquidity`.
2. Attacker sees the transaction in the mempool.
3. Attacker front-runs with a swap that shifts the V3 pool price by 9.9%.
4. The `decreaseLiquidity` tx executes. `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` passes (9.9% < 10%).
5. The function calculates `amountMin` based on the *manipulated* spot price.
6. Liquidity is removed based on the manipulated price's token ratio.
7. Attacker back-runs to arbitrage the pool back to true price, profiting from the imbalance created and the protocol's unfavorable withdrawal.

## Proof of Code
function testSandwichDecreaseLiquidity() public {
    // Setup: Create position for Manager
    // ...
    // 1. Attacker manipulates price
    // 2. Manager calls decreaseLiquidity
    // vm.prank(owner);
    // manager.decreaseLiquidity(...);
    // 3. Verify amounts received are skewed/lossy compared to fair price
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate `amountMin` using the TWAP price rather than the spot price, or require the caller to provide `amountMin` values as arguments.
```

### M-52 / `xUBFAKv8d5CLTHwAZT0HL`
- Finding title: Permissionless `collectFees` enables griefing of fee compounding strategy in `changeRanges`
- Report lines: 4998-5052
```md
## [M-52]. Permissionless `collectFees` enables griefing of fee compounding strategy in `changeRanges`

## id: xUBFAKv8d5CLTHwAZT0HL

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
IncentiveMisalignmentOrGameTheory

## Exploit Type
IncentiveMisalignmentOrGameTheory

## Location
LiquidityManagerETH.collectFees

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: `LiquidityManagerCore.collectFees()` is permissionless (no `OwnerOnly` check) and, after `_collectFees`, it immediately calls `_manageUtilityAmounts(tokens, MAX_BPS, true)` which burns OLAS and transfers the paired token to the treasury. `changeRanges()` (owner-only) decreases liquidity and then collects fees/tokens and attempts to mint the new position with the collected amounts. A third party can front-run a predictable `changeRanges()` with `collectFees()` to extract the accrued fees before the rebalance, meaning those fees cannot be compounded into the new position. No mechanism exists for `changeRanges()` to recover those extracted fees. This is realistically exploitable as a griefing/MEV behavior, though it does not steal funds from the protocol (fees still go to burn/treasury), hence Medium impact as functional/economic degradation rather than direct loss.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `changeRanges` function is designed to rebalance liquidity by removing the old position, collecting all accrued fees and principal (`_collectFees`), and minting a new position with the total amount. This effectively compounds fees into the new liquidity position. However, the `collectFees` function is external and permissionless. It collects pending fees and immediately distributes them (burns OLAS / sends paired token to treasury) via `_manageUtilityAmounts(..., true)`. 

An attacker or griefer can observe a pending `changeRanges` transaction and front-run it with `collectFees`. This extracts the fees before `changeRanges` executes. Consequently, `changeRanges` will only have the principal liquidity to use for the new position, preventing the intended compounding of yield. This permanently separates the yield from the position for that epoch.

## Impact
Functional disruption of the protocol's liquidity management strategy. It forces the burning/extraction of fees instead of allowing the owner to reinvest/compound them, leading to lower future liquidity depth and yield generation than intended.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Owner broadcasts `changeRanges` to rebalance a large position with significant accrued fees, intending to compound them.
2. Attacker (or MEV bot) sees the transaction.
3. Attacker calls `collectFees` on the same pool.
4. `collectFees` executes: fees are collected, OLAS is burned, and the paired token is sent to the treasury.
5. `changeRanges` executes: it decreases liquidity and collects remaining tokens (now only principal). It mints the new position using only the principal.
6. The compounding opportunity is lost.

## Proof of Code
function testGriefCompounding() public {
    // Setup: positions with fees
    // 1. Attacker calls collectFees()
    vm.prank(attacker);
    lm.collectFees(tokens, fee);
    // 2. Owner calls changeRanges()
    vm.prank(owner);
    lm.changeRanges(tokens, fee, shifts, false);
    // Assert that new position liquidity is lower than if step 1 was skipped
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Restrict `collectFees` to `onlyOwner` or authorized keepers, or allow `changeRanges` to pull accumulated funds from the treasury (though complex) to restore compounding capability.
```

### H-53 / `hm-8vRYnTZc-UT60PzVdP`
- Finding title: Token-secured services bypass slashing mechanism due to bond wrapping
- Report lines: 5053-5115
```md
## [H-53]. Token-secured services bypass slashing mechanism due to bond wrapping

## id: hm-8vRYnTZc-UT60PzVdP

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Exploit Type
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Location
ServiceManager.unbond

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This is structurally present in the shown code: for token-secured services, ServiceManager overwrites each agent bond in `agentParams` to `BOND_WRAPPER = 1` before calling `serviceRegistry.create(...)`, while recording real token bonds separately via `serviceRegistryTokenUtility.createWithToken(...)`. Slashing is described as residing in ServiceRegistry and therefore would operate on the 1-wei bond value, not the real token stake. The TokenUtility interface exposes no slashing-related function or callback from ServiceRegistry. As a result, token bonds appear decoupled from the slashing mechanism, making effective slashing impossible for token-secured services.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `ServiceManager` allows creating services secured by ERC20 tokens. To make this compatible with the underlying `ServiceRegistry` (which expects ETH bonds), it sets the bond amount in the Registry to a nominal 1 wei (`BOND_WRAPPER`) while storing the actual token bond in `ServiceRegistryTokenUtility`. The `ServiceRegistry` handles slashing logic by reducing the bond amount it tracks. Since it only tracks 1 wei for token-secured services, any slashing event is capped at 1 wei. The `ServiceManager.unbond` function retrieves the full token refund from `ServiceRegistryTokenUtility` without checking if a slashing event occurred in the Registry. As a result, operators of token-secured services are effectively immune to financial penalties (slashing), breaking the protocol's crypto-economic security model.

## Impact
Malicious operators in token-secured services cannot be slashed, losing at most 1 wei of ETH instead of their staked tokens. This breaks the incentive structure.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Create a service secured by 1,000,000 OLAS.
2. `ServiceRegistry` records bond as 1 wei.
3. Operator misbehaves; protocol/multisig attempts to slash 500,000 OLAS.
4. `ServiceRegistry` slashes `min(slashAmount, bond)`, which is 1 wei.
5. Operator calls `unbond`.
6. `ServiceManager` calls `utility.unbondTokenRefund`, which returns the full 1,000,000 OLAS (as Utility is unaware of Registry slashing).
7. Operator retrieves full stake despite misbehavior.

## Proof of Code
// Pseudo-test demonstrating state mismatch
function testSlashingIneffective() public {
    // Setup token service
    uint256 bond = 1000 ether;
    serviceManager.create(owner, address(token), config, ids, params, thresh);
    
    // Operator bonds
    serviceManager.registerAgents(serviceId, instances, ids);
    
    // Simulate Slash in Registry (mock)
    // Registry reduces bond from 1 wei to 0.
    
    // Unbond
    vm.prank(operator);
    serviceManager.unbond(serviceId);
    
    // Check balance
    assertEq(token.balanceOf(operator), bond); // Should be less if slashed, but is full
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Implement a hook in `ServiceRegistryTokenUtility` to query the `ServiceRegistry` for slashing data (e.g. `getOperatorBalance` vs `originalBond`) before calculating refunds, or ensure `ServiceRegistry` calls out to Utility during slash.
```

### M-54 / `HhcCnvrmE_JlDTCnxW8cq`
- Finding title: Ineffective slippage protection in `decreaseLiquidity` allows value extraction via sandwich attacks
- Report lines: 5116-5185
```md
## [M-54]. Ineffective slippage protection in `decreaseLiquidity` allows value extraction via sandwich attacks

## id: HhcCnvrmE_JlDTCnxW8cq

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
LiquidityManagerETH.decreaseLiquidity

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: `LiquidityManagerCore._decreaseLiquidity` computes `amount0Min/amount1Min` from the current `slot0` spot price (`_getPriceAndObservationIndexFromSlot0`) and applies `(MAX_BPS - maxSlippage)`. Because the baseline is the manipulable spot price, an attacker can manipulate the spot price in the same block (sandwich) so the computed mins reflect the manipulated state, making the slippage check largely self-referential. The intended pre-check `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(pool)` does not reliably prevent manipulation (it can return spot on TWAP failure and is implemented incorrectly). Owner-only access does not prevent MEV extraction when owner transactions are visible (timelock/keeper).
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The functions `decreaseLiquidity`, `increaseLiquidity`, and `changeRanges` in `LiquidityManagerCore` calculate the minimum output amounts (`amountMin`) using the pool's *current spot price* (`slot0`). 

```solidity
(uint160 sqrtPriceX96,) = _getPriceAndObservationIndexFromSlot0(pool);
(amountsMin[0], amountsMin[1]) = LiquidityAmounts.getAmountsForLiquidity(sqrtPriceX96, ...);
amountsMin[0] = amountsMin[0] * (MAX_BPS - maxSlippage) / MAX_BPS;
```

The only protection against price manipulation is the `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` function, which allows the spot price to deviate by up to 10% (`MAX_ALLOWED_DEVIATION`) from the TWAP. 

This design is fundamentally flawed for slippage protection. An attacker can sandwich the transaction by manipulating the pool price by up to ~9.9%. The `decreaseLiquidity` function will then calculate `amountsMin` based on this manipulated price. Since the execution happens atomically against the same pool state, the 'slippage' check (e.g., 1%) effectively checks the execution against the manipulated price itself, not a fair market price. This guarantees the check passes while the protocol suffers significant loss (up to the 10% deviation cap plus user slippage buffer).

## Impact
Loss of funds up to ~10% of the transaction value per operation due to MEV/sandwich attacks.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Protocol Owner submits `changeRanges` to rebalance a large position.
2. Attacker sees the transaction and front-runs it.
3. Attacker swaps in the V3 pool to skew the price by 9.9% (just under the 10% deviation limit).
4. `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` passes.
5. `changeRanges` calls `_decreaseLiquidity`.
6. `_decreaseLiquidity` reads the skewed `sqrtPriceX96`.
7. It calculates `amountsMin` based on the skewed price.
8. Liquidity is removed. The protocol receives amounts consistent with the skewed price (e.g., mostly the devalued token).
9. Attacker back-runs to close the arbitrage, pocketing the difference.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/pol/LiquidityManagerETH.sol";
// Mock libraries would be needed to simulate V3 math fully, 
// but the logic flow demonstrates the vulnerability.

contract TestSlippage is Test {
    function testSlippageCalculation() public {
        // Conceptual test: 
        // 1. Mock V3 pool returns manipulated price P' = P * 1.09
        // 2. Mock Oracle check passes (deviation < 10%)
        // 3. LiquidityManager calculates minOut based on P'
        // 4. Assert minOut accepts P' which is 9% worse than P
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate `amountMin` constraints off-chain and pass them as parameters to the function, or calculate them on-chain using the TWAP price (not spot price). Reduce `MAX_ALLOWED_DEVIATION` significantly or enforce strict slippage against a trusted reference price.
```

### H-55 / `UqwgiRLNmp_qkR0i5nqZF`
- Finding title: Operators can bypass token bond requirement in ServiceManager by communicating directly with ServiceRegistry
- Report lines: 5186-5249
```md
## [H-55]. Operators can bypass token bond requirement in ServiceManager by communicating directly with ServiceRegistry

## id: UqwgiRLNmp_qkR0i5nqZF

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
IncentiveMisalignmentOrGameTheory

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
ServiceManager.registerAgents

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The wrapper design explicitly sets ServiceRegistry bond requirements to `BOND_WRAPPER = 1`. Unless ServiceRegistry itself restricts `registerAgents` to the manager (not shown here), its public interface suggests operators can call it directly and satisfy only the 1-wei bond, bypassing the token-utility deposit path.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `ServiceManager` contract is designed to manage services where operators must post a bond in ERC20 tokens (via `ServiceRegistryTokenUtility`). To achieve this, it wraps the bond requirement by setting the bond value in the `ServiceRegistry` to a nominal `1 wei` (BOND_WRAPPER) and enforcing the actual token transfer in `ServiceManager.registerAgents`. However, the `ServiceRegistry` itself (implied by the wrapper logic and `IService` interface) allows permissionless registration of agents as long as the bond defined in the registry (1 wei) is paid. 

A malicious operator can call `ServiceRegistry.registerAgents` directly, bypassing the `ServiceManager` entirely. By doing so, they only pay the 1 wei bond (in ETH) and avoid depositing the required ERC20 tokens. This results in the service being filled with operators who have no economic stake (token bond) at risk, completely undermining the security model of token-secured services. The Service Owner relies on the `ServiceManager` to enforce security, but the architecture fails to prevent circumvention.

## Impact
Operators can register for token-secured services without paying the token bond, rendering the service economic security mechanism useless.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. User creates a service via `ServiceManager.create` with a high Token bond (e.g., 1000 USDC) and some agent slots.
2. `ServiceManager` sets the bond in `ServiceRegistry` to 1 wei.
3. Attacker calls `ServiceRegistry.registerAgents` directly (bypassing `ServiceManager`), sending only 1 wei per agent.
4. `ServiceRegistry` accepts the registration as the 1 wei bond requirement is met.
5. Attacker occupies the slots without depositing 1000 USDC.
6. Attacker is now an operator in the service with negligible stake.

## Proof of Code
/* This is a conceptual proof as it requires mocking ServiceRegistry behavior */
// Assume ServiceRegistry mock accepts registerAgents with 1 wei if bond is 1 wei.

function testBypassTokenBond() public {
    // Setup: Create service with token bond via Manager
    uint256 serviceId = serviceManager.create(owner, tokenAddress, configHash, agentIds, agentParams, threshold);
    
    // Attack: Register directly on Registry
    // This bypasses the TokenUtility transfer in ServiceManager
    // The attacker pays 1 wei instead of the token bond
    vm.prank(attacker);
    serviceRegistry.registerAgents{value: 1}(attacker, serviceId, agentInstances, agentIds);
    
    // Check: Attacker is registered
    assertTrue(serviceRegistry.exists(serviceId));
    // Check: No tokens deposited in Utility for attacker
    uint256 bond = tokenUtility.getOperatorBond(attacker, serviceId);
    assertEq(bond, 0);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
The `ServiceManager` cannot easily patch `ServiceRegistry` behavior if it is already deployed. Mitigation involves ensuring `ServiceRegistry` uses an `OperatorWhitelist` that restricts registration to calls coming from `ServiceManager` or requires `ServiceOwner` to manually configure an allowlist that enforces `ServiceManager` usage. Alternatively, `ServiceManager` should retain ownership of the service or `ServiceRegistry` must check for token-secured status.
```

### H-56 / `JKukh0TEDERpyoA6wyy8M`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS in _verifyBridgedData due to dirty upper bits in assembly unpacking
- Report lines: 5250-5345
```md
## [H-56]. Permanent DoS in _verifyBridgedData due to dirty upper bits in assembly unpacking

## id: JKukh0TEDERpyoA6wyy8M

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataGnosis._verifyBridgedData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: In VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData, `payloadLength` is a uint32 but is assigned via `payloadLength := mload(add(data, i))` where the loaded 32-byte word includes 28 bytes of preceding data (target suffix + value) and only the last 4 bytes are the intended length. Solidity does not automatically mask “dirty” upper bits for values assigned from inline assembly to smaller types, so `payloadLength` can become an enormous 256-bit value. This then drives `new bytes(payloadLength)` and the copy loop, causing OOG/memory allocation reverts for essentially any normal target/value, bricking verification.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_verifyBridgedData` function in `VerifyBridgedData.sol` uses inline assembly to unpack a packed byte stream containing `target`, `value`, and `payloadLength`. Specifically, it loads `payloadLength` (a `uint32`) using `mload` from an offset that aligns the 4-byte length at the bottom of the 32-byte register. However, `mload` reads the entire 32-byte word, which includes the preceding 28 bytes of data (the last 16 bytes of the `target` address and the 12-byte `value`). 

Solidity 0.8+ does not automatically scrub/mask the upper bits of variables assigned via inline assembly. As a result, the `payloadLength` variable retains these dirty upper bits. When `payloadLength` is subsequently used to allocate memory via `new bytes(payloadLength)`, the value is interpreted as a massive integer (unless the target address ends in 16 zero bytes and the value is 0), causing the transaction to revert due to an Out-Of-Gas or Memory Limit error. This renders the bridge verification logic non-functional for virtually all legitimate targets.

## Impact
The contract is functionally broken. It will revert for almost any valid governance message where the target address does not have a specific 16-byte zero suffix, preventing cross-chain governance execution.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Governance sends a standard cross-chain message to a target contract (e.g., address 0x123...456) on Gnosis chain.
2. The payload is packed as `target` (20 bytes) | `value` (12 bytes) | `length` (4 bytes) | `data`.
3. `GuardCM` calls `processBridgeData` to verify this payload.
4. Inside `_verifyBridgedData`, the assembly block loads `payloadLength`.
5. Because the target address ends in non-zero bytes (0x...456), the loaded word for `payloadLength` contains these bytes in the high-order bits.
6. `new bytes(payloadLength)` attempts to allocate terabytes of memory, causing an immediate revert.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.30;

import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/multisigs/bridge_verifier/ProcessBridgedDataGnosis.sol";

contract ProcessBridgedDataGnosisTest is Test {
    ProcessBridgedDataGnosis verifier;
    address constant TARGET = address(0x1111111111111111111111111111111111111111);
    address constant MEDIATOR = address(0x2);
    uint256 constant CHAIN_ID = 100;

    function setUp() public {
        verifier = new ProcessBridgedDataGnosis();
        // Mock allowlist
        bytes4 selector = bytes4(hex"cafebabe");
        uint256 key = uint256(uint160(TARGET));
        key |= uint256(uint32(selector)) << 160;
        key |= uint256(CHAIN_ID) << 192;
        stdstore.target(address(verifier)).sig("mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds(uint256)").with_key(key).checked_write(true);
    }

    function test_RevertDueToDirtyBits() public {
        // Create payload: target(20) | value(12) | len(4) | data(4)
        bytes memory innerData = hex"cafebabe";
        bytes memory l2Msg = abi.encodePacked(TARGET, uint96(0), uint32(innerData.length), innerData);
        
        // Wrap in mediator payload
        bytes memory medData = abi.encode(l2Msg);
        bytes memory medPayload = abi.encodePacked(verifier.PROCESS_MESSAGE_FROM_FOREIGN(), medData);
        
        // Wrap in L1 payload
        bytes memory l1Data = abi.encode(MEDIATOR, medPayload, uint256(0));
        // Add selector and pack correctly for the manual decoding in contract
        // The contract expects: selector(4) | address(32) | offset(32) | gas(32) | len(32) | data...
        // Reconstruct exact abi.encodeWithSelector layout
        bytes memory callData = abi.encodeWithSelector(
            verifier.REQUIRE_TO_PASS_MESSAGE(),
            MEDIATOR,
            medPayload,
            uint256(1000000)
        );

        // This should pass if logic is correct, but reverts due to dirty bits
        vm.expectRevert(); 
        verifier.processBridgeData(callData, MEDIATOR, CHAIN_ID);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Mask the loaded value in the assembly block to ensure only the lower 32 bits are used:
```solidity
payloadLength := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xffffffff)
```
```

### M-57 / `8wz6XEvPARfYFaA2rTY_R`
- Finding title: DoS in `create` due to front-running of deterministic deployment
- Report lines: 5346-5441
```md
## [M-57]. DoS in `create` due to front-running of deterministic deployment

## id: 8wz6XEvPARfYFaA2rTY_R

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Deterministic deployment DoS via front-run pre-creation

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
SafeMultisigWithRecoveryModule.create

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Code path exists: SafeMultisigWithRecoveryModule.create() always calls ISafeProxyFactory.createProxyWithNonce(safe, safeParams, nonce). createProxyWithNonce uses CREATE2 so the proxy address is deterministic for a given (singleton, initializer, saltNonce). Here, initializer (safeParams) is fully determined by (owners, threshold, recoveryModule, payload, fallbackHandler, …) and saltNonce is the decoded nonce (or 0). There is no check like `if (predicted.code.length>0) return predicted;`. Therefore a mempool adversary can submit the same call first, deploying at that deterministic address, and the victim’s later tx reverts due to address collision. This is a grief/DoS (gas waste + blocked exact configuration until nonce changes), not a fund drain, hence Medium impact. No built-in safeguard present.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `create` function deploys a Safe multisig using `SafeProxyFactory.createProxyWithNonce`, which utilizes `CREATE2`. The salt for the deployment is derived from the initializer (constructed from owners, threshold, and recovery module) and a user-provided `nonce` (extracted from `data` or defaulted to 0). Since the deployment address is deterministic, an attacker can observe a pending `create` transaction and submit an identical transaction with a higher gas price (front-running). The attacker's transaction successfully deploys the proxy at the deterministic address. When the victim's transaction subsequently executes, the `SafeProxyFactory` attempts to deploy to the same address and reverts because the contract already exists (a standard behavior of the Gnosis Safe Proxy Factory for address collisions). This causes the victim's transaction to fail.

In the context of the Olas protocol, this can be used to grief users or block the registration of specific service configurations in the registries if the upstream logic relies on the success of `create`.

## Impact
Denial of service for multisig deployment; users waste gas and are unable to register services with specific configurations unless they manually change parameters (nonce).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. User broadcasts a transaction calling `create(owners, threshold, data)` where `data` encodes a specific nonce (or is empty for nonce 0).
2. Attacker observes the transaction and broadcasts the exact same function call with a higher gas price.
3. Attacker's transaction is mined first, deploying the Safe at address `A`.
4. User's transaction is mined. `SafeProxyFactory` attempts to deploy to `A` but fails because the contract already exists.
5. User's transaction reverts.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.30;

import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "contracts/multisigs/SafeMultisigWithRecoveryModule.sol";

contract MockSafeProxyFactory {
    mapping(address => bool) public existing;
    
    function createProxyWithNonce(
        address _singleton,
        bytes memory initializer,
        uint256 saltNonce
    ) external returns (address proxy) {
        // Deterministic address simulation based on input hash
        proxy = address(uint160(uint256(keccak256(abi.encode(_singleton, initializer, saltNonce)))));
        
        if (existing[proxy]) {
            revert("Create2 call failed");
        }
        
        existing[proxy] = true;
    }
}

contract SafeMultisigTest is Test {
    SafeMultisigWithRecoveryModule safeHelper;
    MockSafeProxyFactory factory;
    address recoveryModule = address(0x123);
    address safeSingleton = address(0x456);
    
    function setUp() public {
        factory = new MockSafeProxyFactory();
        safeHelper = new SafeMultisigWithRecoveryModule(safeSingleton, address(factory), recoveryModule);
    }
    
    function testFrontRunDoS() public {
        address[] memory owners = new address[](1);
        owners[0] = address(0xABC);
        uint256 threshold = 1;
        bytes memory data = ""; // nonce 0
        
        // 1. Attacker front-runs
        vm.prank(address(0xBAD));
        safeHelper.create(owners, threshold, data);
        
        // 2. Victim tries to create same setup
        vm.prank(address(0xCAFE));
        vm.expectRevert("Create2 call failed"); // Expect factory revert
        safeHelper.create(owners, threshold, data);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In `create`, compute the expected proxy address using `CREATE2` logic (or a helper). Check if a contract already exists at that address (`address.code.length > 0`). If it does, return the existing address immediately instead of calling the factory. This ensures the function is idempotent and immune to front-running.
```

### H-58 / `djzXKgCOJALUMcRLVF0na`
- Finding title: Service Owner can trap operator bonds by changing token address in update()
- Report lines: 5442-5519
```md
## [H-58]. Service Owner can trap operator bonds by changing token address in update()

## id: djzXKgCOJALUMcRLVF0na

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
GlobalParamMidFlowManipulation

## Exploit Type
GlobalParamMidFlowManipulation

## Location
ServiceManager.update

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: ServiceManager allows `update(token, ...)` to change the token address for an existing service and then calls `IServiceTokenUtility.createWithToken(serviceId, token, ...)` which (by name and usage) overwrites token/bond configuration. There is no check that all operator token bonds have already been refunded before changing the token. Because later refunds are routed via `unbondTokenRefund(operator, serviceId)`, a token address overwrite can plausibly strand previously deposited tokens if TokenUtility keys refunds by the current token. The ServiceManager code contains no safeguard preventing token changes while bonds exist, so a malicious service owner can create this situation during `TerminatedBonded`-like phases.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `ServiceManager.update` function allows a service owner to change the ERC20 token address used for bonds (`createWithToken`) even after operators have registered and deposited funds. In the Autonolas lifecycle, a service can be updated in the `TerminatedBonded` state (where operators still hold bonds and have not yet unbonded). If the service owner changes the token address during this state, the `ServiceManager` calls `IServiceTokenUtility.createWithToken` with the new token, overwriting the token configuration for that service. However, it does not trigger a refund of the previously deposited tokens. When operators subsequently call `unbond`, `ServiceManager` calls `unbondTokenRefund`, which relies on the *current* token configuration. The utility will attempt to refund the *new* token (which the operator never deposited) or fail, leaving the operators' original tokens stranded in the utility contract.

## Impact
Operators lose their deposited bonds (theft/locking of funds) as the service owner can switch the bond currency to a different token, preventing retrieval of the original assets.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Service Owner creates a service with Token A (e.g., USDC).
2. Operator registers and bonds 1000 USDC via `registerAgents`.
3. Service Owner activates and eventually terminates the service. State becomes `TerminatedBonded`.
4. Operator has not yet unbonded.
5. Service Owner calls `ServiceManager.update` with Token B (e.g., a malicious token or just different).
6. `ServiceManager` calls `TokenUtility.createWithToken(serviceId, TokenB, ...)` overwriting the configuration.
7. Operator calls `unbond`.
8. `ServiceManager` calls `TokenUtility.unbondTokenRefund`.
9. TokenUtility attempts to refund Token B. Operator receives nothing (or worthless Token B) and the USDC remains stuck in the Utility contract.

## Proof of Code
contract ServiceManagerTest is Test {
    ServiceManager sm;
    address registry = makeAddr("registry");
    address tokenUtility = makeAddr("tokenUtility");
    address owner = makeAddr("owner");
    address operator = makeAddr("operator");
    address tokenA = makeAddr("tokenA");
    address tokenB = makeAddr("tokenB");
    uint256 serviceId = 1;

    function setUp() public {
        sm = new ServiceManager(registry, tokenUtility);
    }

    function testUpdateSwapsToken() public {
        // Setup: Update called with Token B
        uint32[] memory agentIds = new uint32[](1);
        agentIds[0] = 1;
        IService.AgentParams[] memory params = new IService.AgentParams[](1);
        params[0] = IService.AgentParams(1, 100);
        bytes32 configHash = bytes32(0);
        
        // Expect reset not called for Token A (since we are in 'else' block for custom token)
        // Expect createWithToken called with Token B
        vm.mockCall(registry, abi.encodeWithSelector(IService.update.selector), abi.encode(true));
        vm.mockCall(tokenUtility, abi.encodeWithSelector(IServiceTokenUtility.createWithToken.selector, serviceId, tokenB, agentIds, [uint256(100)]), "");
        
        // Call update
        vm.prank(owner);
        sm.update(tokenB, configHash, agentIds, params, 1, serviceId);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In `ServiceManager.update`, ensure that if the token address is being changed, there are no active bonds remaining for the service (i.e., require the service to be fully unbonded or `resetServiceToken` to effectively refund or check balance).
```

### H-59 / `aou_NhKMnmWJ8ALKW8Ypg`
- Finding title: Lack of slippage protection in V3 buyback enables value extraction via sandwich attacks
- Report lines: 5520-5654
```md
## [H-59]. Lack of slippage protection in V3 buyback enables value extraction via sandwich attacks

## id: aou_NhKMnmWJ8ALKW8Ypg

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
FlashLoanEconomicManipulation

## Exploit Type
FrontrunMev

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer._buyOLAS

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: In the V3 path, BuyBackBurner._buyOLAS(secondToken, amount, tickSpacing) only calls LiquidityManager.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(pool) as a pre-check and then executes BuyBackBurnerBalancer._performSwap with amountOutMinimum hardcoded to 1 and sqrtPriceLimitX96=0. There is no minOut derived from an oracle/center price and no post-swap price validation. Thus, in thin liquidity conditions or under MEV sandwiching, the swap can execute at a catastrophically bad price yet still succeed (since minOut=1), causing direct value loss on buybacks.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_buyOLAS` function (V3 overload) in `BuyBackBurnerBalancer` executes a swap on a Concentrated Liquidity (V3) pool without enforcing a minimum output amount derived from the oracle price. While it calls `ILiquidityManager(liquidityManager).checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(pool)` to validate the pool state *before* the swap, it does not use the returned price to set `amountOutMinimum` in the swap parameters. Instead, `_performSwap` hardcodes `amountOutMinimum: 1`. Additionally, unlike the V2 path, there is no post-swap check to verify the execution price. 

This allows an attacker (MEV bot) to sandwich the transaction: 
1. Frontrun: Manipulate the pool price (e.g., flash swap) up to the maximum deviation allowed by `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice`.
2. Victim: The `buyBack` transaction executes. The pre-check passes (as the price is within the allowed deviation or the check is loose). The swap executes with `amountOutMinimum: 1`, accepting the manipulated price and suffering 100% slippage tolerance.
3. Backrun: Arbitrage the pool back to true price.

Furthermore, the swap uses `deadline: block.timestamp`, which allows validators to hold the transaction until market conditions are unfavorable.

## Impact
Protocol suffers high slippage and loss of funds (OLAS burned) during buybacks due to sandwich attacks.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker observes a `buyBack` transaction in the mempool.
2. Attacker calculates the max price deviation `LiquidityManager` allows.
3. Attacker buys OLAS in the pool, pushing the price to the limit.
4. `buyBack` executes, swapping tokens for OLAS at the inflated price. Since `amountOutMinimum` is 1, the swap succeeds regardless of poor execution.
5. Attacker sells OLAS, profiting at the protocol's expense.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {BuyBackBurnerBalancer} from "contracts/utils/BuyBackBurnerBalancer.sol";

contract MockLiquidityManager {
    function checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(address) external pure returns (uint160) {
        return 1000; // Returns a price, but it's ignored
    }
    function factoryV3() external pure returns (address) {
        return address(0x123);
    }
}

contract MockSwapRouter {
    struct ExactInputSingleParams {
        address tokenIn;
        address tokenOut;
        int24 tickSpacing;
        address recipient;
        uint256 deadline;
        uint256 amountIn;
        uint256 amountOutMinimum;
        uint160 sqrtPriceLimitX96;
    }
    function exactInputSingle(ExactInputSingleParams calldata params) external payable returns (uint256 amountOut) {
        // Vulnerability: logic allows amountOutMinimum to be 1
        if (params.amountOutMinimum == 1) {
             return 1; // Return minimal amount, simulating worst-case slippage/sandwich
        }
        return params.amountIn; 
    }
}

contract MockERC20 {
    function balanceOf(address) external pure returns (uint256) { return 100 ether; }
    function transfer(address, uint256) external returns (bool) { return true; }
    function approve(address, uint256) external returns (bool) { return true; }
}

contract MockFactory {
    function getPool(address, address, int24) external pure returns (address) { return address(0xABC); }
}

contract BuyBackTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerBalancer burner;
    MockLiquidityManager lm;
    MockSwapRouter router;
    MockERC20 token;
    MockFactory factory;

    function setUp() public {
        lm = new MockLiquidityManager();
        router = new MockSwapRouter();
        token = new MockERC20();
        factory = new MockFactory();
        
        burner = new BuyBackBurnerBalancer(address(lm), address(0xBurn), address(0xTreasury), address(router));
        
        // Initialize logic to set OLAS
        address[] memory accounts = new address[](4);
        accounts[0] = address(token); // OLAS
        accounts[1] = address(token); // Native
        accounts[2] = address(0xOracle);
        accounts[3] = address(0xVault);
        
        bytes memory payload = abi.encode(accounts, bytes32(0), uint256(100));
        burner.initialize(payload);
        
        // Whitelist pool
        address[] memory pools = new address[](1);
        pools[0] = address(0xABC);
        bool[] memory statuses = new bool[](1);
        statuses[0] = true;
        
        vm.prank(address(this)); 
        burner.setV3PoolStatuses(pools, statuses);
    }

    function testV3Slippage() public {
        // Expectation: Protocol should receive ~fair value. 
        // Reality: Receives 1 wei due to lack of slippage protection.
        vm.mockCall(address(0x123), abi.encodeWithSelector(bytes4(keccak256("getPool(address,address,int24)"))), abi.encode(address(0xABC)));
        
        burner.buyBack(address(token), 1 ether, int24(100));
        // If successful, it means the contract accepted 1 unit of output for 1 ether input.
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In `_buyOLAS` (V3), use the `uint160 centerPrice` returned from `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` to calculate a reasonable `amountOutMinimum` for the swap, and pass this value to `_performSwap` instead of 1.
```

### M-60 / `83baltM1bdDHlVMEkRwV_`

## [M-60]. Missing Transaction Deadline and Floating Slippage

## id: 83baltM1bdDHlVMEkRwV_

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore._increaseLiquidity

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The internal Uniswap V3 calls use deadline=block.timestamp, so callers cannot enforce an expiry relative to when they decided to act. Additionally, min amounts are computed in-contract at execution time from the then-current pool price and maxSlippage, rather than being caller-provided bounds. This means that if market conditions change while the tx is pending, the contract will accept the new price context and proceed, rather than reverting based on the caller’s original intent. There is no explicit user-specified slippage/price-limit parameter to protect against such drift.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The functions `_increaseLiquidity` and `_decreaseLiquidity` use `block.timestamp` as the deadline for Uniswap V3 calls. Additionally, the `amountMin` parameters (slippage protection) are calculated dynamically during execution based on the *current* spot price (after passing the oracle check). This combination means there is no protection against market movements between transaction submission and execution. If a transaction sits in the mempool while the price moves significantly (but Spot and TWAP move together), it will execute at the new price, potentially forcing the user to enter/exit a position at a price they did not approve.

## Impact
Liquidity operations executed at unintended prices, leading to portfolio misalignment or loss of value.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Owner submits `increaseLiquidity` when price is 1000. 
2. Network congestion delays tx. Price moves to 1500 (natural market move). 
3. Tx executes. `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` sees Spot 1500 ~= TWAP 1500. 
4. `amountMin` is calculated based on 1500. 
5. Liquidity added at 1500. Owner intended 1000.

## Proof of Code
function testMissingDeadline() public {
    // Set price 1000
    // ...
    // Advance time and move price to 1500
    vm.warp(block.timestamp + 1 hours);
    pool.swap(...);
    // Execute stale tx
    vm.prank(owner);
    manager.increaseLiquidity(...);
    // Assert liquidity added at 1500
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Accept a user-provided `deadline` and `minAmount` (or price limit) parameters in the external functions and pass them to the internal logic and Uniswap calls.
```
