## [H-61]. V3 buyback susceptible to sandwich attacks due to lack of slippage protection

## id: CwF1pka2RFJf4Cg0k_sNI

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
FlashLoanEconomicManipulation

## Exploit Type
FrontrunMev

## Location
BuyBackBurnerUniswap._buyOLAS

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: In `BuyBackBurnerUniswap._performSwap` (V3), the Uniswap V3 swap uses `amountOutMinimum: 1` and `sqrtPriceLimitX96: 0`, i.e., effectively no execution-time slippage protection. The only intended safeguard is the pre-swap call to `ILiquidityManager.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(pool)` from `_buyOLAS` (V3). This does not constrain execution price during the swap, and additionally the `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` implementation fails open on TWAP failure (and is otherwise flawed). A sandwich attacker can front-run to move price, let the protocol swap with no min-out, then back-run to capture value. This is a direct protocol-funds efficiency loss and is exploitable whenever the contract holds meaningful balances.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `BuyBackBurner` (via `BuyBackBurnerUniswap`), the V3 buyback path (`_buyOLAS` -> `_performSwap`) calls `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` to validate that the start price is close to the TWAP. However, it executes the actual swap using `amountOutMinimum: 1` (zero slippage protection) and does not perform a post-swap price check (unlike the V2 path which checks `tradePrice` vs `previousPrice`).

While `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` ensures the *start* price is fair, it does not limit the price impact *during* the swap. An attacker can sandwich the `buyBack` transaction (which may swap large amounts of tokens) by buying OLAS beforehand and selling afterwards, extracting the value lost to slippage. The contract relies solely on the pre-check, leaving the execution vulnerable to unlimited slippage.

## Impact
Loss of protocol funds (buyback efficiency) due to MEV extraction (sandwich attacks).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. `BuyBackBurner` holds a significant amount of `USDC`.
2. Attacker observes a pending `buyBack(USDC, ...)` transaction.
3. Attacker front-runs by buying OLAS on the V3 pool, pushing the spot price up (but remaining within the 10% deviation allowed by `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice`).
4. `buyBack` executes. `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` passes. The swap executes with `amountOutMin: 1`.
5. Due to the large input amount and lack of slippage limit, the swap pushes the price significantly higher (high price impact).
6. Attacker back-runs by selling OLAS, profiting from the artificially inflated price caused by the protocol's swap.

## Proof of Code
function testSandwichV3BuyBack() public {
    // Setup: BuyBackBurner has 100k USDC
    uint256 amount = 100_000e6;
    deal(address(usdc), address(buyBackBurner), amount);
    
    // 1. Attacker front-runs: Swap ETH to OLAS to push price
    vm.startPrank(attacker);
    swap(eth, olas, hugeAmount);
    vm.stopPrank();

    // 2. Protocol buyback executes
    // checkPool passes because deviation < 10%
    // Swap executes with minOut=1, incurring huge slippage
    buyBackBurner.buyBack(address(usdc), amount, fee);

    // 3. Attacker back-runs
    vm.startPrank(attacker);
    swap(olas, eth, balanceOlas);
    vm.stopPrank();
    // Attacker profits, Protocol gets very little OLAS
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate a specific `amountOutMinimum` using the `centerSqrtPriceX96` returned by `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` and the `maxSlippage` parameter, then pass this minimum to the swap router.
```

### M-62 / `LB3imQCeQCWm5NEJAbtuA`
- Finding title: Arithmetic Underflow or Oracle DoS due to Unit Mismatch in maxSlippage
- Report lines: 5774-5849
```md
## [M-62]. Arithmetic Underflow or Oracle DoS due to Unit Mismatch in maxSlippage

## id: LB3imQCeQCWm5NEJAbtuA

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
IntegerMath

## Exploit Type
PricePrecision

## Location
BuyBackBurnerUniswap._buyOLAS

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This is the same code path as 4io3fjPNKVI9NBrbcd1xQ: BuyBackBurner._buyOLAS(V2) computes (100 - maxSlippage) and (100 + maxSlippage) and divides by 100, requiring maxSlippage to be a percent. Elsewhere the system uses 1e18-scaled deviation constants and oracles that likely expect 1e18-scaled slippage. If maxSlippage is configured as 1e17 (10%), arithmetic underflow in 100 - maxSlippage reverts and breaks buyBack. If configured as 10, the oracle validation may become overly strict depending on oracle implementation. The unit mismatch is an implementation defect.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `_buyOLAS` (V2 path), the contract calculates slippage bounds: `lowerBound = (previousPrice * (100 - maxSlippage)) / 100;`. This formula assumes `maxSlippage` is a percentage value (0-100).

However, the contract interacts with an Oracle via `IOracle(poolOracle).validatePrice(maxSlippage)`. The Olas standard `UniswapPriceOracle` and the constant `MAX_ALLOWED_DEVIATION` (defined as `1e17` or 10% in `BuyBackBurner.sol`) indicate that the system expects slippage/deviation in WAD format (1e18 scale).

If `maxSlippage` is configured as `1e17` (10%) to satisfy the Oracle, the calculation `100 - maxSlippage` triggers an integer underflow, causing `buyBack` to revert. If `maxSlippage` is configured as `10` (10%) to satisfy the math, `validatePrice(10)` is called with 10 wei, effectively enforcing zero tolerance and causing DoS.

## Impact
The V2 buyback mechanism is functionally unusable (DoS) for any realistic slippage configuration.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Admin initializes contract with `maxSlippage = 1e17` (standard 10%).
2. User calls `buyBack`.
3. Execution reaches `_buyOLAS`.
4. Calculation `100 - 100000000000000000` causes arithmetic underflow revert.

Alternative:
1. Admin initializes with `maxSlippage = 10`.
2. `validatePrice(10)` calls Oracle. Oracle checks `deviation <= 10 wei`. Fails unless price is exactly equal to TWAP down to the wei.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/utils/BuyBackBurnerUniswap.sol";

contract SlippageUnitMismatch is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerUniswap buyBack;

    function setUp() public {
        buyBack = new BuyBackBurnerUniswap(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(4));
        address[] memory accs = new address[](4);
        accs[0] = address(0x1); accs[1] = address(0x2); accs[2] = address(0x3); accs[3] = address(0x4);
        
        // Case 1: Initialize with WAD slippage (10%)
        bytes memory payload = abi.encode(accs, uint256(1e17));
        buyBack.initialize(payload);
    }

    function testUnderflow() public {
        // Use harness to expose _buyOLAS or replicate logic
        uint256 maxSlippage = buyBack.maxSlippage(); // 1e17
        
        vm.expectRevert(); // Arithmetic over/underflow
        // This mimics the line: (previousPrice * (100 - maxSlippage)) / 100
        uint256 result = (1e18 * (100 - maxSlippage)) / 100;
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Standardize units. If `maxSlippage` is WAD (1e18 scale), change the bound calculation to `(previousPrice * (1e18 - maxSlippage)) / 1e18`.
```

## [M-63]. DoS in `convertToV3` due to Token Ordering Mismatch

## id: qvH2FFK7g7meT3VvVRS48

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StandardViolation

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
LiquidityManagerOptimism._mintV3

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: LiquidityManagerOptimism._mintV3 passes token0=tokens[0], token1=tokens[1] directly to Slipstream mint, and LiquidityManagerCore approves allowances in that same unsorted order. If the underlying position manager/factory logic expects canonical token0/token1 ordering (common in UniswapV3-style periphery), mismatched ordering can cause revert or allowance mismatch. This is particularly relevant because Balancer’s getPoolTokens order is enforced for V2 exit, potentially forcing an order that differs from the V3 canonical sort.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `convertToV3` function requires the input `tokens` array to match the order of the Balancer V2 pool (verified via `getPoolTokens`). It then passes these tokens directly to `ISlipstreamV3.mint` as `token0` and `token1` in the `MintParams` struct, along with `amounts` in the same order. Standard Uniswap V3 / Slipstream logic requires `token0` < `token1` (sorted by address). If a Balancer pool has tokens in reverse order (Address A > Address B) and the Balancer vault returns them in that order, the `tokens` array will be unsorted. Passing unsorted tokens to `mint` will cause the call to fail (revert) or interact with a non-existent pool, making `convertToV3` unusable for such pairs.

## Impact
Permanent Denial of Service for specific token pairs where Balancer token order differs from Uniswap V3 canonical sort order.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Identify a token pair where `Address(TokenA) > Address(TokenB)`.
2. Create a Balancer pool with order `[TokenA, TokenB]`.
3. Call `convertToV3` with `tokens=[TokenA, TokenB]`.
4. `_mintV3` constructs `MintParams(token0: TokenA, token1: TokenB)`.
5. `PositionManager.mint` reverts because it expects sorted tokens or computes the wrong pool address.

## Proof of Code
function testTokenOrderDoS() public {
    // Mock tokens where A > B
    // Call convertToV3 passing [A, B]
    // Expect Revert
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In `_mintV3`, sort `tokens[0]` and `tokens[1]` and their corresponding `amounts` before populating `MintParams`.
```

### H-64 / `3-di4PwAG13Sk9ES6E9N_`
- Finding title: UniswapPriceOracle.validatePrice fails to enforce TWAP validation allowing price manipulation
- Report lines: 5897-6017
```md
## [H-64]. UniswapPriceOracle.validatePrice fails to enforce TWAP validation allowing price manipulation

## id: 3-di4PwAG13Sk9ES6E9N_

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
OracleUsingDEXorTWAP

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
UniswapPriceOracle.validatePrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The validatePrice() implementation does not compute a real TWAP. It reads cumulativePriceLast from the pair, then constructs a new “cumulativePrice” as cumulativePriceLast + tradePrice * elapsedTime, and computes timeWeightedAverage = (cumulativePrice - cumulativePriceLast) / elapsedTime, which algebraically reduces to tradePrice for any elapsedTime>0. Therefore derivation becomes 0 and the function returns true for any slippage>=(0) (except the explicit same-block return false case or division-by-zero if tradePrice==0). This matches the finding’s claim that the check compares spot against itself and can’t detect manipulation. There is no safeguard that forces a minimum observation window, stores past observations, or uses Uniswap’s counterfactual cumulative pricing correctly. Exploitability is high because an attacker can manipulate spot price and still pass validation; capital requirements depend on pool liquidity, but the oracle check itself is effectively bypassed whenever elapsedTime>0.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `validatePrice` function purportedly validates the current price against a TWAP to enforce slippage limits. However, the implementation calculates the 'TWAP' by projecting the current spot price (`tradePrice`) over the time elapsed since the last update (`elapsedTime`). 

The calculation is:
```solidity
uint256 cumulativePrice = cumulativePriceLast + (tradePrice * elapsedTime);
uint256 timeWeightedAverage = (cumulativePrice - cumulativePriceLast) / elapsedTime;
```
Mathematically, this simplifies to `timeWeightedAverage = tradePrice`. 

Consequently, the function compares the current spot price against itself. The derivation is always 0, and the check `derivation <= slippage` always evaluates to `true` (provided `elapsedTime > 0`). This allows an attacker to manipulate the Uniswap pool price in one block and use the manipulated price in the next block. The oracle fails to enforce any historical price smoothing (e.g., the intended 30-minute window), completely bypassing slippage protection.

## Impact
An attacker can manipulate the oracle price via a multi-block attack (e.g., manipulating reserves in block N, executing sensitive transaction in block N+1), bypassing slippage checks. This can lead to significant loss of funds in downstream contracts (e.g., LiquidityManager) that rely on this oracle for fair value checks.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `UniswapPriceOracle` pointing to a mock Pair.
2. Set Pair reserves to reflect a 1:1 price. Call `validatePrice` -> returns true.
3. Attacker manipulates Pair reserves to 100:1 and updates `blockTimestampLast` (simulating a swap).
4. Wait for the next block (advance timestamp by 12 seconds).
5. Call `validatePrice`. The function calculates `elapsedTime = 12`. It projects the current (manipulated) 100:1 price over these 12 seconds.
6. The calculated TWAP is 100:1. The spot price is 100:1. Deviation is 0.
7. `validatePrice` returns `true`, accepting the 100x manipulated price.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;

import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/oracles/UniswapPriceOracle.sol";

contract MockPair {
    uint112 public reserve0;
    uint112 public reserve1;
    uint32 public blockTimestampLast;
    uint256 public price0CumulativeLast;
    uint256 public price1CumulativeLast;
    address public token0;
    address public token1;

    constructor(address _t0, address _t1) {
        token0 = _t0;
        token1 = _t1;
    }

    function getReserves() external view returns (uint112, uint112, uint32) {
        return (reserve0, reserve1, blockTimestampLast);
    }

    function setReserves(uint112 _r0, uint112 _r1, uint32 _ts) external {
        reserve0 = _r0;
        reserve1 = _r1;
        blockTimestampLast = _ts;
    }

    function price0CumulativeLast() external view returns (uint256) { return price0CumulativeLast; }
    function price1CumulativeLast() external view returns (uint256) { return price1CumulativeLast; }
    function token0() external view returns (address) { return token0; }
}

contract UniswapPriceOraclePoC is Test {
    UniswapPriceOracle oracle;
    MockPair pair;
    address token0 = address(0x1);
    address token1 = address(0x2);

    function setUp() public {
        pair = new MockPair(token0, token1);
        // Token1 is the base, so assuming token0 is OLAS. direction=0.
        oracle = new UniswapPriceOracle(token1, 1000, address(pair));
    }

    function testBypassSlippage() public {
        // 1. Initial State: Price 1:1
        pair.setReserves(1000 ether, 1000 ether, uint32(block.timestamp));
        assertTrue(oracle.validatePrice(100)); 

        // 2. Attack: Manipulate price to 100:1
        // Update reserves and timestamp (simulating swap in block N)
        pair.setReserves(10000 ether, 100 ether, uint32(block.timestamp));

        // 3. Move to next block
        vm.warp(block.timestamp + 12);

        // 4. Validate Price
        // Spot price is 100. Expected TWAP (if windowed) would be closer to 1.
        // But implementation returns TWAP = Spot = 100.
        // Validation passes despite 100x change.
        assertTrue(oracle.validatePrice(100));
        
        assertEq(oracle.getPrice(), 100e18);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
To implement a correct TWAP check, the oracle must use a historical cumulative price observation from at least `minWindow` seconds ago (e.g., using a Sliding Window oracle pattern or storing observations). Modifying `UniswapPriceOracle` to store the last observation timestamp and cumulative price, and enforcing `elapsedTime >= MIN_TWAP_PERIOD` (e.g. 30 minutes) before updating, would fix this.
```

### M-65 / `uUpwk2dYbAeiS43_LnaF6`
- Finding title: DoS in UniswapPriceOracle via same-block interaction
- Report lines: 6018-6074
```md
## [M-65]. DoS in UniswapPriceOracle via same-block interaction

## id: uUpwk2dYbAeiS43_LnaF6

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
UniswapPriceOracle.validatePrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The code path exists: validatePrice() returns false when block.timestamp == blockTimestampLast from pair.getReserves(). In UniswapV2, any swap/mint/burn/sync updates blockTimestampLast to the current block timestamp, so a same-block call to validatePrice after an interaction will hit this condition and return false. If downstream contracts use validatePrice as a require()-style slippage gate, a front-running dust swap can reliably force the victim transaction to fail in that block. There is no mitigation in the oracle (no fallback to previous observation, no allowance for elapsedTime==0, no internal caching). This is exploitable as griefing/DoS; impact is Medium because it blocks operations rather than directly stealing funds, and victims can retry in later blocks (though an attacker can repeat the griefing with MEV).
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `validatePrice` function explicitly returns `false` if `block.timestamp == blockTimestampLast`. This condition is met if the Uniswap pair has been updated (via `mint`, `burn`, `swap`, or `sync`) earlier in the same block. 

Snippet:
```solidity
if (block.timestamp == blockTimestampLast) {
    return false;
}
```
An attacker can perform a dust swap on the pair in the same block to force this condition, causing `validatePrice` to fail. If consuming contracts require `validatePrice` to return `true` (as is standard for a slippage guard), this acts as a cheap Denial of Service vector.

## Impact
Griefing and Denial of Service. Critical operations relying on the oracle (e.g. Liquidity Management) can be blocked by anyone for the cost of a minimal swap.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Valid user/keeper intends to call a function that executes `require(oracle.validatePrice(...))`. 
2. Attacker fronts-runs the transaction with a minimal swap on the Uniswap pair.
3. The swap updates `blockTimestampLast` to the current `block.timestamp`.
4. The victim's transaction executes `validatePrice`.
5. `block.timestamp == blockTimestampLast` is true, function returns `false`.
6. Victim transaction reverts.

## Proof of Code
function test_DoS() public {
    pair.setReserves(1e18, 1e18, uint32(block.timestamp));
    // Simulate same block interaction
    bool valid = oracle.validatePrice(100);
    assertFalse(valid);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Instead of failing, consider using the `priceCumulativeLast` from the pair (which is updated to the start of the block during the first interaction) to calculate a valid TWAP, or redesign the oracle to tolerate same-block updates by storing its own observations.
```

## [H-66]. Service Multisig Hijacking via Unchecked Data Payload in RecoveryModule.create

## id: dmqbzCbNZ6OHY_9wxCQOh

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AuthByPass

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
RecoveryModule.create

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: ServiceRegistry.deploy() forwards an opaque user-supplied data blob to the chosen multisigImplementation.create(). RecoveryModule.create() decodes a serviceId from data and then operates on that service’s recorded multisig and configuration, assuming ServiceRegistry ensured authorization. ServiceRegistry does not bind data to the serviceId being deployed, so an attacker can supply a victim serviceId in data. If the attacker configures their own service to have identical agentInstances and threshold to the victim’s serviceId, RecoveryModule.create() will pass owner/threshold/agent checks and can update the victim’s Safe when its sole owner equals the victim serviceOwner (post-recovery state), removing the victim and installing attacker-controlled agents. This is a cross-service confused-deputy takeover.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `RecoveryModule.create` function is intended to be called by the `ServiceRegistry` to update an existing Safe multisig during service redeployment. It decodes the target `serviceId` from the user-supplied `data` parameter and assumes that the call is authorized by the owner of that `serviceId` because it comes from the `ServiceRegistry`. 

However, `ServiceRegistry.deploy` allows the caller (Service A owner) to pass arbitrary `data`. An attacker (Owner A) can craft a `data` payload containing the `serviceId` of a different service (Service B) owned by a victim (Owner B). If Service B is in a state where its multisig is recoverable (owned by Owner B) and the attacker configures Service A with the same agent instances and threshold as Service B, `RecoveryModule` will validate the input against Service B's configuration, proceed to remove Owner B from their Safe, and install Service A's agents as the new owners. The `ServiceRegistry` then links Service A to the victim's Safe. This results in the attacker hijacking the victim's Safe and bricking the victim's service.

## Impact
An attacker can hijack the Gnosis Safe of another user's service, removing the original owner and installing their own agents as controllers. This leads to loss of control over the Safe and its funds.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Bob (Victim) owns Service B, which has been deployed and then terminated. Bob calls `recoverAccess` on Service B, resetting his Safe (Safe B) to be owned solely by him.
2. Alice (Attacker) creates a new Service A. She registers the exact same list of agent instances and threshold as defined in Service B.
3. Alice calls `ServiceRegistry.deploy(Service A, ..., data=abi.encode(Service B ID))`.
4. `ServiceRegistry` verifies Alice owns Service A and calls `RecoveryModule.create` with Service A's agents and the malicious data.
5. `RecoveryModule` decodes `serviceId = B`. It fetches Safe B and verifies that Service A's agents match Service B's required agents (which Alice ensured in step 2).
6. `RecoveryModule` sees Safe B is owned by Bob (Service B owner). It executes the update, removing Bob and adding the agents.
7. `ServiceRegistry` updates Service A record to point to Safe B.
8. Bob has lost ownership of Safe B; it is now controlled by agents defined by Alice.

## Proof of Code
/* In Foundry test */
function testHijackSafe() public {
    // Setup: Bob's service B
    uint256 serviceIdB = 1;
    address safeB = address(0x123); // Mock safe
    mockRegistry.setOwner(serviceIdB, bob);
    mockRegistry.setService(serviceIdB, safeB, agentsB, threshold);
    mockSafe.setOwners([bob]); // Safe B recovered

    // Attack: Alice's service A
    vm.prank(alice);
    // Deploy Service A but pass Service B's ID in data
    // Mock registry calls create(agentsA, threshold, abi.encode(serviceIdB))
    // agentsA == agentsB
    recoveryModule.create(agentsB, threshold, abi.encode(serviceIdB));
    
    // Check Safe B owners changed
    assertFalse(mockSafe.isOwner(bob));
}

## Suggested Mitigation
The `RecoveryModule` cannot independently verify that the caller of `ServiceRegistry.deploy` owns the `serviceId` passed in `data`. The `ServiceRegistry.deploy` function should be updated to strictly encode the correct `serviceId` into the `data` passed to the multisig factory, rather than passing through user-supplied data opaque. Alternatively, `RecoveryModule.create` could verify that `tx.origin` matches the `serviceOwner` (though this reduces composability).
```

### H-67 / `LJkFZ4RftcuTla39JB0E2`
- Finding title: DoS in bridge verifier due to unsafe assembly casting of payloadLength causing OOG
- Report lines: 6141-6210
```md
## [H-67]. DoS in bridge verifier due to unsafe assembly casting of payloadLength causing OOG

## id: LJkFZ4RftcuTla39JB0E2

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UnsafeAssembyTypeCasts

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataWormhole.processBridgeData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Although this report references ProcessBridgedDataWormhole, the actual vulnerable code is in VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData(), which ProcessBridgedDataWormhole calls. The assembly reads payloadLength using mload without masking into a uint32, leaving dirty high bits from neighboring packed fields. That dirty value is then used in new bytes(payloadLength), typically causing an out-of-gas/memory allocation revert for normal non-zero target addresses. This creates a practical DoS that prevents bridged message verification and therefore blocks cross-chain governance processing.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData`, inline assembly is used to parse the packed `l2Message`. Specifically, the code extracts `payloadLength` (a `uint32`) by reading a 32-byte word from memory offset `data + i` (where `i` ends up at 36 for the first iteration). 

```solidity
// i is incremented to 36
payloadLength := mload(add(data, i))
```

The `mload` instruction reads 32 bytes. Based on the packed data structure (Target: 20 bytes, Value: 12 bytes, Length: 4 bytes), reading at offset 36 (relative to the length prefix) captures the last 16 bytes of the `target`, the 12 bytes of `value`, and the 4 bytes of `payloadLength`. 

Since `mload` fills the entire 32-byte stack slot and the code does not mask the upper bits, `payloadLength` typically contains dirty bits from the non-zero `target` address. This results in `payloadLength` holding a value approx. 2^224. 

Subsequently, `new bytes(payloadLength)` attempts to allocate this massive amount of memory, causing the transaction to always revert (Out of Gas or Memory Limit). This bricks the verification logic for any message with a non-zero target address.

## Impact
The bridge verification functionality is permanently broken (DoS) for all valid messages, preventing cross-chain governance actions.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Construct a valid packed payload: `Target` = `0x000...01` (20 bytes), `Value` = 0 (12 bytes), `Len` = 1 (4 bytes), `Payload` = `0xAA`.
2. Call `processBridgeData` with this payload.
3. The assembly block reads `payloadLength`. The word loaded is `0x...01...00...01` (Target + Value + Len).
4. `payloadLength` is interpreted as a huge integer.
5. `new bytes(payloadLength)` reverts due to memory limits.

## Proof of Code
contract VerifyTest is Test {
    ProcessBridgedDataWormhole verifier;
    function setUp() public {
        verifier = new ProcessBridgedDataWormhole();
    }
    function testDoS() public {
        // Mock payload: Selector(4) + Tuple(uint16, address, bytes, uint256, uint256)
        // Inside bytes: Packed [Target(20)|Value(12)|Len(4)|Payload]
        address target = address(0x1234);
        bytes memory packed = abi.encodePacked(target, uint96(0), uint32(1), bytes1(0xaa));
        bytes memory data = abi.encode(uint16(1), address(this), packed, uint256(0), uint256(0));
        bytes memory fullData = abi.encodePacked(verifier.SEND_MESSAGE(), data);
        
        vm.expectRevert(); // Reverts due to OOG/Memory, verifying DoS
        verifier.processBridgeData(fullData, address(this), 1);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Mask the loaded value to ensure only the lower 32 bits are used: `payloadLength := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xffffffff)`.
```

### M-68 / `JhJ83ItjZbNxicWpj9o_m`
- Finding title: Stale multisig authorization in IdentityRegistryBridger allows evicted service owners to act on behalf of agents
- Report lines: 6211-6325
```md
## [M-68]. Stale multisig authorization in IdentityRegistryBridger allows evicted service owners to act on behalf of agents

## id: JhJ83ItjZbNxicWpj9o_m

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccessControlOrAuthByPass

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
IdentityRegistryBridger.validationRequest

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: `validationRequest()` authorizes solely via cached `mapMultisigAgentIds[msg.sender]` and does not consult `ServiceRegistry` for the current multisig. If a service’s controlling multisig changes (redeploy/rotation) and `updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds` (or `updateAgentWallet` by manager) is not called, the old multisig mapping remains and the old multisig can still pass `agentId == mapMultisigAgentIds[msg.sender]` and submit validation requests as that agent. This is a real stale-auth condition in today’s code; it is exploitable by whoever controls the old multisig keys until state is synced.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `IdentityRegistryBridger` contract caches the relationship between service multisigs and agent IDs in the `mapMultisigAgentIds` mapping. This mapping is updated via `register` or the sync functions `linkServiceIdAgentIds` / `updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds`. 

However, the `validationRequest` function uses this *cached* mapping to authorize callers: `uint256 checkAgentId = mapMultisigAgentIds[msg.sender]; if (agentId != checkAgentId) revert...`. 

Crucially, if a service is redeployed or its ownership structure changes such that the canonical `ServiceRegistry` records a new multisig address, the `IdentityRegistryBridger` is not automatically updated. This creates a state desynchronization where the *previous* multisig address retains its entry in `mapMultisigAgentIds`. Consequently, the evicted/previous service owner can still successfully call `validationRequest` for the agent ID, bypassing the authorization intent which is to restrict actions to the *current* service multisig.

## Impact
A previous service owner (who retains control of the old multisig keys) can impersonate the current agent identity and submit validation requests. This breaks the binding between the active service deployment and its identity, potentially leading to incorrect validation data submissions, spam, or reputation damage to the identity.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. **Setup**: A Service `S1` is deployed with Multisig `M1`. It is registered in `IdentityRegistryBridger`, linking `S1` to `Agent A1` and mapping `M1 -> A1`.
2. **Rotation**: The Service `S1` is redeployed or updated in the canonical `ServiceRegistry` (e.g. via `ServiceManager`) such that its controlling multisig changes to `M2`. The `IdentityRegistryBridger` sync functions are *not* called.
3. **Exploit**: The holder of `M1` keys calls `IdentityRegistryBridger.validationRequest(validator, A1, uri, hash)`. 
4. **Check**: The contract checks `mapMultisigAgentIds[msg.sender]`. Since `msg.sender` is `M1`, it returns `A1`. The check `A1 == A1` passes.
5. **Result**: The call is forwarded to the `ValidationRegistry` as a valid request from `A1`, despite `M1` no longer being the authorized multisig for `S1`.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;

import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/IdentityRegistryBridger.sol";

// Minimal Mocks
contract MockServiceRegistry {
    mapping(uint256 => address) public multisigs;
    function mapServices(uint256 id) external view returns (uint96, address, bytes32, uint32, uint32, uint32, IServiceRegistry.ServiceState) {
        return (0, multisigs[id], bytes32(0), 0, 0, 0, IServiceRegistry.ServiceState.Deployed);
    }
    function setMultisig(uint256 id, address m) external { multisigs[id] = m; }
    function tokenURI(uint256) external pure returns (string memory) { return ""; }
    function totalSupply() external pure returns (uint256) { return 100; }
}

contract MockIdentityRegistry {
    function register(string memory, IIdentityRegistry.MetadataEntry[] memory) external pure returns (uint256) { return 1; }
    function setMetadata(uint256, string memory, bytes memory) external {}
    function setAgentUri(uint256, string calldata) external {}
}

contract MockValidationRegistry {
    function validationRequest(address, uint256, string calldata, bytes32) external {}
}

contract BridgerTest is Test {
    IdentityRegistryBridger bridger;
    MockServiceRegistry serviceRegistry;
    MockIdentityRegistry identityRegistry;
    MockValidationRegistry validationRegistry;
    
    address manager = address(0x123);
    address multisig1 = address(0x1);
    address multisig2 = address(0x2);
    
    function setUp() public {
        serviceRegistry = new MockServiceRegistry();
        identityRegistry = new MockIdentityRegistry();
        validationRegistry = new MockValidationRegistry();
        
        bridger = new IdentityRegistryBridger(
            address(identityRegistry),
            address(0x0),
            address(validationRegistry),
            address(serviceRegistry)
        );
        bridger.initialize();
        bridger.changeManager(manager);
    }
    
    function testStaleAuthExploit() public {
        // 1. Setup Service 1 with Multisig 1
        serviceRegistry.setMultisig(1, multisig1);
        
        // 2. Register via Manager (populates mapMultisigAgentIds)
        vm.prank(manager);
        bridger.register(1, multisig1, "uri");
        
        // 3. Rotate Multisig in Registry to Multisig 2
        serviceRegistry.setMultisig(1, multisig2);
        
        // 4. Old Multisig 1 calls validationRequest
        // This SHOULD fail as M1 is no longer the multisig, but it SUCCEEDS due to stale cache
        vm.prank(multisig1); 
        bridger.validationRequest(address(0x99), 1, "req", bytes32(0));
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Modify `validationRequest` to verify the caller against the *current* state of the `ServiceRegistry`. Since `mapMultisigAgentIds` can be stale, the contract should retrieve the `serviceId` associated with the `agentId` (e.g., by reading the `SERVICE_ID_METADATA_KEY` from `IdentityRegistry` or passing `serviceId` as a trusted argument verified against `mapServiceIdAgentIds`) and then confirm that `msg.sender == IServiceRegistry(serviceRegistry).mapServices(serviceId).multisig`.
```

### H-69 / `k-XwCqA93ZGX_42MX8Ceo`

```md
## [H-69]. No slippage protection in V3 buyback allows value loss via price impact and sandwich attacks

## id: k-XwCqA93ZGX_42MX8Ceo

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
BuyBackBurnerUniswap._performSwap

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: BuyBackBurnerUniswap._performSwap(V3) hardcodes amountOutMinimum=1 and _buyOLAS(V3) lacks a post-swap check. The only control is a pre-swap LiquidityManager check of the pool price, which does not constrain execution output. This exposes the protocol to large price impact and MEV sandwich/JIT liquidity extraction, especially because buyBack may swap the entire held balance. This can lead to materially less OLAS received/burned than expected, i.e., direct value loss.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurnerUniswap` contract enables permissionless buybacks of OLAS tokens using Uniswap V3. In the V3-specific `_performSwap` function, the `amountOutMinimum` parameter passed to the `exactInputSingle` call is hardcoded to `1`. While the `_buyOLAS` function calls `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` (which presumably validates the start price against a TWAP within a tolerance, e.g. 10%), it fails to protect against the price impact of the swap itself or sandwich attacks that stay within the oracle's tolerance.

Specifically:
1. `_performSwap` hardcodes `amountOutMinimum: 1`.
2. `_buyOLAS` performs a pre-swap check (`checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice`) but, unlike the V2 implementation in the base contract, does NOT perform a post-swap price check.
3. If `buyBack` is called with a large amount of tokens (e.g. accumulated fees), the execution will suffer 100% slippage tolerance, accepting any amount of OLAS > 0. This exposes the protocol to significant value loss from high price impact or MEV sandwich attacks.

## Impact
Protocol assets intended for burning are swapped at unfavorable rates, resulting in fewer OLAS burned and value loss for the protocol.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. The `BuyBackBurnerUniswap` holds a large balance of `TokenA`.
2. An attacker (or keeper) calls `buyBack(TokenA, amount)`.
3. The `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` verifies the pool price is within 10% of TWAP.
4. `_performSwap` executes `exactInputSingle` with `amountOutMinimum: 1`.
5. Due to the large size of the swap or a sandwich attack (front-running the trade to the edge of the 10% tolerance), the swap executes at a very poor rate.
6. The protocol receives and burns significantly less OLAS than the market value of `TokenA`.

## Proof of Code
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/utils/BuyBackBurnerUniswap.sol";

contract MockRouter {
    function exactInputSingle(IRouterV3.ExactInputSingleParams calldata params) external payable returns (uint256 amountOut) {
        // Check if slippage is unprotected
        if (params.amountOutMinimum == 1) {
            // Simulate huge slippage / sandwich
            return 1; // Return minimal amount
        }
        return params.amountIn;
    }
}

contract MockLiquidityManager {
    function checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(address) external view returns (uint160) {
        return 0; // Pass check
    }
    function factoryV3() external view returns (address) {
        return address(0);
    }
}

contract MockERC20 {
    function approve(address, uint256) external returns (bool) { return true; }
    function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint256) { return 1000 ether; }
    function transfer(address, uint256) external returns (bool) { return true; }
}

contract BuyBackSlippageTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerUniswap burner;
    MockRouter router;
    MockLiquidityManager lm;
    MockERC20 token;
    MockERC20 olas;
    
    function setUp() public {
        router = new MockRouter();
        lm = new MockLiquidityManager();
        token = new MockERC20();
        olas = new MockERC20();
        
        burner = new BuyBackBurnerUniswap(address(lm), address(0x1), address(0x2), address(router));
        
        // Init logic mock
        bytes memory payload = abi.encode(new address[](0), 0);
        // Inject OLAS address for test simplicity via storage or harness, 
        // here we assume initialization works or we mock internal vars if needed.
        // For this PoC, we just test _performSwap logic if accessible or exposed via buyBack.
    }

    function testSlippageIsOne() public {
         // We need to bypass the _buyOLAS logic constraints to test _performSwap directly
         // OR verify bytecode/source logic. 
         // Since _performSwap is internal, we act as if we are calling buyBack.
         // The MockRouter will verify the param.
         
         // In a real fork test, one would call burner.buyBack(...)
         // and assert that the router received amountOutMinimum == 1.
         // This is demonstrated by the MockRouter returning 1 above.
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate a `minAmountOut` based on the oracle price (retrieved from `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` or similar) and the `maxSlippage` setting. Pass this calculated minimum to `exactInputSingle` instead of `1`.
```

### H-70 / `Vqaa5wvGj_hR-cd_Zyblb`
- Finding title: Broken Uniswap V3 integration due to incorrect IRouterV3 interface definition
- Report lines: 6437-6475
```md
## [H-70]. Broken Uniswap V3 integration due to incorrect IRouterV3 interface definition

## id: Vqaa5wvGj_hR-cd_Zyblb

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Standard Violation

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
BuyBackBurnerUniswap._performSwap

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: IRouterV3.ExactInputSingleParams in BuyBackBurnerUniswap omits the Uniswap V3 SwapRouter ‘deadline’ field and thus defines a different tuple type. Function selectors depend on the full signature, so exactInputSingle((address,address,uint24,address,uint256,uint256,uint160)) will not match the standard Uniswap V3 router’s exactInputSingle((address,address,uint24,address,uint256,uint256,uint256,uint160)). Consequently, calls to a standard Uniswap V3 router will revert (function not found), making the V3 buyBack path non-functional. This is an immediate, reproducible DoS of V3 swaps.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `IRouterV3` interface defined in `BuyBackBurnerUniswap.sol` omits the `deadline` field in the `ExactInputSingleParams` struct. The standard Uniswap V3 `ExactInputSingleParams` struct contains 8 fields (including `deadline`), whereas the contract's definition contains only 7. This mismatch results in a different function selector for `exactInputSingle`. When `_performSwap` calls the router, it sends calldata with the wrong function selector, causing the call to revert on any standard Uniswap V3 router. As a result, Uniswap V3 buybacks are completely non-functional (DoS).

## Impact
Permanent Denial of Service for all Uniswap V3 buyback operations.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
The `BuyBackBurnerUniswap` contract attempts to call `exactInputSingle` on the provided `swapRouter`. Because the struct definition in the interface differs from the implementation (missing `deadline`), the ABI-encoded function selector differs. The router does not recognize the selector and reverts.

## Proof of Code
contract V3InterfaceTest { struct ParamsMissing { address a; address b; uint24 c; address d; uint256 f; uint256 g; uint160 h; } struct ParamsStandard { address a; address b; uint24 c; address d; uint256 e; uint256 f; uint256 g; uint160 h; } function getSelectorMissing() public pure returns (bytes4) { return bytes4(keccak256("exactInputSingle((address,address,uint24,address,uint256,uint256,uint160))")); } function getSelectorStandard() public pure returns (bytes4) { return bytes4(keccak256("exactInputSingle((address,address,uint24,address,uint256,uint256,uint256,uint160))")); } } // Run this in foundry: assert(V3InterfaceTest.getSelectorMissing() != V3InterfaceTest.getSelectorStandard());

## Suggested Mitigation
Update the `IRouterV3` interface to include the `uint256 deadline;` field in the `ExactInputSingleParams` struct (placed after `recipient`). Ensure `deadline` is populated (e.g., with `block.timestamp`) in `_performSwap`.
```

## [M-71]. Missing execution-level slippage protection in swaps

## id: tWb_Ueup5URWVMUw2PJQK

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

BuyBackBurnerUniswap._performSwap

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Both swap paths lack meaningful execution min-out: V2 uses amountOutMin=0; V3 uses amountOutMinimum=1. Pre-swap oracle validation exists (validatePrice/checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice), but it does not bind the swap output atomically, and in V3 there is no post-swap check. Therefore, large swaps or MEV conditions can lead to severe value loss. The issue is directly in _performSwap implementations and the surrounding buyBack logic that can swap full balances.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_performSwap` function executes swaps passing `0` (for V2) or `1` (for V3) as the minimum output amount (`amountOutMin` / `amountOutMinimum`). Although there are pre-swap oracle validations (`validatePrice` / `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice`), relying solely on pre-checks without enforcing constraints atomically during the swap execution leaves the protocol vulnerable to sandwich attacks if the oracle validation is imperfect, stale, or does not account for intra-block price movements adequately.

## Impact
Potential loss of funds due to sandwich attacks or high slippage during swaps.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker observes `buyBack` transaction. 2. Attacker front-runs to manipulate pool price (sandwich). 3. `buyBack` executes. `validatePrice` checks price (if based on Spot, it might pass manipulated price; if TWAP, it might pass if deviation allows). 4. `swapExactTokensForTokens` executes with `amountOutMin: 0`, accepting the manipulated price. 5. Attacker back-runs to profit.

## Proof of Code
// N/A - Logic flaw inspection. Call arguments are visible in code: IUniswap(router).swapExactTokensForTokens(..., 0, ...)

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate the expected minimum output amount based on the validated oracle price and `maxSlippage`, and pass this value as `amountOutMin` / `amountOutMinimum` to the router swap functions.

### H-72 / `P--z4sP9m_Z39RbA3gJXl`
- Finding title: High Slippage Vulnerability in `buyBack` due to Lack of Output Enforcement
- Report lines: 6515-6646
```md
## [H-72]. High Slippage Vulnerability in `buyBack` due to Lack of Output Enforcement

## id: P--z4sP9m_Z39RbA3gJXl

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
FlashLoanEconomicManipulation

## Exploit Type
FrontrunMev

## Location
BuyBackBurnerUniswap._performSwap

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Across both swap paths, output enforcement is effectively absent: V2 uses amountOutMin=0 and relies on oracle-based checks that may not reflect execution price (and do not constrain output), and V3 uses amountOutMinimum=1 with only a pre-swap pool check. buyBack can also default to swapping the entire token balance. These design choices allow severe slippage/value loss from low liquidity, price impact, or MEV sandwich/JIT liquidity, resulting in materially less OLAS received and burned than expected. This is directly exploitable by MEV actors and/or by triggering swaps when conditions are unfavorable.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurnerUniswap` contract performs token swaps on Uniswap V2 and V3 to buy back OLAS tokens. In both `_performSwap` implementations, the contract fails to enforce a minimum output amount derived from the oracle price, essentially allowing unlimited slippage during execution.

In the V2 path (`_performSwap` via `swapExactTokensForTokens`), `amountOutMin` is hardcoded to `0`. While `_buyOLAS` performs a post-swap check (`tradePrice` vs `previousPrice`), this check relies on `IOracle(poolOracle).getPrice()`. If the oracle returns a Time-Weighted Average Price (TWAP), as is standard to avoid manipulation, the `tradePrice` will not reflect the instantaneous slippage incurred during the swap. Thus, the check passes even if the swap yields near-zero tokens.

In the V3 path (`_performSwap` via `exactInputSingle`), `amountOutMinimum` is set to `1`. The code relies solely on `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` (a pre-swap check of the pool's start price). It does not verify the available liquidity depth or the final execution price. 

Attackers can exploit this by sandwiching the transaction (V2/V3) or providing Just-In-Time (JIT) low liquidity (V3) to extract nearly all value from the buyback, causing the protocol to burn dust amounts while spending full treasury funds.

## Impact
Direct loss of treasury funds allocated for buybacks due to MEV extraction or high slippage.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Protocol queues a `buyBack` transaction for 10,000 USDC.
2. Attacker observes tx in mempool.
3. Attacker front-runs by buying OLAS on the pool, pushing the spot price significantly up (but potentially keeping TWAP stable).
4. `buyBack` executes. The pre-swap oracle check (if checking Spot vs TWAP) allows execution up to `maxSlippage`. The swap executes with `amountOutMin: 0` (V2) or `1` (V3).
5. Due to the manipulated pool state and lack of output enforcement, the swap executes at a terrible rate (e.g., getting 1 OLAS for 10,000 USDC).
6. The V2 post-swap check compares Oracle Price (TWAP) pre and post swap. Since TWAP changes slowly, the check passes.
7. Attacker back-runs, selling OLAS for profit.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.30;

import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {BuyBackBurnerUniswap} from "contracts/utils/BuyBackBurnerUniswap.sol";
import {IERC20} from "contracts/utils/BuyBackBurnerUniswap.sol";
import {IUniswap, IOracle} from "contracts/utils/BuyBackBurnerUniswap.sol";

// Mock Contracts to simulate Vulnerability
contract MockERC20 is IERC20 {
    mapping(address => uint256) public override balanceOf;
    function approve(address, uint256) external override returns (bool) { return true; }
    function transfer(address to, uint256 amount) external override returns (bool) {
        balanceOf[msg.sender] -= amount;
        balanceOf[to] += amount;
        return true;
    }
    function mint(address to, uint256 amount) external { balanceOf[to] += amount; }
}

contract MockRouter is IUniswap {
    function swapExactTokensForTokens(
        uint256 amountIn,
        uint256 amountOutMin,
        address[] calldata,
        address,
        uint256
    ) external override returns (uint256[] memory amounts) {
        // SIMULATE ATTACK: Return 1 wei despite high input, proving lack of slippage check
        amounts = new uint256[](2);
        amounts[0] = amountIn;
        amounts[1] = 1; 
        return amounts;
    }
}

contract MockOracle is IOracle {
    function getPrice() external view override returns (uint256) { return 1e18; } // Fixed TWAP
    function validatePrice(uint256) external view override returns (bool) { return true; }
    function updatePrice() external override returns (bool) { return true; }
}

contract BuyBackSlippageTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerUniswap burner;
    MockERC20 token;
    MockERC20 olas;
    MockRouter router;
    MockOracle oracle;

    function setUp() public {
        token = new MockERC20();
        olas = new MockERC20();
        router = new MockRouter();
        oracle = new MockOracle();
        
        burner = new BuyBackBurnerUniswap(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(router));
        
        address[] memory accounts = new address[](4);
        accounts[0] = address(olas);
        accounts[1] = address(0);
        accounts[2] = address(oracle);
        accounts[3] = address(router);
        
        burner.initialize(abi.encode(accounts, 1000)); // init
        
        address[] memory t = new address[](1); t[0] = address(token);
        address[] memory o = new address[](1); o[0] = address(oracle);
        vm.prank(address(this)); // owner
        burner.setV2Oracles(t, o);
        
        token.mint(address(burner), 1000e18);
    }

    function testExploitSlippage() public {
        // Execute buyBack. Router returns 1 wei. Oracle returns valid price.
        // If slippage protection worked, this should revert.
        burner.buyBack(address(token), 1000e18);
        
        // Bridge2Burner (address 2) receives 1 wei instead of 1000e18 expected
        assertEq(olas.balanceOf(address(2)), 1);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate the expected minimum output amount based on the oracle price and `maxSlippage` (e.g., `minOut = amountIn * oraclePrice * (1 - slippage)`). Pass this `minOut` to `swapExactTokensForTokens` and `exactInputSingle` instead of `0` or `1`.
```

### H-73 / `1k4zX3kGnivn6e6bZHKZr`
- Finding title: Critical logic error in getPrice returns inverted price (OLAS per Token instead of Token per OLAS)
- Report lines: 6647-6697
```md
## [H-73]. Critical logic error in getPrice returns inverted price (OLAS per Token instead of Token per OLAS)

## id: 1k4zX3kGnivn6e6bZHKZr

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StandardViolation

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
BalancerPriceOracle.getPrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Code path exists: getPrice sets balanceIn = balances[direction] (intended secondToken) and balanceOut = balances[(direction+1)%2] (intended OLAS), then returns (balanceOut*1e18)/balanceIn. That computes OLAS_reserve / secondToken_reserve (scaled), i.e., the inverse of “OLAS price in secondToken units” under typical conventions and contradicts NatSpec “Gets the current OLAS token price”. Because direction is chosen so that balanceIn is the secondToken in both token orderings, the inversion happens consistently. Any consumer using getPrice as “price of OLAS” will be systematically wrong.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `getPrice` function is documented to return "the current OLAS token price" in 1e18 format. In standard DeFi pricing (and implied by the protocol's use of a Quote/Second token), the price of OLAS should be expressed as `QuoteAmount / OLASAmount`. However, the implementation calculates `(balanceOut * 1e18) / balanceIn` where `balanceOut` corresponds to the OLAS balance and `balanceIn` corresponds to the `secondToken` (Quote) balance. 

This formula results in `OLASAmount / QuoteAmount`, which is the price of the `secondToken` denominated in `OLAS`, i.e., the inverse of the intended price. For example, if 1 OLAS = 10 USDC, the oracle should return 10e18 (assuming normalized decimals). Instead, it returns 0.1e18. This fundamentally breaks any downstream logic relying on the oracle for asset valuation.

## Impact
The oracle returns the inverse of the correct price. Protocol functions relying on this for bonding, liquidity provision, or slippage checks will operate on incorrect valuations, leading to potential theft of funds (underpricing) or broken functionality (overpricing).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy a MockBalancerVault with a pool containing 100 OLAS and 1000 USDC.
2. Deploy BalancerPriceOracle pointing to this pool.
3. Call `getPrice()`.
4. Expected result: 10e18 (10 USDC per OLAS).
5. Actual result: 0.1e18 (0.1 OLAS per USDC).

## Proof of Code
contract MockVault { function getPoolTokens(bytes32) external view returns (address[] memory t, uint256[] memory b, uint256) { t = new address[](2); t[0] = address(0x1); t[1] = address(0x2); b = new uint256[](2); b[0] = 100e18; b[1] = 1000e18; return (t, b, 0); } } 
// In test: 
// MockVault returns 100 OLAS (token0), 1000 Quote (token1). 
// Oracle checks tokens[0] != secondToken. If secondToken is token1, direction=1. 
// balanceIn = balances[1] (1000), balanceOut = balances[0] (100). 
// Result: (100e18 * 1e18) / 1000e18 = 0.1e18. 
// Expected: 10e18.

## Suggested Mitigation
Invert the calculation to `(balanceIn * 1e18) / balanceOut` to return the price of OLAS in terms of the second token. Ensure `balanceIn` corresponds to the Quote token and `balanceOut` to OLAS. Additionally, normalize decimals if the tokens differ (e.g. USDC has 6 decimals).
```

### M-74 / `EZknjLeOq4uoWAhF2QDnG`

## [M-74]. Incorrect price scaling for non-18 decimal tokens in BalancerPriceOracle

## id: EZknjLeOq4uoWAhF2QDnG

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
ERC20DecimalsMismatch

## Exploit Type
ERC20DecimalsMismatch

## Location
BalancerPriceOracle.getPrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: getPrice uses raw Balancer vault balances (token native decimals) and applies a fixed 1e18 scaling: (balanceOut*1e18)/balanceIn, without normalizing decimals of olas/secondToken. For a 6-decimal secondToken like USDC, this yields values off by 1e12 relative to an intended 1e18 “price” representation. There is no decimals() query nor scaling factor stored in constructor. This is a real arithmetic/unit bug and will affect any deployment where tokens do not share the same decimals.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `getPrice()` function computes `(balanceOut * 1e18) / balanceIn`. It implicitly assumes `balanceIn` (the second token) has 18 decimals. If `secondToken` has fewer decimals (e.g., USDC with 6), the resulting price is inflated by `10^(18 - decimals)`. For USDC, the price is multiplied by 10^12, returning an erroneously huge value.

## Impact
Protocol logic using this oracle with non-18 decimal tokens (like USDC/USDT) will receive incorrect pricing data (off by orders of magnitude), potentially leading to massive over-issuance of bonds or loss of funds.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy oracle with OLAS (18 dec) and USDC (6 dec).
2. Pool balances: 1 OLAS (1e18), 1 USDC (1e6).
3. `getPrice` returns `(1e18 * 1e18) / 1e6 = 1e30`.
4. Expected 1e18 price (1:1 value). Result is 1e12 times larger.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {BalancerPriceOracle} from "contracts/oracles/BalancerPriceOracle.sol";

contract DecimalsTest is Test {
    // Concept only, assumes MockVault setup as above but with 1e6 balance for token2
    function testDecimals() public {
         // See description.
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Fetch `decimals()` of `secondToken` in constructor and normalize `balanceIn` to 18 decimals before division: `balanceInScaled = balanceIn * 10**(18 - tokenDecimals)`.
```

### H-75 / `z-6aLr4kqgSC3TUvhhDEF`
- Finding title: Oracle calculates inverted price (Base/Quote reversal) and lacks decimal normalization
- Report lines: 6748-6824
```md
## [H-75]. Oracle calculates inverted price (Base/Quote reversal) and lacks decimal normalization

## id: z-6aLr4kqgSC3TUvhhDEF

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Exploit Type
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Location
BalancerPriceOracle.getPrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Both sub-issues are present in code: (1) inversion as in 1k4z… because getPrice returns OLAS_reserve/secondToken_reserve, contrary to “OLAS token price” expectation; and (2) no decimal normalization, so differing decimals (e.g., USDC 6) cause orders-of-magnitude error despite “1e18 format” NatSpec. No guard verifies token decimals or scales reserves to a common unit. This combination can catastrophically mislead any downstream accounting that assumes a conventional quote-per-OLAS price in 1e18.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `getPrice()` function calculates the spot price as `balanceOut * 1e18 / balanceIn`. Based on the logic in the constructor, `balanceIn` is always the `secondToken` and `balanceOut` is always the `olas` token. The resulting value is `ReserveOLAS / ReserveSecondToken`, which represents the price of the **SecondToken** denominated in OLAS (or `1 / PriceOfOLAS`). This contradicts the documentation (`Gets the current OLAS token price`) and standard oracle conventions. Additionally, the calculation assumes both tokens have 18 decimals; if they differ (e.g., USDC), the price is scaled incorrectly by orders of magnitude.

## Impact
Consumers of the oracle receive the inverted price (e.g., if OLAS price doubles, oracle reports it halved). This leads to catastrophic failure in downstream systems (e.g., severe under/over-valuation of collateral or incorrect exchange rates).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy Oracle for OLAS (18 decimals) and USDC (6 decimals).
2. Pool has 10 OLAS and 100 USDC. Real Price of OLAS = 10 USDC.
3. `getPrice()` logic:
   - `direction` selects USDC as `balanceIn` (denominator).
   - `balanceOut` = 10 * 1e18.
   - `balanceIn` = 100 * 1e6.
4. Calculation: `(10 * 1e18 * 1e18) / (100 * 1e6)` = `1e37 / 1e8` = `1e29`.
5. Expected Price (10 USDC scaled to 1e18) = `10 * 1e18` = `1e19`.
6. The result is off by `1e10` factor and moves inversely to the real price.

## Proof of Code
contract OracleTest is Test {
    BalancerPriceOracle oracle;
    MockVault vault;
    address olas = address(0x1);
    address usdc = address(0x2);
    
    function testInvertedPrice() public {
        vault = new MockVault();
        // 10 OLAS (1e18), 100 USDC (1e6)
        // Real price 10 USDC.
        vault.setBalances(10e18, 100e6);
        
        oracle = new BalancerPriceOracle(olas, usdc, 10, 100, address(vault), bytes32("pool"));
        
        uint256 price = oracle.getPrice();
        // Logic does: (10e18 * 1e18) / 100e6 = 1e30ish
        // Expected: 10e18 (Price is 10)
        // It actually returns 100000000000000000000000000000
        // Which is 1e29. 
        assertEq(price, 1e29);
    }
}

contract MockVault {
    uint256[] bals = new uint256[](2);
    function setBalances(uint256 a, uint256 b) public { bals[0]=a; bals[1]=b; }
    function getPoolTokens(bytes32) external view returns (address[] memory t, uint256[] memory b, uint256 l) {
        t = new address[](2); t[0]=address(0x1); t[1]=address(0x2);
        return (t, bals, 0);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Fix the formula to `(balanceIn * 1e18) / balanceOut` (assuming 1e18 scaling target) and normalize token decimals before division.
```

### H-76 / `KI_jmfNZBW1JHh_ibxJFP`
- Finding title: Critical slippage protection bypass enables 100% theft via sandwich attacks in illiquid pools
- Report lines: 6825-6889
```md
## [H-76]. Critical slippage protection bypass enables 100% theft via sandwich attacks in illiquid pools

## id: KI_jmfNZBW1JHh_ibxJFP

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Slippage protection calculated on-chain from spot price

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore._decreaseLiquidity

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: While the report mislabels the timestamp as the 'oldest' observation, the code can still skip TWAP protection when the latest observation is older than SECONDS_AGO or when observe() fails (fail-open). In those cases, sandwich/price-manipulation around owner liquidity operations becomes feasible, and the potential economic loss is not inherently low nor especially rare for low-activity pools.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `LiquidityManagerCore` contract calculates `amountsMin` (slippage parameters) dynamically based on the current `slot0` spot price within functions like `increaseLiquidity`, `decreaseLiquidity`, and `convertToV3`. While the `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` function attempts to guard against spot price manipulation by comparing it to a TWAP, this check is entirely bypassed if the pool has been inactive for 30 minutes (`oldestTimestamp + SECONDS_AGO < block.timestamp`) or if the TWAP observation fails. 

In these bypass scenarios, the contract accepts the current spot price as the source of truth for slippage calculations. An attacker can manipulate the pool price drastically (e.g., via flash loan), call the liquidity management function (or front-run/sandwich the owner's transaction), and force the protocol to add or remove liquidity at a manipulated rate, extracting value. Additionally, the `deadline` parameter in Uniswap V3 calls is set to `block.timestamp`, effectively disabling transaction expiration protection and allowing validators to hold transactions for favorable execution.

## Impact
High. An attacker can drain a significant portion of the liquidity or tokens provided by the manager by manipulating the pool price before execution.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker identifies a pool managed by `LiquidityManager` that has not had a new observation in >30 minutes. 
2. Attacker observes the Owner submitting a `decreaseLiquidity` transaction. 
3. Attacker front-runs with a massive swap, pushing the price of TokenA to near zero. 
4. The `LiquidityManager` transaction executes: 
   - `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` sees old timestamp, bypasses TWAP check, returns manipulated spot price. 
   - `_decreaseLiquidity` calculates `amountsMin` based on this manipulated price (expecting almost 0 TokenA). 
   - It calls `positionManager.decreaseLiquidity`. 
   - Uniswap returns the tokens based on the manipulated price (mostly TokenB, negligible TokenA). 
5. Attacker back-runs, swapping back to profit from the price dislocation and the victim's unfavorable trade execution.

## Proof of Code
contract VulnerabilityTest is Test {
    LiquidityManagerHarness manager;
    MockPool pool;
    
    function testSlippageBypass() public {
        // 1. Setup stale pool (older than 30m)
        pool.setTimestamp(block.timestamp - 1801);
        // 2. Manipulate price (10x drop)
        uint160 manipulatedPrice = 1000;
        pool.setSlot0(manipulatedPrice);
        
        // 3. Manager calculates amountsMin based on manipulated price
        // This call will SUCCEED with very low amountsMin, permitting the theft
        vm.prank(owner);
        manager.decreaseLiquidity(tokens, ...);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate `amountsMin` off-chain and pass them as arguments to the functions, or derive `amountsMin` strictly from the TWAP price (if fresh) and revert if the pool is stale/manipulated. Do not use spot price for slippage bounds.
```

### M-77 / `nksVzMl7jX62iXLhn3u27`

```md
## [M-77]. On-chain slippage calculation ignores mempool volatility

## id: nksVzMl7jX62iXLhn3u27

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore._decreaseLiquidity

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: In _increaseLiquidity() / _decreaseLiquidity(), min amounts are derived from the execution-time slot0 price and maxSlippage, rather than being passed in by the caller as fixed bounds. This means natural price moves between submission and inclusion will be accepted (min amounts adjust to the new price), eliminating a common protection against unintended execution context. There is no alternative safeguard like a caller-specified price limit or deadline.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The contract calculates `amountMin` (slippage protection) dynamically on-chain based on the `slot0` price at the time of execution. In `decreaseLiquidity` and `increaseLiquidity`, the code fetches the current price using `_getPriceAndObservationIndexFromSlot0` and applies `maxSlippage` to it. 

This protects against sandwich attacks (intra-block manipulation) assuming the oracle check passes, but it fails to protect against price movements that occur between transaction submission and execution (mempool delay). If the price crashes naturally while the transaction is pending, the `amountMin` will be recalculated based on the crashed price, and the transaction will execute, locking in the loss. Standard practice is for the caller to provide `amountMin` as an argument.

## Impact
Loss of user funds due to market volatility during transaction delay.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Owner submits `decreaseLiquidity` when price is 1000.
2. Transaction lingers in mempool.
3. Market price crashes to 500.
4. Transaction executes. Contract reads price 500.
5. Calculates `amountMin` based on 500.
6. Withdraws liquidity at rate 500. Owner loses 50% value intended to be preserved.

## Proof of Code
function test_Slippage_Mempool() public {
    // Mock price move
    // Call decreaseLiquidity
    // Assert amountsOut are lower than original expectation
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Allow `amount0Min` and `amount1Min` to be passed as arguments to `decreaseLiquidity`, `increaseLiquidity`, and `convertToV3`.
```

## [M-78]. Transaction deadline set to block.timestamp disables staleness protection

## id: vt3GSJkXQkgQtFr_Jnbn1

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore._decreaseLiquidity

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Both IPositionManagerV3.DecreaseLiquidityParams and IncreaseLiquidityParams are constructed with deadline: block.timestamp. This makes the deadline check in the position manager always pass at execution time and provides no caller control over expiry. The codebase does not include any other explicit staleness guard in these paths.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
In `LiquidityManagerCore.sol`, the internal functions `_decreaseLiquidity` and `_increaseLiquidity` construct Uniswap V3 parameters with `deadline: block.timestamp`. 

```solidity
IPositionManagerV3.DecreaseLiquidityParams memory params = IPositionManagerV3.DecreaseLiquidityParams({
    // ...
    deadline: block.timestamp
});
```

Setting the deadline to `block.timestamp` effectively disables the expiration check, allowing validators to hold the transaction in the mempool until market conditions change to be unfavorable to the protocol (within the bounds of the `amountMin` slippage settings). While `maxSlippage` provides asset-value protection, the lack of a timestamp check exposes the protocol to execution at an unintended time, potentially after the decision-making context (e.g., oracle prices) has shifted.

## Impact
Protocol transactions can be delayed and executed at suboptimal times, potentially leading to increased slippage or execution in unfavorable market conditions.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Owner submits `decreaseLiquidity` transaction.
2. Validator holds the transaction for several blocks/hours.
3. Market volatility occurs.
4. Validator includes the transaction. Since `deadline` is `block.timestamp` (current block time), the Uniswap `PositionManager` accepts the transaction, even though it was generated based on stale data.

## Proof of Code
function testDeadlineIsBlockTimestamp() public {
        // 1. Fast forward time significantly
        vm.warp(block.timestamp + 1 days);
        
        // 2. Execute liquidity function
        // Ideally, this should fail if the original intent was 1 day ago.
        // But with block.timestamp, it passes.
        // (Code would invoke _decreaseLiquidity via harness)
    }

## Suggested Mitigation
Accept a user-provided `deadline` parameter in external functions (e.g., `decreaseLiquidity`, `increaseLiquidity`) and pass it to the internal logic, enforcing that the transaction is executed within a reasonable timeframe.
```

### H-79 / `cRgBM0t3oCUgH9KkD24pq`
- Finding title: Incorrect assembly masking in BridgeMessenger._processData causes permanent DoS of cross-chain governance
- Report lines: 6999-7071
```md
## [H-79]. Incorrect assembly masking in BridgeMessenger._processData causes permanent DoS of cross-chain governance

## id: cRgBM0t3oCUgH9KkD24pq

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UnsafeAssembyTypeCasts

## Exploit Type
IntegerMath

## Location
BridgeMessenger._processData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: BridgeMessenger._processData exists and uses inline assembly with mload(add(data,i)) to parse packed (target,value,payloadLength,payload). This implementation is unsafe as written: (1) it does not offset by 32 bytes to skip the bytes-array length word, so reads are misaligned; and (2) it does not shift/mask down to 160/96/32 bits. Because the variables are assigned inside assembly, Solidity will not necessarily clean upper bits afterwards, so value/payloadLength can be interpreted as huge numbers, triggering InsufficientBalance or memory allocation reverts.

This breaks a core invariant: authenticated bridge payloads should be executable. Any legitimate cross-chain governance message processed by OptimismMessenger.processMessageFromSource or WormholeMessenger.receiveWormholeMessages will reach _processData and can consistently revert, producing a DoS of governance execution on L2.

No mitigating guards exist in _processData (no masking, bounds checks beyond DEFAULT_DATA_LENGTH). It is exploitable in the sense that it prevents protocol functionality (permissionless in that any delivered message triggers the failure), with high impact and common likelihood once used.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `BridgeMessenger._processData`, the assembly block uses `mload` to read `value` (uint96) and `payloadLength` (uint32) from a tightly packed byte stream. The `mload` instruction reads a full 32-byte word. 

For `value`, the code reads at offset `i=32` (relative to data start). The memory at this location contains `Target` (20 bytes) followed by `Value` (12 bytes). Loading 32 bytes places `Target` in the higher-order bits of the resulting word. The code assigns this dirty 32-byte word to the `uint96 value` variable. 

Critically, Solidity 0.8+ does not automatically clean dirty higher-order bits for stack variables assigned within assembly blocks. When `value` is subsequently used in the comparison `if (value > address(this).balance)`, the full dirty 32-byte value (which includes the address of the target in the high bits) is used, causing the value to be interpreted as an extremely large number. This causes the check to fail and revert with `InsufficientBalance`, blocking all legitimate bridge messages.

Similarly, `payloadLength` is loaded without masking, reading `Target` and `Value` data into its high bits, which would cause `new bytes(payloadLength)` to attempt a massive allocation and revert.

## Impact
Functional permanent DoS of the cross-chain governance system. Valid governance proposals sent from L1 to L2s (Optimism, Wormhole) will permanently revert upon reception, preventing any protocol updates or fund management on L2s.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Governance sends a valid payload: Target(20b) + Value(12b) + Len(4b) + Data.
2. `OptimismMessenger` receives the message and calls `_processData`.
3. Assembly loads `value` via `mload`. The high bits contain the `Target` address.
4. `value` becomes a huge integer (approx `Target << 96`).
5. `value > address(this).balance` evaluates to true.
6. Transaction reverts with `InsufficientBalance`.

## Proof of Code
contract PoC {
    function testAssemblyBug() public {
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(address(0x1234), uint96(100), uint32(0));
        // Mocking the assembly logic in a test environment
        uint96 value;
        assembly {
             // i = 0 initially
             // i := add(i, 20) -> 20. target read ok.
             // i := add(i, 12) -> 32
             let i := 32
             value := mload(add(data, i))
        }
        // This assertion fails if value is not masked, showing it is dirty
        // In actual contract, (value > balance) checks full word
        uint256 dirtyValue = uint256(bytes32(abi.encode(value))); // Force read as uint256 to see dirty bits?
        // Actually simpler: in solidity logic:
        bool isHuge = value > 1000 ether;
        require(isHuge, "Value should be dirty and huge");
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Apply bitmasks in the assembly block to clean the higher-order bits. For `value` (uint96), use `and(..., 0xffffffffffffffffffffffff)`. For `payloadLength` (uint32), use `and(..., 0xffffffff)`.
```
