## [M-80]. DoS of L2 Dispenser via Reverting Staking Target in Batch

## id: qkSvgXD16qdC3Pbs_SFqn

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
ExternalCallAfterStateChange

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
DefaultTargetDispenserL2._processData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: A reverting target can be caused by an untrusted staking instance (buggy/malicious), and will revert the whole batch without requiring any admin error. While governance may intervene operationally, the DoS vector is not inherently a privileged-role misuse risk.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `DefaultTargetDispenserL2._processData` function iterates over a batch of targets received from L1. Inside the loop, it performs an external call to `IStaking(target).deposit(amount)`. This call is made directly without a `try/catch` block or low-level call error handling. If a single staking target in the batch reverts (e.g., due to a bug, a malicious upgrade by the service owner, or intentional griefing), the entire batch transaction reverts. Since L1 constructs batches containing multiple targets, a single malicious or broken target can prevent reward distribution for all other honest targets in the same batch. This can also cause the cross-chain message to be marked as failed or stuck, depending on the bridge architecture, requiring manual DAO intervention to resolve.

## Impact
Operational Denial of Service (DoS) preventing staking reward distribution. Valid services in the same batch as a reverting target cannot receive their funds. Requires DAO maintenance to fix.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. A malicious actor (or a user with a broken service) controls a Staking Target address included in the rewards batch (e.g., a Service Staking Proxy).
2. The actor upgrades the staking implementation to a contract that always reverts on `deposit()`, or the contract enters a state where `deposit()` reverts.
3. L1 calculates rewards and sends a cross-chain message to `DefaultTargetDispenserL2` containing a batch of targets, including the malicious one and several honest ones.
4. The `_processData` function executes on L2. It successfully processes honest targets until it reaches the malicious target.
5. The call `IStaking(maliciousTarget).deposit(amount)` reverts.
6. The entire `_processData` transaction reverts. No rewards are distributed, and the batch cannot be processed.

## Proof of Code
pragma solidity ^0.8.25;

import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/staking/DefaultTargetDispenserL2.sol";

contract MockToken {
    mapping(address => uint256) public balanceOf;
    function approve(address, uint256) external returns (bool) { return true; }
    function transfer(address, uint256) external returns (bool) { return true; }
}

contract MockStakingFactory {
    function verifyInstanceAndGetEmissionsAmount(address) external view returns (uint256) {
        return 100 ether;
    }
}

contract RevertingStaking {
    function deposit(uint256) external {
        revert("Griefing");
    }
}

contract ConcreteDispenser is DefaultTargetDispenserL2 {
    constructor(address _olas, address _factory, address _relayer, address _l1Processor, uint256 _chainId)
        DefaultTargetDispenserL2(_olas, _factory, _relayer, _l1Processor, _chainId) {}

    function processDataExposed(bytes memory data) external {
        _processData(data, false);
    }

    function _sendMessage(uint256, bytes memory, bytes32) internal override returns (uint256, uint256) {
        return (0, 0);
    }
    function _receiveMessage(address, address, bytes memory) internal override {}
}

contract DispenserTest is Test {
    ConcreteDispenser dispenser;
    MockToken olas;
    MockStakingFactory factory;
    RevertingStaking badTarget;

    function setUp() public {
        olas = new MockToken();
        factory = new MockStakingFactory();
        badTarget = new RevertingStaking();
        dispenser = new ConcreteDispenser(address(olas), address(factory), address(0x1), address(0x2), 1);
    }

    function testDoS() public {
        address[] memory targets = new address[](1);
        targets[0] = address(badTarget);
        uint256[] memory amounts = new uint256[](1);
        amounts[0] = 100;
        bytes32 batchHash = keccak256("batch");
        
        bytes memory data = abi.encode(targets, amounts, batchHash);
        
        // Should revert due to bad target
        vm.expectRevert("Griefing");
        dispenser.processDataExposed(data);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Wrap the `IStaking(target).deposit(amount)` call in a `try/catch` block. If the deposit fails, treat the amount as withheld (similar to the `limitAmount == 0` case) or queue the request, allowing the rest of the batch to process successfully.
```

### M-81 / `_P26IPhcmULODB978Alby`

```md
## [M-81]. Lack of fault isolation in `_processData` allows a single reverting target to block reward distribution

## id: _P26IPhcmULODB978Alby

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
CheapGriefingOrDosProfit

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
DefaultTargetDispenserL2._processData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Batch-wide revert from IStaking(target).deposit() is a permissionless griefing/availability risk stemming from untrusted external calls. It does not require privileged-role error; governance intervention is merely a mitigation path.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_processData` function iterates over a batch of targets received from L1 to distribute staking rewards. Inside the loop, it performs a low-level call to `StakingFactory` to verify the target, which correctly handles reverts by withholding funds. However, immediately after verification, it calls `IStaking(target).deposit(amount)` directly. If this external call reverts (e.g., due to the target staking contract reaching a cap, being paused, or having a runtime error), the entire transaction reverts. This causes a Denial of Service for the entire batch, blocking rewards for all other honest targets included in the same message. The owner must then manually intervene to process the batch via `processDataMaintenance`, creating an operational bottleneck and delaying protocol rewards.

## Impact
A single malfunctioning or capped staking contract prevents the distribution of rewards to all other participants in the batch, requiring governance intervention.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. L1 sends a bridge message containing a batch of 10 targets. 9 are healthy, 1 is a valid Staking Proxy that has reached its `MaxTotalStaked` cap.
2. The bridge relayer calls `_receiveMessage` -> `_processData` on L2.
3. The loop processes healthy targets successfully.
4. When it reaches the capped target, `IStaking(target).deposit(amount)` reverts because the cap is hit.
5. The entire `_processData` transaction reverts. The bridge message is marked as failed.
6. No rewards are distributed until the DAO manually reconstructs and submits a payload excluding the failing target.

## Proof of Code
contract MockStaking { function deposit(uint256) external pure { revert("Cap Reached"); } } 
// In a Foundry test:
function testBatchRevert() public {
    address[] memory targets = new address[](2);
    uint256[] memory amounts = new uint256[](2);
    targets[0] = address(new MockStaking()); // Reverts
    targets[1] = address(0x123); // Honest
    amounts[0] = 100;
    amounts[1] = 100;
    bytes memory data = abi.encode(targets, amounts, bytes32(0));
    
    // Expect revert due to lack of try/catch
    vm.expectRevert("Cap Reached");
    dispenser.processData(data); 
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Wrap the `IStaking(target).deposit(amount)` call in a `try/catch` block. If the deposit fails, the amount should be treated as withheld (similar to the verification failure path) or queued, ensuring that the rest of the batch can be processed successfully.
```

### H-82 / `vXaP9G2rBd9fQWQi3dXCA`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS in bridge verification due to dirty high bits in inline assembly variable
- Report lines: 7237-7316
```md
## [H-82]. Permanent DoS in bridge verification due to dirty high bits in inline assembly variable

## id: vXaP9G2rBd9fQWQi3dXCA

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UnsafeAssembyTypeCasts

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataPolygon.processBridgeData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: In VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData, assembly assigns `payloadLength := mload(add(data, i))` into a Solidity `uint32 payloadLength` without masking. The loaded 32-byte word contains non-zero high bits (tail of target/value), and Solidity explicitly warns that upper bits are not guaranteed cleaned for <32-byte types written in assembly. Subsequent use in `new bytes(payloadLength)` can interpret a huge 256-bit value and revert due to impossible memory allocation. No masking or cleanup is present. This can make any normal message (non-zero target) revert, creating a functional DoS for the Polygon bridge verifier.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData`, the code uses inline assembly to unpack the `payloadLength` from the packed `data`. It loads a 32-byte word from memory at an offset that aligns the `payloadLength` (4 bytes) with the least significant bytes of the word. However, due to the packed nature of the data, this 32-byte word also contains the preceding `value` (12 bytes) and the last 16 bytes of the `target` address in its higher bits. 

The assembly block assigns this dirty 32-byte value to the `uint32 payloadLength` local variable. Solidity documentation states that variables of types shorter than 32 bytes assigned in inline assembly are not guaranteed to have their upper bits cleaned, and the compiler assumes they are clean for subsequent operations. 

When `payloadLength` is used in `new bytes(payloadLength)`, the compiler uses the dirty value (which interprets the non-zero `target` address bits as part of the length), resulting in a value >> 2^128. This causes the memory allocation to attempt to allocate an impossible amount of memory, leading to an Out-of-Gas revert (or memory expansion revert). As `target` is strictly checked to be non-zero, this condition holds for ALL valid payloads, rendering the bridge verification permanently unusable.

## Impact
The verification contract permanently reverts for any valid bridged payload containing a non-zero target. This completely breaks the cross-chain governance bridge to Polygon, preventing any authorized governance actions from being executed on L2.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Governance creates a valid payload to send to Polygon: `target = 0x123...`, `value = 0`, `len = 4`, `payload = ...`. 
2. The data is packed as `target(20)|value(12)|len(4)|payload...`. 
3. The `ProcessBridgedDataPolygon.processBridgeData` function is called (e.g., by GuardCM) to verify this payload. 
4. `_verifyBridgedData` is executed. Inside the assembly block, `mload` reads a 32-byte word ending in `len`. The word starts with `target` bytes. 
5. `payloadLength` (uint32) is assigned this dirty word. 
6. `new bytes(payloadLength)` is called. Since `target` is non-zero, the effective length is enormous. 
7. The transaction reverts due to memory allocation failure/OOG. 
8. Verification fails, blocking the governance action.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/multisigs/bridge_verifier/ProcessBridgedDataPolygon.sol";

contract DosTest is Test {
    ProcessBridgedDataPolygon verifier;
    function setUp() public { verifier = new ProcessBridgedDataPolygon(); }

    function test_DoS_DirtyAssembly() public {
        // Construct valid packed payload: target(20)|val(12)|len(4)|bytes(4)
        address target = address(0x1234567890123456789012345678901234567890);
        uint96 val = 0;
        uint32 len = 4;
        bytes memory p = hex"deadbeef";
        bytes memory packed = abi.encodePacked(target, val, len, p);
        
        // Wrap in sendMessageToChild format
        address tunnel = address(0x1111);
        bytes memory l2Msg = packed;
        bytes memory inner = abi.encode(tunnel, l2Msg);
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(verifier.SEND_MESSAGE_TO_CHILD(), inner);

        // Expect revert due to OOG/Memory expansion from dirty payloadLength
        vm.expectRevert();
        verifier.processBridgeData(data, tunnel, 137);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Clean the upper bits of the loaded value in the assembly block:

```solidity
// Inside assembly block
payloadLength := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xffffffff)
```
```

### H-83 / `BiuM3M6JsvzMQdVnMwPsP`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS of bridge verification due to missing allowlist configuration setters in ProcessBridgedDataPolygon
- Report lines: 7317-7400
```md
## [H-83]. Permanent DoS of bridge verification due to missing allowlist configuration setters in ProcessBridgedDataPolygon

## id: BiuM3M6JsvzMQdVnMwPsP

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UnincentivizedMaintenanceOrKeeperlessProgress

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataPolygon.processBridgeData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: VerifyData defines `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds` and _verifyData reverts unless the packed (target, selector, chainId) key is set. In the provided code, there is no constructor initialization and no external/admin setter in VerifyData/VerifyBridgedData/ProcessBridgedDataPolygon to populate the mapping. Therefore, in this code as-written, the mapping remains empty forever and any call that reaches _verifyData will revert NotAuthorized, making the verifier unusable for legitimate governance messages.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `ProcessBridgedDataPolygon` contract is designed to verify cross-chain governance payloads by checking them against an allowlist (`mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds`) defined in the inherited `VerifyData` contract. The verification logic in `_verifyData` strictly requires that the `(target, selector, chainId)` combination is enabled in this mapping; otherwise, it reverts with `NotAuthorized`. 

However, neither `ProcessBridgedDataPolygon`, `VerifyBridgedData`, nor `VerifyData` expose any public or external function to modify this mapping. Since the mapping is empty by default and cannot be updated (even by an admin, as no setter or initialization logic exists), every call to `processBridgeData` will inevitably fail. This renders the contract functionally useless and permanently blocks any cross-chain governance actions that rely on this verifier.

Relevant snippet from `VerifyData.sol`:
```solidity
mapping(uint256 => bool) public mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds;
// ... No setter function ...
function _verifyData(address target, bytes memory data, uint256 chainId) internal {
    // ...
    if (!mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds[targetSelectorChainId]) {
        revert NotAuthorized(target, targetSelector, chainId);
    }
}
```

## Impact
Functional Denial of Service. The contract cannot perform its intended function of verifying bridge data, blocking the execution of legitimate cross-chain governance proposals.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `ProcessBridgedDataPolygon`.
2. Attempt to call `processBridgeData` with a valid payload (e.g., `sendMessageToChild` encoding a call to a target).
3. The function strips the header and decodes the payload, calling `_verifyBridgedData`, which calls `_verifyData`.
4. `_verifyData` checks `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds` for the target.
5. Since the map is empty and cannot be populated, the check fails and the transaction reverts with `NotAuthorized`.
6. No workaround exists as there are no functions to authorize the target.

## Proof of Code
contract ProcessBridgedDataTest is Test {
    ProcessBridgedDataPolygon verifier;
    
    function setUp() public {
        verifier = new ProcessBridgedDataPolygon();
    }

    function test_RevertAlwaysDueToNoConfig() public {
        // Construct valid payload: selector + target + offset + length + encoded (target, value, len, payload)
        // But since we can't whitelist, it should fail.
        address target = address(0x123);
        bytes memory l2Msg = abi.encodePacked(target, uint96(0), uint32(4), bytes4(0xdeadbeef));
        // Encoded args for sendMessageToChild(address, bytes)
        bytes memory args = abi.encode(address(0x456), l2Msg);
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(verifier.SEND_MESSAGE_TO_CHILD(), args);
        
        // Expect NotAuthorized because we couldn't whitelist the target
        // and we cannot add it because there is no setter.
        vm.expectRevert(abi.encodeWithSelector(NotAuthorized.selector, target, bytes4(0xdeadbeef), 137));
        verifier.processBridgeData(data, address(0x456), 137);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add an authenticated setter function to `VerifyData` or `ProcessBridgedDataPolygon` to allow the governance or owner to manage the allowlist. For example:
```solidity
function setAllowedTargetSelectorChainId(address target, bytes4 selector, uint256 chainId, bool allowed) external onlyOwner {
    // ... implementation setting mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds ...
}
```
```

### H-84 / `dKTWipsF_P7g1n0l-ewwE`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS of Tokenomics checkpointing via front-running donations
- Report lines: 7401-7456
```md
## [H-84]. Permanent DoS of Tokenomics checkpointing via front-running donations

## id: dKTWipsF_P7g1n0l-ewwE

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
GovernanceFrontrunDoS

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
Tokenomics.checkpoint

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Tokenomics.checkpoint reverts if `lastDonationBlockNumber == block.number`, and trackServiceDonations sets lastDonationBlockNumber to the current block whenever a donation is tracked. An attacker can mempool-front-run a checkpoint tx by sending a donation tx with higher priority in the same block, forcing checkpoint to revert. This can be repeated indefinitely to stall epoch advancement and reward settlement. Cost exists (minimum donation), but if combined with self-owned services it can be largely recouped (wash-donation mechanics), making sustained DoS feasible. No on-chain safeguard (e.g., using previous block number) is present.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `Tokenomics.checkpoint()` function enforces a check `if (lastDonationBlockNumber == block.number) revert SameBlockNumberViolation();`. This is intended to prevent flash-loan manipulations of rewards within the same block. However, this creates a critical Denial-of-Service vector. An attacker can monitor the mempool for a pending `checkpoint()` transaction and front-run it with a `Treasury.depositServiceDonationsETH()` transaction (which updates `lastDonationBlockNumber` to the current block number). When the `checkpoint()` transaction executes in the same block, it reverts. By repeatedly front-running `checkpoint()` calls, an attacker can permanently prevent epoch advancement, freezing protocol rewards, inflation updates, and treasury rebalancing.

## Impact
Protocol functionality is halted; epochs cannot advance, rewards are not distributed, and treasury funds are not rebalanced.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker observes a pending `checkpoint()` transaction in the mempool.
2. Attacker submits a `Treasury.depositServiceDonationsETH()` transaction with a slightly higher gas price, ensuring it is included in the same block but before `checkpoint()`.
3. `depositServiceDonationsETH` calls `Tokenomics.trackServiceDonations`, which sets `lastDonationBlockNumber = block.number`.
4. `checkpoint()` executes, sees `lastDonationBlockNumber == block.number`, and reverts with `SameBlockNumberViolation()`.
5. The epoch fails to advance. The attacker can repeat this for every attempt.

## Proof of Code
function test_DoS_checkpoint_via_frontrun() public {
    // Setup: Assume initial state with valid epoch
    vm.warp(tokenomics.mapEpochTokenomics(tokenomics.epochCounter() - 1).epochPoint.endTime + tokenomics.epochLen() + 1);

    // 1. Attacker front-runs with a donation
    vm.prank(address(0xbad));
    uint256[] memory ids = new uint256[](1); ids[0] = 1;
    uint256[] memory amounts = new uint256[](1); amounts[0] = 0.065 ether;
    treasury.depositServiceDonationsETH{value: 0.065 ether}(ids, amounts);

    // 2. Honest keeper tries to checkpoint in same block
    vm.expectRevert(abi.encodeWithSelector(Tokenomics.SameBlockNumberViolation.selector));
    tokenomics.checkpoint();
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Remove the `SameBlockNumberViolation` revert or modify the logic to allow checkpoints. To prevent flash loan attacks, consider using average balances or snapshots from the previous block instead of blocking simultaneous actions.
```

### H-85 / `X9Dqb6seCIUWJZHWchS00`
- Finding title: Treasury drain via high price impact in V3 buyBack due to missing post-swap slippage check
- Report lines: 7457-7531
```md
## [H-85]. Treasury drain via high price impact in V3 buyBack due to missing post-swap slippage check

## id: X9Dqb6seCIUWJZHWchS00

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
BuyBackBurner.buyBack

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Pool whitelisting + a pre-swap TWAP/center-price sanity check does not cap execution slippage for large exact-input swaps in illiquid V3 pools, nor does it provide an amountOutMin/post-swap bound; a permissionless caller can still trigger a highly value-destructive trade (and MEV sandwiching remains possible).
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `buyBack` function for Uniswap V3 (lines 191-218) lacks a mechanism to limit the price impact (slippage) of the swap execution. While it calls `ILiquidityManager(liquidityManager).checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(pool)` before the swap, this only verifies that the pool's starting spot price is close to the TWAP. It does not enforce a minimum output amount (`amountOutMin`) nor does it check the post-swap price, unlike the V2 implementation which explicitly checks `tradePrice` bounds (lines 149-152). 

If the `buyBack` function is called with a large `secondTokenAmount` relative to the V3 pool's liquidity, the swap will execute with excessive slippage, causing the protocol to sell treasury assets at a significantly unfavorable rate. Because `buyBack` is permissionless and defaults to swapping the entire contract balance if `secondTokenAmount` exceeds it, an attacker can trigger this function to drain the protocol's treasury value by forcing trades through illiquid or manipulated pools (sandwich attacks), effectively burning treasury funds for minimal OLAS return.

## Impact
Significant loss of treasury funds due to high slippage trades or MEV sandwich attacks.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. The `BuyBackBurner` contract accumulates a large balance of a whitelisted token (e.g., 1,000,000 USDC).
2. The corresponding Uniswap V3 pool (USDC/OLAS) has low liquidity or is temporarily unbalanced but within TWAP range.
3. An attacker calls `buyBack(USDC, 1,000,000 USDC, feeTier)`.
4. `_buyOLAS` calls `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice`. Since the spot price is close to TWAP (or manipulated to be so), the check passes.
5. `_buyOLAS` calls `_performSwap` (which performs an exact input swap) with the full 1,000,000 USDC.
6. Due to lack of `amountOutMin` or post-swap price checks, the swap traverses the tick range deeply, incurring e.g., 50% slippage.
7. The protocol receives 50% less OLAS than market value. The attacker (via back-running or simply as a griefer) causes this loss.

## Proof of Code
contract BuyBackPoC is Test {
    BuyBackBurner burner;
    MockLiquidityManager lm;
    MockToken token;
    MockToken olas;
    
    function testV3Slippage() public {
        // Setup mocks
        token = new MockToken("USDC", "USDC");
        olas = new MockToken("OLAS", "OLAS");
        lm = new MockLiquidityManager();
        burner = new ConcreteBuyBackBurner(address(lm), address(0x2), address(0x3), address(0x4));
        
        burner.setOLAS(address(olas));
        burner.setV3PoolStatus(address(0x123), true);

        // Fund burner
        token.mint(address(burner), 1000000 ether);
        
        // Execute buyBack
        // The mock logic simulates a swap that returns 1 wei of OLAS for 1M tokens due to lack of slippage check
        // and verifies the transaction does not revert.
        burner.buyBack(address(token), 1000000 ether, 3000);
        
        // Assert virtually all value was lost
        assertEq(olas.balanceOf(address(0x2)), 1); // Only 1 wei received
    }
}
// Mock contracts would be needed to simulate the swap behavior returning 1 wei.

## Suggested Mitigation
Enforce a minimum output amount for the V3 swap derived from the oracle price and `maxSlippage`, or implement a post-swap price check similar to the V2 implementation to ensure the execution price does not deviate excessively from the starting price.
```

### H-86 / `I7GBCNbgUYc6XhxoK7zEh`
- Finding title: BuyBackBurner V3 buyBack lacks execution slippage protection and fails open on fresh pools
- Report lines: 7532-7663
```md
## [H-86]. BuyBackBurner V3 buyBack lacks execution slippage protection and fails open on fresh pools

## id: I7GBCNbgUYc6XhxoK7zEh

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
FrontrunMev

## Exploit Type
FrontrunMev

## Location
BuyBackBurner.buyBack

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Two real issues exist in the current code: (1) V3 swaps use `amountOutMinimum: 1` (no execution slippage protection) in `BuyBackBurnerUniswap._performSwap`. (2) `LiquidityManagerCore.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` explicitly returns the spot `centerSqrtPriceX96` when TWAP retrieval via `observe` fails (`if (!success) return centerSqrtPriceX96;`), which commonly occurs on young/insufficient-history pools. Therefore, on such pools the pre-check provides no manipulation resistance, enabling extreme price manipulation and value extraction via sandwiching, subject to the pool being whitelisted in `mapV3Pools`.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurner` contract's V3 implementation of `_buyOLAS` relies solely on `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` in `LiquidityManager` for slippage protection. This function checks if the current spot price deviates from the TWAP by more than `MAX_ALLOWED_DEVIATION` (10%). However, there are two critical flaws:

1. **Execution Slippage Missing**: The actual swap in `BuyBackBurnerUniswap._performSwap` uses `amountOutMinimum: 1`. This allows MEV bots to sandwich the transaction up to the 10% deviation limit allowed by the pre-check. Since 10% is a large margin, attackers can consistently extract value from every buyback operation.

2. **Oracle Check Fails Open**: The `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` function in `LiquidityManagerCore` catches failures from the TWAP oracle (`getTwapFromOracle`) and falls back to returning the spot price without any deviation check (`if (!success) return centerSqrtPriceX96;`). The Uniswap V3 `observe` function reverts if the pool is not initialized or if the observation history is insufficient (e.g., pools younger than 30 minutes). If a new pool is whitelisted and a buyback is triggered, the protection is completely bypassed, allowing attackers to manipulate the price arbitrarily and drain the buyback funds.

## Impact
Protocol assets used for buybacks can be drained (up to 10% on mature pools, up to 100% on fresh pools) via sandwich attacks.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `LiquidityManager` and `BuyBackBurner`.
2. Create a mock Uniswap V3 pool that reverts on `observe` calls (simulating a fresh pool).
3. Whitelist this pool in `BuyBackBurner`.
4. Attacker manipulates the pool price significantly (e.g., 50% drop in OLAS price).
5. Attacker calls `buyBack(token, amount)`.
6. `LiquidityManager` calls `observe`, it reverts, so `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` returns the manipulated spot price.
7. `BuyBackBurner` executes the swap at the manipulated price with `amountOutMin: 1`.
8. Attacker profits; protocol burns significantly less OLAS than market value.

## Proof of Code
/**
 * To run: forge test --match-test testSandwichFreshPool -vv
 */
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {BuyBackBurnerUniswap} from "contracts/utils/BuyBackBurnerUniswap.sol";
import {LiquidityManagerETH} from "contracts/pol/LiquidityManagerETH.sol";

contract MockPool {
    uint160 public sqrtPriceX96;
    constructor(uint160 _p) { sqrtPriceX96 = _p; }
    function setPrice(uint160 _p) external { sqrtPriceX96 = _p; }
    function slot0() external view returns (uint160, int24, uint16, uint16, uint16, uint8, bool) {
        return (sqrtPriceX96, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, true);
    }
    function observe(uint32[] calldata) external view returns (int56[] memory, uint160[] memory) {
        revert("Oldest observation too new");
    }
    function increaseObservationCardinalityNext(uint16) external {}
    function observations(uint256) external view returns (uint32, int56, uint160, bool) {
        return (uint32(block.timestamp), 0, 0, true);
    }
}

contract MockRouter {
    function exactInputSingle(ExactInputSingleParams calldata params) external payable returns (uint256 amountOut) {
        return 1; // return dummy
    }
    struct ExactInputSingleParams {
        address tokenIn; address tokenOut; uint24 fee; address recipient; uint256 amountIn; uint256 amountOutMinimum; uint160 sqrtPriceLimitX96;
    }
}

contract MockFactory {
    address public pool;
    function getPool(address, address, uint24) external view returns (address) { return pool; }
    function setPool(address _p) external { pool = _p; }
}

contract TestSandwich is Test {
    LiquidityManagerETH lm;
    BuyBackBurnerUniswap bb;
    MockPool pool;
    MockRouter router;
    MockFactory factory;
    address token = address(0x1); address olas = address(0x2);
    
    function testSandwichFreshPool() public {
        // Setup
        router = new MockRouter();
        factory = new MockFactory();
        // Deploy LM and BB
        lm = new LiquidityManagerETH(olas, address(this), address(this), address(this), 100, address(this), address(this));
        // Mock Position Manager as factory provider
        vm.mockCall(address(this), abi.encodeWithSignature("factory()"), abi.encode(address(factory)));
        
        bb = new BuyBackBurnerUniswap(address(lm), address(this), address(this), address(router));
        bb.initialize(abi.encode([olas, token, address(this), address(this)], uint256(100)));
        
        // Create pool with normal price (sqrt(1) * 2^96)
        pool = new MockPool(79228162514264337593543950336);
        factory.setPool(address(pool));
        
        // Whitelist pool
        address[] memory pools = new address[](1);
        pools[0] = address(pool);
        bool[] memory statuses = new bool[](1);
        statuses[0] = true;
        bb.setV3PoolStatuses(pools, statuses);
        
        // Attack: Manipulate price to be extremely low (high slippage)
        // This represents a sandwich front-run
        pool.setPrice(7922816251426433759354395033); // 1/10th price
        
        // Execute BuyBack
        vm.mockCall(token, abi.encodeWithSignature("balanceOf(address)"), abi.encode(100 ether));
        vm.mockCall(token, abi.encodeWithSignature("approve(address,uint256)"), abi.encode(true));
        
        // This call succeeds despite the huge price deviation because observe() reverts in MockPool
        // and checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice returns the manipulated spot price.
        bb.buyBack(token, 100 ether, int24(500));
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
1. In `LiquidityManagerCore.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice`, revert if the oracle observation fails instead of returning the spot price.
2. In `BuyBackBurner` V3 implementation, calculate `amountOutMinimum` using the validated price (from `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` or an independent oracle query) adjusted by `maxSlippage`, instead of hardcoding `1`.
```

### M-87 / `IBGtgMux0r8u1-TvfZUVo`
- Finding title: Loose slippage bounds in V2 buyback allow value extraction
- Report lines: 7664-7716
```md
## [M-87]. Loose slippage bounds in V2 buyback allow value extraction

## id: IBGtgMux0r8u1-TvfZUVo

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Oracle

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
BuyBackBurner.buyBack (V2 overload)

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The V2 buyback path does not pass any minimum-output bound into the Balancer swap (limit=0). Instead, it relies on oracle-based checks before/after swap (validatePrice + getPrice comparisons). Even assuming validatePrice limits pre-swap manipulation, the execution itself can still be forced to clear at an unfavorable rate (e.g., MEV pushing price to the edge of allowed deviation), and the swap will not revert because no minOut is enforced at the router/vault level. This enables systematic value leakage up to whatever deviation the oracle checks allow, making the slippage protection looser than standard minOut-based protection.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_buyOLAS` (V2) function in `BuyBackBurner.sol` implements slippage protection by comparing the post-swap spot price (`tradePrice`) to the pre-swap spot price (`previousPrice`), ensuring deviation is within `maxSlippage`. However, `previousPrice` itself is only validated to be within `maxSlippage` of the oracle (TWAP) price via `validatePrice`. 

This effectively stacks the slippage tolerance: an attacker can shift the pre-swap price by `maxSlippage` relative to TWAP, and then the swap can shift the price by another `maxSlippage` relative to the pre-swap price. With `maxSlippage` defaulting to 10% (1e17), this allows a total deviation of ~20% from fair value. Additionally, `_performSwap` passes `limit: 0` to Balancer, foregoing protocol-level protection in the swap call itself. This looseness allows sandwich attacks to extract significant value from buyback operations.

## Impact
Value leakage during buybacks due to stacked slippage tolerances (up to 2x `maxSlippage`).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Oracle TWAP is 100. `maxSlippage` is 10%.
2. Attacker swaps in the pool, moving spot price to 110 (10% deviation). `validatePrice` still passes.
3. `buyBack` is called. It reads `previousPrice` = 110.
4. `buyBack` swaps, pushing price to 121 (another 10%).
5. Post-swap check: `tradePrice` (121) vs `previousPrice` (110) + 10% tolerance (121). The check passes.
6. Net result: Buyback executed at avg price ~115, while fair price was 100. Attacker extracts the difference.

## Proof of Code
function testV2DoubleSlippage() public {
    // Setup mocks as per previous pattern
    // 1. Mock Oracle to return TWAP = 100
    // 2. Mock Pool to have Spot = 110 (Pre-swap)
    // 3. Call buyBack
    // 4. Mock Pool executes swap, pushing Spot to 121
    // 5. Assert transaction succeeds despite 21% deviation from TWAP
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate a minimum output amount (`limit`) based on the Oracle/TWAP price (not the pre-swap spot price) and `maxSlippage`. Pass this strict limit directly to the `IBalancer.swap` call.
```

### H-88 / `cj1k3o7yhdcj5LuTka4ta`
- Finding title: Oracle manipulation on fresh pools via unsafe fallback in `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice`
- Report lines: 7717-7818
```md
## [H-88]. Oracle manipulation on fresh pools via unsafe fallback in `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice`

## id: cj1k3o7yhdcj5LuTka4ta

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
OracleUsingDEXorTWAP

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
LiquidityManagerETH.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: `LiquidityManagerCore.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` obtains `observationIndex` from `slot0()` and then reads `observations(observationIndex)` as the “oldest” timestamp, but this index is the most recently written observation. It then proceeds to TWAP retrieval and, crucially, fails open: if the staticcall to `getTwapFromOracle` fails (e.g., pool lacks required history), it returns the spot price without deviation checks. This enables price manipulation on young/insufficient-history pools (and also undermines intended TWAP validation generally), allowing sandwich/value loss during liquidity operations and buybacks.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The function `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` in `LiquidityManagerCore.sol` aims to ensure a pool has sufficient history before performing operations. However, it incorrectly retrieves the *most recent* observation timestamp (via `slot0.observationIndex`) and treats it as the *oldest* timestamp. The check `if (oldestTimestamp + SECONDS_AGO < block.timestamp)` will evaluate to `false` for any pool that has been updated within the last 30 minutes (1800 seconds). 

Consequently, the function proceeds to query the oracle via `getTwapFromOracle`, requesting an observation 1800 seconds ago. For pools younger than 30 minutes (common during `convertToV3` for new deployments), the `observe` call reverts (standard Uniswap V3 behavior for insufficient history). The `try/catch` mechanism (using `staticcall`) catches this failure and unsafely falls back to the current spot price (`centerSqrtPriceX96`) without any deviation check. 

This creates a critical window where an attacker (e.g., MEV searcher) can manipulate the spot price of a fresh pool and sandwich the Owner's `convertToV3` or `increaseLiquidity` transactions, causing the protocol to deposit liquidity at a significantly skewed price, leading to immediate value loss.

## Impact
Protocol Owned Liquidity (POL) can be drained or devalued by manipulating the spot price during initial liquidity provision or rebalancing on fresh pools.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. The Protocol Owner submits a `convertToV3` transaction to migrate liquidity to a newly created Uniswap V3 pool (age < 30 mins).
2. An Attacker detects this transaction in the mempool.
3. The Attacker front-runs the transaction by performing a large swap in the V3 pool, significantly skewing the spot price (e.g., +50%).
4. The `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` function executes:
    a. It fetches the latest observation timestamp (approx. `block.timestamp`).
    b. The check `latest + 1800 < now` evaluates to `false`.
    c. It attempts `getTwapFromOracle` which calls `pool.observe([1800, 0])`.
    d. Since the pool is young, `observe` reverts.
    e. The `staticcall` failure is caught, and the function returns the manipulated `centerSqrtPriceX96`.
5. The Protocol calculates amounts and mints liquidity based on this skewed price, depositing assets at an unfavorable ratio.
6. The Attacker back-runs the transaction, swapping back to the true price and capturing the value lost by the Protocol's mispriced deposit.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {LiquidityManagerETH} from "contracts/tokenomics/pol/LiquidityManagerETH.sol";

contract MockUniswapV3 {
    uint32 public timestamp;
    
    function setTimestamp(uint32 _ts) external {
        timestamp = _ts;
    }

    // slot0 returns observationIndex=0, indicating index 0 is the last written
    function slot0() external view returns (uint160, int24, uint16, uint16, uint16, uint8, bool) {
        return (uint160(100), 0, 0, 1, 1, 0, true);
    }

    // Return most recent timestamp at index 0
    function observations(uint256) external view returns (uint32, int56, uint160, bool) {
        return (timestamp, 0, 0, true);
    }

    // Simulate revert on observe for fresh pool
    function observe(uint32[] calldata) external view returns (int56[] memory, uint160[] memory) {
        revert("OLD");
    }
}

contract ExploitTest is Test {
    LiquidityManagerETH lm;
    MockUniswapV3 pool;

    function setUp() public {
        pool = new MockUniswapV3();
        lm = new LiquidityManagerETH(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(4), 10, address(5), address(6));
    }

    function testOracleFallbackUnsafe() public {
        // Set pool last update to now (active pool)
        pool.setTimestamp(uint32(block.timestamp));
        
        // Call checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice
        // 1. Checks timestamp: now + 1800 < now -> FALSE
        // 2. Calls getTwapFromOracle -> observe -> REVERTS
        // 3. Catches revert -> Returns spot (100)
        uint160 price = lm.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(address(pool));
        
        // Assert it returned spot price (100) instead of reverting
        assertEq(price, 100);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
1. Correctly identify pool age by checking `observationCardinality` or traversing the observation ring buffer to find the actual oldest timestamp.
2. Crucially, if the oracle `observe` call fails (indicating insufficient history), the transaction should **revert** rather than falling back to an unsafe spot price. Do not allow privileged operations on fresh pools until the TWAP window is populated.
```

### H-89 / `400iUCMq_rahJz-kt1Ka2`
- Finding title: Oracle check fails open on TWAP failure allowing 100% price manipulation on young pools
- Report lines: 7819-7907
```md
## [H-89]. Oracle check fails open on TWAP failure allowing 100% price manipulation on young pools

## id: 400iUCMq_rahJz-kt1Ka2

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
OracleUsingDEXorTWAP

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
LiquidityManagerETH.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The code explicitly fails open: `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` does `address(this).staticcall(abi.encodeCall(this.getTwapFromOracle,(pool)))` and if `success` is false it returns the spot `centerSqrtPriceX96` without enforcing the deviation bound. This permits arbitrary spot manipulation on pools where Uniswap `observe` cannot serve the requested window (often young pools). Since downstream actions (e.g., buybacks, liquidity provision) rely on this check, they can execute based on manipulated spot pricing with severe economic loss.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The function `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` in `LiquidityManagerCore` is designed to protect against price manipulation by verifying that the current spot price (`slot0`) does not deviate by more than 10% (`MAX_ALLOWED_DEVIATION`) from the 30-minute TWAP. The TWAP is retrieved via `getTwapFromOracle`, which makes a `staticcall` to `IUniswapV3(pool).observe`. 

However, if the `observe` call reverts (which happens if the pool is 'young'—initialized less than 30 minutes ago—or lacks sufficient history for the requested window), the `staticcall` returns failure. Instead of reverting, the code catches the failure and returns the *unvalidated* spot price (`centerSqrtPriceX96`).

```solidity
// LiquidityManagerCore.sol
(bool success, bytes memory returnData) = address(this).staticcall(payload);
if (!success) {
    return centerSqrtPriceX96; // Fail-open: returns spot price without check
}
```

This behavior allows an attacker to create or initialize a new V3 pool, significantly manipulate the spot price (far beyond the 10% limit), and trigger protocol operations (like `convertToV3` or `buyBack`) that rely on this check. The protocol will then accept the manipulated price as valid, leading to severe loss of funds or bad liquidity placement.

## Impact
Protocol can be forced to mint liquidity or buy back tokens at arbitrarily manipulated prices (100% loss feasible) when interacting with new or empty pools.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker observes a pending `convertToV3` transaction (or creates a `buyBack` opportunity) for a token pair where the V3 pool does not exist or is empty.
2. Attacker creates and initializes the V3 pool.
3. Attacker performs a massive swap to skew the price by 50%.
4. Attacker executes the target function (`convertToV3` or `buyBack`).
5. `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` is called. It queries `slot0` (gets skewed price) and tries to `observe` 30 minutes of history.
6. `observe` reverts because the pool is only seconds old.
7. The `staticcall` fails, so the function returns the skewed spot price immediately.
8. The protocol calculates `amountsMin` based on this skewed price and executes the liquidity addition or swap, realizing the loss.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/pol/LiquidityManagerETH.sol";

contract MockV3Pool {
    uint160 public sqrtPriceX96 = 1000;
    function slot0() external view returns (uint160, int24, uint16, uint16, uint16, uint8, bool) {
        return (sqrtPriceX96, 0, 0, 10, 10, 0, true);
    }
    function observations(uint256) external view returns (uint32, int56, uint160, bool) {
        return (uint32(block.timestamp), 0, 0, true);
    }
    function observe(uint32[] calldata) external view returns (int56[] memory, uint160[] memory) {
        revert("Not enough history");
    }
    function increaseObservationCardinalityNext(uint16) external {}
}

contract TestFailOpen is Test {
    LiquidityManagerETH lm;
    MockV3Pool pool;

    function setUp() public {
        pool = new MockV3Pool();
        lm = new LiquidityManagerETH(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(4), 10, address(5), address(6));
    }

    function testOracleFailOpen() public {
        // Even if observe reverts, function returns spot price (1000) without revert
        uint160 price = lm.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(address(pool));
        assertEq(price, 1000);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Do not fail open. If the TWAP cannot be calculated, the transaction should revert or require an explicit admin override/fallback. Remove `if (!success) { return centerSqrtPriceX96; }` and allow the revert to propagate.
```

### H-90 / `8-Cn3RdyqPGJ2ljgn2OS9`
- Finding title: Ineffective slippage protection in `buyBack` allows sandwich attacks due to TWAP usage
- Report lines: 7908-8013
```md
## [H-90]. Ineffective slippage protection in `buyBack` allows sandwich attacks due to TWAP usage

## id: 8-Cn3RdyqPGJ2ljgn2OS9

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer.buyBack

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The V2 buyBack path reads oracle prices before/after swap and swaps on Balancer with limit=0. If the oracle is TWAP-based (as stated in documentation), a single buyBack transaction will not materially change the TWAP value in that same transaction/block, so the after-swap oracle check can pass even when the execution price was heavily impacted by MEV/sandwiching. Because the swap itself has no minOut limit, the protocol can receive far less OLAS than expected while the oracle-based slippage checks remain satisfied.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurner` contract's `_buyOLAS` function (used by `buyBack`) attempts to enforce slippage protection by comparing the oracle price before and after the swap. However, the documentation states that the oracles used are 30-minute TWAPs. A TWAP oracle's value does not update instantaneously to reflect the spot price change caused by a single transaction. Consequently, the `IBalancer.swap` call in `BuyBackBurnerBalancer._performSwap` is executed with a `limit` of 0 (no minimum output), and the subsequent check `tradePrice >= lowerBound` validates the stable TWAP rather than the actual swap execution price. This allows MEV bots to front-run the `buyBack` call, manipulate the pool's spot price significantly (sandwich attack), and force the protocol to swap at a terrible rate, while the TWAP check passes successfully.

## Impact
High. Attackers can systematically extract value from every buyback transaction, draining the protocol's funds by forcing swaps at manipulated prices.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. The system is configured with a Balancer pool for TokenA/OLAS and a corresponding TWAP oracle.
2. Attacker observes a pending `buyBack(TokenA, amount)` transaction.
3. Attacker front-runs the transaction by buying a large amount of OLAS in the pool, driving the spot price of OLAS up by 50%.
4. The `buyBack` transaction executes:
    a. Reads `previousPrice` from the TWAP oracle (unaffected by the attacker's spot trade).
    b. Calls `balancerVault.swap` with `limit: 0`. The swap executes at the manipulated spot price, receiving significantly less OLAS than fair value.
    c. Reads `tradePrice` from the TWAP oracle (still unaffected).
    d. The check `tradePrice >= lowerBound` passes because the TWAP hasn't moved.
5. Attacker back-runs by selling the OLAS, profiting from the price difference at the expense of the protocol.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";

interface IOracle {
    function getPrice() external view returns (uint256);
    function validatePrice(uint256) external view returns (bool);
    function balancerVault() external view returns (address);
    function balancerPoolId() external view returns (bytes32);
}

contract BuyBackSlippageTest is Test {
    // Mocks
    address oracle = address(0x123);
    address balVault = address(0x456);
    address secondToken = address(0x789);
    address olas = address(0xABC);
    
    // Target
    BuyBackBurnerBalancer burner;
    
    function setUp() public {
        // Deploy target (mocking dependencies)
        burner = new BuyBackBurnerBalancer(address(0x1), address(0x2), address(0x3), address(0x4));
        
        // Setup basic state via storage manipulation or mock calls if needed
        // Ideally, we mock the oracle and vault behavior
    }

    function testExploitSlippage() public {
        // 1. Setup Mock Oracle to behave like a TWAP (returns constant price)
        vm.mockCall(oracle, abi.encodeWithSelector(IOracle.getPrice.selector), abi.encode(1e18));
        vm.mockCall(oracle, abi.encodeWithSelector(IOracle.validatePrice.selector), abi.encode(true));
        vm.mockCall(oracle, abi.encodeWithSelector(IOracle.balancerVault.selector), abi.encode(balVault));
        vm.mockCall(oracle, abi.encodeWithSelector(IOracle.balancerPoolId.selector), abi.encode(bytes32(0)));

        // 2. Setup burner with this oracle
        address[] memory tokens = new address[](1);
        tokens[0] = secondToken;
        address[] memory oracles = new address[](1);
        oracles[0] = oracle;
        
        // Prank owner to set oracle
        vm.startPrank(burner.owner());
        burner.setV2Oracles(tokens, oracles);
        vm.stopPrank();

        // 3. Mock Token Balance
        vm.mockCall(secondToken, abi.encodeWithSelector(IERC20.balanceOf.selector, address(burner)), abi.encode(1000e18));
        vm.mockCall(secondToken, abi.encodeWithSelector(IERC20.approve.selector), abi.encode(true));
        vm.mockCall(olas, abi.encodeWithSelector(IERC20.balanceOf.selector), abi.encode(0));
        vm.mockCall(olas, abi.encodeWithSelector(IERC20.transfer.selector), abi.encode(true));

        // 4. Mock Balancer Swap to return very little OLAS (Sandwiched)
        // Returning 1 wei of OLAS for 1000 tokens input
        vm.mockCall(balVault, abi.encodeWithSelector(IBalancer.swap.selector), abi.encode(1));

        // 5. Execute buyBack
        // It should NOT revert, meaning the slippage check passed despite 99.9% loss
        burner.buyBack(secondToken, 1000e18);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate the minimum amount out (`limit`) based on the oracle price *before* the swap and pass this value to the `IBalancer.swap` call. Do not rely on a post-swap oracle check if the oracle is a TWAP.
```

### H-91 / `jWEGvkqpvkI5YPhg3AaCj`
- Finding title: Incompatibility with USDT causes DoS in `buyBack` and `transfer` functions

```md
## [H-91]. Incompatibility with USDT causes DoS in `buyBack` and `transfer` functions

## id: jWEGvkqpvkI5YPhg3AaCj

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StandardViolation

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer.buyBack

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: USDT is itself non-compliant (void-return) and is directly impacted; the downgrade reason that it requires a non-standard token other than USDT is incorrect.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurnerBalancer` contract declares the `IERC20` interface with `approve` and `transfer` functions returning a `bool`. However, the USDT token on Ethereum Mainnet does not return a value (void). In Solidity 0.8.x, calling a function that expects a return value when none is provided results in a transaction revert due to return data size mismatch. This affects `_performSwap` (which calls `approve`) and `transfer` (which calls `transfer`).

## Impact
Funds stuck. USDT and other non-compliant ERC20 tokens cannot be swapped for OLAS or swept from the contract, leading to permanent lockup of these assets.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Contract holds USDT.
2. User calls `buyBack(address(USDT), amount)`.
3. `_performSwap` calls `IERC20(USDT).approve(...)`.
4. The internal EVM call succeeds, but Solidity's return data decoding checks fail because `returndatasize` is 0 but expected `bool` (32 bytes).
5. Transaction reverts.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/utils/BuyBackBurnerBalancer.sol";

contract MockUSDT {
    // No return value, compliant with mainnet USDT
    function approve(address spender, uint256 value) external {}
    function transfer(address to, uint256 value) external {}
    function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint256) { return 1000 ether; }
}

contract USDTTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerBalancer burner;
    MockUSDT usdt;

    function setUp() public {
        usdt = new MockUSDT();
        burner = new BuyBackBurnerBalancer(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(4));
        // Initialize with dummy data
        address[] memory accounts = new address[](4);
        accounts[0] = address(0); accounts[1] = address(0); accounts[2] = address(0); accounts[3] = address(this);
        burner.initialize(abi.encode(accounts, bytes32(0), uint256(0)));
    }

    function testUSDTDoS() public {
        // This should revert due to missing return value decoding error
        vm.expectRevert(); 
        // Calling low-level approve to simulate the internal call in _performSwap
        // Ideally we call buyBack, but we need full setup. 
        // We can verify interface mismatch directly:
        IERC20(address(usdt)).approve(address(this), 100);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use OpenZeppelin's `SafeERC20` library for `safeApprove` and `safeTransfer`, or modify the `IERC20` interface to remove the return value requirement (or use a wrapper that handles both compliant and non-compliant tokens).
```

### M-92 / `Qs9QLuY6JrnlYO9mcXARP`
- Finding title: USDT buybacks and transfers permanently revert due to incorrect IERC20 interface
- Report lines: 8088-8167
```md
## [M-92]. USDT buybacks and transfers permanently revert due to incorrect IERC20 interface

## id: Qs9QLuY6JrnlYO9mcXARP

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StandardViolation

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer.buyBack

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: USDT itself is the canonical non-standard token affected by bool-return interface calls; the downgrade reason excluding USDT is incorrect.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurnerBalancer` and its parent `BuyBackBurner` define the `IERC20` interface with `approve` and `transfer` functions returning `bool`. 
```solidity
interface IERC20 {
    function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) external returns (bool);
}
```
However, the USDT token contract (and other non-standard ERC20s) does not return a value for these functions. In Solidity 0.8+, calling a function that expects a return value when none is provided results in a transaction revert due to return data size validation mismatch. 

Since `buyBack` calls `approve` and `transfer` calls `transfer` using this interface, all interactions with USDT will revert. This makes it impossible to perform buybacks with USDT or sweep stuck USDT funds from the contract.

## Impact
Denial of Service for USDT buybacks and inability to recover/sweep USDT funds.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `BuyBackBurnerBalancer`.
2. Send USDT to the contract.
3. Call `buyBack(USDT, amount)`. The transaction reverts because `approve` expects a bool but USDT returns nothing.
4. Call `transfer(USDT)`. The transaction reverts because `transfer` expects a bool.

## Proof of Code
import {Test} from "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {BuyBackBurnerBalancer} from "contracts/utils/BuyBackBurnerBalancer.sol";

contract MockUSDT {
    // USDT signature: no return value
    function approve(address, uint256) external {}
    function transfer(address, uint256) external {}
    function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint256) { return 1000; }
}

contract USDTFailTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerBalancer buyBack;
    MockUSDT usdt;

    function setUp() public {
        buyBack = new BuyBackBurnerBalancer(address(1), address(1), address(1), address(1));
        usdt = new MockUSDT();
    }

    function testUSDTRevert() public {
        // Mock storage for oracle
        vm.store(address(buyBack), bytes32(uint256(6)), bytes32(uint256(uint160(address(this))))); // owner
        address[] memory t = new address[](1); t[0] = address(usdt);
        address[] memory o = new address[](1); o[0] = address(1);
        buyBack.setV2Oracles(t, o);

        // Should revert due to interface mismatch
        vm.expectRevert();
        buyBack.buyBack(address(usdt), 100);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use OpenZeppelin's `SafeERC20` library for all token interactions, or modify the `IERC20` interface to not expect return values and use low-level calls for verification.
```

### H-93 / `V4JYUwWBWtHJrkgG0IHJT`
- Finding title: MEV theft via lack of slippage protection in V3 buyback
- Report lines: 8168-8254
```md
## [H-93]. MEV theft via lack of slippage protection in V3 buyback

## id: V4JYUwWBWtHJrkgG0IHJT

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
FrontrunMev

## Exploit Type
FrontrunMev

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer.buyBack (V3 overload)

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The V3 swap path uses ISwapRouter.exactInputSingle with amountOutMinimum=1 (and sqrtPriceLimitX96=0), meaning virtually any output amount is accepted. The only on-chain check is LiquidityManager.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(pool), which does not translate into a concrete minOut. Therefore, MEV can sandwich or exploit low-liquidity conditions so the burner spends substantial secondToken and receives dust OLAS (still satisfying minOut=1). This is a direct value-loss vector on buyback funds.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_buyOLAS` function for V3 pools in `BuyBackBurnerBalancer.sol` fails to enforce a minimum output amount for the swap. While it calls `ILiquidityManager(liquidityManager).checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(pool)` to validate the starting pool price, it ignores the returned price and proceeds to call `_performSwap` with `amountOutMinimum: 1` and `sqrtPriceLimitX96: 0`. 

This configuration relies entirely on the pre-swap price check, which only validates that the *starting* spot price is not manipulated relative to the TWAP. It offers zero protection against *execution* slippage. An attacker can sandwich the `buyBack` transaction by manipulating the pool's liquidity depth (e.g., JIT liquidity removal) or simply executing a large front-run swap that shifts the price within the allowable tolerance of `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` but consumes all liquidity, causing the victim's swap to execute at a catastrophic rate. The `buyBack` function does not verify the received `olasAmount` and does not revert on high slippage, allowing the attacker to drain the `secondToken` funds for negligible OLAS.

## Impact
Direct theft of protocol funds allocated for buybacks via MEV/sandwich attacks.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker identifies a pending `buyBack(token, amount, ...)` transaction targeting a Uniswap V3 pool.
2. Attacker front-runs the transaction with two actions:
   a. (Optional) Remove deep liquidity positions to thin the order book.
   b. Swap `token` -> `OLAS` to push the price up to the limit allowed by `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` (e.g., +5-10%).
3. The `buyBack` transaction executes. `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` passes (price is at the edge of tolerance). The swap executes with `amountOutMinimum: 1`. Due to the pre-shift and/or thinned liquidity, the swap incurs massive slippage, yielding tiny amounts of OLAS for the input `token`.
4. Attacker back-runs by swapping `OLAS` -> `token` (and restoring liquidity), pocketing the difference.

## Proof of Code
contract V3SlippageTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerBalancer burner;
    MockSwapRouter router;
    MockLiquidityManager manager;
    address token = address(0x1);
    address olas = address(0x2);
    
    function testExploit() public {
        // Setup mocks
        router = new MockSwapRouter();
        manager = new MockLiquidityManager();
        // Deploy burner with mocks
        burner = new BuyBackBurnerBalancer(address(manager), address(0x3), address(0x4), address(router));
        // Initialize burner
        address[] memory accs = new address[](4);
        accs[0] = olas; accs[1] = address(0x5); accs[2] = address(0x6); accs[3] = address(0x7);
        bytes memory payload = abi.encode(accs, bytes32(0), uint256(1e16));
        burner.initialize(payload);
        
        // Mock token balance
        deal(token, address(burner), 100 ether);
        
        // Exploit: Router returns 1 wei for 100 ether input (slippage)
        // Expectation: Tx succeeds (no revert), funds lost
        burner.buyBack(token, 100 ether, int24(100));
    }
}

contract MockSwapRouter {
    function exactInputSingle(ExactInputSingleParams calldata params) external returns (uint256) {
        // Return 1 wei regardless of input, simulating 100% slippage theft
        return 1;
    }
    struct ExactInputSingleParams {
        address tokenIn; address tokenOut; int24 tickSpacing; address recipient;
        uint256 deadline; uint256 amountIn; uint256 amountOutMinimum; uint160 sqrtPriceLimitX96;
    }
}
contract MockLiquidityManager {
    function checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(address) external view returns (uint160) { return 0; }
    function factoryV3() external view returns (address) { return address(0); }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In `_buyOLAS` (V3), use the price returned by `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` (or fetch it from an oracle) to calculate a strict `amountOutMinimum`. Pass this value to `_performSwap` and ensure it is used in the `exactInputSingle` call instead of `1`.
```

## [M-94]. Permissionless transfer() function allows denial-of-service of the buy-back mechanism

## id: WfS6hMuIe2Yw1aGcbGmmJ

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccessControl

## Exploit Type
AccessControl

## Location
BuyBackBurner.transfer

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: BuyBackBurner.transfer(address token) is external and has no access control. It sweeps the entire token balance to treasury (or OLAS to bridge2Burner). A third party can front-run or continuously call transfer(secondToken) whenever funds arrive, making subsequent buyBack calls revert (buyBack adjusts amount to current balance, which becomes 0 and triggers ZeroValue). This does not steal funds (they go to treasury) but can indefinitely prevent buyback-and-burn execution and grief keepers by wasting gas.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `transfer(address token)` function in `BuyBackBurner` (inherited by `BuyBackBurnerBalancer`) is `external` and permissionless. It transfers the entire balance of the specified token to the treasury (or bridge). An attacker can monitor the mempool for `buyBack` calls and front-run them with `transfer(token)`. This sweeps the tokens to the treasury, causing the `buyBack` to fail (or swap 0), effectively preventing the protocol from burning OLAS and maintaining the intended deflationary mechanism.

## Impact
Functional DoS of the buyback-and-burn mechanism; disruption of protocol tokenomics.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Contract holds 1000 USDC intended for buyback.
2. Keeper sends `buyBack(USDC, 1000)`.
3. Attacker front-runs with `transfer(USDC)`.
4. `transfer` executes, sending 1000 USDC to Treasury.
5. `buyBack` executes. Balance is now 0. It swaps 0 (or reverts if logic requires >0, effectively wasting keeper gas).
6. The OLAS burn never happens.

## Proof of Code
function test_Transfer_Grief() public { 
 // Setup burner with tokens 
 // Attacker calls transfer 
 burner.transfer(address(token)); 
 // Keeper calls buyBack 
 vm.expectRevert(); // or assert 0 swapped 
 burner.buyBack(address(token), 100); 
 }

## Suggested Mitigation
Restrict `transfer` to an authorized role (e.g. `owner` or `liquidityManager`) to prevent griefing, while still allowing fund rescue.
```

## [H-95]. Lack of execution slippage protection in V3 `buyBack` allows fund draining via low liquidity pools

## id: g4mHfEJ4VP1c-jjCSRHsX

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer._performSwap (V3)

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: BuyBackBurnerBalancer._performSwap (V3) hardcodes amountOutMinimum=1, so the router will accept nearly any execution price. Since buyBack is permissionless, an attacker can trigger it when the pool is illiquid (naturally or after liquidity removal), causing the swap to experience extreme price impact and return negligible OLAS. The pre-check checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice only validates starting conditions and does not enforce execution bounds, so protocol funds can be burned/converted at highly unfavorable rates.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `buyBack` function (V3 overload) in `BuyBackBurnerBalancer` executes a swap on a concentrated liquidity router (Slipstream/Uniswap V3) with `amountOutMinimum` hardcoded to `1` and no router-enforced deadline. While the function performs a pre-swap check via `LiquidityManager.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` to ensure the pool's start price is not manipulated relative to its TWAP, it fails to validate the *execution* price or the *amount received* after the swap. 

In scenarios where the V3 pool has low liquidity (naturally or induced by an attacker withdrawing liquidity), a swap of a significant amount of `secondToken` will suffer massive slippage, returning a dust amount of `OLAS`. Since the `buyBack` function is permissionless, an attacker can trigger this function when the contract holds funds and the pool is illiquid, effectively burning the protocol's assets to the benefit of the remaining liquidity providers (which could be the attacker).

## Impact
Direct loss of protocol funds (tokens intended for buyback) due to unrestrained slippage.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker identifies a whitelisted V3 pool with low liquidity or creates a situation of low liquidity (e.g. by withdrawing their own LP position). 2. The `BuyBackBurnerBalancer` contract holds a significant balance of `secondToken`. 3. Attacker calls `buyBack(secondToken, amount)`. 4. The pre-check `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` passes because the spot price (tick) matches the TWAP (attacker did not skew the price, just removed depth). 5. The swap executes via `ISwapRouter.exactInputSingle` with `amountOutMinimum: 1`. 6. Due to low liquidity, the price impact is extreme (e.g., 90%). 7. The protocol receives a fraction of the expected OLAS. The value is lost to LPs.

## Proof of Code
contract V3SlippageTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerBalancer bbb;
    address mockRouter = address(0x123);
    address mockLiquidityManager = address(0x456);
    address mockToken = address(0x789);
    address mockOlas = address(0xabc);

    function testSlippage() public {
        // Setup logic mocking strict components
        // 1. Mock LiquidityManager to return valid check
        // 2. Mock SwapRouter to return 1 wei for a large input
        // 3. Call buyBack
        // 4. Assert no revert and loss of value
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate a reasonable `amountOutMinimum` based on the oracle price (retrieved from `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` or the oracle) and the `maxSlippage` parameter. Pass this calculated minimum to the `ISwapRouter.exactInputSingle` call.
```

### M-96 / `NyerjqKoTQv6MQ6cQkPze`

## [M-96]. Incompatibility with USDT and non-standard ERC20s causes DoS in `buyBack` and `transfer`

## id: NyerjqKoTQv6MQ6cQkPze

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StandardViolation

## Exploit Type
UncheckedERC20Return

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer._performSwap

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: USDT-like void-return tokens (including USDT itself) trigger this issue; it is incorrect to claim it requires a non-standard token other than USDT.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The contract defines the `IERC20` interface with functions `approve` and `transfer` returning a `bool`. Standard ERC20 tokens return a boolean, but widely used tokens like USDT (on Ethereum mainnet) do not return any value. 

In Solidity 0.8.x, calling a function that declares a return value when none is returned results in a revert due to abi decoding failure. As `buyBack` calls `approve` on `secondToken` and `transfer` calls `transfer` on tokens, any interaction with USDT will strictly revert. This bricks the buyback functionality for USDT and prevents sweeping USDT funds from the contract.

## Impact
Permanent Denial of Service for USDT and other non-compliant ERC20 tokens. Funds sent to the contract in these tokens may be stuck.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `BuyBackBurnerBalancer`. 2. Send USDT to the contract. 3. Call `buyBack(usdtAddress, amount)`. 4. The contract calls `IERC20(usdt).approve(...)`. 5. USDT executes the approval but returns no data. 6. Solidity's decoder expects a bool, finds empty data, and reverts the transaction.

## Proof of Code
contract USDTIncompatibilityTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerBalancer bbb;
    MockUSDT usdt;
    
    function testUSDTRevert() public {
        usdt = new MockUSDT(); // Implements transfer/approve without return
        // ... deploy bbb ...
        vm.expectRevert(); // Reverts due to decoding error
        bbb.buyBack(address(usdt), 100);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use OpenZeppelin's `SafeERC20` library for all token interactions (`safeApprove`, `safeTransfer`) to handle non-standard ERC20 return behaviors correctly.
```

## [M-97]. Hardcoded zero slippage parameters in _performSwap expose protocol to MEV sandwich attacks

## id: 7ziaUz0z7ymCRuQhi0HJ0

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer._performSwap

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The implementation hardcodes unsafe swap bounds: Balancer V2 uses limit=0 (no minimum amountOut) and V3 uses amountOutMinimum=1. While there are oracle/pool checks elsewhere (validatePrice and checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice), these checks are not used to compute an enforceable minOut for the swap execution. Consequently, MEV can sandwich to force execution at the worst available rate within whatever checks allow, or exploit low liquidity to produce dust outputs, causing significant value leakage from protocol buyback funds.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_performSwap` functions for both Balancer and Slipstream hardcode slippage parameters to unsafe values (`limit=0` for Balancer, `amountOutMinimum=1` for Slipstream). While the contract checks the oracle price deviation via `validatePrice` or `LiquidityManager`, this only verifies the pool's state *relative to the oracle* (often a TWAP) or bounds the *price impact*, but does not enforce a minimum output for the swap execution itself. If the `maxSlippage` allows for e.g. 10% deviation, an MEV bot can sandwich the transaction to force execution at the worst allowed price (capturing the 10% value), whereas a calculated `limit` based on the oracle price would enforce tighter execution (e.g. 1% slippage).

## Impact
Systematic loss of funds (value leakage) to MEV bots during every buyback, up to the configured `maxSlippage` tolerance.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Protocol configures `maxSlippage` to 10%.
2. Keeper calls `buyBack`.
3. MEV bot sees tx in mempool.
4. MEV bot frontruns: buys OLAS, pushing spot price to +9.9% (just within bounds).
5. `buyBack` executes. `validatePrice` passes (9.9% < 10%). Swap executes with `limit=0` / `amountOutMin=1`, accepting the bad price.
6. MEV bot backruns: sells OLAS for profit.
7. Protocol receives ~10% less OLAS than fair value.

## Proof of Code
function test_Slippage_Zero() public { 
 // Conceptual test checking the limit param passed to Balancer 
 // Mock Balancer Vault to capture call arguments 
 // Call _performSwap 
 // Assert limit argument was 0 
 }

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate the expected output amount based on the oracle price (or `previousPrice`) and a reasonable slippage tolerance (e.g., 1%), and pass this calculated value as `limit` or `amountOutMinimum` to the swap function.
```

## [M-98]. DoS on BuyBack for USDT due to Unsafe Approve

## id: KFQ43vPRCfQWoorM7xv8K

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UncheckedReturn

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
BuyBackBurnerBalancer._performSwap

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: USDT itself exhibits the non-standard approve behavior (void return, and in some cases nonzero->nonzero allowance restrictions). The issue does not require a non-standard token other than USDT.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurnerBalancer` contract uses the standard `IERC20` interface which expects `approve` to return `bool`. USDT's implementation of `approve` returns nothing (void), causing calls to revert on compliant EVMs/Solidity versions that verify return data size. Furthermore, the contract attempts to `approve` `secondTokenAmount` without first resetting the allowance to 0. USDT and similar tokens revert if `approve` is called with a non-zero value when the current allowance is already non-zero. If a buyback fails partially or leaves dust allowance, subsequent buybacks for USDT will be permanently DoS'd.

## Impact
Buyback functionality is completely broken for USDT (a major stablecoin likely to be used as `secondToken`).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. `buyBack` is called with USDT as `secondToken`. 2. `IERC20(usdt).approve(router, amount)` is called. 3. Transaction reverts because USDT does not return a boolean, and Solidity 0.8+ checks for return data existence for non-void interface functions. 4. Alternatively, if allowance was left non-zero from a prior interaction, `approve` reverts due to USDT's race-condition protection.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {BuyBackBurnerBalancer} from "contracts/BuyBackBurnerBalancer.sol";

contract MockUSDT {
    function approve(address, uint256) external { /* No return */ }
    function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint256) { return 1000; }
    function transfer(address, uint256) external { /* No return */ }
}

contract TestUSDTDoS is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerBalancer bbb;
    MockUSDT usdt;
    
    function setUp() public {
        usdt = new MockUSDT();
        bbb = new BuyBackBurnerBalancer(address(0x1), address(0x2), address(0x3), address(0x4));
    }

    function testUSDTRevert() public {
        // This will revert because bbb expects bool return
        vm.expectRevert();
        // We call performSwap logic via buyBack or directly if exposed. 
        // For unit test we can just mock the interface call failure proof:
        // See description.
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use OpenZeppelin's `SafeERC20` library for all token approvals and transfers. Specifically use `safeApprove` (deprecated) or `forceApprove` to handle the return value and the non-zero allowance reset automatically.
```
