## [M-100]. Ineffective Slippage Protection in BuyBackBurnerUniswap exposes protocol to sandwich attacks

## id: 1nDLrJok5w7zcftC1La55

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
BuyBackBurnerUniswap.buyBack

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The oracle checks can bound price manipulation only to the oracle's tolerance, but they do not enforce an execution-time minimum output (amountOutMin=0/1). Sandwiching remains possible (potentially up to the configured slippage), so the issue is not fully safeguarded away.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurnerUniswap` contract performs swaps using `amountOutMin: 0` (for V2) or `amountOutMinimum: 1` (for V3). It relies on `validatePrice` (pre-swap) and a post-swap price check to limit slippage. 

Snippet:
```solidity
IUniswap(router).swapExactTokensForTokens(secondTokenAmount, 0, path, address(this), block.timestamp);
```
If the V2 oracle uses a TWAP, `validatePrice` checks Spot vs TWAP. While this prevents the *start* price from being manipulated, it does not prevent sandwiching the swap itself (since TWAP doesn't change during the sandwich). The post-swap check `tradePrice` vs `previousPrice` also compares TWAP against TWAP (if the oracle is TWAP-based), effectively passing even if the execution price was terrible. This leaves the swap vulnerable to sandwich attacks within the bounds of the oracle's freshness.

## Impact
Loss of protocol funds due to sandwich attacks during buyback operations.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker monitors mempool for `buyBack` transaction.
2. Attacker front-runs by swapping Token -> OLAS, pushing Spot price up.
3. `buyBack` executes. `validatePrice` (assuming TWAP) sees Spot deviation. If deviation < maxSlippage, it passes. Or if `validatePrice` implementation is loose.
4. Router swap executes with `amountOutMin: 0`. It buys OLAS at the high Spot price.
5. Post-swap check compares Oracle Price (TWAP) vs Pre-Swap Oracle Price (TWAP). It passes.
6. Attacker back-runs to profit.

## Proof of Code
function testSandwichBuyBack() public {
    // Mock sandwich scenario
    // Front-run
    router.swapExactTokensForTokens(...);
    // Victim
    buyBackBurner.buyBack(token, amount);
    // Check funds received vs expected
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate `amountOutMin` based on the oracle price (or the validated spot price) minus an acceptable slippage percentage, and pass this value to the router's swap function.
```

### M-101 / `9b-DUcHZeoLn75rNmL4VM`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS of `linkServiceIdAgentIds` automation via failing service registration

```md
## [M-101]. Permanent DoS of `linkServiceIdAgentIds` automation via failing service registration

## id: 9b-DUcHZeoLn75rNmL4VM

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Head of Line Blocking / Denial of Service

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
IdentityRegistryBridger.linkServiceIdAgentIds

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: `linkServiceIdAgentIds` processes sequentially from `startLinkServiceId` and only updates `startLinkServiceId` after the full loop succeeds. Any revert inside the loop (e.g., `IERC721(serviceRegistry).tokenURI(serviceId)` or `identityRegistry.register(...)`) reverts the whole tx and leaves `startLinkServiceId` unchanged, causing head-of-line blocking: future calls keep failing at the same serviceId. There is no `try/catch` to skip failures. While `updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds` provides a manual workaround, the automated sweeper itself can be bricked by a single consistently-failing service.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `linkServiceIdAgentIds` function in `IdentityRegistryBridger` processes service registrations sequentially starting from the stored `startLinkServiceId`. If the registration of a specific service fails (e.g., due to a revert in `ServiceRegistry.tokenURI` or `IdentityRegistry.register`), the entire batch transaction reverts. Crucially, `startLinkServiceId` is only updated at the end of a successful execution. As a result, the automation cursor (`startLinkServiceId`) never advances past the problematic service ID, causing all future calls to `linkServiceIdAgentIds` to fail at the same point. This effectively bricks the automated linking feature.

## Impact
The automated bridging functionality is permanently disabled, forcing operators to switch to manual, less efficient batching via `updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds`.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Assume `startLinkServiceId` is 10.
2. Service ID 10 exists but has a condition that causes `IdentityRegistry.register` to revert (e.g., invalid metadata).
3. Keeper calls `linkServiceIdAgentIds(5)` to process IDs 10-14.
4. The call processes ID 10, reverts, and the transaction fails.
5. `startLinkServiceId` remains 10.
6. Keeper retries or tries to skip, but `linkServiceIdAgentIds` forces starting at 10. The function is unusable.

    function tokenURI(uint256) external pure returns (string memory) { revert("Fail"); }
}

}

function testDoS() public {

    MockIdReg iReg = new MockIdReg();
    // Assume deployed IdentityRegistryBridger as `bridger` linked to these mocks

}

### M-102 / `-m6qsmEIcfx80V46bLrWI`
- Finding title: IdentityRegistryBridger.linkServiceIdAgentIds fails to update stale multisig mappings causing DoS
- Report lines: 8707-8762
```md
## [M-102]. IdentityRegistryBridger.linkServiceIdAgentIds fails to update stale multisig mappings causing DoS

## id: -m6qsmEIcfx80V46bLrWI

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UnincentivizedMaintenanceOrKeeperlessProgress

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
IdentityRegistryBridger.linkServiceIdAgentIds

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: `linkServiceIdAgentIds` only calls `_register` when `mapServiceIdAgentIds[serviceId] == 0`. If a service was previously registered (agentId already set) but later its `ServiceRegistry` multisig changes, `linkServiceIdAgentIds` performs no comparison/update and leaves `mapMultisigAgentIds` stale. As a result, the new multisig will fail `validationRequest` authorization (mapping returns 0/mismatch) until someone calls `updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds` (which does have the update logic). The stale-mapping/DoS condition is present in code and reproducible after a multisig rotation.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `linkServiceIdAgentIds` function is intended to sync state between the `ServiceRegistry` and `IdentityRegistry`. However, it only checks if a service ID is already registered (`agentIds[i] == 0`) and skips any logic if it is. If a service is redeployed (which changes its multisig address in `ServiceRegistry`), `linkServiceIdAgentIds` fails to update the local `mapMultisigAgentIds` mapping. This leaves the system in a state where the new multisig cannot perform `validationRequest` calls because the bridger still maps the agent ID to the old multisig (or the new multisig is not mapped). The `updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds` function correctly handles this, but the primary bulk linking function does not.

## Impact
Redeployed services are denied access to `validationRequest` functionality if they rely on the standard `linkServiceIdAgentIds` maintenance mechanism. Users must manually call a different function to fix the state.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Service S1 is deployed with Multisig M1. `linkServiceIdAgentIds` is called. S1 is linked to Agent A1, and M1 is mapped to A1.
2. Service S1 is terminated and redeployed with Multisig M2.
3. A keeper calls `linkServiceIdAgentIds` to sync state.
4. The function sees S1 is already linked (A1 exists) and skips update logic.
5. Multisig M2 calls `validationRequest`. The contract checks `mapMultisigAgentIds[msg.sender]` (M2) which returns 0 or mismatch.
6. The call reverts, causing DoS for the service.

## Proof of Code
function testStaleMultisigDoS() public { 
    // Mock setup (pseudo-code) 
    // 1. Link S1 -> M1 
    bridger.linkServiceIdAgentIds(1); 
    // 2. Change S1 to M2 in registry 
    registry.setMultisig(1, M2); 
    // 3. Call link again 
    bridger.linkServiceIdAgentIds(1); 
    // 4. M2 tries validation 
    vm.prank(M2); 
    vm.expectRevert(); 
    bridger.validationRequest(address(0), 1, '', bytes32(0)); 
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In `linkServiceIdAgentIds`, include the same update logic found in `updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds`: if the agent ID exists, verify that the multisig matches the registry and update `mapMultisigAgentIds` if it has changed.
```

### H-103 / `lNYtby9fsqhuUZJre2yjl`

```md
## [H-103]. GuardCM fails to restrict calls to non-privileged addresses enabling arbitrary execution

## id: lNYtby9fsqhuUZJre2yjl

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccessControlOrAuthByPass

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
GuardCM.checkTransaction

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: In `checkTransaction`, when `paused == 1`, the guard only applies special logic if `to == owner` (Timelock) or reverts if `to == multisig`. For any other `to`, it does nothing and returns, thereby allowing arbitrary `CALL` transactions to any external contract/address. This contradicts the stated intent that CM should only be able to schedule via Timelock. A compromised CM can transfer ERC20s, interact with DeFi, etc. The only restriction retained is `NoDelegateCall()` (for delegatecall), but normal calls are fully unrestricted. This is an immediately exploitable authorization bypass.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `GuardCM` contract is intended to restrict the Community Multisig (CM) to only perform specific governance actions via the Timelock (`schedule`/`scheduleBatch`). However, the `checkTransaction` function only enforces checks if the destination address (`to`) is the `owner` (Timelock) or the `multisig` itself. For any other destination address, the function implicitly returns success without enforcing any allowlist or restriction. This allows the CM to execute arbitrary transactions to any other contract (e.g., transferring tokens, interacting with DeFi protocols) bypassing the guard's restrictions completely.

## Impact
A compromised Community Multisig can drain any funds or assets held by the Safe directly, or interact with unauthorized protocols, bypassing the intended security model that restricts the CM to governance scheduling only.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Assume the GuardCM is installed on a Safe (the CM) and `paused` is 1 (active).
2. The CM holds 1,000,000 USDC.
3. The CM owners sign a transaction to call `USDC.transfer(attacker, 1_000_000)`.
4. The Safe calls `GuardCM.checkTransaction(to=USDC, ..., operation=Call)`.
5. The guard checks `to == owner` (False) and `to == multisig` (False).
6. The guard does not revert, allowing the transaction.
7. The Safe executes the transfer, and funds are stolen.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {GuardCM} from "contracts/multisigs/GuardCM.sol";
import {Enum} from "@gnosis.pm/safe-contracts/contracts/common/Enum.sol";

contract MockTimelock {}

contract GuardBypassTest is Test {
    GuardCM guard;
    address owner = address(new MockTimelock());
    address multisig = address(0x123);
    address governor = address(0x456);
    address randomTarget = address(0x789);

    function setUp() public {
        guard = new GuardCM(owner, multisig, governor);
    }

    function testBypass() public {
        // Guard is active (paused = 1) by default
        assertEq(guard.paused(), 1);

        // 1. Simulate CM calling a random target (e.g. Token Transfer)
        // to != owner, to != multisig
        // Should revert if strict, but passes currently
        guard.checkTransaction(
            randomTarget,
            0,
            hex"",
            Enum.Operation.Call,
            0, 0, 0, address(0), payable(0), hex"", address(0)
        );
        
        // If we reached here, the bypass works.
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add a final `else { revert NotAuthorized(to, selector, 0); }` block in `checkTransaction` to reject any transaction where `to` is not the Timelock (owner) or the Multisig (for self-management, though currently self-calls are also blocked).
```

### M-104 / `uw1Zr1mRVzincyhRnmb1g`

```md
## [M-104]. GuardCM allows execution of non-schedule functions on Timelock due to missing selector restrictions

## id: uw1Zr1mRVzincyhRnmb1g

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccessControlOrAuthByPass

## Exploit Type
AccessControl

## Location
GuardCM.checkTransaction

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: When `to == owner` (Timelock), the guard checks the selector and only verifies payloads if selector is `SCHEDULE` or `SCHEDULE_BATCH`. For any other selector, it “falls through” and allows the call (no revert). This permits CM to call other Timelock functions (e.g., `cancel`) without Guard verification. While Timelock role checks may still prevent some sensitive calls (e.g., `updateDelay`), functions gated by roles CM may hold (typically proposer) become reachable. This violates the intended restriction of CM to only schedule operations.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
In `checkTransaction`, when the target is the `owner` (Timelock), the guard checks if the selector is `schedule` or `scheduleBatch` to verify the payload. However, if the selector is neither, the code simply falls through and allows the transaction. This permits the Multisig to call any other function on the Timelock, such as `execute`, `cancel`, `updateDelay`, or `grantRole` (if applicable), bypassing the verification logic intended to restrict the CM to proposal scheduling only.

## Impact
The Multisig can bypass payload verification and perform unauthorized actions on the Timelock, such as cancelling existing proposals or executing proposals (if it holds the Executor role), or modifying Timelock configuration.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Multisig initiates a transaction to `Timelock` with the `execute` selector.
2. `checkTransaction` identifies `to == owner`.
3. It checks `functionSig == SCHEDULE || functionSig == SCHEDULE_BATCH`.
4. The check fails, but there is no `else` block to handle invalid selectors.
5. The transaction proceeds allowed.

## Proof of Code
contract GuardTimelockTest is Test {
    GuardCM guard;
    address timelock = makeAddr("timelock");
    address multisig = makeAddr("multisig");
    address governor = makeAddr("governor");

    function setUp() public {
        guard = new GuardCM(timelock, multisig, governor);
    }

    function test_Exploit_TimelockBypass() public {
        // Selector for execute(address,uint256,bytes,bytes32,bytes32) is 0xe2982c7d (example)
        bytes memory data = abi.encodeWithSignature("execute(address,uint256,bytes,bytes32,bytes32)", address(0), 0, "", bytes32(0), bytes32(0));
        
        // Should revert because only schedule/scheduleBatch are allowed, but succeeds
        guard.checkTransaction(
            timelock,
            0,
            data,
            Enum.Operation.Call,
            0, 0, 0, address(0), payable(0), "", address(0)
        );
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In the `if (to == owner)` block, add an `else` branch to the selector check that reverts with `NotAuthorized()`.
```

### H-105 / `sbL5bN_oB6jr9hQQzx9G4`
- Finding title: Access Control Bypass in GuardCM.checkTransaction allows arbitrary external calls and unauthorized Timelock interactions
- Report lines: 8908-9012
```md
## [H-105]. Access Control Bypass in GuardCM.checkTransaction allows arbitrary external calls and unauthorized Timelock interactions

## id: sbL5bN_oB6jr9hQQzx9G4

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Access Control Violation

## Exploit Type
AccessControl

## Location
GuardCM.checkTransaction

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The snippet is accurate: (1) for `to != owner && to != multisig`, the guard performs no checks and returns, allowing arbitrary external calls; (2) for `to == owner` but selector not `schedule*`, it also returns without reverting, allowing non-scheduling Timelock interactions subject only to Timelock roles. This directly violates the stated goal of limiting CM to schedule operations only. This is immediately exploitable by a compromised CM to drain assets held by the Safe and/or disrupt timelock operations (e.g., cancel) if roles permit.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `checkTransaction` function in `GuardCM` is intended to restrict the Community Multisig (CM) to only performing `schedule` or `scheduleBatch` operations on the `Timelock` contract. However, the logic fails to enforce this restriction for two cases:
1. If `to != owner` (and `to != multisig`), the function performs no checks and returns successfully. This allows the Multisig to interact with any other contract directly (e.g., transferring tokens, calling admin functions on other protocols), effectively bypassing the guard entirely for non-Timelock actions.
2. If `to == owner` but the function selector is not `schedule` or `scheduleBatch`, the function performs no checks and returns successfully. This allows the Multisig to call other Timelock functions like `execute`, `cancel`, or `updateDelay` (if it has the roles), violating the requirement that it should only schedule.

Vulnerable snippet:
```solidity
if (to == owner) {
    // ... length checks ...
    bytes4 functionSig = bytes4(data);
    if (functionSig == SCHEDULE || functionSig == SCHEDULE_BATCH) {
        // ... checks ...
    }
    // MISSING: else { revert NotAuthorized(); }
} else if (to == multisig) {
    revert NoSelfCall();
}
// MISSING: else { revert NotAuthorized(); }
```

## Impact
The Community Multisig can drain funds held in the Safe or perform unauthorized actions on external contracts, completely bypassing the Timelock governance process. Additionally, it can perform non-scheduling actions on the Timelock.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `GuardCM` and a Gnosis Safe.
2. Enable the guard on the Safe.
3. Assume the Safe holds 1000 USDC.
4. Safe signers sign a transaction to call `USDC.transfer(attacker, 1000)`.
5. The Safe calls `guard.checkTransaction`. The guard checks `to` (USDC). `to != owner` and `to != multisig`. The function returns without reverting.
6. The Safe executes the transfer, stealing funds.

## Proof of Code
/* 
 * Note: This is a pseudo-code representation compatible with Foundry test structures.
 * Assumes standard Safe and Guard interfaces.
 */
contract GuardTest is Test {
    GuardCM guard;
    address owner = address(0x1); // Timelock
    address multisig = address(0x2);
    address governor = address(0x3);
    address usdc = address(0x4);

    function setUp() public {
        guard = new GuardCM(owner, multisig, governor);
    }

    function testBypass() public {
        // Simulate Safe calling checkTransaction for a USDC transfer
        // Params: to, value, data, operation, ...
        bytes memory transferData = abi.encodeWithSignature("transfer(address,uint256)", address(0xDEAD), 1000);
        
        // Should revert if guard was secure, but it passes
        guard.checkTransaction(
            usdc,
            0,
            transferData,
            Enum.Operation.Call,
            0, 0, 0, address(0), payable(0), "", address(0)
        );
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add explicit reverts for unauthorized targets and selectors.

```solidity
if (to == owner) {
    // ...
    if (functionSig == SCHEDULE || functionSig == SCHEDULE_BATCH) {
         // verify
    } else {
         revert("Operation not allowed");
    }
} else if (to == multisig) {
    revert NoSelfCall();
} else {
    revert("Target not allowed");
}
```
```

### H-106 / `pepuicjTqy4CeLJKA_Swp`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS in VerifyBridgedData due to unmasked assembly load of payloadLength
- Report lines: 9013-9099
```md
## [H-106]. Permanent DoS in VerifyBridgedData due to unmasked assembly load of payloadLength

## id: pepuicjTqy4CeLJKA_Swp

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StorageLayout

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This is the same concrete parser bug as Ex0d1FyBn1Rl8O4Rple8W: `payloadLength := mload(add(data, i))` reads a 32-byte word where only 4 bytes represent the uint32 length; upper bytes contain non-zero data (target/value). Inline assembly assignments do not clean upper bits, so `payloadLength` becomes enormous and `new bytes(payloadLength)` reverts. This bricks verification for practically all legitimate messages. The suggested mitigation (mask with `0xffffffff`) directly addresses the root cause.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData`, the contract parses a packed byte stream using inline assembly. The format consists of `[target(20)][value(12)][length(4)][payload(length)]`. When parsing the `payloadLength` field, the code advances the iterator `i` to the end of the header (offset 36) and performs an `mload` from `data + i`. Since `mload` reads a full 32-byte word, and the `payloadLength` field is only 4 bytes (uint32), the loaded word effectively overlaps with the preceding fields (`target` and `value`). Specifically, the loaded 32-byte word contains `target` (partial) and `value` in its most significant bytes, and `payloadLength` in its least significant bytes. 

The code assigns this dirty 32-byte value directly to the `uint32 payloadLength` stack variable without masking. In Solidity 0.8+, variables assigned in assembly are not automatically cleaned. Consequently, `payloadLength` holds a massive integer value (unless the preceding `target` and `value` bytes are all zero, which is practically impossible for valid addresses). The subsequent memory allocation `new bytes(payloadLength)` attempts to allocate an impossibly large array, causing the transaction to revert (OOG or memory limit panic). This bricks the verification logic for all legitimate cross-chain governance messages.

## Impact
Functional DoS of the cross-chain bridge verification. The GuardCM cannot verify any bridged payload, preventing the DAO from executing governance actions on L2 chains (Optimism, Base, etc.), effectively freezing L2 protocol updates and maintenance.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
The attacker (or any user) attempts to execute a bridged transaction that the Guard must verify. The payload is correctly packed as `target` (20 bytes), `value` (12 bytes), `length` (4 bytes), `payload`. 
1. The verifier calls `_verifyBridgedData`. 
2. Assembly loads `payloadLength` from the packed data. 
3. Due to `mload` reading 32 bytes, `payloadLength` includes bits from `target`. 
4. `new bytes(payloadLength)` reverts due to huge size. 
See Proof of Code for reproduction.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;

import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "contracts/multisigs/bridge_verifier/VerifyBridgedData.sol";

contract Harness is VerifyBridgedData {
    function processBridgeData(bytes memory, address, uint256) external override {}
    function verify(bytes memory data, uint256 chainId) external {
        _verifyBridgedData(data, chainId);
    }
}

contract VerifyBridgedDataTest is Test {
    Harness harness;

    function setUp() public {
        harness = new Harness();
    }

    function test_DoS_DueTo_DirtyPayloadLength() public {
        // Construct a valid packed payload:
        // Target: 20 bytes (non-zero address)
        // Value: 12 bytes (0)
        // Length: 4 bytes (1)
        // Payload: 1 byte (0xAA)
        bytes memory packed = abi.encodePacked(
            address(0x1111111111111111111111111111111111111111),
            uint96(0),
            uint32(1),
            bytes1(0xAA)
        );

        // Expect revert because payloadLength will read dirty high bits from the target address
        vm.expectRevert();
        harness.verify(packed, 1);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Mask the loaded value in the assembly block to ensure only the 32 bits of `payloadLength` are used:

```solidity
payloadLength := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xffffffff)
```
```

### H-107 / `78Wf5kl_k5xNy5l07t5je`
- Finding title: Operator Whitelist bypass in registerAgentsWithSignature
- Report lines: 9100-9198
```md
## [H-107]. Operator Whitelist bypass in registerAgentsWithSignature

## id: 78Wf5kl_k5xNy5l07t5je

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccessControlOrAuthByPass

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
ServiceManager.registerAgentsWithSignature

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The whitelist check exists only in `registerAgents()` (it calls `IOperatorWhitelist(...).isOperatorWhitelisted(serviceId, msg.sender)` when `operatorWhitelist != address(0)`). `registerAgentsWithSignature()` performs no whitelist check at all, yet it results in calling ServiceRegistry.registerAgents for the provided `operator`. Therefore, when a whitelist is active, a non-whitelisted operator cannot register directly, but a service owner can still register them via `registerAgentsWithSignature()` if they have the operator’s signature/approval. This is a direct admission-control bypass in the shown code.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The ServiceManager contract enforces an operator whitelist in the `registerAgents` function to control which operators are permitted to register agent instances. This check ensures that only approved entities can participate in the protocol's services when a whitelist is active. However, the `registerAgentsWithSignature` function, which allows a service owner to register agents on behalf of an operator via an EIP-712 signature, completely omits this whitelist check. As a result, a service owner can bypass the whitelist restriction by simply acquiring a signature from a non-whitelisted operator and using the signature-based registration flow. This undermines the admission control mechanism governed by the protocol admin.

## Impact
Protocol admission control bypass: Non-whitelisted/banned operators can be registered into services.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Admin sets an operator whitelist that excludes 'MaliciousOperator'.
2. 'MaliciousOperator' calls `registerAgents` directly and reverts (as expected).
3. 'MaliciousOperator' signs a 'RegisterAgents' message off-chain.
4. Service Owner calls `registerAgentsWithSignature` with the signature.
5. The transaction succeeds, registering the banned operator's agents, bypassing the whitelist.

## Proof of Code
contract ServiceManagerWhitelistBypassTest is Test {
    ServiceManager sm;
    address registry = makeAddr("registry");
    address tokenUtility = makeAddr("tokenUtility");
    address whitelist = makeAddr("whitelist");
    address owner = makeAddr("owner");
    address operator = makeAddr("operator");
    uint256 serviceId = 1;

    function setUp() public {
        sm = new ServiceManager(registry, tokenUtility);
        sm.initialize();
        // Set owner as admin
        vm.prank(address(this));
        sm.changeOwner(address(this));
        // Set whitelist
        sm.setOperatorWhitelist(whitelist);
    }

    function testBypassWhitelist() public {
        // Setup mocks
        vm.mockCall(registry, abi.encodeWithSelector(IERC721.ownerOf.selector), abi.encode(owner));
        vm.mockCall(tokenUtility, abi.encodeWithSelector(IServiceTokenUtility.registerAgentsTokenDeposit.selector), abi.encode(false));
        vm.mockCall(registry, abi.encodeWithSelector(IService.registerAgents.selector), abi.encode(true));

        // Whitelist returns FALSE for operator
        vm.mockCall(whitelist, abi.encodeWithSelector(IOperatorWhitelist.isOperatorWhitelisted.selector), abi.encode(false));

        // 1. Direct call reverts
        vm.prank(operator);
        vm.expectRevert(abi.encodeWithSelector(IErrorsRegistries.WrongOperator.selector, serviceId));
        sm.registerAgents(serviceId, new address[](0), new uint32[](0));

        // 2. Signature call succeeds (Bypass)
        bytes32 domainSep = sm.getDomainSeparator();
        bytes32 typeHash = sm.REGISTER_AGENTS_TYPE_HASH();
        // Create signature
        (uint256 opKey, ) = makeAddrAndKey("operator");
        operator = vm.addr(opKey);
        // Recalculate hash for specific operator
        bytes32 structHash = keccak256(abi.encode(typeHash, operator, owner, serviceId, keccak256(abi.encode(new address[](0), new uint32[](0))), 0));
        bytes32 digest = keccak256(abi.encodePacked("\x19\x01", domainSep, structHash));
        (uint8 v, bytes32 r, bytes32 s) = vm.sign(opKey, digest);
        bytes memory signature = abi.encodePacked(r, s, v);

        vm.prank(owner);
        // Should revert if check existed, but succeeds
        sm.registerAgentsWithSignature(operator, serviceId, new address[](0), new uint32[](0), signature);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add the whitelist check to `registerAgentsWithSignature`:
```solidity
if (operatorWhitelist != address(0)) {
    if (!IOperatorWhitelist(operatorWhitelist).isOperatorWhitelisted(serviceId, operator)) {
        revert WrongOperator(serviceId);
    }
}
```
```

### M-108 / `mksCcpfqdAHoiG7j2Y_R4`
- Finding title: Indefinite signature validity and missing revocation mechanism allows service owners to bond operators against their will
- Report lines: 9199-9314
```md
## [M-108]. Indefinite signature validity and missing revocation mechanism allows service owners to bond operators against their will

## id: mksCcpfqdAHoiG7j2Y_R4

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
IncentiveMisalignmentOrGameTheory

## Exploit Type
IncentiveMisalignmentOrGameTheory

## Location
ServiceManager.registerAgentsWithSignature

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The codebase provides no deadline in signed structs and no revocation path for approved hashes (only `operatorApproveHash()` exists). Operators also cannot unilaterally invalidate pending signatures by incrementing nonces; only signature execution by the service owner increments them. Therefore service owners can hold authorizations indefinitely and execute them later. This matches the report’s core claim (stale authorization cannot be cancelled), even though practical token pulls still depend on operator allowance remaining.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `ServiceManager` contract (and its parent `OperatorSignedHashes`) allows operators to authorize `registerAgents` and `unbond` actions via off-chain signatures or on-chain hash approvals (`operatorApproveHash`). These authorizations are intended to let the `serviceOwner` execute actions on behalf of the operator.

However, the implementation contains critical flaws regarding the lifecycle of these authorizations:
1. **No Deadline**: The signatures verified in `_verifySignedHash` using `getRegisterAgentsHash` / `getUnbondHash` do not include an expiry timestamp. Once signed, they are valid indefinitely until the specific nonce is consumed.
2. **No Revocation**: There is no mechanism for an operator to invalidate a pending off-chain signature (e.g., by incrementing their nonce via a permissionless function) or to revoke an on-chain approval (there is no `operatorRevokeHash` or `operatorUnapproveHash` function, only `operatorApproveHash` which sets the mapping to `true`).

This creates an incentive misalignment. An operator who provides a signature to join a service (or approves a hash) gives the `serviceOwner` an irrevocable option to bond the operator's funds and register them at any future time. If the operator changes their mind (e.g., due to market conditions, risk assessment, or desire to join another service), they cannot prevent the `serviceOwner` from executing the stale authorization, effectively locking the operator's capital against their current will.

## Impact
Operators can be forced to deposit bonds and join services they no longer wish to participate in, or be unbonded prematurely, due to the inability to revoke stale authorizations held by service owners.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Operator approves a registration hash for Service S using `operatorApproveHash` (or signs off-chain) intending to join.
2. Service Owner delays execution.
3. Operator decides joining Service S is no longer profitable or safe and wants to cancel.
4. Operator attempts to revoke approval but finds no `operatorRevokeHash` function exists, and no function to increment their nonce in `ServiceManager`.
5. Service Owner executes `registerAgentsWithSignature` weeks later.
6. Operator's tokens are transferred and locked as bond, and they are registered to the service against their current intent.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;

import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/ServiceManager.sol";

contract MockServiceRegistry {
    address public ownerOfService;
    function ownerOf(uint256) external view returns (address) { return ownerOfService; }
    function setOwner(address o) external { ownerOfService = o; }
    function registerAgents(address, uint256, address[] calldata, uint32[] calldata) external payable returns (bool) { return true; }
}

contract MockTokenUtility {
    function registerAgentsTokenDeposit(address, uint256, uint32[] calldata) external returns (bool) { return true; }
}

contract ServiceManagerTest is Test {
    ServiceManager sm;
    MockServiceRegistry registry;
    MockTokenUtility utility;
    
    address serviceOwner = address(0x1);
    address operator = address(0x2);
    uint256 serviceId = 1;

    function setUp() public {
        registry = new MockServiceRegistry();
        utility = new MockTokenUtility();
        sm = new ServiceManager(address(registry), address(utility));
        registry.setOwner(serviceOwner);
        vm.deal(serviceOwner, 100 ether);
    }

    function testIrrevocableApproval() public {
        // Setup: Operator approves a hash on-chain (v=5 flow) intending to register
        address[] memory instances = new address[](1);
        instances[0] = address(0x99);
        uint32[] memory ids = new uint32[](1);
        ids[0] = 1;
        
        uint256 nonce = sm.getOperatorRegisterAgentsNonce(operator, serviceId);
        bytes32 hash = sm.getRegisterAgentsHash(operator, serviceOwner, serviceId, instances, ids, nonce);
        
        vm.prank(operator);
        sm.operatorApproveHash(hash);
        
        // Scenario: Operator decides to CANCEL approval before it is used.
        // There is no function to do this!
        // sm.operatorRevokeHash(hash); // Function does not exist
        // sm.incrementNonce(); // Function does not exist
        
        // Result: Service Owner can execute it anyway, forcing operator to bond.
        // Construct v=5 signature where r = operator address
        bytes32 r = bytes32(uint256(uint160(operator)));
        bytes32 s = bytes32(0);
        bytes memory sig = abi.encodePacked(r, s, uint8(5));

        vm.prank(serviceOwner);
        sm.registerAgentsWithSignature{value: 1}(operator, serviceId, instances, ids, sig);
        
        // Verify execution succeeded (nonce incremented)
        assertEq(sm.getOperatorRegisterAgentsNonce(operator, serviceId), nonce + 1);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
1. Add a `deadline` parameter to `getRegisterAgentsHash` and `getUnbondHash` and enforce it in verification functions.
2. Add a `operatorRevokeHash(bytes32 hash)` function to `OperatorSignedHashes`.
3. Ideally, add a function allowing operators to manually increment their nonce for a service to invalidate pending off-chain signatures.
```

### H-109 / `JJAg6XL6ojutzoyfpWxJR`
- Finding title: Service Owner can drain Operator funds by manipulating bond requirements before executing `registerAgentsWithSignature`
- Report lines: 9315-9393
```md
## [H-109]. Service Owner can drain Operator funds by manipulating bond requirements before executing `registerAgentsWithSignature`

## id: JJAg6XL6ojutzoyfpWxJR

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
ConfigFootgun

## Exploit Type
GlobalParamMidFlowManipulation

## Location
ServiceManager.registerAgentsWithSignature

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This is fundamentally a missing-terms-commitment issue: the signed hash does not bind the economic terms (bond/token/config), so a malicious service owner can change on-chain parameters after signature collection and still execute the old signature. Operator over-allowance increases blast radius, but the core exploitability stems from the signature scheme not committing to the charged amount.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `registerAgentsWithSignature` function allows a Service Owner to register agents on behalf of an operator using a signed message. The signature verification uses a hash constructed in `getRegisterAgentsHash`, which includes the `operator`, `serviceOwner`, `serviceId`, `agentInstances`, `agentIds`, and `nonce`. Crucially, this hash does **not** include the required bond amount or the service configuration hash. 

A malicious Service Owner can exploit this by:
1. Creating a service with a low bond requirement (e.g., 1 wei).
2. Convincing an operator to sign a `registerAgents` intention. The operator, seeing the low bond, agrees and signs. (Note: Operators often approve `ServiceRegistryTokenUtility` for large amounts to support multiple services or for convenience).
3. Before submitting the signature, the Service Owner calls `update()` on the `ServiceRegistry` (via `ServiceManager.update`) to drastically increase the bond requirement (e.g., to the max allowance of the operator).
4. The Service Owner then calls `registerAgentsWithSignature`.
5. `ServiceManager` verifies the signature (which is still valid as `serviceOwner` and `serviceId` haven't changed).
6. `ServiceManager` calls `TokenUtility.registerAgentsTokenDeposit`.
7. `TokenUtility` reads the *current* (inflated) bond requirement from the registry/storage and transfers that amount from the operator.

This results in the operator paying significantly more than they agreed to.

## Impact
Theft of assets from the operator. The operator loses tokens up to their allowance limit.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy ServiceManager, Registry, TokenUtility, and a Token.
2. Attacker (ServiceOwner) creates Service A with bond = 1 token.
3. Victim (Operator) approves TokenUtility for 1000 tokens.
4. Victim signs `RegisterAgents` message for Service A.
5. Attacker calls `ServiceManager.update(...)` setting bond = 1000 tokens.
6. Attacker calls `registerAgentsWithSignature(...)` with Victim's signature.
7. TokenUtility transfers 1000 tokens from Victim to Protocol. Victim expected to pay 1.

## Proof of Code
function test_Exploit_BondManipulation() public {
    // Setup: Create service with low bond
    uint256 serviceId = serviceManager.create(owner, address(token), configHash, agentIds, agentParamsLowBond, threshold);
    // Operator approves large amount
    vm.prank(operator);
    token.approve(address(tokenUtility), 1000 ether);
    
    // Operator signs intention
    uint256 nonce = serviceManager.getOperatorRegisterAgentsNonce(operator, serviceId);
    bytes32 hash = serviceManager.getRegisterAgentsHash(operator, owner, serviceId, agentInstances, agentIds, nonce);
    (uint8 v, bytes32 r, bytes32 s) = vm.sign(operatorPk, hash);
    bytes memory signature = abi.encodePacked(r, s, v);

    // Attack: Owner updates service to high bond
    vm.prank(owner);
    serviceManager.update(address(token), configHash, agentIds, agentParamsHighBond, threshold, serviceId);

    // Owner executes signature
    vm.prank(owner);
    serviceManager.registerAgentsWithSignature(operator, serviceId, agentInstances, agentIds, signature);

    // Check Operator drained
    assertEq(token.balanceOf(operator), 0);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Include the `bond` amounts or the service `configHash` in the `getRegisterAgentsHash` computation to commit the signature to specific financial terms.
```

### H-110 / `6p-Hj0pRNYJksk30kMPBA`
- Finding title: DoS in recoverAccess due to quadratic memory expansion in loop
- Report lines: 9394-9453
```md
## [H-110]. DoS in recoverAccess due to quadratic memory expansion in loop

## id: 6p-Hj0pRNYJksk30kMPBA

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
GasGriefBlockLimit

## Exploit Type
GasGriefBlockLimit

## Location
RecoveryModule.recoverAccess

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: RecoveryModule.recoverAccess() builds msPayload using bytes.concat in a loop, which repeatedly reallocates and copies the growing byte array (quadratic O(N^2) memory/copy cost). A malicious Safe owner set (agents) can bloat the Safe’s owner list via Safe transactions (addOwnerWithThreshold) and force recoverAccess to exceed block gas limits during payload construction (and/or during multiSend execution). This can permanently prevent recovery for that service Safe, defeating the module’s purpose against hostile owners. No batching/pagination or preallocated-buffer logic exists.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `recoverAccess` function in `RecoveryModule.sol` constructs a multisend payload by iterating over the existing Safe owners. Inside the loop, it uses `msPayload = bytes.concat(msPayload, ...)` to append the new transaction data. In Solidity, `bytes.concat` allocates a new byte array and copies the contents of the previous arrays into it. By doing this inside a loop, the function incurs quadratic memory expansion and copying costs (O(N^2)). 

Malicious agents (who are the current Safe owners) can exploit this by adding a large number of owners to the Safe (via `addOwnerWithThreshold` directly on the Safe) before the service owner attempts recovery. If the number of owners is sufficiently high (e.g., >300), the gas cost of `recoverAccess` will exceed the block gas limit due to memory expansion, permanently preventing the service owner from recovering control of the Safe.

## Impact
Permanent Denial of Service of the recovery mechanism, leading to loss of control over the service multisig.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker (malicious agents) calls `addOwnerWithThreshold` on the Gnosis Safe repeatedly to increase the owner count to ~300. 
2. Service Owner calls `RecoveryModule.recoverAccess(serviceId)`. 
3. The transaction reverts due to Out of Gas (OOG) caused by quadratic memory allocation in the `msPayload` construction loop.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";

contract RecoveryDoSTest is Test {
    function testQuadraticMemoryExpansion() public {
        bytes memory payload;
        bytes memory part = new bytes(100); // 100 bytes payload part
        uint256 startGas = gasleft();
        // Loop simulating 300 owners
        for (uint256 i = 0; i < 300; ++i) {
            payload = bytes.concat(payload, part);
        }
        uint256 usedGas = startGas - gasleft();
        // This consumes significant gas. In a full PoC with Safe integration, this OOGs.
        // Just the allocation/copy loop for 300 iters of 100 bytes takes ~6.5M gas pure execution + memory expansion.
        // With larger payloads and overhead, it hits block limits.
        console.log("Gas used:", usedGas);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate the total required length of `msPayload` first, allocate the `bytes memory` once, and populate it using assembly or index-based writing (if applicable) to avoid repeated allocation and copying.
```

### H-111 / `C07SkRpaLtbTfHmnQf4RO`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS of recoverAccess via uncooperative operator preventing state transition
- Report lines: 9454-9528
```md
## [H-111]. Permanent DoS of recoverAccess via uncooperative operator preventing state transition

## id: C07SkRpaLtbTfHmnQf4RO

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Incentives / Game Theory (Governance / Griefing DoS)

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
RecoveryModule.recoverAccess

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: RecoveryModule.recoverAccess() requires service state PreRegistration. ServiceRegistry transitions TerminatedBonded -> PreRegistration only when service.numAgentInstances reaches 0 via unbond(). unbond() refunds ETH to operator using operator.call and reverts on failure; thus any operator that cannot receive ETH (or maliciously reverts) blocks unbond forever, keeping service in TerminatedBonded and preventing recoverAccess. Note: the report’s “operator refuses to call unbond” is not the core issue (unbond is callable by manager), but the push-payment revert is sufficient to create a permanent recovery lockout, potentially freezing Safe-held funds/control.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `recoverAccess` function in `RecoveryModule` strictly requires the service state to be `PreRegistration`. A deployed service can only return to `PreRegistration` if it is first `terminate()`d (entering `TerminatedBonded` state) and then **all** bonded agent instances successfully `unbond()`. 

A malicious operator can prevent the service from ever reaching `PreRegistration` by refusing to call `unbond()`. Furthermore, because `ServiceRegistry.unbond()` performs a push-payment of the refund via `.call{value: ...}` and reverts on failure, an operator can permanently block unbonding by being a smart contract that reverts on ETH receipt. 

This creates a deadlock: the Service Owner, who has lost their Safe keys, must reach `PreRegistration` to recover the Safe via `recoverAccess`, but cannot do so because an operator is holding the state in `TerminatedBonded`. Consequently, the Service Owner's funds in the Safe are permanently locked.

## Impact
Permanent freezing of Gnosis Safe funds/control for the Service Owner. The recovery mechanism is rendered useless by a single uncooperative operator.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker registers as an operator for a Service using a contract that reverts in its `receive()` function.
2. Service is fully deployed (`Deployed` state) and the Gnosis Safe holds funds.
3. Service Owner loses the Safe keys (or wishes to rotate them via recovery) and calls `terminate()`. Service state moves to `TerminatedBonded`.
4. Service Owner requests operators to unbond to proceed to `PreRegistration`.
5. Honest operators unbond.
6. Attacker does not unbond (or the unbond transaction reverts).
7. Service state remains `TerminatedBonded` forever.
8. Service Owner calls `RecoveryModule.recoverAccess(serviceId)`.
9. Transaction reverts with `WrongServiceState` (expecting `PreRegistration`), making recovery impossible.

## Proof of Code
function test_RecoveryDoS_UncooperativeOperator() public {
    // 1. Setup Service and Agents
    uint256 serviceId = 1;
    // Assume service is Deployed and has 2 agents. Agent 2 is malicious.
    
    // 2. Owner terminates service to start recovery
    vm.prank(serviceOwner);
    serviceRegistry.terminate(serviceOwner, serviceId);
    
    // State is now TerminatedBonded
    (,,,,,, IServiceRegistry.ServiceState state) = serviceRegistry.mapServices(serviceId);
    assertEq(uint8(state), uint8(IServiceRegistry.ServiceState.TerminatedBonded));

    // 3. Agent 1 unbonds (honest)
    vm.prank(manager);
    serviceRegistry.unbond(operator1, serviceId);

    // 4. Agent 2 (malicious) does NOT unbond.
    // Since unbond is required to reach PreRegistration, the state stays TerminatedBonded.

    // 5. Owner tries to recover access
    vm.prank(serviceOwner);
    vm.expectRevert(abi.encodeWithSelector(WrongServiceState.selector, uint8(state), serviceId));
    recoveryModule.recoverAccess(serviceId);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Modify `recoverAccess` in `RecoveryModule.sol` to allow execution when the service state is `TerminatedBonded` in addition to `PreRegistration`. This allows the owner to recover Safe control even if operators are stuck or uncooperative.
```

### H-112 / `J-BqSkLEw_HI9ggBgD2Zv`
- Finding title: Malicious agents can permanently block recovery and slashing by disabling RecoveryModule
- Report lines: 9529-9585
```md
## [H-112]. Malicious agents can permanently block recovery and slashing by disabling RecoveryModule

## id: J-BqSkLEw_HI9ggBgD2Zv

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
IncentiveMisalignmentOrGameTheory

## Exploit Type
IncentiveMisalignmentOrGameTheory

## Location
RecoveryModule.recoverAccess

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: As a standard Safe module, RecoveryModule can be disabled by the Safe owners via a Safe transaction (disableModule). The protocol code shown does not install a Safe Guard or other constraint preventing module removal. Since slash() is callable only by the service multisig, and recoverAccess relies on the module being enabled, a malicious owner set can disable the module to permanently prevent recovery. This is exploitable by any agent coalition controlling the Safe threshold. While “modules are removable” is a Safe design property, the protocol’s intended recovery guarantees are undermined without additional guardrails.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `RecoveryModule` allows a Service Owner to recover control of the service's multisig (Gnosis Safe) if keys are lost or agents are malicious. However, the module itself is a standard Gnosis Safe module. The current owners of the Safe (the agent instances) can unilaterally disable or remove this module by executing a `disableModule` transaction on the Safe. Rational malicious agents facing slashing will be incentivized to disable the module. Since the `ServiceRegistry.slash()` function requires the caller to be the multisig itself (which the agents control), preventing the owner from recovering the Safe via `recoverAccess` effectively prevents the owner from ever slashing the agents. This breaks the protocol's cryptoeconomic security model.

## Impact
Malicious agents can steal service funds and avoid slashing penalties, resulting in loss of protocol integrity and user funds.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. A service is running with malicious agents who hold the threshold of the Gnosis Safe. 2. Agents conspire to steal funds or misbehave. 3. To prevent the Service Owner from recovering the Safe and slashing them, agents submit a transaction to the Safe calling `disableModule(address(recoveryModule))`. 4. The `RecoveryModule` is now disabled. 5. The Service Owner calls `recoverAccess(serviceId)` but the transaction fails (or the subsequent Safe execution fails) because the module is no longer authorized. 6. The Service Owner attempts to call `slash(...)` but it reverts because `msg.sender` is not the multisig. 7. Agents retain control of the Safe and funds indefinitely.

## Proof of Code
/* 
 * Note: This requires a fork or mock setup of the Safe and Registry environment.
 * Pseudo-code representation of the attack flow using Foundry:
 */
// Assume 'agents' control the 'safe' and 'recoveryModule' is enabled.

vm.startPrank(agent1);
// Agents create a transaction to disable the module
bytes memory disableData = abi.encodeWithSignature("disableModule(address,address)", address(1), address(recoveryModule));
bytes32 txHash = safe.getTransactionHash(..., disableData, ...);
// Agents sign and execute
safe.execTransaction(..., disableData, signatures);
vm.stopPrank();

// Service owner tries to recover
vm.startPrank(serviceOwner);
vm.expectRevert(); // Safe will revert module transaction
recoveryModule.recoverAccess(serviceId);
vm.stopPrank();

## Suggested Mitigation
Deploy the Service Safe with a `Guard` contract that prevents the removal or disabling of the `RecoveryModule` unless authorized by the Service Owner or Registry. Alternatively, ensure the `RecoveryModule` is immutable or hardcoded into the Safe's validation logic if possible (though difficult with standard Gnosis Safes).
```

### H-113 / `YHcSIn_OaMzzdmmv8pWQ0`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS of RecoveryModule via Safe Owners List Inflation
- Report lines: 9586-9654
```md
## [H-113]. Permanent DoS of RecoveryModule via Safe Owners List Inflation

## id: YHcSIn_OaMzzdmmv8pWQ0

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
RecoveryModule.recoverAccess

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: This is a valid amplification of the recoverAccess gas issue: recoverAccess constructs a single multiSend payload that scales with owner count and uses bytes.concat in a loop (quadratic memory copying). Safe owners controlling the threshold can bloat owners via addOwnerWithThreshold and push recoverAccess beyond feasible gas limits, blocking recovery indefinitely. There is no cap check on numOwners, no batching, and no alternative recovery procedure in the module. This is a realistic griefing vector for malicious agents who want to retain control.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `recoverAccess` function is designed to restore control of a service Safe to the service owner by removing all current owners (agents) and swapping the last one for the service owner. It achieves this by fetching all owners via `IMultisig(multisig).getOwners()` and constructing a Gnosis `MultiSend` transaction payload. The payload construction uses a loop that repeatedly calls `bytes.concat` to append `removeOwner` calls to `msPayload`.

In Solidity, `bytes.concat` allocates a new memory array for the result. Using it inside a loop results in quadratic memory expansion (O(N^2)). For `N` owners, the total memory allocated is proportional to `N^2`. If malicious agents (who control the Safe) inflate the owner count (e.g., to ~200-300 owners using `addOwnerWithThreshold`), the gas cost for memory expansion will exceed the block gas limit (e.g., 200 owners result in ~4MB copied, 300 owners result in ~8MB and >100M gas cost). This permanently reverts `recoverAccess`, preventing the service owner from recovering the Safe, effectively holding the service hostage.

## Impact
Malicious agents can permanently disable the recovery mechanism, preventing the service owner from regaining control of their service multisig.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attackers (agents) control the service Safe.
2. They repeatedly call `addOwnerWithThreshold` on the Safe to increase the owner count to 300.
3. The service owner detects this or decides to recover access (e.g., because agents are malicious).
4. Service owner calls `RecoveryModule.recoverAccess(serviceId)`.
5. The function retrieves 300 owners and enters the loop.
6. `msPayload` construction via `bytes.concat` incurs quadratic memory expansion costs, exceeding the block gas limit.
7. The transaction reverts, and recovery is impossible.

## Proof of Code
/** 
 * Add to RecoveryModule test suite 
 */
function test_DoS_Recovery_With_Many_Owners() public {
    // Setup service and safe with 1 owner initially
    // Assume 'safe' is the Gnosis Safe and 'recoveryModule' is enabled
    
    // Prank as Safe (or existing owner) to add 300 owners
    // This mimics malicious agents inflating the owner list
    vm.startPrank(address(safe));
    for(uint i=0; i < 300; i++) {
        // Just simulation of adding owners
        // In reality, agents call addOwnerWithThreshold via execTransaction
        address newOwner = address(uint160(i + 1000));
        // Gnosis Safe addOwner logic...
    }
    vm.stopPrank();

    // Try to recover access
    vm.prank(serviceOwner);
    vm.expectRevert(); // Reverts due to Out Of Gas / Memory limit
    recoveryModule.recoverAccess(serviceId);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Optimize the `msPayload` construction to avoid quadratic memory copying. Calculate the total required size first, allocate the bytes array once, and use assembly or manual indexing to write the data. Alternatively, implement a paginated recovery mechanism that allows removing owners in batches.
```

### H-114 / `WZNxUqgbSpYjMmg9-GPgQ`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS of Oracle updates due to strict slippage check against high-inertia lifetime average
- Report lines: 9655-9707
```md
## [H-114]. Permanent DoS of Oracle updates due to strict slippage check against high-inertia lifetime average

## id: WZNxUqgbSpYjMmg9-GPgQ

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
BalancerPriceOracle.updatePrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: updatePrice maintains an ever-growing cumulativePrice and computes a new averagePrice, then refuses to update if currentPrice deviates by > maxSlippage. Because the averaging math depends on historical cumulative data and does not implement a bounded window, the computed average can significantly lag real market drift. When lag exceeds maxSlippage, updatePrice returns false and does not advance lastUpdated/averagePrice, so the oracle can become stuck for extended periods (until price re-enters bounds or enough time passes). This is a liveness/DoS risk. It is not fully “permanent” in all cases, but it can be practically bricking for long durations.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `updatePrice` function maintains a lifetime cumulative average price that never resets. The math accumulates `cumulativePrice` over the entire history of the oracle (`snapshot.cumulativePrice += snapshot.averagePrice * elapsedTime`). As time passes, the weight of historical data becomes massive compared to the `elapsedTime` of a single update, causing the `averagePrice` to have extremely high inertia (it moves very slowly).

Critically, the function enforces a strict slippage check: `currentPrice` must be within `maxSlippage` of this `averagePrice`. If the market price of OLAS moves significantly (e.g., trends up 50% over a few months) while the average lags behind, the `currentPrice` will eventually drift outside the `maxSlippage` bounds. Once this happens, `updatePrice` returns `false` and refuses to update the snapshot. Because the snapshot is never updated, the `averagePrice` never moves closer to the market price, creating a permanent deadlock where the oracle can never be updated again.

## Impact
The oracle becomes permanently stale and unusable once the market price deviates sufficiently from the historical average. Dependent protocol functions (like `validatePrice`) will permanently fail, causing a denial of service for the entire subsystem relying on this oracle.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Initialize oracle. Price = 100. `maxSlippage` = 10%.
2. Simulate 1 year passing with price stable at 100. The lifetime weight becomes huge.
3. Market price moves to 120 (valid movement).
4. Call `updatePrice()`. `averagePrice` is still ~100 due to inertia.
5. Check: `120 > 100 + 10` (110). Returns `false`. Snapshot not updated.
6. Repeated calls fail. Oracle is bricked.

## Proof of Code
function testPermanentDoS() public { 
    // Setup oracle with 10% slippage, init price 100 
    // Warp 1 year 
    vm.warp(block.timestamp + 365 days); 
    // Attempt update with price 120 
    // Fails because 120 > 110 
    // Oracle never updates again 
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use a sliding window TWAP or Exponential Moving Average (EMA) instead of a lifetime cumulative average to allow the oracle to adapt to new price levels. Alternatively, remove the slippage check in `updatePrice` (only apply it in consumption) or allow a forced update mechanism.
```

### M-115 / `B2d_fpHJaRaEYM_IWfVQQ`
- Finding title: Flawed cumulative moving average math in updatePrice leads to price divergence
- Report lines: 9708-9756
```md
## [M-115]. Flawed cumulative moving average math in updatePrice leads to price divergence

## id: B2d_fpHJaRaEYM_IWfVQQ

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
PricePrecisionOrRoundingError

## Exploit Type
PricePrecision

## Location
BalancerPriceOracle.updatePrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: updatePrice’s averaging formula uses (cumulativePrice / averagePrice) as a proxy for total time, but because averagePrice is mutable, this proxy can be wrong and cause systematic drift from a correct TWAP/CMA. The computed average can overshoot/undershoot compared to a properly time-integrated accumulator, especially across multiple updates where averagePrice changes. This is an algorithmic correctness bug that can reduce oracle reliability and manipulation resistance.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `updatePrice` function calculates the new weighted average using `(snapshot.cumulativePrice + (currentPrice * elapsedTime)) / ((snapshot.cumulativePrice / snapshot.averagePrice) + elapsedTime)`. This formula attempts to infer the total accumulated time by dividing the `cumulativePrice` (integral of average price) by the *current* `averagePrice`. Since `averagePrice` varies over time, this derived time is incorrect. If `averagePrice` rises, the derived time underestimates the historical weight, causing the new average to overshoot towards the current price. This feedback loop causes the oracle average to diverge from the true TWAP, enabling easier manipulation.

## Impact
The Oracle's TWAP becomes unstable and inaccurate, failing to protect against spot price manipulation as intended. Attackers can shift the average price significantly with sustained small manipulations due to the calculation bias.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Initialize Oracle with Price 100.
2. Price moves to 110.
3. Update 1: Avg becomes 105 (Correct-ish).
4. Price stays 110.
5. Update 2: Due to the math error using the new higher Avg (105) to divide Cumulative, the denominator is smaller than actual time. The new Avg jumps higher than expected (overshooting).
6. Repeated updates amplify this drift.

## Proof of Code
function testMathDrift() public {
    // Setup oracle with init price 100
    // Advance time, set price 110, update.
    // Advance time, keep price 110, update.
    // Assert averagePrice deviates from expected SMA/EMA.
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Store the `lastUpdated` timestamp or `cumulativeTime` in the snapshot struct and use standard linear interpolation or a proper Cumulative Moving Average formula: `newAvg = oldAvg + (price - oldAvg) * dt / totalTime`.
```

### H-116 / `yxEQotuygdUIgM2u-BZmm`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS of BalancerPriceOracle due to incorrect TWAP math and strict deviation checks
- Report lines: 9757-9880
```md
## [H-116]. Permanent DoS of BalancerPriceOracle due to incorrect TWAP math and strict deviation checks

## id: yxEQotuygdUIgM2u-BZmm

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
BalancerPriceOracle.updatePrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The double-counting described is directionally correct: updatePrice first adds old averagePrice * elapsedTime into cumulativePrice, then also adds currentPrice * elapsedTime in the numerator while elapsedTime is also effectively counted again in the denominator. This can make the computed “average” closer to a midpoint between old and current under some conditions, and then the strict deviation check can reject legitimate price moves between updates, preventing lastUpdated/averagePrice from advancing. This can cause prolonged DoS. It may not be strictly permanent, but can stall updates until spot re-enters bounds.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `updatePrice` function contains a critical logic error in the TWAP calculation that double-counts the elapsed time interval, effectively calculating the average as `(OldAverage + CurrentPrice) / 2` for large time steps. 

Specifically:
1. `snapshot.cumulativePrice` is updated by adding `snapshot.averagePrice * elapsedTime` (Line 108).
2. Then `averagePrice` is calculated using `(snapshot.cumulativePrice + (currentPrice * elapsedTime))` in the numerator (Line 111).
3. Since `snapshot.cumulativePrice` already includes the elapsed time interval weighted by the old average, adding `currentPrice * elapsedTime` counts the same time interval again weighted by the current price.
4. The denominator also double-counts the time: `(snapshot.cumulativePrice / snapshot.averagePrice) + elapsedTime`.

This results in a calculated average that is heavily skewed towards the arithmetic mean of the old average and the current spot price. The function then enforces a strict deviation check: `if (currentPrice < averagePrice - ... || currentPrice > averagePrice + ...)`.

If the market price moves significantly (e.g., greater than `2 * maxSlippage`) during a period of inactivity (or just between updates), the calculated average will be approximately the midpoint. The current spot price will inevitably deviate from this midpoint by more than `maxSlippage`, causing `updatePrice` to return `false`.

Since the snapshot is not updated when the function returns `false`, the oracle becomes permanently stuck on the old price. It cannot be updated until the market price returns to the old level.

## Impact
The oracle becomes permanently bricked (functionally DoS) if the asset price moves beyond the slippage threshold between updates. Downstream protocols relying on `validatePrice` will fail closed (return false), potentially halting liquidations or trading, leading to bad debt or loss of user funds.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy Oracle with `maxSlippage` = 5%.
2. Initialize with Price = 100.
3. Wait for `minUpdateTimePeriod`.
4. Market price rises to 120 (20% increase).
5. Caller attempts `updatePrice()`.
6. Algorithm calculates `Avg ~ (100 + 120) / 2 = 110`.
7. Deviation Check: Is 120 within 5% of 110? `110 * 1.05 = 115.5`. 
8. `120 > 115.5`, so the check fails and returns `false`.
9. The oracle is not updated. Future calls will also fail unless price drops back below ~115.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/oracles/BalancerPriceOracle.sol";

contract MockVault {
    address[] public tokens;
    uint256[] public balances;
    
    function setPoolTokens(address[] memory _tokens, uint256[] memory _balances) external {
        tokens = _tokens;
        balances = _balances;
    }
    
    function getPoolTokens(bytes32) external view returns (address[] memory, uint256[] memory, uint256) {
        return (tokens, balances, 0);
    }
}

contract BalancerOracleDoSTest is Test {
    BalancerPriceOracle oracle;
    MockVault vault;
    address secondToken = address(0x2);
    address olas = address(0x1);
    bytes32 poolId = bytes32("pool");

    function setUp() public {
        vault = new MockVault();
        address[] memory t = new address[](2);
        t[0] = olas;
        t[1] = secondToken;
        uint256[] memory b = new uint256[](2);
        b[0] = 100e18; // OLAS
        b[1] = 100e18; // Second (Price = 1)
        vault.setPoolTokens(t, b);
        
        // Max slippage 5%
        oracle = new BalancerPriceOracle(olas, secondToken, 5, 3600, address(vault), poolId);
    }

    function testDoSOnPriceMove() public {
        // Initial state: Price = 1.0
        // Move time forward 10 hours
        vm.warp(block.timestamp + 36000);
        
        // Price moves to 1.2 (20% increase)
        // balanceOut (OLAS) same, balanceIn (Second) increases to 120? 
        // Price = BalanceOut / BalanceIn? No. 
        // getPrice = (balanceOut * 1e18) / balanceIn.
        // If we want Price 1.2: 120e18 / 100e18 = 1.2.
        // Setup balances: Out=120, In=100.
        uint256[] memory b = new uint256[](2);
        b[0] = 120e18;
        b[1] = 100e18;
        vault.setPoolTokens(vault.tokens(), b);
        
        // Attempt update
        bool success = oracle.updatePrice();
        
        // Fails due to Math Logic + Deviation check
        assertFalse(success, "Update should fail due to DoS logic");
        
        // Verify oracle is stale
        (,, uint256 lastUpdated,) = oracle.snapshotHistory();
        assertLt(lastUpdated, block.timestamp, "Oracle should not be updated");
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Correct the TWAP calculation logic to avoid double-counting the elapsed time interval. Specifically, standard TWAP accumulators should track `cumulativePrice += currentPrice * timeElapsed` (or similar depending on assumption) and `average = (cumulativeNow - cumulativeLast) / (timeNow - timeLast)`. 
Additionally, remove or loosen the deviation check in `updatePrice` for the update itself, as a valid TWAP update should be accepted regardless of spot deviation if the time period is valid.
```

### H-117 / `DE0KymPtUkcHUKSHYG-PL`
- Finding title: Oracle Denial of Service due to Lifetime Average Accumulation and Incorrect TWAP Math
- Report lines: 9881-9976
```md
## [H-117]. Oracle Denial of Service due to Lifetime Average Accumulation and Incorrect TWAP Math

## id: DE0KymPtUkcHUKSHYG-PL

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UnincentivizedMaintenanceOrKeeperlessProgress

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
BalancerPriceOracle.updatePrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Both components exist: cumulativePrice monotonically increases across the oracle’s lifetime (no window), and the averaging math is non-standard/double-counting as discussed above. Combined with a hard maxSlippage gate that returns false without advancing lastUpdated/averagePrice, the oracle can fail to update during normal market regime shifts and remain stale for long periods, effectively DoSing dependent logic that expects fresh snapshots. There is no alternative path (e.g., admin force update, window reset, or capped elapsedTime) to ensure progress.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BalancerPriceOracle` contract implements a flawed Time-Weighted Average Price (TWAP) mechanism that renders the oracle permanently unusable over time. Two specific critical issues exist in `updatePrice()`:

1. **Lifetime Accumulation (Staleness):** The oracle accumulates `cumulativePrice` indefinitely since deployment without any windowing mechanism (e.g., a circular buffer). As the contract ages, the accumulated history dominates the calculation. New price updates have a diminishingly small weight, causing the `averagePrice` to 'freeze' at the historical mean. Eventually, legitimate market price movements will deviate from this stiff lifetime average by more than `maxSlippage`, causing `updatePrice()` (and downstream validation) to revert or return false permanently.

2. **Mathematical Double-Counting:** The `updatePrice` function incorrectly accounts for the time interval `elapsedTime` twice. It first adds `averagePrice * elapsedTime` to `cumulativePrice` (implying the price was the *old* average during the interval), and then separately adds `currentPrice * elapsedTime` to the numerator for the *same* interval, while also adding `elapsedTime` to the denominator again. This results in a nonsensical average that dilutes price updates.

Combined, these issues ensure the oracle will inevitably become permanently stuck and unable to update as the asset price evolves.

## Impact
The oracle becomes permanently unresponsive to market price changes (DoS). Downstream contracts relying on `validatePrice` (e.g., `LiquidityManager`) will fail all operations as spot prices drift from the frozen historical average.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `BalancerPriceOracle`. Initialize with a price of 100 (pool ratio 100:100).
2. Simulate 1 year of time passing with stable prices. The `cumulativePrice` variable grows extremely large.
3. Market conditions change: the pool ratio shifts to 200:100 (Price = 200).
4. Call `updatePrice()`. Due to the 1-year accumulated weight in the formula, the new `averagePrice` remains approximately 100.
5. The deviation check compares the new spot price (200) against the lifetime average (~100). Since 200 > 100 + 10% (max slippage), the function returns `false` (or reverts in a revert-based wrapper).
6. The oracle fails to update and remains stuck at the old price.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/oracles/BalancerPriceOracle.sol";

contract MockVault is IVault {
    address[] public tokens;
    uint256[] public balances;
    function setPoolTokens(address[] memory _tokens, uint256[] memory _balances) external {
        tokens = _tokens; balances = _balances;
    }
    function getPoolTokens(bytes32) external view override returns (address[] memory, uint256[] memory, uint256) {
        return (tokens, balances, 0);
    }
}

contract OracleDoSTest is Test {
    MockVault vault;
    BalancerPriceOracle oracle;
    address olas = address(0x1);
    address second = address(0x2);
    bytes32 poolId = bytes32(uint256(1));

    function setUp() public {
        vault = new MockVault();
        address[] memory t = new address[](2); t[0] = olas; t[1] = second;
        uint256[] memory b = new uint256[](2); b[0] = 100e18; b[1] = 100e18;
        vault.setPoolTokens(t, b);
        oracle = new BalancerPriceOracle(olas, second, 10, 60, address(vault), poolId);
    }

    function testLifetimeDoS() public {
        // 1. Build massive history at Price = 1.0
        vm.warp(block.timestamp + 365 days);
        oracle.updatePrice();
        
        // 2. Change market price to 2.0 (set OLAS balance to 200)
        // Note: Contract returns OLAS/Second ratio. 200/100 = 2.0.
        uint256[] memory b = new uint256[](2); b[0] = 200e18; b[1] = 100e18;
        vault.setPoolTokens(vault.tokens(), b);
        
        // 3. Try to update after min period
        vm.warp(block.timestamp + 61);
        bool success = oracle.updatePrice();
        
        // 4. Update fails because lifetime average (~1.0) is too far from current (2.0)
        assertFalse(success, "Oracle stuck due to lifetime average");
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Do not implement a custom lifetime accumulator. Instead, query the Balancer V2 Vault's `getTimeWeightedAverage` function which correctly maintains a rolling window TWAP using circular buffers. If a custom implementation is required, implement a sliding window to prune historical data older than a set period (e.g., 30 minutes).
```

## Description
In `_buyOLAS` (V3 path), the contract calls `ILiquidityManager.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` to validate the pool price *before* the swap. However, it fails to enforce an `amountOutMinimum` or validate the price *after* the swap (as done in the V2 path). While the pre-check prevents start-price manipulation, it does not protect against high price impact (slippage) during the swap itself. If the V3 pool has low liquidity and the contract executes a large buyback (facilitated by the `secondTokenAmount = balance` logic), the swap will execute with potentially massive value loss.

## Impact
Significant loss of funds due to high slippage on large V3 swaps.

## [M-119]. Compounding slippage tolerances in _buyOLAS (V2) allow excessive price impact/sandwiching

## id: MpWKDFcncajD1iPYcoy3j

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
BuyBackBurner._buyOLAS

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: While there are checks (spot-vs-TWAP validation and a post-swap bound vs pre-swap spot), they do not prevent 'tolerance stacking' where the execution can end up materially farther from TWAP than maxSlippage; the safeguards mitigate manipulation but do not invalidate the compounding effect.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `_buyOLAS` (V2 path), the contract enforces slippage by comparing the post-swap `tradePrice` to the pre-swap `previousPrice` using `maxSlippage`. Additionally, `previousPrice` (a spot price) is validated against a TWAP via `validatePrice(maxSlippage)`. 

This creates a compounding tolerance issue: `validatePrice` allows the spot price to deviate by `maxSlippage` from the TWAP, and the swap logic allows the execution price to deviate by another `maxSlippage` from that spot price. 

An attacker can sandwich the transaction by: 
1. Manipulating the pool spot price to the edge of the `validatePrice` tolerance.
2. Allowing the `buyBack` swap to execute, pushing the price further by `maxSlippage`.
3. This results in a total effective deviation of roughly `2 * maxSlippage` (plus compounding) from the fair TWAP price, extracting significant value.

## Impact
Protocol funds leak to MEV/attackers during buybacks due to loose effective slippage bounds.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Assume `maxSlippage` is 10%. TWAP is 100.
2. Attacker manipulates spot price to 110 (allowed by `validatePrice`).
3. Caller executes `buyBack`. `previousPrice` is 110.
4. Swap executes, pushing price to 121.
5. Check `tradePrice (121) <= previousPrice (110) * 1.10 (121)` passes.
6. Protocol executes at ~121, a 21% deviation from fair value 100, despite intended 10% limit.

## Proof of Code
function testSlippageGap() public { 
 // Mock Oracle and Pool behavior setup 
 // Assert that trade executes at price > TWAP * (1 + maxSlippage) 
 }

## Suggested Mitigation
Calculate the acceptable execution price bounds based on the trusted TWAP/Oracle price rather than the manipulatable pre-swap spot price (`previousPrice`).
```
