## [H-120]. MEV Extraction on BuyBackBurner V3 buybacks due to excessive hardcoded slippage tolerance

## id: 9L7DhYsZ0bFs7YkRBOlEn

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
MEV sandwich on loose slippage

## Exploit Type
FrontrunMev

## Location
BuyBackBurner._buyOLAS

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Code path exists: BuyBackBurner._buyOLAS(V3) only calls LiquidityManager.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(pool) and then swaps; it does not use BuyBackBurner.maxSlippage for V3. Moreover, LiquidityManagerCore.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice’s deviation check is effectively broken (spot is overwritten by TWAP before computing deviation), so this “protection” can be bypassed entirely, making MEV even easier than the reported 10% bound. buyBack(V3) is permissionless and swaps the contract’s entire balance, enabling sandwiching whenever balances accumulate. Pool whitelisting limits which pools can be attacked, but does not prevent price manipulation within a whitelisted pool.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurner` contract's `buyBack` function (V3 variant via `_buyOLAS`) relies on `LiquidityManager`'s `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` for slippage/price protection. It does not pass the contract's `maxSlippage` configuration to the check. Instead, `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` enforces a hardcoded `MAX_ALLOWED_DEVIATION` of 10% (`1e17`). 

Since `buyBack` is permissionless and swaps the entire token balance of the contract, this 10% tolerance effectively allows any caller to sandwich the buyback transaction. An attacker can manipulate the pool spot price up to 9.9% away from the TWAP, trigger the buyback (forcing the protocol to buy OLAS at a 9.9% premium), and then backrun to profit. The configured `maxSlippage` in `BuyBackBurner` is ignored for V3 swaps.

## Impact
Systematic leakage of ~10% of all buyback funds to MEV bots/attackers.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. `BuyBackBurner` accumulates 100,000 USDC. 
2. Attacker observes this. 
3. Attacker flashes/swaps in the USDC/OLAS V3 pool to increase OLAS price by 9% relative to TWAP. 
4. Attacker calls `BuyBackBurner.buyBack(USDC, ...)`. 
5. `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` passes (9% < 10%). 
6. Protocol swaps 100,000 USDC for OLAS at the inflated price. 
7. Attacker swaps back, pocketing the price difference.

## Proof of Code
function test_BuyBack_MEV_Leak() public { 
    // Setup BuyBackBurner and mocks... 
    // Set MaxSlippage to 1% in BuyBackBurner (should be ignored) 
    buyBackBurner.changeMaxSlippage(100); 
    
    // Mock Pool with 9% deviation 
    mockPool.setDeviation(900); // 9% 
    
    // Call buyBack 
    buyBackBurner.buyBack(address(token), amount); 
    
    // Assert swap executed despite >1% deviation 
    assertTrue(swapExecuted); 
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Update `BuyBackBurner` to enforce `maxSlippage` in V3 swaps, either by passing it to `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` (if updated) or by performing an explicit `minAmountOut` check or deviation check against `maxSlippage` locally before swapping.
```

```md
## [M-121]. Incorrect slippage math and unit mismatch in V2 buyback path

## id: 4io3fjPNKVI9NBrbcd1xQ

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
IntegerMath

## Exploit Type
IntegerMath

## Location
BuyBackBurner._buyOLAS

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: In BuyBackBurner._buyOLAS(V2), slippage bounds are computed as (previousPrice * (100 ± maxSlippage)) / 100, implying maxSlippage is a percent in [0..100]. However, the codebase also defines MAX_ALLOWED_DEVIATION = 1e17 (10% in 1e18 precision) and passes maxSlippage into IOracle.validatePrice(maxSlippage), suggesting WAD-style values. If maxSlippage is set to 1e17, 100 - maxSlippage underflows and reverts, DoSing buyBack. If set to small ints (e.g., 10), validatePrice may interpret it as ~0 slippage. The mismatch is directly in code.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In the inherited `BuyBackBurner._buyOLAS` function (used by the V2 path), the slippage bounds are calculated as:
`uint256 lowerBound = (previousPrice * (100 - maxSlippage)) / 100;`

This formula assumes `maxSlippage` is an integer percentage (e.g., 5 for 5%). However, `maxSlippage` is also passed to `IOracle(poolOracle).validatePrice(maxSlippage)`. Autonolas oracles typically use WAD (1e18) precision for values. 

If `maxSlippage` is set to a WAD value (e.g., `1e17` for 10%), the calculation `(100 - maxSlippage)` will underflow, reverting the transaction. 
If `maxSlippage` is set to an integer (e.g., `10`), and the Oracle expects WAD, `validatePrice` receives `10 wei`, effectively enforcing zero tolerance, causing legitimate swaps to fail (DoS).

## Impact
DoS of the V2 buyback functionality due to revert (underflow) or impossible slippage constraints.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Initialize `BuyBackBurnerUniswap` with `maxSlippage = 1e17` (10% in WAD).
2. Call `buyBack` for a V2 token.
3. Execution enters `_buyOLAS`.
4. Calculation `100 - 1e17` underflows. Transaction reverts.

## Proof of Code
function testSlippageMath() public {
    uint256 maxSlippage = 1e17; // 10%
    uint256 previousPrice = 1e18;
    // This mimics the vulnerable line
    // unchecked { uint256 val = 100 - maxSlippage; } // reverts in checked mode
    vm.expectRevert();
    uint256 lowerBound = (previousPrice * (100 - maxSlippage)) / 100;
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Standardize units. If `maxSlippage` is WAD, use `(previousPrice * (1e18 - maxSlippage)) / 1e18`. Ensure `validatePrice` receives the correct format expected by the Oracle.
```

## [M-122]. Unlimited slippage due to missing deadline check in liquidity operations

## id: qos1BVx1RuKJ51FmH2-F8

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Value transfers executed without meaningful slippage bounds or time bounds

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.convertToV3

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: Liquidity operations hardcode deadline=block.timestamp, which provides no meaningful time-bound. Additionally, slippage bounds are derived at execution time rather than caller-provided, meaning the operation will generally execute under whatever the current market state is when it lands on-chain (subject only to maxSlippage/oracle logic). This removes standard caller-level protection against delayed execution.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The functions `convertToV3`, `increaseLiquidity`, and `decreaseLiquidity` interact with the Uniswap V3 `PositionManager` and `Router`. These standard interfaces require a `deadline` parameter to ensure transactions are executed promptly or reverted if delayed. The `LiquidityManagerCore` contract hardcodes this parameter to `block.timestamp` (e.g., `deadline: block.timestamp`).

This effectively disables the deadline protection, as `block.timestamp <= block.timestamp` is always true. If the transaction is delayed in the mempool (e.g., due to low gas or network congestion), the market price can drift significantly. Since `amountsMin` is calculated based on the *execution-time* spot price (via `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice`), the owner has no protection against price movements that occur between signing the transaction and its inclusion in a block.

## Impact
Users (Protocol Owner) are exposed to unlimited slippage/price drift, potentially resulting in loss of value.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept

Transaction executes. `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` sees Spot=50, TWAP=50 (since it moved slowly). 5. `amountsMin` is calculated based on Price 50. 6. Liquidity is added at Price 50. Owner intended Price 100.

    // This is structural; demonstrates logic
    // vm.warp to future
    // execute tx
    // verify it succeeds despite delay
}

## Suggested Mitigation

```md
## [M-123]. Liquidity Provision at Manipulated Price via convertToV3

## id: jo0vgC_0_1EY0tFR5bj1d

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
FlashLoanEconomicManipulation

## Exploit Type
FlashLoanEconomicManipulation

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.convertToV3

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: `convertToV3` relies on `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(v3Pool)` and then uses the returned `sqrtP` for tick/amount optimization and minting. Because `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` can fail open (returning manipulable spot price when TWAP retrieval fails) and is otherwise incorrect, an attacker can manipulate the pool price prior to execution, causing the protocol to deposit at an unfavorable price/ratio. The attacker can then arbitrage against the newly provided liquidity. This is owner-only but still MEV-exploitable against predictable governance/keeper transactions.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `LiquidityManagerCore` functions `convertToV3`, `increaseLiquidity`, and `decreaseLiquidity` rely on `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` to validate the pool price before modifying positions. This function allows a spot price deviation of up to 10% from the TWAP. 

The `convertToV3` function uses the current spot price (`sqrtP`) to calculate the optimal tick ranges and token amounts via `neighborhoodScanner`. If the price is manipulated by 9.9%, the contract will calculate liquidity amounts and ranges based on this distorted price and mint the position. 

Although the function is `OwnerOnly`, transactions from the DAO/Timelock are visible in the mempool. An attacker can sandwich these transactions: manipulating the price before execution to force the protocol to provide liquidity at a bad ratio, and then arbitrage against the new liquidity immediately after.

## Impact
Protocol Owned Liquidity is deposited at unfavorable rates, resulting in immediate loss of value to arbitrageurs/MEV bots.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. DAO submits `convertToV3` transaction.
2. Attacker sees tx in mempool.
3. Attacker front-runs: Swaps in the V3 pool to shift price by 9.9%.
4. `convertToV3` executes: `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` passes. `neighborhoodScanner` optimizes amounts for the manipulated price. `mint` adds liquidity at the distorted ratio.
5. Attacker back-runs: Trades against the new liquidity to restore the price, extracting value from the protocol's misplaced liquidity.

## Proof of Code
function testSandwichLiquidityProvision() public {
    // 1. Frontrun: Push price
    uniV3Router.exactInputSingle(params_push_price);
    // 2. Owner action (simulated)
    vm.prank(owner);
    liquidityManager.convertToV3(tokens, 0, fee, ticks, 0, true);
    // 3. Backrun: Arb
    uniV3Router.exactInputSingle(params_arb);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Reduce `MAX_ALLOWED_DEVIATION` to a safer threshold (e.g., 1-2%) for liquidity provision operations, or use the TWAP price directly for calculating optimal amounts/ticks instead of the spot price.
```

### H-124 / `fNbUyjCFM3Ujcm3_tCjND`
- Finding title: Oracle manipulation protection bypassed for mature pools due to inverted logic in checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice
- Report lines: 10297-10404
```md
## [H-124]. Oracle manipulation protection bypassed for mature pools due to inverted logic in checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice

## id: fNbUyjCFM3Ujcm3_tCjND

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
OracleUsingDEXorTWAP

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: The specific 'mature pools always bypass' explanation is incorrect because the code reads observations(observationIndex) where observationIndex is the latest observation index; however, the function still has a real fail-open/bypass condition whenever the latest observation is older than SECONDS_AGO (and it also fails open if observe() fails). These paths can be exploited via price manipulation around owner operations and are not necessarily low impact or rare in low-activity pools.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The function `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` is intended to protect against spot price manipulation by verifying that the spot price does not deviate significantly from the TWAP. However, the logic determining when to perform this check is inverted. 

The code checks `if (oldestTimestamp + SECONDS_AGO < block.timestamp)`. `oldestTimestamp` is the timestamp of the oldest observation in the Uniswap V3 pool. For a mature pool (older than `SECONDS_AGO` = 30 mins), `oldestTimestamp` will be sufficiently old such that `oldestTimestamp + SECONDS_AGO` is indeed less than `block.timestamp`. 

When this condition is true (i.e., for any mature pool with sufficient history), the function effectively returns the spot price (`centerSqrtPriceX96`) immediately, **skipping** the TWAP deviation check. The check is only attempted if the pool is very young, which is the exact opposite of the intended behavior (checking TWAP when sufficient history exists).

Snippet:
```solidity
// Check if the pool had enough activity during last SECONDS_AGO period
if (oldestTimestamp + SECONDS_AGO < block.timestamp) {
    return centerSqrtPriceX96; // @audit-issue Returns spot price immediately for mature pools
}
```

## Impact
This vulnerability completely disables the oracle manipulation protection for all active, mature pools. An attacker can manipulate the pool's spot price (e.g., via flash loan/swap) and trigger liquidity operations (e.g., `convertToV3` or `increaseLiquidity` via the owner). The contract will accept the manipulated spot price to calculate minimum amounts and tick ranges, causing the protocol to mint liquidity at an unfavorable price, leading to immediate arbitrage loss (sandwich attack) and permanent loss of funds.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Assume a mature Uniswap V3 pool exists (age > 30 mins). `oldestTimestamp` is `now - 1 hour`. `SECONDS_AGO` is 1800 (30 mins).
2. Condition `(now - 3600) + 1800 < now` evaluates to `true`.
3. Attacker manipulates the pool price significantly (e.g., +50%).
4. A liquidity operation is triggered (e.g., `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` is called).
5. The function enters the `if` block and returns the manipulated spot price without checking TWAP.
6. The protocol uses this manipulated price for slippage and range calculations.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {LiquidityManagerCore} from "contracts/pol/LiquidityManagerCore.sol";

contract Harness is LiquidityManagerCore {
    constructor() LiquidityManagerCore(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(4), 10) {}
    function _burn(uint256) internal override {}
    function _checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2(address[] memory, bytes32) internal override returns (uint256[] memory) {}
    function _feeAmountTickSpacing(int24) internal override view returns (int24) {}
    function _getV3Pool(address[] memory, int24) internal override view returns (address) {}
    function _mintV3(address[] memory, uint256[] memory, uint256[] memory, int24[] memory, int24, uint160) internal override returns (uint256, uint128, uint256[] memory) {}

    function testCheck(address pool) external returns (uint160) {
        return checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(pool);
    }
}

contract OracleBugTest is Test {
    Harness manager;
    address mockPool = address(0x123);

    function setUp() public {
        manager = new Harness();
    }

    function testOracleBypass() public {
        // Mock Uniswap V3 Pool behavior
        // slot0 returns spot price 100
        vm.mockCall(
            mockPool,
            abi.encodeWithSelector(bytes4(keccak256("slot0()"))),
            abi.encode(uint160(100), int24(0), uint16(1), uint16(100), uint16(100), uint8(0), true)
        );
        // observations returns oldestTimestamp = now - 3600 (Mature pool)
        vm.mockCall(
            mockPool,
            abi.encodeWithSelector(bytes4(keccak256("observations(uint256)")), 1),
            abi.encode(uint32(block.timestamp - 3600), int56(0), uint160(0), true)
        );

        // Call checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice
        // It should SKIP the TWAP check (which would otherwise call 'observe' or staticcall self)
        // We prove this by observing it returns 100 successfully without reverting or checking deviation
        uint160 price = manager.testCheck(mockPool);
        assertEq(price, 100);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Invert the condition logic to ensure TWAP is checked when history is sufficient. 

```solidity
// If the pool does NOT have enough activity (history is too short), use spot.
if (oldestTimestamp + SECONDS_AGO > block.timestamp) {
    return centerSqrtPriceX96;
}
```
```

### H-125 / `5wcAEE1lj6R45Y00XcVkC`
- Finding title: Oracle protection bypass in checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice allows sandwich attacks on new pools
- Report lines: 10405-10499
```md
## [H-125]. Oracle protection bypass in checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice allows sandwich attacks on new pools

## id: 5wcAEE1lj6R45Y00XcVkC

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StaleOracleAcceptance

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice performs a staticcall to getTwapFromOracle (which calls pool.observe). If that call fails (insufficient observation history/cardinality), the function returns the unchecked spot sqrtPrice (centerSqrtPriceX96) instead of reverting. This is a fail-open oracle guard: for new/low-history pools, the contract will accept spot price even though it attempted a TWAP safety check. This can be exploited by MEV around owner liquidity actions on such pools.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `LiquidityManagerCore` contract employs a `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` function to safeguard against spot price manipulation by comparing the current spot price with a Time-Weighted Average Price (TWAP). The function attempts to fetch the TWAP via `getTwapFromOracle`, which executes a static call to the pool's `observe` function requesting data from `SECONDS_AGO` (1800 seconds).

However, the function implements a 'fail-open' mechanism:
```solidity
(bool success, bytes memory returnData) = address(this).staticcall(payload);
// If the call has failed - observe was not successful, meaning the pool has not have enough activity yet
if (!success) {
    return centerSqrtPriceX96;
}
```
If `observe` fails—which typically occurs in new pools or pools where `observationCardinality` has recently been increased but the buffer hasn't filled sufficiently to cover the 30-minute window—the function silently catches the error and returns the unchecked spot price (`centerSqrtPriceX96`).

This vulnerability allows an attacker to sandwich governance/admin transactions (like `convertToV3` or `increaseLiquidity`) on such pools. The attacker can manipulate the spot price immediately before the transaction. The `observe` call fails due to insufficient history, causing the protocol to accept the manipulated spot price as valid. The protocol then calculates `amountMin` based on this manipulated price and adds liquidity at an unfavorable rate, allowing the attacker to arbitrage the difference.

## Impact
Loss of protocol funds due to liquidity provision at manipulated prices (MEV/Sandwich attack).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker observes a pending `convertToV3` transaction for a new or low-activity pool.
2. Attacker front-runs by performing a massive swap to skew the spot price.
3. The `convertToV3` transaction executes. `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` calls `observe`, which reverts (due to low cardinality/insufficient history).
4. The function catches the revert and returns the manipulated spot price.
5. `convertToV3` calculates `amountMin` using the manipulated price and mints liquidity.
6. Attacker back-runs to reverse the swap, profiting from the mispriced liquidity addition.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {LiquidityManagerCore} from "../contracts/pol/LiquidityManagerCore.sol";

contract MockPool {
    uint160 public sqrtPriceX96 = 1000;
    function slot0() external view returns (uint160, int24, uint16, uint16, uint16, uint8, bool) {
        return (sqrtPriceX96, 0, 0, 1, 1, 0, true);
    }
    function observations(uint256) external view returns (uint32, int56, uint160, bool) {
        return (uint32(block.timestamp), 0, 0, true);
    }
    function observe(uint32[] calldata) external view returns (int56[] memory, uint160[] memory) {
        revert("Not enough history");
    }
}

contract ConcreteLiquidityManager is LiquidityManagerCore {
    constructor() LiquidityManagerCore(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(4), 1) {}
    function _burn(uint256) internal override {}
    function _checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2(address[] memory, bytes32) internal override returns (uint256[] memory) {}
    function _feeAmountTickSpacing(int24) internal override view returns (int24) {}
    function _getV3Pool(address[] memory, int24) internal override view returns (address) {}
    function _mintV3(address[] memory, uint256[] memory, uint256[] memory, int24[] memory, int24, uint160) internal override returns (uint256, uint128, uint256[] memory) {}
}

contract OracleBypassTest is Test {
    ConcreteLiquidityManager manager;
    MockPool pool;

    function setUp() public {
        manager = new ConcreteLiquidityManager();
        pool = new MockPool();
    }

    function testOracleBypass() public {
        // Even though observe reverts, the manager should return the spot price
        uint160 price = manager.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(address(pool));
        assertEq(price, 1000, "Should return spot price on observe failure");
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Do not fail open. If `observe` fails, the transaction should revert to prevent operations on a pool with insufficient oracle history. Alternatively, require a manual override flag for new pools.
```

### H-126 / `9-AwQGUwFghK560RFl_74`
- Finding title: Broken Price Deviation Check in checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice due to Variable Shadowing
- Report lines: 10500-10605
```md
## [H-126]. Broken Price Deviation Check in checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice due to Variable Shadowing

## id: 9-AwQGUwFghK560RFl_74

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Oracle Price Manipulation

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: In checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(), centerSqrtPriceX96 is first set to the spot sqrt price from slot0. Later, decoding the TWAP result overwrites the same variable: (twapPrice, centerSqrtPriceX96) = abi.decode(...). The subsequent instantPrice computation uses this overwritten TWAP sqrt price, so instantPrice ~= twapPrice and computed deviation becomes ~0 regardless of actual spot-vs-TWAP difference. This breaks the intended manipulation guard and can allow heavily manipulated spot prices to pass the deviation check during owner-triggered liquidity operations, enabling MEV/sandwich value extraction.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `LiquidityManagerCore.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice`, the function is designed to prevent liquidity operations when the pool's Spot price deviates significantly from the TWAP price. However, a critical bug exists where the local variable `centerSqrtPriceX96` (initially holding the Spot price) is overwritten by the TWAP sqrt price returned from `getTwapFromOracle` due to variable shadowing in the return tuple decoding.

Vulnerable snippet:
```solidity
(twapPrice, centerSqrtPriceX96) = abi.decode(returnData, (uint256, uint160));
```

Subsequently, `instantPrice` is calculated using this overwritten `centerSqrtPriceX96` (now TWAP), resulting in the function comparing `TWAP` against `TWAP`. The calculated deviation is always near zero, completely bypassing the slippage/manipulation protection. This allows an attacker to sandwich owner transactions (e.g., `convertToV3`, `increaseLiquidity`) even when the pool price is heavily manipulated.

## Impact
Bypasses critical slippage/manipulation protection, allowing MEV bots or attackers to sandwich protocol liquidity operations, leading to significant loss of Protocol Owned Liquidity.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker observes a pending `increaseLiquidity` transaction from the protocol owner.
2. Attacker manipulates the Uniswap V3 pool price (Spot) significantly away from the TWAP (e.g., 50% deviation) via a flash loan.
3. The owner's transaction executes `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice`. Due to the shadowing bug, the function compares the TWAP-derived price against the TWAP-derived price, calculating 0 deviation and passing the check.
4. The protocol mints liquidity at the manipulated Spot price, resulting in a suboptimal position or immediate value loss.
5. Attacker back-runs the transaction to profit from the price arbitrage.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/pol/LiquidityManagerCore.sol";

// Mock concrete implementation
contract MockManager is LiquidityManagerCore {
    constructor() LiquidityManagerCore(address(1), address(1), address(1), address(1), 1) {}
    function _burn(uint256) internal override {}
    function _checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2(address[] memory, bytes32) internal override returns (uint256[] memory) {}
    function _feeAmountTickSpacing(int24) internal view override returns (int24) { return 60; }
    function _getV3Pool(address[] memory, int24) internal view override returns (address) { return address(0); }
    function _mintV3(address[] memory, uint256[] memory, uint256[] memory, int24[] memory, int24, uint160) internal override returns (uint256, uint128, uint256[] memory) {}
}

contract BrokenCheckTest is Test {
    MockManager manager;
    address mockPool = address(0x123);

    function setUp() public {
        manager = new MockManager();
    }

    function test_ExploitDeviation() public {
        // 1. Mock Slot0 to return a manipulated price (e.g. 2x price => sqrt(2) * 2^96)
        // sqrt(2) * 2^96 approx 112045541949572279837463876454
        uint160 spotSqrtP = 112045541949572279837463876454;
        bytes memory slot0Data = abi.encode(spotSqrtP, int24(0), uint16(0), uint16(1), uint16(1), uint8(0), true);
        vm.mockCall(mockPool, abi.encodeWithSelector(IUniswapV3.slot0.selector), slot0Data);

        // 2. Mock observations to return current timestamp (active pool)
        bytes memory obsData = abi.encode(uint32(block.timestamp), int56(0), uint160(0), true);
        vm.mockCall(mockPool, abi.encodeWithSelector(IUniswapV3.observations.selector, 0), obsData);

        // 3. Mock getTwapFromOracle to return Normal price (e.g. 1.0 => 2^96)
        // This mimics the internal call behavior
        uint160 twapSqrtP = 79228162514264337593543950336;
        uint256 twapPrice = 1e18;
        vm.mockCall(
            address(manager),
            abi.encodeWithSelector(LiquidityManagerCore.getTwapFromOracle.selector, mockPool),
            abi.encode(twapPrice, twapSqrtP)
        );

        // 4. Execution
        // Ideally should revert because Spot (2.0) deviates > 10% from TWAP (1.0)
        // But it returns the TWAP sqrtPrice due to the bug
        uint160 result = manager.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice(mockPool);
        
        assertEq(result, twapSqrtP, "Should return TWAP sqrtPrice due to overwrite");
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use a distinct variable name when decoding the return values from `getTwapFromOracle` to avoid overwriting `centerSqrtPriceX96`. 

```diff
- (twapPrice, centerSqrtPriceX96) = abi.decode(returnData, (uint256, uint160));
+ (twapPrice, uint160 twapSqrtPriceX96) = abi.decode(returnData, (uint256, uint160));
+ centerSqrtPriceX96 = twapSqrtPriceX96;
// Note: Ensure `instantPrice` is calculated using the ORIGINAL centerSqrtPriceX96 (Spot) before updating it, or maintain separate variables for Spot and TWAP prices for comparison.
```
```

### M-127 / `cLFoV-3eONgdRdPLXkcJm`
- Finding title: Hardcoded 10% price deviation tolerance enables sandwich attacks on PoL operations

```md
## [M-127]. Hardcoded 10% price deviation tolerance enables sandwich attacks on PoL operations

## id: cLFoV-3eONgdRdPLXkcJm

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice

## Finding Status: Valid
### Finding Status Justification: `MAX_ALLOWED_DEVIATION` is hardcoded to 10% in `LiquidityManagerCore`. Even if the TWAP/spot comparison were implemented correctly, allowing up to ~10% deviation is wide enough for profitable sandwich/MEV extraction against large protocol-owned-liquidity operations, especially since min-amounts are computed from spot. In the current codebase, the check is additionally flawed/fail-open, which further worsens the situation. This is an on-chain economic attack surface (not just configuration), though frequency depends on when large operations are executed.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `LiquidityManagerCore` contract enforces a hardcoded `MAX_ALLOWED_DEVIATION` of 10% (`1e17`) in `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice`. This function validates that the spot price does not deviate from the TWAP by more than 10%. PoL operations like `increaseLiquidity`, `decreaseLiquidity`, and `convertToV3` rely on the spot price (checked against this loose bound) to calculate `amountsMin` for slippage protection. Since 10% is a very wide margin for stable or correlated pairs, and owner transactions (often via Timelock) are predictable, an attacker can front-run the transaction to skew the pool price by up to ~9.9%. The protocol then adds or removes liquidity at this manipulated price, suffering immediate value loss (impermanent loss realized) which the attacker captures via back-running. The `maxSlippage` parameter only protects against price movement *between* the check and execution, not against the manipulated spot price itself being accepted as the baseline.

## Impact
Loss of Protocol Owned Liquidity value up to ~10% per operation due to sandwich attacks.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Owner schedules `increaseLiquidity` for a large PoL position in a Uniswap V3 pool.
2. Attacker detects the transaction in the mempool (or Timelock queue).
3. Attacker front-runs with a large swap, shifting the spot price by 9%.
4. `LiquidityManager` executes: `checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice` sees deviation < 10% and passes. It calculates `amountsMin` based on the *manipulated* spot price.
5. Liquidity is added at the manipulated ratio.
6. Attacker back-runs, reversing the swap and arbitraging the pool, extracting value from the liquidity added at the bad price.

## Proof of Code
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
// Pseudocode for PoC logic
contract SandwichTest is Test {
    function testSandwichPoL() public {
        // Setup pool with price 1000
        // Attacker swaps to price 1090 (9% deviation)
        // Owner calls increaseLiquidity()
        //   -> checkPoolAndGetCenterPrice() passes (9% < 10%)
        //   -> calculates liquidity amounts based on 1090 price
        //   -> adds liquidity
        // Attacker swaps back to 1000
        // Protocol position is now composed of tokens valued at 1090, suffering immediate loss when pool returns to 1000.
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Lower `MAX_ALLOWED_DEVIATION` significantly for stable pairs or allow it to be passed as a parameter. Ideally, calculate liquidity amounts and `amountsMin` using the Oracle TWAP price rather than the Spot price, or allow the owner to explicitly define `minAmounts` based on off-chain calculation.





Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase

### M-128 / `Cw_aTD49NUl9aQ1d5kKYs`
- Finding title: DoS and stuck funds with USDT due to incorrect IERC20 interface

```md
## [M-128]. DoS and stuck funds with USDT due to incorrect IERC20 interface

## id: Cw_aTD49NUl9aQ1d5kKYs

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UncheckedReturn

## Exploit Type
UncheckedReturn

## Location
BuyBackBurnerUniswap.transfer

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: Matches the code: BuyBackBurnerUniswap calls IERC20(token).approve(...) where IERC20.approve returns (bool), and BuyBackBurner.transfer calls IERC20(token).transfer(...) where transfer returns (bool). USDT-style tokens that return no data will cause ABI decoding revert. This makes both buyBack (approve step) and sweeping transfer (transfer step) unusable for USDT, effectively locking it in the contract until an upgrade. There is no SafeERC20 wrapper or low-level call path to tolerate missing return data.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The contract uses a standard `IERC20` interface which defines `approve` and `transfer` as returning a `bool`. However, USDT (and some other legacy tokens) does not return a value. When `BuyBackBurnerUniswap` calls `approve` in `_performSwap` or `transfer` in `transfer` (for sweeping), the EVM expects a boolean return value. Since USDT returns nothing (void), the ABI decoding fails, causing the transaction to revert.

`IERC20(secondToken).approve(router, secondTokenAmount);`
`IERC20(token).transfer(treasury, tokenAmount);`

## Impact
Medium. The `buyBack` function will permanently revert for USDT, preventing the protocol from processing one of the most common stablecoins. Additionally, any USDT held by the contract cannot be swept to the treasury using `transfer`, leading to stuck funds (until contract implementation is upgraded).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `BuyBackBurnerUniswap`.
2. Send USDT to the contract.
3. Call `transfer(usdtAddress)` to sweep funds to treasury.
4. The call reverts because `IERC20(token).transfer` expects a bool return, but USDT implementation returns void.
5. Call `buyBack(usdtAddress, ...)`.
6. The call reverts at `IERC20(secondToken).approve(...)` for the same reason.

## Proof of Code
function testUSDTRevert() public {
    // Mock USDT with no return value
    MockERC20NoReturn usdt = new MockERC20NoReturn("USDT", "USDT", 6);
    usdt.mint(address(buyBack), 1000e6);
    
    // Attempt transfer
    vm.expectRevert(); // Reverts due to ABI decoding error
    buyBack.transfer(address(usdt));
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use OpenZeppelin's `SafeERC20` library or Solmate's `SafeTransferLib` for all ERC20 interactions (`approve` and `transfer`). These libraries correctly handle tokens that do not return a value.
```

### M-129 / `ggVlAIs4QlTKAmdAw0JTV`
- Finding title: DoS with USDT-like tokens due to unsafe approve usage in LiquidityManagerCore

```md
## [M-129]. DoS with USDT-like tokens due to unsafe approve usage in LiquidityManagerCore

## id: ggVlAIs4QlTKAmdAw0JTV

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Approve Race Condition / Token Standards

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.increaseLiquidity

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: LiquidityManagerCore.convertToV3/changeRanges/increaseLiquidity call IToken(token).approve(positionManagerV3, amount) without first resetting allowance to 0. For tokens enforcing “set-to-zero-first” (USDT-like), this can revert on subsequent operations if any non-zero allowance remains. Because Uniswap/Slipstream mint/increase commonly spends < approved amount (rounding/ticks), residual allowance is realistic, causing repeated management calls to revert. No force-approve/safeApprove helper is used.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `LiquidityManagerCore` contract uses `IToken(token).approve(spender, amount)` in `convertToV3`, `changeRanges`, and `increaseLiquidity`. Some ERC20 tokens (e.g., USDT) revert if `approve` is called with a non-zero value when the current allowance is already non-zero. 

Uniswap V3's `PositionManager` pulls only the tokens required for the liquidity mint, which due to rounding and price slippage, is often slightly less than the `amountDesired` approved by `LiquidityManager`. This leaves a dust allowance on the token contract. Subsequent calls to `approve` will then revert, permanently preventing further liquidity management for that token pair.

## Impact
Permanent Denial of Service for liquidity management of USDT or similar tokens after the first successful transaction leaves dust allowance.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. `LiquidityManager` holds USDT.
2. Owner calls `increaseLiquidity` with `amountDesired`.
3. Contract approves `PositionManager` for `amountDesired`.
4. `PositionManager` uses slightly less than `amountDesired` due to tick rounding.
5. Allowance remains > 0.
6. Owner calls `increaseLiquidity` again.
7. `USDT.approve` reverts because current allowance is not 0.

## Proof of Code
function testUnsafeApprove() public {
    // Mock USDT behavior
    vm.mockCall(
        address(usdt),
        abi.encodeWithSelector(IERC20.approve.selector),
        abi.encode(false) // Reverts or returns false on non-zero to non-zero
    );
    // Simulation logic would show revert on second call
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeTransferLib.safeApprove` (if available and handles reset) or explicitly set allowance to 0 before setting the new value: `IToken(token).approve(spender, 0); IToken(token).approve(spender, amount);`.
```

```md
## [M-130]. Incompatibility with non-standard ERC20 tokens (USDT) causes permanent fund lockup

## id: wxjvOeK3hy-l3Ob9w0CPK

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UncheckedReturn

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
BuyBackBurnerUniswap.buyBack / transfer

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: In BuyBackBurnerUniswap, IERC20.approve is declared as returning (bool) and called via high-level Solidity call: IERC20(secondToken).approve(...). In BuyBackBurner, IERC20.transfer is declared as returning (bool) and called via IERC20(token).transfer(...) and IERC20(olas).transfer(...). For tokens like USDT that return no data for approve/transfer, Solidity’s ABI decoding for a typed return will revert. This breaks buyBack (approve) and transfer sweeping (transfer), potentially locking such tokens in the contract until an upgrade changes the call pattern (e.g., SafeERC20/low-level call).
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurnerUniswap` and `BuyBackBurner` contracts define the `IERC20` interface with `approve` and `transfer` functions returning a `bool`. 
```solidity
interface IERC20 {
    function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) external returns (bool);
    function transfer(address to, uint256 amount) external returns (bool);
}
```
However, major tokens like USDT (on Ethereum Mainnet) do not return a boolean value (they return void). Since Solidity 0.8.x, calls to external functions enforce a return data size check. If the interface expects a return value (bool) but the token returns nothing, the transaction will revert.

This affects both the `buyBack` function (which calls `approve`) and the `transfer` function (which calls `transfer`). Consequently, any USDT collected by the protocol cannot be swapped for OLAS nor swept to the treasury; the funds are functionally stuck until the contract implementation is upgraded.

## Impact
Permanent lockup of USDT and other non-compliant ERC20 tokens collected as fees. While upgradeable, this constitutes a valid denial of service for supported assets.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. `BuyBackBurnerUniswap` holds 1000 USDT.
2. Anyone calls `buyBack(USDT, ...)` or `transfer(USDT)`.
3. The contract calls `USDT.approve()` or `USDT.transfer()`.
4. USDT executes the logic but returns no data.
5. The EVM/Solidity runtime checks for return data, finds empty buffer, and reverts the transaction.

## Proof of Code
/* 
// Mock USDT with no return
contract MockUSDT {
    function transfer(address, uint) external {}
    function approve(address, uint) external {}
}

function testUSDTStuck() public {
    MockUSDT usdt = new MockUSDT();
    // ... fund burner ...
    vm.expectRevert(); // Reverts due to missing return data
    burner.transfer(address(usdt));
}
*/

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeERC20` library from OpenZeppelin or Solmate's `SafeTransferLib` for all ERC20 interactions. Alternatively, modify the `IERC20` interface to not expect a return value and use low-level calls or relaxed interfaces.
```

## [M-131]. Permanent DoS for USDT pairs due to unhandled approval race condition

## id: rZmbzXQXG8-5M2pVmLGoM

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StandardViolation

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
LiquidityManagerOptimism.convertToV3

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: In LiquidityManagerCore.convertToV3 the contract approves token0/token1 amounts directly for positionManagerV3 without zero-reset. If the token reverts on non-zero-to-non-zero allowance updates (USDT-like), subsequent convertToV3 calls for that token can revert when any allowance dust remains from earlier mint/increase operations.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
In `convertToV3`, the contract calls `IToken(token).approve(positionManagerV3, amount)` for both tokens involved in the V3 position. The `amount` approved is the current balance of the contract. Popular tokens like USDT revert if `approve` is called with a non-zero value when the current allowance is already non-zero.

Uniswap V3's `mint` function typically consumes only the amount of tokens required for the liquidity position, which often differs from the provided `amountDesired` (the full balance) due to tick rounding and price slippage. This leaves a residual non-zero allowance on the `positionManagerV3`. When `convertToV3` is called again for the same token pair (e.g., to add more liquidity later), the `approve` call attempts to overwrite the non-zero allowance with a new non-zero value, causing USDT to revert.

## Impact
The `convertToV3` function becomes permanently unusable for USDT pairs after the first successful interaction, preventing liquidity management.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Protocol holds USDT and WETH.
2. Owner calls `convertToV3`. `approve(manager, 1000 USDT)` is called.
3. `manager.mint(...)` consumes only 900 USDT due to tick alignment.
4. Allowance for `manager` remains at 100 USDT.
5. Later, owner calls `convertToV3` again with new funds.
6. Contract calls `approve(manager, 500 USDT)`.
7. USDT contract reverts because `currentAllowance` (100) is > 0.

## Proof of Code
function testUSDTDoS() public {
    address usdt = address(new MockUSDT()); // Reverts on approve change
    // ... setup manager ...
    
    vm.startPrank(owner);
    liquidityManager.convertToV3([usdt, weth], ...);
    // First call succeeds but leaves allowance

    vm.expectRevert("Approve fail");
    liquidityManager.convertToV3([usdt, weth], ...);
    vm.stopPrank();
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeTransferLib.safeApprove` (if available and compatible) or explicitly set the allowance to 0 before setting the new amount: `IToken(token).approve(spender, 0); IToken(token).approve(spender, amount);`.
```

### M-132 / `qe-v6vOLtuHsv8brub-Le`

## [M-132]. Incompatibility with USDT and non-standard ERC20s causes Denial of Service in transferTokens

## id: qe-v6vOLtuHsv8brub-Le

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
NonStandardERC20Behavior

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
WormholeRelayerTimelock.transferTokens

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: Calling `IERC20(token).approve(...)` with a USDT-like token will revert deterministically due to missing return data; this is a contract-level ERC20-compatibility bug, not a user/admin mistake. While only the timelock can trigger it, the failure is not the result of privileged misuse—it's inherent to the implementation when interacting with widely-used nonstandard tokens.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `transferTokens` function approves the `wormholeTokenBridge` to spend tokens using the `IERC20` interface, which defines `approve` as returning a `bool`. However, commonly used tokens like USDT (on Ethereum Mainnet) do not return a boolean value (they return `void`). When `IERC20(token).approve(...)` is executed, the Solidity runtime verifies the returned data size. Since USDT returns 0 bytes instead of 32 bytes, the check fails, and the transaction reverts. This renders the contract incapable of bridging USDT or any other non-standard ERC20 token that deviates from the return value specification, despite USDT being an in-scope asset.

## Impact
The Governance/Timelock is permanently unable to bridge supported assets like USDT via this contract. Since the contract is immutable, a new contract must be deployed to restore this functionality.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `WormholeRelayerTimelock`. 
2. Deploy a Mock USDT contract that has an `approve` function which updates allowance but returns nothing (void). 
3. Impersonate the Timelock address. 
4. Call `transferTokens` passing the Mock USDT address. 
5. The transaction reverts due to the return data size check failure in the high-level `approve` call.

## Proof of Code
interface IMockUSDT { function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) external; }
contract MockUSDT { function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) external {} }

contract TestUSDTDoS is Test {
    WormholeRelayerTimelock relayer;
    MockUSDT usdt;
    address timelock = makeAddr("timelock");
    address wormholeCore = makeAddr("core");
    address wormholeRelayer = makeAddr("relayer");
    address wormholeTokenBridge = makeAddr("bridge");
    uint16 refundChain = 1;

    function setUp() public {
        vm.etch(wormholeCore, hex"00"); // Mock code
        vm.mockCall(wormholeCore, abi.encodeWithSignature("messageFee()"), abi.encode(0));
        relayer = new WormholeRelayerTimelock(timelock, wormholeCore, wormholeRelayer, wormholeTokenBridge, refundChain);
        usdt = new MockUSDT();
    }

    function testTransferTokensRevertsOnNoReturn() public {
        vm.prank(timelock);
        // Expect revert due to missing boolean return value
        vm.expectRevert(); 
        relayer.transferTokens(address(usdt), 100, 2, bytes32(uint256(1)), address(1));
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use OpenZeppelin's `SafeERC20` library for approvals, or use a low-level call to `approve` and validate that the call succeeded (and if data is returned, that it decodes to true).
```

### H-133 / `7XjuqUZkfoH2bEWB21XQI`

## [H-133]. Incompatibility with USDT leads to permanent fund loss in WormholeRelayerTimelock

## id: 7XjuqUZkfoH2bEWB21XQI

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
NonStandardERC20Behavior

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
WormholeRelayerTimelock.transferTokens

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: If USDT-like tokens are ever used, the funds-lock is a deterministic consequence of (1) the strict high-level `approve` expecting a bool and (2) the absence of any rescue/withdraw path; this can occur even when following the contract’s own workflow (“transfer tokens to this contract prior to calling `transferTokens`”). Therefore it is not properly characterized as mere user/admin mistake or governance misconfiguration.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `WormholeRelayerTimelock.transferTokens` function calls `IERC20(token).approve(...)`, expecting a `bool` return value as defined in the interface. However, USDT (and other non-standard ERC20 tokens) has an `approve` function that returns `void`. In Solidity 0.8.30, calling a function that returns fewer bytes than expected causes the transaction to revert. Since the `transferTokens` workflow requires tokens to be transferred to the contract *before* execution, and there is no mechanism to withdraw tokens other than successfully executing `transferTokens`, any USDT sent to this contract by the Timelock for bridging will be permanently stuck.

## Impact
Permanent loss of funds (USDT) sent to the contract for bridging, as the bridging function will always revert.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `WormholeRelayerTimelock` and a Mock USDT contract that does not return a boolean on `approve`.
2. The Timelock (DAO) sends USDT to the `WormholeRelayerTimelock` contract, intending to bridge it.
3. The Timelock calls `transferTokens`.
4. The call reverts due to the return data size mismatch in `approve`.
5. Since the contract has no rescue function and `transferTokens` is permanently broken for USDT, the funds are locked forever.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/bridges/WormholeRelayerTimelock.sol";

contract MockUSDT {
    function approve(address, uint256) external { /* returns void */ }
}

contract MockWormhole {
    function messageFee() external pure returns (uint256) { return 0; }
    function transferTokens(address,uint256,uint16,bytes32,uint256,uint32) external payable returns (uint64) { return 0; }
}

contract WormholeRelayerTimelockTest is Test {
    WormholeRelayerTimelock relayer;
    MockUSDT usdt;
    MockWormhole wormhole;
    address timelock = address(0x123);

    function setUp() public {
        usdt = new MockUSDT();
        wormhole = new MockWormhole();
        vm.etch(timelock, address(1).code);
        relayer = new WormholeRelayerTimelock(timelock, address(wormhole), address(wormhole), address(wormhole), 1);
    }

    function test_USDT_Revert() public {
        vm.prank(timelock);
        vm.expectRevert(); // Reverts because USDT approve returns void
        relayer.transferTokens(address(usdt), 100, 1, bytes32(uint256(1)), timelock);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use OpenZeppelin's `SafeERC20` library for approvals, or use a low-level call to `approve` and ignore the return data if the call is successful.
```

## [M-134]. Permanent DoS on USDT pools due to unhandled approval behavior

## id: 7zQqYCZBOblhnxthPypM-

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore._increaseLiquidity

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: The contract repeatedly calls IToken(token).approve(spender, amount) without first resetting allowance to 0. Tokens that enforce the “set allowance to 0 before changing from non-zero” rule (USDT-style) can revert when a non-zero residual allowance remains. Because Uniswap position operations can consume slightly less than approved, a dust allowance can persist, making later approvals revert and breaking future liquidity operations for that token. No allowance-reset or SafeTransferLib approval helper is used.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The contract uses the standard `IToken(token).approve(spender, amount)` call in functions `convertToV3`, `changeRanges`, and `increaseLiquidity`. Tokens like USDT (and others compliant with the approval race condition protection pattern) revert if `approve` is called with a non-zero amount when the current allowance is already non-zero.

When the `LiquidityManager` adds liquidity to Uniswap V3, it approves the calculated `amounts`. The `positionManagerV3` pulls the required tokens, but due to slippage or exact math, it often pulls slightly less than the fully approved amount, leaving a 'dust' allowance. 

In subsequent calls to add liquidity or rebalance, the contract attempts to `approve` a new amount. Since the previous allowance is non-zero (dust), the `approve` call will revert for USDT, permanently bricking the management functionality for that pool.

## Impact
The contract becomes unusable for managing liquidity of USDT pairs (or other tokens with safe approval logic) after the first interaction, locking operations.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Initialize `LiquidityManager` with a USDT/OLAS pool.
2. Owner calls `convertToV3` to add liquidity. 
3. Contract approves `PositionManager` for 1000 USDT.
4. `PositionManager` consumes 999 USDT for the position.
5. Allowance remains at 1 USDT.
6. Later, Owner calls `increaseLiquidity` (or `convertToV3` again if adding more).
7. Contract attempts `IToken(USDT).approve(PositionManager, 2000 USDT)`.
8. USDT contract checks allowance (1) and new amount (2000). Reverts because allowance is not 0.
9. Transaction fails.

## Proof of Code
        // Mock test logic
        function testUSDTApprovalDoS() public {
            // 1. Approve 100
            token.approve(spender, 100);
            // 2. Spend 50
            vm.prank(spender); 
            token.transferFrom(address(this), address(0), 50);
            // 3. Try to approve 200 -> Reverts for USDT mock
            vm.expectRevert("Approve 0 first");
            token.approve(spender, 200);
        }

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeTransferLib.safeApprove` (if available and handles this) or explicitly reset allowance to 0 before setting the new amount: `IToken(token).approve(spender, 0); IToken(token).approve(spender, amount);`.
```

StandardViolation

StandardViolation

BuyBackBurnerUniswap._performSwap

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: BuyBackBurnerUniswap’s IERC20.approve is typed to return bool and is called directly in _performSwap. For USDT-like tokens that return no value, the call will revert due to return-data decoding expectations. This denies buyBack execution for such tokens and, combined with BuyBackBurner.transfer using bool-return transfer, can lock those tokens in the contract until an upgrade.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurnerUniswap` contract declares the `IERC20` interface for `approve` as returning a `bool`. Standard ERC20 tokens return a boolean, but many popular tokens (most notably USDT on Mainnet) do not return any value. When the contract calls `approve` on such a token, the Solidity runtime expects a return value; finding none, it reverts the transaction. Since USDT is a high-volume asset likely to be collected for buybacks, this effectively denies service for USDT buybacks.

## Impact
Buyback functionality is permanently broken for USDT and other non-standard ERC20 tokens, locking these assets in the contract or forcing manual admin intervention via `transfer`.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `BuyBackBurnerUniswap`.
2. Send USDT to the contract.
3. Call `buyBack(USDT_ADDRESS, amount)`.
4. `_performSwap` calls `IERC20(USDT).approve(...)`.
5. The transaction reverts because USDT's `approve` function does not return a boolean, causing a decoding error.

## Proof of Code
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/utils/BuyBackBurnerUniswap.sol";

contract MockUSDT {
    // No return value, like real USDT
    function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) external {
        // do nothing
    }
}

contract USDTTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerUniswap burner;
    MockUSDT usdt;

    function setUp() public {
        usdt = new MockUSDT();
        // Deploy burner with dummy args
        burner = new BuyBackBurnerUniswap(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(4));
    }

    function testApproveReverts() public {
        // We expose the internal approve call via buyBack or test harness.
        // Simulating the call made by burner:
        address target = address(usdt);
        
        // This mimics the burner's interface definition
        // interface IERC20 { function approve(address, uint256) external returns (bool); }
        
        vm.expectRevert(); // Expect revert due to missing return data
        IERC20(target).approve(address(this), 100);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use OpenZeppelin's `SafeERC20` library for approvals, or implement a safe approval wrapper that handles tokens with missing return values.
```

The `BuyBackBurner` contract uses the `IERC20` interface which defines `transfer` as returning a `bool` (line 12). However, major tokens like USDT do not return a boolean value (they return void). When `transfer(address token)` (line 274) or `buyBack` (line 247) is called with USDT, the transaction will revert due to Solidity's return data size validation failure. This causes funds to be stuck if USDT is sent to the contract. Additionally, for tokens that return `false` on failure (e.g., EURS) instead of reverting, the contract ignores the return value (line 346: `IERC20(token).transfer(treasury, tokenAmount);`), emitting a `TokenTransferred` event even when the transfer failed, leading to state inconsistencies.

## [M-139]. BuyBackBurner transfer function is incompatible with USDT causing stuck funds

## id: QzuaCrpKxq5HsNfwRdhfx

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
NonStandardERC20Behavior

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
BuyBackBurner.transfer

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: USDT incompatibility stems from unsafe ERC20 handling (expecting a boolean return). Given USDT’s prevalence, funds can become stuck without requiring a special 'user mistake' beyond interacting with a common token; using SafeERC20 would prevent this class.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `BuyBackBurner` contract manually defines the `IERC20` interface with `function transfer(address to, uint256 amount) external returns (bool);`. This interface enforces that the called function must return a boolean value. However, the USDT token contract on Ethereum Mainnet (and other non-compliant tokens) does not return any value from its `transfer` function. When `BuyBackBurner.transfer` is called for such tokens, the Solidity 0.8.x compiled code checks for the presence of returned data (expecting 32 bytes for `bool`). Since USDT returns no data, this check fails, causing the transaction to revert. This renders the `transfer` function unusable for sweeping USDT funds accumulated in the contract (e.g. from fees/donations), causing them to be stuck until the implementation is upgraded.

## Impact
Protocol fees or funds in USDT (a major stablecoin) cannot be swept to the treasury, leading to temporarily stuck funds.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy a MockUSDT contract that mimics mainnet USDT (no return value on transfer).
2. Deploy a harness contract inheriting `BuyBackBurner`.
3. Send MockUSDT to the harness contract (simulating revenue).
4. Call `transfer(address(mockUSDT))` on the harness.
5. The transaction reverts due to the return data size check failure, demonstrating that funds cannot be swept.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.30;

import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "contracts/utils/BuyBackBurner.sol";

// Mock USDT that returns nothing
contract MockUSDT {
    mapping(address => uint256) public balanceOf;
    function transfer(address to, uint256 amount) external {
        balanceOf[msg.sender] -= amount;
        balanceOf[to] += amount;
    }
    function mint(address to, uint256 amount) external {
        balanceOf[to] += amount;
    }
}

// Concrete harness since BuyBackBurner is abstract
contract BuyBackBurnerHarness is BuyBackBurner {
    constructor(address l, address b, address t, address s) BuyBackBurner(l, b, t, s) {}
    function _initialize(bytes memory) internal override {}
    function _performSwap(address, uint256, address) internal override returns (uint256) { return 0; }
    function _performSwap(address, uint256, int24) internal override returns (uint256) { return 0; }
    function getV3Pool(address, address[] memory, int24) public view override returns (address) { return address(0); }
}

contract BuyBackBurnerUSDTTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerHarness burner;
    MockUSDT usdt;
    address treasury = address(0x1);
    address bridge = address(0x2);

    function setUp() public {
        usdt = new MockUSDT();
        burner = new BuyBackBurnerHarness(address(0x3), bridge, treasury, address(0x4));
        usdt.mint(address(burner), 1000);
    }

    function testTransferUSDTFails() public {
        // This should fail because interface expects bool but MockUSDT returns void
        vm.expectRevert(); 
        burner.transfer(address(usdt));
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use the `SafeERC20` library from OpenZeppelin or `SafeTransferLib` from Solmate (which is included in the project dependencies) to handle ERC20 `transfer` calls. These libraries handle non-compliant tokens that do not return a boolean.
```

## [M-142]. Incompatibility with USDT due to unchecked return value in `IToken` interface

## id: JI6HNvpppRzYZ729aUL8t

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: The project’s IToken interface defines approve(...) returns (bool), and LiquidityManagerCore calls IToken(token).approve(...) in convertToV3/changeRanges/increaseLiquidity. Tokens like mainnet USDT implement approve with no return data (void). Calling via an interface expecting a bool can revert due to ABI return-data decoding size mismatch. There is no SafeTransferLib.safeApprove-like wrapper or low-level call handling to accept empty return data.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `LiquidityManagerCore` contract defines the `IToken` interface with `function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) external returns (bool);`. It calls this function on tokens in `convertToV3`, `changeRanges`, and `increaseLiquidity` (e.g., `IToken(tokens[0]).approve(...)`). While standard ERC20 tokens return a boolean, major tokens like USDT (on Mainnet) do not return any value. When the contract calls `approve` on USDT, the Solidity runtime validates that the returned data size matches the interface definition (32 bytes for `bool`). Since USDT returns 0 bytes, the call reverts. This renders the Liquidity Manager unusable for any pair involving USDT on Mainnet.

## Impact
Functional Denial of Service for any pools involving USDT, preventing liquidity management.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `LiquidityManager`.
2. Attempt to call `convertToV3` for an OLAS/USDT pair.
3. The contract attempts `IToken(usdt).approve(...)`.
4. The transaction reverts because the returned data length from USDT (0) does not match the expected length (32).

## Proof of Code
function testUSDTRevert() public {
    // Mock USDT behavior (no return)
    MockUSDT usdt = new MockUSDT(); 
    vm.expectRevert(); // Reverts due to decoding error
    manager.convertToV3([address(usdt), address(olas)], ...);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeTransferLib.safeApprove` or perform a low-level call to `approve` and manually verify the success boolean only if return data is present.

## [M-143]. DoS on USDT pools due to unsafe approval logic and interface mismatch

## id: r6cOkw6D2QtLpFy8E8aco

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.convertToV3

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: Two issues are present: (1) approve is called through IToken expecting a bool return, which can revert on USDT-like tokens that return no data; (2) allowances are not reset to 0 before setting a new non-zero allowance, which can revert on tokens enforcing the zero-reset rule. LiquidityManagerCore has no approval helper to handle either behavior, so USDT-style tokens can cause immediate or eventual DoS.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `LiquidityManagerCore` contract uses `IToken(token).approve(spender, amount)` which fails for two reasons on non-standard ERC20 tokens like USDT: 
1. USDT's `approve` function does not return a boolean, causing the call to revert due to return data decoding errors on Solidity 0.8+.
2. USDT requires the allowance to be set to 0 before setting a new non-zero value. The contract approves the full `amounts` balance but `mint` or `increaseLiquidity` may not consume the exact approved amount (due to tick rounding), leaving a residual non-zero allowance. Subsequent calls to `approve` will revert.

## Impact
The protocol is incompatible with USDT and other non-standard ERC20s. Any attempt to manage liquidity for such pools will permanently revert after the first interaction or immediately due to interface mismatch, denying service.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `LiquidityManager` with a USDT/OLAS pool.
2. Call `convertToV3`.
3. The function calls `IToken(usdt).approve(pm, amount)`.
4. Since `IToken` interface expects `returns (bool)` but USDT returns `void`, the transaction reverts with `EvmError: Revert` or decoding error.
5. Alternatively, if it didn't revert there, subsequent calls would fail when `approve` is called on a non-zero current allowance.

## Proof of Code
function testUSDTDoS() public {
    // Mock USDT with no-return approve and race condition check
    MockUSDT usdt = new MockUSDT();
    address[] memory tokens = new address[](2);
    tokens[0] = address(usdt);
    tokens[1] = address(olas);
    
    vm.expectRevert(); // Decodes error or USDT revert
    vm.prank(owner);
    liquidityManager.convertToV3(tokens, bytes32(0), 500, new int24[](2), 0, false);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeTransferLib.safeApprove` (if available in the library version) or implement a safe approval helper that handles void returns and resets allowance to 0 before setting a new value.
```

## [M-144]. DoS on USDT and compliant tokens due to missing zero-allowance reset

## id: 8okM7nnF4Ot8kQh2CAvmn

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StandardViolation

## Exploit Type
StandardViolation

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.convertToV3

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: The contract does not reset allowances to 0 before setting a new allowance in convertToV3/changeRanges/increaseLiquidity. For tokens that revert on non-zero to non-zero allowance changes, any residual allowance (often created by partial token consumption by Uniswap) can permanently break future operations. No mitigation exists in the code.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `convertToV3`, `changeRanges`, and `increaseLiquidity` functions call `IToken(token).approve(positionManagerV3, amount)` to authorize the Uniswap V3 Position Manager to spend tokens. However, they do not reset the allowance to 0 before setting the new value. 

Some tokens, notably USDT, revert if `approve` is called with a non-zero value while the current allowance is non-zero (to prevent approval race conditions). If a previous operation (e.g. `mint` or `increaseLiquidity`) consumed slightly less than the approved amount (leaving residual allowance), subsequent calls to these functions will revert, permanently locking operations for that pool/token pair.

## Impact
Permanent DoS for pools involving USDT or other tokens with strict approval logic.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Contract holds USDT. 2. `convertToV3` is called. It approves 1000 USDT to PositionManager. 3. PositionManager consumes 999 USDT. Allowance remains 1. 4. Next `increaseLiquidity` call attempts to `approve` 500 USDT. 5. USDT contract checks `allowance > 0` and reverts. 6. Functionality is bricked.

## Proof of Code
function testUSDTApprovalDoS() public {
    MockUSDT usdt = new MockUSDT();
    manager.setToken(address(usdt));
    usdt.mint(address(manager), 2000);
    
    // First op leaves dust
    manager.simulateApprove(address(usdt), address(pm), 1000);
    usdt.simulateSpend(address(manager), address(pm), 999);
    
    // Second op tries to approve again -> Reverts
    vm.expectRevert("USDT approval fail");
    manager.simulateApprove(address(usdt), address(pm), 500);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeTransferLib.safeApprove` or manually set allowance to 0 before setting the new value: `token.approve(spender, 0); token.approve(spender, amount);`.
```

## [M-145]. Permanent DOS on USDT Pair Liquidity Operations due to Unsafe Approve

## id: 3DE-PE06Iq3axBAhCW1NQ

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Unchecked Return / Token Standard

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.convertToV3

## Finding Status: InvalidERC20EdgeCase
### Finding Status Justification: Approvals are set directly to a non-zero amount without a prior approve(0). Tokens with USDT-style allowance-change protection can revert on subsequent calls if a dust allowance remains. Since Uniswap position operations may not spend the full approved amount, leaving residual allowance is realistic, causing operational DoS for such tokens.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The contract calls `IToken(token).approve(positionManagerV3, amount)` without resetting the allowance to zero first. This pattern is incompatible with tokens like USDT (Tether), which revert if `approve` is called with a non-zero value while the current allowance is non-zero.

In functions like `convertToV3`, `changeRanges`, and `increaseLiquidity`, the contract approves `amounts` (current balance) to the Position Manager. The Position Manager then pulls the required tokens (`amountsIn`). Due to Uniswap V3 math and tick rounding, `amountsIn` is often slightly less than the approved `amounts`, leaving a dust allowance. Subsequent calls to these functions will attempt to `approve` again, causing USDT to revert, effectively permanently breaking liquidity management for USDT pairs.

## Impact
Denial of Service for managing liquidity of USDT pairs.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. `LiquidityManager` manages an OLAS/USDT pool.
2. Owner calls `convertToV3`. `amounts` = [1000 USDT, 1000 OLAS].
3. Contract approves 1000 USDT to Position Manager.
4. Position Manager uses 999 USDT. Allowance = 1 USDT.
5. Later, owner calls `increaseLiquidity` with 500 USDT.
6. Contract calls `approve(500)` on USDT.
7. USDT reverts because allowance is 1 (non-zero).
8. Transaction fails; liquidity cannot be managed.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {LiquidityManagerCore} from "../contracts/pol/LiquidityManagerCore.sol";

contract MockUSDT {
    uint256 public allowance = 1; // Simulate dust allowance
    function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) external returns (bool) {
        if (allowance > 0 && amount > 0) revert("USDT approve fail");
        allowance = amount;
        return true;
    }
    function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint256) { return 1000; }
}

contract MockLiquidityManager is LiquidityManagerCore {
    constructor(address _olas, address _treasury, address _pm, address _scanner) 
        LiquidityManagerCore(_olas, _treasury, _pm, _scanner, 1) {}
    // Stubs
    function _burn(uint256) internal override {}
    function _checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2(address[] memory, bytes32) internal override returns (uint256[] memory) {return new uint256[](2);}
    function _feeAmountTickSpacing(int24) internal view override returns (int24) {return 60;}
    function _getV3Pool(address[] memory, int24) internal view override returns (address) {return address(0x123);}
    function _mintV3(address[] memory, uint256[] memory, uint256[] memory, int24[] memory, int24, uint160) internal override returns (uint256, uint128, uint256[] memory) {return (1, 100, new uint256[](2));}
    function testApprove(address token) external {
        IToken(token).approve(address(0x3), 100);
    }
}

contract USDTTest is Test {
    MockLiquidityManager lm;
    MockUSDT usdt;

    function setUp() public {
        usdt = new MockUSDT();
        lm = new MockLiquidityManager(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(4));
    }

    function testUsdtDos() public {
        // Mocking IToken interface call
        vm.expectRevert("USDT approve fail");
        lm.testApprove(address(usdt));
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeTransferLib.safeApprove` (which handles the reset to 0) or explicitly set allowance to 0 before setting the new value (`IToken(token).approve(spender, 0); IToken(token).approve(spender, amount);`).





Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidFutureSpeculation

### M-146 / `h5_fqio9-fdFJh51tocCq`
- Finding title: DoS in IdentityRegistryBridger via shared Service Multisig collision
