```md
## [M-160]. Unchecked return value of `dispenser.retain()` leads to accounting mismatch

## id: VRIxqhcVmxiaWVT3SGMA8

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UncheckedLowLevelCallResults

## Exploit Type
UncheckedReturn

## Location
Tokenomics.updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions

## Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions performs a low-level call to dispenser.retain() and simply breaks the loop on failure without reverting, then proceeds to change inflation parameters and finally resets stakingIncentive to 0. If retain fails mid-process, some staking emissions may not have been retained/refunded, yet Tokenomics clears its returned-staking accumulator anyway, causing state/accounting desynchronization. The use of low-level call is explicitly to avoid revert, but the lack of robust failure handling combined with unconditional clearing makes the mismatch real.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresAdminRole


## Description
In `Tokenomics.updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions`, the code loops to call `dispenser.retain()` via a low-level call. If the call fails (`success == false`), the loop breaks, but the function proceeds to reset `stakingIncentive` to 0. If the `dispenser` failed to process the retain (e.g., due to revert or OOG), the funds are effectively left in the dispenser/accounting limbo, but Tokenomics writes them off as cleared. This creates a permanent accounting desynchronization.

## Impact
Loss of accounting integrity and potential loss of funds if `dispenser` fails to retain but `Tokenomics` clears the debt.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Admin calls `updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions`. 2. Loop calls `dispenser.retain()`. 3. Dispenser logic reverts (e.g. paused or error). 4. Loop breaks. 5. `stakingIncentive` is set to 0. 6. The retained amounts are not returned/burned but state is cleared.

## Proof of Code
function testUncheckedRetain() public {
        // Mock dispenser failure
        vm.mockCall(address(dispenser), abi.encodeCall(IDispenser.retain, ()), abi.encode(false)); // Fails silently in low level call
        // ... setup epochs ...
        tokenomics.updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions(50, 40, 10, 0);
        // Check that stakingIncentive is 0 despite failure
        // (Access mapping via helper or getter if available, or assume state change from code analysis)
    }

## Suggested Mitigation
Revert the transaction if `dispenser.retain()` fails, or handle the partial failure by not clearing `stakingIncentive`.
```

### M-161 / `6jIrty8WsZiSgjrXbkaYw`
- Finding title: DoS of large governance actions due to gas-inefficient loop in `ProcessBridgedDataPolygon`
- Report lines: 12946-13006
```md
## [M-161]. DoS of large governance actions due to gas-inefficient loop in `ProcessBridgedDataPolygon`

## id: 6jIrty8WsZiSgjrXbkaYw

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
GasGriefBlockLimit

## Exploit Type
GasGriefBlockLimit

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataPolygon.processBridgeData

## Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: ProcessBridgedDataPolygon copies `data` into `payload` byte-by-byte in a for-loop, which is O(N) and substantially more expensive than word-copy/identity-precompile approaches. This can cause legitimate very-large payload verifications to run out of gas and fail, limiting governance batch size. Triggering requires a large message (governance/CM choosing a big payload), so it is not a typical external attacker vector but can still DoS large governance actions.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `processBridgeData` function and its internal `_verifyBridgedData` helper use gas-inefficient byte-by-byte copy loops to handle payload data. Specifically, `processBridgeData` copies the payload from `data` (memory) to a new memory array, and `_verifyBridgedData` extracts inner payloads similarly.

```solidity
// ProcessBridgedDataPolygon.sol:48-51
bytes memory payload = new bytes(data.length - SELECTOR_DATA_LENGTH);
for (uint256 i = 0; i < payload.length; ++i) {
    payload[i] = data[i + SELECTOR_DATA_LENGTH];
}
```

This O(N) copy operation consumes roughly 100-200 gas per byte (including loop overhead and memory expansion). For large payloads, such as complex contract upgrades or batched governance actions (which can legally approach the block gas limit of ~30M gas or the bridge limit of ~100KB), this inefficient copying can causing the transaction to revert due to Out of Gas (OOG), effectively creating a denial of service for legitimate, large governance messages. While Ethereum calldata limits allow for large payloads, this verification logic imposes a much stricter practical limit (approx 20-30KB safe limit vs 100KB+ capacity), potentially blocking emergency upgrades or large state changes.

## Impact
Legitimate, large governance payloads (e.g., protocol upgrades) may become impossible to execute due to gas limits, leading to a functional Denial of Service on the governance capability.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. The Olas governance (Timelock) schedules a cross-chain transaction to upgrade a contract on Polygon.
2. The upgrade requires deploying a new implementation contract with bytecode size ~24KB (Spurious Dragon limit) or larger if batched.
3. The bridge message payload `data` passed to `processBridgeData` is ~25KB.
4. The `processBridgeData` function performs a byte-by-byte copy. 25,000 iterations * ~100 gas = 2.5M gas.
5. `_verifyBridgedData` parses this and may perform additional copies if the inner structure is complex.
6. If the payload is larger (e.g. 100KB batch), the cost scales to ~10M+ gas, potentially exceeding the transaction gas limit allocated by the relayer or the block gas limit, causing the governance action to fail continuously.

## Proof of Code
contract GasTest { function testGas() public { bytes memory largeData = new bytes(50000); // 50KB
 uint256 start = gasleft();
 bytes memory payload = new bytes(largeData.length);
 for (uint256 i = 0; i < largeData.length; ++i) {
 payload[i] = largeData[i];
 }
 require(gasleft() > start - 5000000); // Fails if cost > 5M gas
 } }

## Suggested Mitigation
Replace the manual byte-by-byte copy loop with assembly-based memory copying (using `mload`/`mstore` in 32-byte chunks) or the Identity precompile (address 0x04) to significantly reduce gas costs and support larger payloads.
```

### M-162 / `tkyWqd2wMNK4CnaRib79T`
- Finding title: Bridged transaction `value` is ignored during verification allowing unauthorized ETH spending
- Report lines: 13007-13074
```md
## [M-162]. Bridged transaction `value` is ignored during verification allowing unauthorized ETH spending

## id: tkyWqd2wMNK4CnaRib79T

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Unchecked Bridged Value

## Exploit Type
AccessControl

## Location
VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData

## Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: Code confirms the 12-byte `value` field in the packed stream is not verified/allowlisted; only (target, selector, chainId) are checked in _verifyData. Therefore, if the actual message executor uses that `value` to forward ETH, the verifier does not constrain ETH outflow per call. Exploitability depends on whether the execution environment actually forwards value from a balance-holding contract and whether any allowlisted selector can be invoked with value (i.e., payable). Because those are realistic but configuration-dependent conditions, likelihood is occasional and the situation is partly governance/operational risk (what is allowlisted and whether ETH is held), but the root issue exists in code.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_verifyBridgedData` function parses the bridged payload, which contains a 12-byte `value` field. The assembly block extracts `target` and `payloadLength` but explicitly skips the `value` field using `i := add(i, 16)` (skipping 12 bytes of value and 4 bytes of length offset). 

Crucially, the verification logic in `_verifyData` only validates `target`, `selector`, and `chainId`. It does not enforce any restrictions on the `value` associated with the call. Since the L2 execution logic (likely the bridge messenger) will execute the call with the provided value, authorized targets can be called with arbitrary ETH amounts attached. This allows an attacker to drain the L2 governance avatar's ETH balance by calling any authorized function that is payable (or by simply attaching value to non-payable functions if the execution context allows it), bypassing the intent of the Guard which typically restricts spending.

## Impact
Unauthorized draining of ETH from the L2 avatar address to allowed target contracts.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. The L2 Avatar holds 100 ETH.
2. A target contract `Target` with function `deposit()` is allowed in the Guard.
3. Attacker submits a bridged payload calling `Target.deposit()` with `value` set to 100 ETH.
4. `_verifyBridgedData` verifies `Target` and `deposit` selector, but ignores `value`.
5. Verification passes.
6. The payload is bridged and executed on L2. The Avatar calls `Target.deposit{value: 100 ether}()`.
7. Funds are moved to `Target` unexpectedly.

## Proof of Code
contract VerifyValueTest is Test, VerifyBridgedData {
    function processBridgeData(bytes memory, address, uint256) external override {}

    function testIgnoreValue() public {
        address target = address(0x123);
        uint256 chainId = 1;
        // Authorize target
        uint256 key = uint256(uint160(target)) | (uint256(uint32(0x11223344)) << 160) | (chainId << 192);
        mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds[key] = true;

        // Create payload with HUGE value (max uint96)
        bytes memory payload = abi.encodePacked(
            target, 
            uint96(type(uint96).max), // Value
            uint32(4), // Len
            bytes4(0x11223344) // Selector
        );
        // Pad payload to match expected structure if needed, simplified here for logic demo
        // The test would show _verifyBridgedData does not revert despite high value
        // Note: _verifyBridgedData is internal, exposed via harness in real test
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Include `value` in the `_verifyData` authorization check (e.g., require `value == 0` for most calls, or add a `maxAmount` to the allowlist).
```

### L-163 / `cc7sVrn3lOXGbxcAHZQvk`
- Finding title: Gas Griefing via Inefficient Byte-by-Byte Copying in Verification Loop
- Report lines: 13075-13124
```md
## [L-163]. Gas Griefing via Inefficient Byte-by-Byte Copying in Verification Loop

## id: cc7sVrn3lOXGbxcAHZQvk

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
GasGriefBlockLimit

## Exploit Type
GasGriefBlockLimit

## Location
VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData

## Finding Status: InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData copies each inner payload byte-by-byte (`payload[j] = data[i + j]`), which is gas-inefficient for large payloads and/or multiple batched messages. This can cause large, but otherwise valid, governance messages to become non-executable due to gas limits. It is not a typical external attacker drain vector, but it can DoS large governance batches (especially if governance attempts big upgrade/calldata batches), hence an occasional, governance-driven availability risk.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_verifyBridgedData` function manually copies bytes from the input `data` to a new `payload` array using a for-loop: `payload[j] = data[i + j]`. This operation is O(N) with high gas overhead for large payloads compared to using `bytes.concat` or assembly `mcopy` (in newer EVM versions) or precompiled identity copy. An attacker or a legitimate large governance payload could exhaust the block gas limit, causing the verification (and thus the governance action) to fail (DoS).

## Impact
DoS of large valid governance messages due to exceeding block gas limit.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Create a bridged message with a very large payload (e.g., 50KB or more).
2. Call `processBridgeData`.
3. The loop runs 50,000 times, performing memory reads and writes plus loop overhead.
4. Transaction runs out of gas.

## Proof of Code
function testGasGrief() public {
    uint256 size = 50000;
    bytes memory largeData = new bytes(size); // ... fill data ...
    // call processBridgeData
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `slice` utility with assembly `mstore`/`mload` or `calldatacopy` (if applicable) to copy memory chunks efficiently.





Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
```

### M-164 / `Lm6xqCIMX-eQVFFeFp3oN`
- Finding title: Proxy fallback exceeds 2300 gas stipend causing DoS on ETH transfers
- Report lines: 13125-13186
```md
## [M-164]. Proxy fallback exceeds 2300 gas stipend causing DoS on ETH transfers

## id: Lm6xqCIMX-eQVFFeFp3oN

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
BuyBackBurnerProxy.fallback

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: This is not purely a user mistake: any external contract that uses `transfer`/`send` (notably WETH9 `withdraw`) will be unable to send ETH to the proxy regardless of user intent. It is an integration/design incompatibility rather than a user-only error, though the overall impact can still be considered low if the protocol does not rely on ETH push transfers.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `fallback` function in `BuyBackBurnerProxy` performs an `sload` operation (reading the implementation address from storage) before delegating. In a cold access scenario, `sload` costs 2100 gas, and the `delegatecall` costs 100 gas, plus memory expansion and overhead. This exceeds the 2300 gas stipend provided by legacy `transfer` or `send` calls. Consequently, standard contracts that push ETH via `transfer` (such as canonical WETH9 `withdraw`) will revert when attempting to send ETH to this proxy.

## Impact
The proxy is incompatible with contracts using standard `transfer`/`send` for ETH distribution (e.g., unwrapping WETH), causing a Denial of Service for these specific funding flows.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy `BuyBackBurnerProxy` with a valid implementation.
2. Attempt to send 1 wei to the proxy using Solidity's `transfer` or `send`.
3. The transaction reverts due to OutOfGas.

## Proof of Code
contract ProxyGasTest is Test {
    BuyBackBurnerProxy proxy;
    MockImpl impl;

    function setUp() public {
        impl = new MockImpl();
        proxy = new BuyBackBurnerProxy(address(impl), abi.encodeWithSignature("init()"));
    }

    function test_ReceiveETH_Transfer_Reverts() public {
        address payable p = payable(address(proxy));
        // This mimics WETH.withdraw behavior using transfer (2300 gas limit)
        vm.expectRevert(); 
        p.transfer(1 wei);
    }
}

contract MockImpl {
    function init() external {}
    // Implementation might have receive, but proxy fails before reaching it
    receive() external payable {}
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add an empty `receive() external payable {}` to the `BuyBackBurnerProxy` contract to allow receiving ETH with low gas, or ensure all integrations use `.call{value: ...}`.
```

### L-165 / `PIgF7wUUlr9gYVm8ev0X4`
- Finding title: ETH dust from service donations permanently locked in Treasury due to rounding
- Report lines: 13187-13252
```md
## [L-165]. ETH dust from service donations permanently locked in Treasury due to rounding

## id: PIgF7wUUlr9gYVm8ev0X4

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
PrecisionDriftAccumulation

## Exploit Type
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Location
Tokenomics.trackServiceDonations

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: This is the same rounding-loss mechanism as other donation truncation reports: `_trackServiceDonations` uses `amounts[i] / numServiceUnits`, discarding remainder, while totalDonationsETH and Treasury.ETHFromServices account the full donation. The remainder portion is never assigned to unit pendingRelativeReward/topUp, so it never becomes claimable and never gets moved to ETHOwned. Since ETHFromServices is not owner-withdrawable, this dust remains stuck. It is common because divisibility is not guaranteed.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `Tokenomics.trackServiceDonations`, the donation amount for each service unit is calculated using integer division:

```solidity
uint96 amount = uint96(amounts[i] / numServiceUnits);
```

Any remainder (dust) from this division is excluded from the `pendingRelativeReward` and `pendingRelativeTopUp` accumulators. However, the full donation amount is recorded in `Tokenomics.totalDonationsETH` and added to `Treasury.ETHFromServices`. 

When rewards are claimed (`withdrawToAccount`) or treasury shares rebalanced (`rebalanceTreasury`), only the allocated amounts are withdrawn. The calculated unit rewards (sum of `amount`) will strictly be less than or equal to the total donation minus treasury share. The remainders accumulate in `Treasury.ETHFromServices` but are never attributable to any unit or the treasury. Since `Treasury.withdraw` for ETH enforces checks against `ETHOwned` (which is distinct from `ETHFromServices`), this dust remains permanently locked in the contract balance.

## Impact
Accumulation of stuck ETH in the Treasury contract. While individual amounts are dust, they can accumulate over time with frequent donations and many service units.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Create a service with 3 units.
2. Donate 100 wei to the service via `Treasury.depositServiceDonationsETH`.
3. `Tokenomics` calculates `amount = 100 / 3 = 33`.
4. Total credited to units = `33 * 3 = 99`.
5. `Treasury.ETHFromServices` increases by 100.
6. Users claim rewards (99 wei total). Treasury rebalances (0 wei if treasury fraction is low/zero).
7. 1 wei remains in `ETHFromServices`, unclaimable and unwithdrawable.

## Proof of Code
function test_DustLock() public {
    // Setup service with 3 units
    uint256[] memory serviceIds = new uint256[](1);
    serviceIds[0] = 1;
    uint256[] memory amounts = new uint256[](1);
    amounts[0] = 100;
    
    // Donate
    vm.prank(donator);
    treasury.depositServiceDonationsETH{value: 100}(serviceIds, amounts);
    
    // Verify discrepancy
    // ... (assertions showing ETHFromServices > claimed rewards)
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Track the remainder of `amounts[i] % numServiceUnits` and allocate it to the Treasury's share (`ETHOwned`) or a random unit to ensure conservation of funds.
```

### M-166 / `uuR4wxW-kLLartNnAs8x5`
- Finding title: ETH Dust Permanently Locked in Treasury due to Donation Truncation
- Report lines: 13253-13303
```md
## [M-166]. ETH Dust Permanently Locked in Treasury due to Donation Truncation

## id: uuR4wxW-kLLartNnAs8x5

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
RoundingError

## Exploit Type
RoundingError

## Location
Tokenomics._trackServiceDonations

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: In Tokenomics._trackServiceDonations, per-unit accounting uses `amounts[i] / numServiceUnits` (integer division), dropping remainders. But Tokenomics.checkpoint computes epoch-level rewards from totalDonationsETH using full amounts, and Treasury tracks full ETHFromServices. Since user-claimable rewards are ultimately based on truncated per-unit accumulations, the dropped remainder portion is never attributed to any unit and is not moved to ETHOwned. Treasury has no mechanism to withdraw ETHFromServices (only ETHOwned is withdrawable by owner), so the dust accumulates as permanently stuck ETH. Trigger is straightforward whenever donations are not divisible by unit counts.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `Tokenomics._trackServiceDonations`, the donation amount assigned to each unit is calculated using integer division: `uint96 amount = uint96(amounts[i] / numServiceUnits);`. Any remainder (`amounts[i] % numServiceUnits`) is discarded from the unit's `pendingRelativeReward` but is still included in the global `totalDonationsETH` tracked by Tokenomics and the `ETHFromServices` balance in the Treasury. When `checkpoint()` runs, the Treasury's share is calculated on the *full* donation amount and rebalanced. However, the users' share is claimed based on the *truncated* amounts via `accountOwnerIncentives`. The difference (the cumulative remainders) remains in `Treasury.ETHFromServices` indefinitely. Since `Treasury.withdraw` is restricted to `ETHOwned`, there is no mechanism to withdraw this stuck ETH.

## Impact
Accumulation of stuck ETH in the Treasury contract. While individual amounts are dust, systematic behavior or griefing (donating `units - 1` wei) can lock larger amounts over time.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker creates a service with 3 units. 2. Attacker donates 2 wei to the service. 3. `amount` per unit is `2 / 3 = 0`. 4. `pendingRelativeReward` is 0. 5. `totalDonationsETH` increases by 2 wei. 6. `checkpoint()` runs; Treasury takes its share (e.g., 50% = 1 wei). 7. The remaining 1 wei is expected to be rewards, but claimable rewards are 0. 8. The 1 wei remains locked in `ETHFromServices`.

## Proof of Code
contract DustTest is Test {
    Tokenomics tokenomics;
    // Mock Treasury and Registry logic would be needed here
    // Simplified PoC logic:
    function test_DustLock() public {
        uint256 donation = 2;
        uint256 units = 3;
        uint256 perUnit = donation / units;
        uint256 totalClaimable = perUnit * units;
        uint256 stuck = donation - totalClaimable;
        assertEq(stuck, 2);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Track the accumulated remainder dust in `Tokenomics` and allocate it to the Treasury during `checkpoint` (add to `incentives[1]`), or allow a sweep function in `Treasury` to move excess `ETHFromServices` to `ETHOwned`.
```

## [L-167]. DoS of fee collection via front-running due to strict zero-value check

## id: vKVn8lnlkJRoKWJ-ylo9u

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
IncentiveGameTheory

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.collectFees

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: Same root cause as the batch-DoS issue: `collectFees` is permissionless and reverts when fees are already collected, so a front-runner can force subsequent callers to revert and waste gas. This is trivially exploitable whenever fees exist and the victim’s transaction is observable. While it disrupts automation/keepers and wastes gas, it does not directly steal funds from the protocol, so the impact is Low.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `collectFees` function in `LiquidityManagerCore` reverts with `ZeroValue()` if the collected amounts of both tokens are zero (`if (amounts[0] == 0 && amounts[1] == 0) revert ZeroValue();`). This allows an attacker to grief any automated keeper or user attempting to collect fees by front-running their transaction with a `collectFees` call of their own. The attacker collects the pending fees, leaving 0 for the victim, causing the victim's transaction to revert and waste gas.

## Impact
Disruption of automated fee collection services; wasted gas for honest callers.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Keeper submits `collectFees()`.
2. Attacker sees transaction and submits `collectFees()` with higher gas price.
3. Attacker's tx executes, claiming fees.
4. Keeper's tx executes, collects 0 fees, and reverts due to `ZeroValue` check.

## Proof of Code
function testCollectFeesGriefing() public {
    // Setup fees
    // Attacker calls collectFees
    // Victim calls collectFees -> reverts
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Remove the revert on zero fees or allow specifying a `minCollected` parameter (which can be 0).
```

### L-168 / `Ei_v0POKeaJIyk1Qfh-GV`
- Finding title: AMM Operations Lack Expiration Deadline allowing Transaction Holding
- Report lines: 13350-13396
```md
## [L-168]. AMM Operations Lack Expiration Deadline allowing Transaction Holding

## id: Ei_v0POKeaJIyk1Qfh-GV

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
TimestampDependentLogic

## Exploit Type
TimestampDependentLogic

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore._mintV3

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: In LiquidityManagerOptimism._mintV3 and LiquidityManagerCore._increaseLiquidity/_decreaseLiquidity, deadline is hardcoded to block.timestamp, which always passes at inclusion and provides no meaningful expiry. This can allow delayed inclusion (especially on L2 sequencers) under changed market conditions. It is a weaker issue than direct theft but increases exposure to unfavorable execution.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
Functions `_mintV3`, `increaseLiquidity`, and `decreaseLiquidity` in `LiquidityManagerCore` (and by extension `LiquidityManagerOptimism`) explicitly set the `deadline` parameter for Uniswap V3 interactions to `block.timestamp`. This effectively disables the expiration check, allowing a malicious validator or sequencer to hold the transaction in the mempool and execute it at a later time when market conditions are less favorable (though still within slippage bounds), potentially leading to worse execution price.

## Impact
Transactions may be executed at unfavorable times chosen by block producers, potentially maximizing slippage against the protocol.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Owner submits `increaseLiquidity` transaction. 2. Validator holds the transaction for several blocks. 3. Price moves unfavorably. 4. Validator includes transaction. 5. Due to `deadline: block.timestamp`, the transaction does not revert and executes at the worse price.

## Proof of Code
function testMissingDeadline() public {
    // Advance time significantly
    vm.warp(block.timestamp + 1 days);
    
    // Call function with logic that would normally require a deadline
    // Observe that passing block.timestamp allows it to succeed regardless of delay
    vm.prank(owner);
    liquidityManager.increaseLiquidity(tokens, fee, 0);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Allow the caller to specify a `deadline` parameter in external functions (e.g., `convertToV3`, `increaseLiquidity`) and pass this value to the underlying AMM calls.
```

### M-169 / `HUn4M6hrKg9Dj5U6xXsnC`
- Finding title: Ineffective transaction deadline due to `block.timestamp` usage

```md
## [M-169]. Ineffective transaction deadline due to `block.timestamp` usage

## id: HUn4M6hrKg9Dj5U6xXsnC

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
LiquidityManagerOptimism._mintV3

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: deadline is set to block.timestamp in _mintV3 and in increase/decrease liquidity params, making deadline checks ineffective. This increases risk of stale execution (especially on L2 sequencers). The functions are owner-only, limiting who can trigger them, but MEV/sequencer delays remain possible.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
In `LiquidityManagerOptimism._mintV3` (and `LiquidityManagerCore` decrease/increase functions), the `deadline` parameter for Uniswap V3 operations is hardcoded to `block.timestamp`. 

Snippet:
```solidity
ISlipstreamV3.MintParams memory params = ISlipstreamV3.MintParams({
    ...
    deadline: block.timestamp,
    ...
});
```

This effectively disables the deadline protection, as the check `block.timestamp <= params.deadline` will always evaluate to true at the time of execution. Transactions sitting in the mempool for extended periods can be executed later under unfavorable market conditions.

## Impact
Transactions submitted by the owner can be executed significantly later than intended if network congestion occurs, potentially resulting in execution at stale prices (within slippage bounds, but temporally decoupled).

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Owner submits `convertToV3` transaction.
2. Transaction lingers in mempool due to low gas.
3. Market conditions change.
4. Transaction is mined hours later. `deadline` is set to the *current* `block.timestamp` during execution, so it passes validation, whereas a fixed deadline would have caused a revert.

## Proof of Code
function testDeadlineBypass() public {
    vm.warp(1000);
    // Call convertToV3
    // Verify in mock PositionManager that deadline == 1000
    // If it was a fixed timestamp (e.g. 1000) and warped to 2000, it would fail. Here it always passes.
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add a `deadline` parameter to external functions (`convertToV3`, `changeRanges`, etc.) and pass it to the underlying calls.
```

### M-170 / `RfaHsAPSK8DN0zR0t18F8`
- Finding title: Missing deadline check in _mintV3 allows pending transactions to be executed at unfavorable times
