```md
## [M-170]. Missing deadline check in _mintV3 allows pending transactions to be executed at unfavorable times

## id: RfaHsAPSK8DN0zR0t18F8

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
LiquidityManagerOptimism._mintV3

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: _mintV3 sets deadline=block.timestamp, so any inclusion time satisfies the position manager’s deadline check. A delayed transaction can still execute under changed price conditions. This is an execution-safety weakness but not necessarily a direct drain on its own.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
In `LiquidityManagerOptimism._mintV3`, the `deadline` parameter for the Uniswap V3 `mint` call is set to `block.timestamp`. This effectively disables the deadline protection mechanism, as the check `require(block.timestamp <= params.deadline)` in the position manager will always pass during execution. This allows a validator or miner to hold the transaction and execute it at a later block where market conditions may be unfavorable to the protocol, bypassing the user's intent to execute within a specific timeframe.

## Impact
Transactions can be executed indefinitely later than intended, exposing the protocol to unfavorable price shifts.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Owner submits a `convertToV3` transaction with specific parameters.
2. The transaction lingers in the mempool due to low gas or network congestion.
3. Market conditions change significantly.
4. A miner picks up the transaction hours later.
5. The `deadline` is set to the current `block.timestamp` during execution, so the call succeeds despite the delay, potentially minting a position with suboptimal parameters.

## Proof of Code
contract MockSlipstream { function mint(MintParams calldata params) external payable returns (uint256, uint128, uint256, uint256) { require(block.timestamp <= params.deadline, 'Expired'); return (1, 100, params.amount0Desired, params.amount1Desired); } struct MintParams { address token0; address token1; int24 tickSpacing; int24 tickLower; int24 tickUpper; uint256 amount0Desired; uint256 amount1Desired; uint256 amount0Min; uint256 amount1Min; address recipient; uint256 deadline; uint160 sqrtPriceX96; } } 

function testDeadlineBypass() public { 
  LiquidityManagerOptimism lm = new LiquidityManagerOptimism(address(1), address(2), address(mockSlipstream), address(4), 10, address(6), address(7), address(8)); 
  // Warp time forward simulating delay 
  vm.warp(block.timestamp + 10000); 
  // Call mint logic (exposed via harness or similar) 
  // Internal logic sets deadline = block.timestamp, preventing expiry. 
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Accept a user-provided `deadline` parameter in external functions (e.g., `convertToV3`) and pass it down to `_mintV3`.
```

### M-171 / `Y7nv64kP7efYc01RMSMqY`
- Finding title: DoS of valid SEND_MESSAGE payloads due to incorrect minimum data length check in ProcessBridgedDataWormhole
- Report lines: 13506-13577
```md
## [M-171]. DoS of valid SEND_MESSAGE payloads due to incorrect minimum data length check in ProcessBridgedDataWormhole

## id: Y7nv64kP7efYc01RMSMqY

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataWormhole.processBridgeData

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: Supporting SEND_MESSAGE_REFUND is a workaround (users can choose a different selector), not a safeguard that prevents the SEND_MESSAGE path from incorrectly reverting for otherwise-valid smaller payloads.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `ProcessBridgedDataWormhole` contract enforces a global minimum data length of 324 bytes (`MIN_WORMHOLE_PAYLOAD_LENGTH`) for all inputs. However, it supports two distinct function selectors: `SEND_MESSAGE` and `SEND_MESSAGE_REFUND`. The `SEND_MESSAGE` selector corresponds to `sendPayloadToEvm(uint16,address,bytes,uint256,uint256)`, which has 5 parameters. 

A valid `SEND_MESSAGE` call with a minimal inner payload (e.g., 36 bytes for a single target call, padded to 64 bytes) results in a total calldata length of 260 bytes (4 bytes selector + 5*32 bytes static args + 32 bytes bytes-length + 64 bytes payload). 

Because 260 < 324, these valid, standard messages are always rejected by the length check `if (data.length < MIN_WORMHOLE_PAYLOAD_LENGTH)`. The constant 324 appears to be derived from the minimum size of the `SEND_MESSAGE_REFUND` variant (which has 2 extra arguments, adding 64 bytes: 260 + 64 = 324). Applying this stricter bound to both selectors effectively breaks the `SEND_MESSAGE` functionality for small/standard payloads.

## Impact
Valid governance or bridge messages using the standard `SEND_MESSAGE` selector with small payloads (e.g. configuration changes, pausing) are permanently blocked, forcing users to use the more expensive/complex `SEND_MESSAGE_REFUND` or artificially pad data.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker (or valid user) constructs a standard `sendPayloadToEvm` calldata using the `SEND_MESSAGE` selector to bridge a minimal command (e.g. 36 bytes).
2. The resulting calldata length is 260 bytes.
3. The user calls `processBridgeData` (or it is called by GuardCM).
4. The contract checks `260 < 324` and reverts with `IncorrectDataLength`.
5. The valid message cannot be processed.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/multisigs/bridge_verifier/ProcessBridgedDataWormhole.sol";

contract TestDoS is Test {
    ProcessBridgedDataWormhole verifier;
    bytes4 constant SEND_MESSAGE = bytes4(keccak256(bytes("sendPayloadToEvm(uint16,address,bytes,uint256,uint256)")));

    function setUp() public {
        verifier = new ProcessBridgedDataWormhole();
    }

    function test_RevertOnValidSmallPayload() public {
        // Construct valid SEND_MESSAGE payload
        // 5 args: uint16, address, bytes, uint256, uint256
        // Inner payload: 36 bytes (20 target + 12 val + 4 len), padded to 64
        bytes memory inner = new bytes(36);
        bytes memory data = abi.encodeWithSelector(SEND_MESSAGE, uint16(1), address(0x123), inner, uint256(0), uint256(0));
        
        // Length should be 260
        assertEq(data.length, 260);

        // Expect revert due to incorrect length check (requires 324)
        vm.expectRevert(abi.encodeWithSelector(IncorrectDataLength.selector, 324, 260));
        verifier.processBridgeData(data, address(0), 0);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Lower `MIN_WORMHOLE_PAYLOAD_LENGTH` to 260 (the minimum size for `SEND_MESSAGE`), or apply different length checks based on the `functionSig` (324 for `SEND_MESSAGE_REFUND`, 260 for `SEND_MESSAGE`).
```

### M-172 / `eTmpP7x1bkOUKAjmpwEjz`
- Finding title: Authorization bypass in `validationRequest` allows unauthorized calls for Agent ID 0
- Report lines: 13578-13625
```md
## [M-172]. Authorization bypass in `validationRequest` allows unauthorized calls for Agent ID 0

## id: eTmpP7x1bkOUKAjmpwEjz

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AuthByPass

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
IdentityRegistryBridger.validationRequest

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: In `validationRequest`, `checkAgentId = mapMultisigAgentIds[msg.sender]` defaults to 0 for unlinked callers. If the caller supplies `agentId = 0`, the access check `if (agentId != checkAgentId) revert` is bypassed. The contract then calls `validationRegistry.validationRequest(..., 0, ...)`. There is no local guard `agentId > 0` or `checkAgentId != 0`. Whether downstream rejects `agentId=0` is unknown from provided code, but the bridger-side authorization bypass is concrete and trivially reproducible.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `validationRequest` function in `IdentityRegistryBridger` verifies the caller's authority by checking `mapMultisigAgentIds[msg.sender] == agentId`. However, for any unauthorized sender, `mapMultisigAgentIds[msg.sender]` returns `0` (default value). If the caller passes `agentId = 0`, the check `0 == 0` passes, bypassing the authorization. The function then proceeds to call `IValidationRegistry.validationRequest` with `agentId = 0`. This allows any user to emit `ValidationRequestSubmitted` events and spam the validation registry/off-chain validators with fake requests for Agent ID 0.

## Impact
Attackers can bypass authorization to spam the Validation Registry with requests for Agent ID 0, potentially griefing off-chain validators or polluting event logs.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker (EOA) calls `IdentityRegistryBridger.validationRequest(validatorAddr, 0, 'uri', 'hash')`.
2. `mapMultisigAgentIds[attacker]` returns 0.
3. The check `if (agentId != checkAgentId)` evaluates to `if (0 != 0)`, which is false.
4. The function executes `IValidationRegistry.validationRequest(..., 0, ...)` and emits `ValidationRequestSubmitted`.

## Proof of Code
function testAuthBypassAgentZero() public {
    // Deploy mocks and bridger...
    vm.prank(address(0xbad));
    // Pass agentId = 0
    bridger.validationRequest(address(0x123), 0, "uri", bytes32(0));
    // Verify event emitted or registry called (mock check)
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add a check `require(checkAgentId > 0, 'Sender not linked');` before comparing IDs, or ensure `agentId > 0`.
```

### L-173 / `CNirvWxVKEx63dXa026Ad`
- Finding title: Swapped Arguments in Error definitions lead to misleading revert logs
- Report lines: 13626-13669
```md
## [L-173]. Swapped Arguments in Error definitions lead to misleading revert logs

## id: CNirvWxVKEx63dXa026Ad

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Custom

## Exploit Type
Custom

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataPolygon.processBridgeData

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: Bug exists: ProcessBridgedDataPolygon reverts `IncorrectDataLength(data.length, MIN_POLYGON_PAYLOAD_LENGTH)` while the error is defined as (expected, provided). Similarly, VerifyBridgedData reverts `DataLengthIncorrect(payloadLength, SELECTOR_DATA_LENGTH)` with swapped semantics. This is trivially triggerable by short inputs and produces misleading decoded revert data, but does not change control flow security; it only harms observability/debugging.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `ProcessBridgedDataPolygon.processBridgeData`, the `IncorrectDataLength` error is reverted with swapped arguments. The definition is `error IncorrectDataLength(uint256 expected, uint256 provided)`, but the code calls `revert IncorrectDataLength(data.length, MIN_POLYGON_PAYLOAD_LENGTH)`. This passes the provided length as 'expected' and the minimum length as 'provided'. Similarly, in `VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData`, `DataLengthIncorrect` is defined as `(expected, provided)` but called as `(payloadLength, SELECTOR_DATA_LENGTH)`, swapping the values.

## Impact
Debugging and monitoring tools will display incorrect error information (e.g., stating 'Expected 164, Provided 20' as 'Expected 20, Provided 164'), confusing incident response.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Caller provides `data` with length 20.
2. Contract reverts `IncorrectDataLength(20, 164)`.
3. ABI decoder interprets 20 as `expected` and 164 as `provided`.

## Proof of Code
function testSwappedError() public {
    vm.expectRevert(abi.encodeWithSelector(ProcessBridgedDataPolygon.IncorrectDataLength.selector, 20, 164));
    verifier.processBridgeData(new bytes(20), address(0), 1);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Swap the arguments in the revert statements to match the error definition: `revert IncorrectDataLength(MIN_POLYGON_PAYLOAD_LENGTH, data.length);`.
```

### M-174 / `hWeTP8Ki-W5gfxHj6G8Bg`
- Finding title: Permanent skipping of undeployed services in `linkServiceIdAgentIds` enables Griefing
- Report lines: 13670-13737
```md
## [M-174]. Permanent skipping of undeployed services in `linkServiceIdAgentIds` enables Griefing

## id: hWeTP8Ki-W5gfxHj6G8Bg

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
IdentityRegistryBridger.linkServiceIdAgentIds

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: Inside `linkServiceIdAgentIds`, if `mapServices(serviceId)` returns `multisig == address(0)`, the function `continue`s (skips registration) but still advances the global cursor by setting `startLinkServiceId = lastServiceId` at the end. Therefore, services that are not deployed at the time their IDs are swept are permanently skipped by the sequential automation. Because the function is permissionless, a griefer can intentionally advance the cursor over undeployed services. This does not permanently block manual fixing (`updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds` can still be called), so impact is mainly loss of automation/UX.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `IdentityRegistryBridger.linkServiceIdAgentIds` function iterates through a range of service IDs to register them in the Identity Registry. It maintains a global `startLinkServiceId` state variable to track the next ID to process. Within the loop, the function retrieves service details via `IServiceRegistry(serviceRegistry).mapServices(serviceId)`. If a service exists but has not yet been deployed (indicated by `multisig == address(0)`), the function explicitly skips the registration logic using `continue`. However, the loop continues to increment, and critically, `startLinkServiceId` is updated to the end of the processed range (`lastServiceId`) at the end of the function.

This behavior creates a vulnerability where valid services are permanently skipped by the automated linking mechanism. An attacker (or any user) can call `linkServiceIdAgentIds` covering a range of service IDs that are currently in `PreRegistration` (undeployed). The function will skip them and advance the global `startLinkServiceId` pointer past them. When these services are subsequently deployed, future calls to `linkServiceIdAgentIds` will start from the new `startLinkServiceId`, effectively ignoring the now-ready services. This forces service owners to perform manual intervention via `updateOrLinkServiceIdAgentIds` and breaks any protocol automation relying on the `linkServiceIdAgentIds` sweeper.

## Impact
Functional Denial of Service of the automated bridging mechanism for targeted services. Services deployed after being 'skipped' will not be automatically registered in the Identity Registry.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker observes that `startLinkServiceId` is 10.
2. A new Service ID 10 is created in the ServiceRegistry but is in `PreRegistration` state (`multisig` is `address(0)`).
3. Attacker calls `IdentityRegistryBridger.linkServiceIdAgentIds(1)`. The function checks Service 10, sees it is undeployed, skips it, and updates `startLinkServiceId` to 11.
4. The Service ID 10 owner completes setup and the service transitions to `Deployed` state (`multisig` is now set).
5. Protocol keepers or users call `linkServiceIdAgentIds(...)`. The function starts processing from ID 11.
6. Service 10 is never revisited or linked automatically.

## Proof of Code
function test_Griefing_LinkServiceId() public {
    IdentityRegistryBridger bridger = new IdentityRegistryBridger(address(0x1), address(0x2), address(0x3), address(mockServiceRegistry));
    bridger.initialize();

    // 1. Setup: Service 10 exists but is undeployed (multisig = 0)
    vm.mockCall(address(mockServiceRegistry), abi.encodeWithSelector(IServiceRegistry.totalSupply.selector), abi.encode(100));
    // MapServices returns multisig=0 for ID 10
    vm.mockCall(address(mockServiceRegistry), abi.encodeWithSelector(IServiceRegistry.mapServices.selector, 10), abi.encode(0, address(0), bytes32(0), 0, 0, 0, 1));

    // 2. Attacker calls link covering ID 10 (assuming startLink is 1, need to cover up to 10)
    // We call it loop to advance to 10 or just call with 10 if start is 1
    bridger.linkServiceIdAgentIds(10);

    // 3. Verify startLinkServiceId advanced past 10
    assertEq(bridger.startLinkServiceId(), 11);

    // 4. Service 10 deploys (multisig set)
    vm.mockCall(address(mockServiceRegistry), abi.encodeWithSelector(IServiceRegistry.mapServices.selector, 10), abi.encode(0, address(0xABC), bytes32(0), 0, 0, 0, 4));

    // 5. Subsequent calls start from 11, skipping 10
    bridger.linkServiceIdAgentIds(1);
    // Service 10 remains unlinked (mock registry register not called)
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Modify `linkServiceIdAgentIds` to either stop iteration and not advance `startLinkServiceId` upon encountering a skipped (undeployed) service, or allow `startLinkServiceId` to be manually reset/managed.
```

### M-175 / `WoL3NZf5UT3hZ5L1LXESe`
- Finding title: Silent failure in recoverAccess due to unchecked return value
- Report lines: 13738-13787
```md
## [M-175]. Silent failure in recoverAccess due to unchecked return value

## id: WoL3NZf5UT3hZ5L1LXESe

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UncheckedLowLevelCallResults

## Exploit Type
UncheckedReturn

## Location
RecoveryModule.recoverAccess

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: IMultisig.execTransactionFromModule() returns a boolean and Gnosis Safe typically returns false (not revert) on internal execution failure. RecoveryModule.recoverAccess() ignores this return value and unconditionally emits AccessRecovered. If the multiSend delegatecall fails (e.g., out-of-gas, or a removeOwner/swapOwner reverts), the Safe state remains unchanged but off-chain observers/users see a success event. There is no post-condition check (e.g., verifying getOwners()) and no require(success), so the function can produce false-positive success signals.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `RecoveryModule.recoverAccess`, the contract executes the recovery transaction on the Safe via `IMultisig(multisig).execTransactionFromModule(..., IMultisig.Operation.DelegateCall)`. This function returns a `bool success`. The `RecoveryModule` ignores this return value. 

If the inner transaction (the `multiSend` batch) fails due to out-of-gas or other errors, `execTransactionFromModule` returns `false`, but `recoverAccess` continues execution. It subsequently releases the `_locked` guard and emits the `AccessRecovered` event. This leaves the system in an inconsistent state where off-chain observers and the service owner believe recovery was successful, while the Safe owners actually remain unchanged.

## Impact
Misleading state events and user confusion; service owner believes they have control when they do not.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. `recoverAccess` is called. 
2. `execTransactionFromModule` is invoked. 
3. The inner `multiSend` reverts (e.g. one of the `removeOwner` calls fails due to state mismatch or specific gas issue). 
4. `execTransactionFromModule` returns `false`. 
5. `recoverAccess` ignores the `false` return, emits `AccessRecovered`, and succeeds.

## Proof of Code
function testSilentFailure() public {
    // Mock multisig that returns false
    vm.mockCall(multisig, abi.encodeWithSelector(IMultisig.execTransactionFromModule.selector), abi.encode(false));
    recoveryModule.recoverAccess(serviceId);
    // Assert event emitted but return value ignored
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Add `require(IMultisig(multisig).execTransactionFromModule(...), "Recovery execution failed");` to ensure the transaction succeeded before emitting events.
```

### M-176 / `hGypbmuwsqA66Z53f4q42`
- Finding title: recoverAccess emits success event even if recovery transaction fails, violating post-conditions
- Report lines: 13788-13877
```md
## [M-176]. recoverAccess emits success event even if recovery transaction fails, violating post-conditions

## id: hGypbmuwsqA66Z53f4q42

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UncheckedReturn

## Exploit Type
UncheckedReturn

## Location
RecoveryModule.recoverAccess

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: RecoveryModule.recoverAccess() unconditionally emits AccessRecovered after calling execTransactionFromModule, without checking the returned success flag and without verifying post-state (owners/threshold). Because Safe module execution returns false on failure, any failure in multiSend (OOG, revert in removeOwner/swapOwner) will still produce a success event and unlock the reentrancy guard. This violates the implied post-condition “event means recovery happened,” creating misleading off-chain state and user confusion. No existing checks mitigate this.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `recoverAccess` function constructs a governance transaction to swap Safe owners and executes it via `execTransactionFromModule`. However, it ignores the boolean return value of this execution.

```solidity
// RecoveryModule.sol:159
// Execute module call
IMultisig(multisig).execTransactionFromModule(multiSend, 0, payload, IMultisig.Operation.DelegateCall);

emit AccessRecovered(msg.sender, serviceId);
```

If the inner transaction fails (e.g., due to insufficient gas limits in the internal Safe call, or state inconsistencies in the Safe that cause `removeOwner` to revert), `execTransactionFromModule` returns `false` (standard Safe behavior). The `RecoveryModule` ignores this, emits `AccessRecovered`, and unlocks. The Safe owners are NOT updated, violating the invariant that successful execution results in the Service Owner gaining control. This provides misleading assurance to the user and off-chain indexers.

## Impact
The `AccessRecovered` event is emitted without the actual recovery taking place. Users may believe they have regained control when they have not. While funds are not directly stolen, the module fails to guarantee its core functionality and post-conditions.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Service Owner calls `recoverAccess` with a gas limit that is sufficient for the `RecoveryModule` logic but insufficient for the inner Safe `multiSend` execution.
2. `IMultisig(multisig).execTransactionFromModule` executes, the inner delegatecall fails out of gas, catching the error and returning `false`.
3. `recoverAccess` continues execution, emits `AccessRecovered`, and returns success.
4. Service Owner observes the success event but still does not have ownership of the Safe.

## Proof of Code
/* 
Add this to a test file. 
Assumes mocks for IMultisig that returns false on exec.
*/

contract MockMultisigFail {
    function getOwners() external pure returns (address[] memory) {
        address[] memory owners = new address[](1);
        owners[0] = address(0x1);
        return owners;
    }
    function execTransactionFromModule(address, uint256, bytes calldata, uint8) external pure returns (bool) {
        return false; // Simulate failure
    }
}

contract RecoveryUncheckedTest is Test {
    RecoveryModule recovery;
    MockRegistry registry;
    MockMultisigFail multisig;
    address constant SERVICE_OWNER = address(0xABCD);

    function setUp() public {
        registry = new MockRegistry(); // Mock registry returns PreRegistration
        multisig = new MockMultisigFail();
        recovery = new RecoveryModule(address(0x999), address(registry));
    }

    function testRecoverAccessUncheckedReturn() public {
        // Setup mock registry to return the failing multisig
        // ... (MockRegistry implementation needs to return address(multisig))
        
        vm.startPrank(SERVICE_OWNER);
        // Call should succeed despite inner failure
        recovery.recoverAccess(1);
        // Event AccessRecovered is emitted (checked via vm.expectEmit if needed)
        vm.stopPrank();
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Require the return value of the execution call: `require(IMultisig(multisig).execTransactionFromModule(...), "Recovery failed");`
```

### M-177 / `YUC7uB28ZCLYVSLTsA9mU`
- Finding title: Lack of user-specified slippage or deadline bounds exposes protocol to market movements
- Report lines: 13878-13920
```md
## [M-177]. Lack of user-specified slippage or deadline bounds exposes protocol to market movements

## id: YUC7uB28ZCLYVSLTsA9mU

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Exploit Type
SlippageMissingOrInsufficient

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.convertToV3

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToLowImpact
### Finding Status Justification: LiquidityManagerCore does not accept user-provided amountMin/deadline parameters for V3 operations; mins are derived from execution-time computations and deadline is set to block.timestamp. This can allow execution under different market conditions than intended if a transaction is delayed. While slippage mins exist (maxSlippage), they are not anchored to a user-committed price at signing time.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
Functions like `convertToV3`, `decreaseLiquidity`, and `increaseLiquidity` in `LiquidityManagerCore` do not accept `amountMin` or `deadline` parameters from the caller. Instead, they calculate minimum amounts based on the `sqrtPriceX96` fetched *at the time of execution* and the configured `maxSlippage`. `block.timestamp` is used as the deadline for Uniswap calls. This protects against sandwich attacks on the *execution* itself, but fails to protect the user (Owner) from price movements while the transaction is pending in the mempool. If the price moves significantly between signing and execution, the transaction proceeds using the new price, potentially executing trades/liquidity provision at a value unacceptable to the Owner.

## Impact
Protocol can suffer loss of value if market moves against the intended price while a transaction is pending.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Owner signs `increaseLiquidity` when price is 1000. 2. Transaction stays in mempool. Price moves to 1200. 3. Transaction executes. Contract reads 1200. Calculates `amountMin` based on 1200 (minus 1% slippage). 4. Liquidity added at 1200. 5. Owner accepts 20% worse price than intended.

## Proof of Code
contract TestMissingSlippage is Test { function testDynamicSlippage() public { // Concept test: verify amountsMin depends on current spot // Mock pool price change // Assert amountsMin changes } }

## Suggested Mitigation
Add `amount0Min`, `amount1Min`, and `deadline` parameters to sensitive external functions to allow the caller to enforce their intent.





Finding Status: InvalidByDesign
```

### M-178 / `AUIyDyMLaqT1hgK2rZAxN`
- Finding title: Minority Censorship via Permissionless Proposal Front-running
- Report lines: 13921-13989
```md
## [M-178]. Minority Censorship via Permissionless Proposal Front-running

## id: AUIyDyMLaqT1hgK2rZAxN

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
GovernanceFrontrunDoS

## Exploit Type
GovernanceFrontrunDoS

## Location
GovernorOLAS.propose

## Finding Status: InvalidByDesign
### Finding Status Justification: Code path exists: GovernorOLAS.propose() just calls OZ Governor.propose(), where proposalId is derived from hashProposal(targets, values, calldatas, descriptionHash) and does not include msg.sender. Thus identical calldata/description creates the same proposalId regardless of proposer, so a mempool front-run with the same proposal can cause the original tx to revert due to an existing proposalId. This is a real, reproducible griefing/censorship vector.

However, this behavior matches OpenZeppelin Governor’s design (proposalId is content-addressed), so it is largely “by design”. The practical exploit is delaying/censoring a specific exact proposal payload (until the honest proposer salts description/changes content). Impact is governance-process DoS/delay, not fund loss. No on-chain safeguard exists (other than off-chain practice of unique descriptions).
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `GovernorOLAS` contract (inheriting OZ Governor logic) determines the unique `proposalId` by hashing the proposal content (targets, values, calldatas, descriptionHash), without including the `msg.sender` (proposer). 

This allows an attacker who monitors the mempool to front-run any call to `propose()` with an identical transaction. By doing so, the attacker's transaction is processed first, making them the registered proposer of that specific payload. The original proposer's transaction then reverts with `Governor: proposal already exists`.

If the attacker holds sufficient voting power to meet the `proposalThreshold`, they retain the exclusive right to cancel the proposal via `cancel()`. This enables a minority token holder (meeting only the threshold, not a majority) to hijack and subsequently cancel any proposal they disagree with, forcing the community to modify proposal parameters (e.g., salt the description) and restart the voting process repeatedly.

## Impact
A minority actor can effectively veto or significantly delay proposals they dislike without needing a majority vote, causing griefing and governance inefficiency.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Honest user broadcasts `propose(targets, ..., description)`.
2. Attacker (holding `proposalThreshold` votes) sees the tx and sends the exact same `propose` call with higher gas.
3. Attacker's tx confirms first. `proposalId` is created with Attacker as proposer.
4. User's tx reverts.
5. Attacker waits for the proposal to gain support or reach a critical time, then calls `cancel(proposalId)`.
6. The proposal is dead. User must re-propose with a different description.

## Proof of Code
function testFrontRunProposal() public {
    // Attacker setup
    vm.prank(attacker);
    governor.propose(targets, values, calldatas, "Proposal A");
    uint256 id = governor.hashProposal(targets, values, calldatas, keccak256(bytes("Proposal A")));
    
    // Honest user tries to propose same content
    vm.prank(honestUser);
    vm.expectRevert("Governor: proposal already exists");
    governor.propose(targets, values, calldatas, "Proposal A");

    // Attacker cancels
    vm.prank(attacker);
    governor.cancel(targets, values, calldatas, keccak256(bytes("Proposal A")));
    assertEq(uint(governor.state(id)), uint(ProposalState.Canceled));
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Include `msg.sender` in the `hashProposal` calculation to make proposal IDs unique to the proposer, or restrict `cancel` to only be callable if the proposer's voting power drops below the threshold.





Finding Status: InvalidFutureSpeculation
```

### M-179 / `eL7IuKNAqxDZc79uVkeBk`
- Finding title: Supply Cap logic violates monotonicity invariant causing cap decrease at Year 10
- Report lines: 13990-14052
```md
## [M-179]. Supply Cap logic violates monotonicity invariant causing cap decrease at Year 10

## id: eL7IuKNAqxDZc79uVkeBk

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Exploit Type
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Location
TokenomicsConstants.getSupplyCapForYear

## Finding Status: InvalidFutureSpeculation
### Finding Status Justification: Not exploitable/low impact/low likelihood are not fully supported: the cap regression (cap(10) < cap(9)) is real and, if getSupplyCapForYear is used anywhere for on-chain cap enforcement (likely given it is a TokenomicsConstants primitive), it can cause protocol-wide reverts/DoS once supply exceeds the lower year-10 cap. This is potentially high impact and not a 'rare' condition if the system mints anywhere near the year-9 table cap before year 10.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `getSupplyCapForYear` function defines the maximum token supply schedule. For the first 10 years (0-9), it uses a hardcoded array where the Year 9 cap is `803,867,052e18`. For year 10 and beyond, it switches to a dynamic calculation using `_calculateSupplyCapAfterYear10` which starts from the constant `SUPPLY_CAP_YEAR10` (`761,726,593e18`). 

Calculating the Year 10 cap: `761,726,593e18 * 1.02 = 776,961,124e18` (approx).
This value is ~27 million tokens lower than the Year 9 cap (`803,867,052e18`). This violates the invariant that the token supply cap must be non-decreasing. If the actual token supply grows into the range between 777M and 803M during the first 10 years (which is permitted by the Year 9 cap), the protocol will experience a Denial of Service at Year 10, as the cap effectively shrinks below the existing supply.

## Impact
The authorized supply cap decreases at Year 10. If `totalSupply` is within the valid range of Year 9 (e.g. 780M), it will exceed the cap in Year 10, causing minting or checkpointing functions to revert.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Call `getSupplyCapForYear(9)` and observe `803,867,052e18`.
2. Call `getSupplyCapForYear(10)` and observe `~776,961,124e18`.
3. Verify `cap(10) < cap(9)`, confirming the regression.

## Proof of Code
pragma solidity ^0.8.30;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/TokenomicsConstants.sol";

contract Harness is TokenomicsConstants {}

contract CapTest is Test {
    Harness h;
    function setUp() public {
        h = new Harness();
    }
    function testCapDrop() public {
        uint256 cap9 = h.getSupplyCapForYear(9);
        uint256 cap10 = h.getSupplyCapForYear(10);
        emit log_named_uint("Cap Year 9", cap9);
        emit log_named_uint("Cap Year 10", cap10);
        // Invariant: Supply cap should not decrease
        assertGt(cap10, cap9, "Supply Cap decreased at Year 10");
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Ensure `SUPPLY_CAP_YEAR10` matches the Year 9 value of the `getSupplyCapForYear` schedule (update it to `803,867,052e18` or introduce a separate constant `MAX_SUPPLY_CAP_YEAR10` for the higher schedule).
```

### M-180 / `BJnRIM5tSKusbSb_8JkEm`
- Finding title: Non-monotonic supply cap curve in TokenomicsConstants triggers potential DoS
- Report lines: 14053-14114
```md
## [M-180]. Non-monotonic supply cap curve in TokenomicsConstants triggers potential DoS

## id: BJnRIM5tSKusbSb_8JkEm

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Arithmetic Invariant

## Exploit Type
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Location
TokenomicsConstants.getSupplyCapForYear

## Finding Status: InvalidFutureSpeculation
### Finding Status Justification: Same as the prior finding: the non-monotonicity is deterministic, and if any core protocol logic relies on getSupplyCapForYear for cap checks, it can create a hard DoS/brick condition (high impact). That outcome is not inherently 'rare' if supply approaches the year-9 cap prior to year 10.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `getSupplyCapForYear` function violates the invariant that the supply cap should be non-decreasing. For years 0-9, it returns values from a hardcoded array, ending at `803,867,052e18` for year 9. For years >= 10, it switches to a calculation based on `SUPPLY_CAP_YEAR10` (`761,726,593e18`) increasing by 2% annually. 

At year 10, the calculated value is `761,726,593e18 * 1.02 = 776,961,124e18`. This is significantly lower than the year 9 cap (`803M` vs `776M`). 

If the protocol's total supply grows into the range `(776M, 803M]` during year 9 (which is permitted), then upon transitioning to year 10, the total supply will exceed the new supply cap. This will likely cause minting or reward distribution functions to revert (DoS) due to the cap check, freezing protocol inflation for approximately 2 years until the calculated cap catches up to the year 9 level.

## Impact
Protocol inflation and rewards may be blocked for up to 2 years if total supply reaches the upper bound of the Year 9 cap.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy a contract inheriting `TokenomicsConstants`. 2. Call `getSupplyCapForYear(9)` and store the result. 3. Call `getSupplyCapForYear(10)` and store the result. 4. Assert that `cap(10) >= cap(9)`. The assertion fails as `cap(10) < cap(9)`.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/TokenomicsConstants.sol";

contract ConcreteConstants is TokenomicsConstants {}

contract SupplyCapTest is Test {
    ConcreteConstants c;
    function setUp() public { c = new ConcreteConstants(); }

    function testSupplyCapMonotonicity() public {
        uint256 cap9 = c.getSupplyCapForYear(9);
        uint256 cap10 = c.getSupplyCapForYear(10);
        // Invariant: Supply cap should not decrease
        assertGt(cap10, cap9, "Supply cap decreased at Year 10");
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
In `getSupplyCapForYear`, for the `else` block (years >= 10), use the Year 9 array value (`803_867_052e18`) as the base for the compound interest calculation instead of `SUPPLY_CAP_YEAR10`, ensuring the curve is continuous and monotonic.





Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidGovernanceRisk
```

### M-181 / `kwZqJip6TbQ4WUh4C990y`
- Finding title: Storage Corruption Risk via Delegatecall to Bridge Verifiers
- Report lines: 14115-14163
```md
## [M-181]. Storage Corruption Risk via Delegatecall to Bridge Verifiers

## id: kwZqJip6TbQ4WUh4C990y

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UntrustedDelegateCall

## Exploit Type
UntrustedDelegateCall

## Location
GuardCM._verifySchedule

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: The risk stems from the `delegatecall` design itself: even if governance selects verifiers in good faith, any verifier bug/unintended storage write can corrupt GuardCM; it’s not necessarily a mere operator “mistake”.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresAdminRole


## Description
The `_verifySchedule` function uses `delegatecall` to invoke configured `verifierL2` contracts (`bridgeParams.verifierL2.delegatecall(...)`). This executes the verifier logic in the context of `GuardCM`'s storage. If a verifier contract is malicious, buggy, or simply has a colliding storage layout (e.g., writing to slot 1 which holds `governor` and `paused`), it can corrupt `GuardCM`'s state. While verifiers are set by the owner, using `delegatecall` violates the principle of least privilege, as read-only verification or external processing is sufficient.

## Impact
Potential complete takeover or bricking of the Guard if a verifier writes to storage. Corruption of `paused` state or `governor` address.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Owner configures a `verifierL2` that has a state variable at Slot 1 (or writes to Slot 1).
2. Multisig schedules a transaction targeting the bridge mediator associated with that verifier.
3. `_verifySchedule` delegatecalls the verifier.
4. Verifier writes to Slot 1, overwriting `paused` and `governor` in `GuardCM`.
5. Guard is broken or paused.

## Proof of Code
contract MaliciousVerifier {
    function processBridgeData(bytes memory, address, uint256) external {
        // Overwrite slot 1 (governor + paused)
        assembly { sstore(1, 2) } // Set paused = 2
    }
}
// PoC test would configure this verifier and trigger checkTransaction

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `staticcall` if the verifier logic is pure, or ensure verifiers strictly adhere to a safe storage layout (e.g., using unstructured storage or appending only). Ideally, refactor to avoid `delegatecall` for external verification.
```

### H-182 / `AijNRtIqKSkPQhug82ksx`
- Finding title: Unclaimed rewards theft via registry key collision in changeRegistries
- Report lines: 14164-14227
```md
## [H-182]. Unclaimed rewards theft via registry key collision in changeRegistries

## id: AijNRtIqKSkPQhug82ksx

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
ExternalProtocolKeyCollision

## Exploit Type
ExternalProtocolKeyCollision

## Location
Tokenomics.changeRegistries

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: Tokenomics.changeRegistries allows the owner to swap componentRegistry/agentRegistry. Incentives are stored in `mapUnitIncentives[unitType][unitId]` without scoping to the registry address. accountOwnerIncentives verifies ownership against the *current* registry via ownerOf(unitId). Therefore, after a registry change, any overlapping unitId in the new registry can claim incentives accrued under the old registry’s unitId. There is no settlement/clearing requirement enforced before registry change, and no generation/registry keying. This is exploitable if/when the owner performs a registry migration and IDs overlap (very plausible), hence governance-associated but still a real theft vector.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresAdminRole


## Description
The `Tokenomics` contract tracks rewards using `unitId` keys derived from external registries (`ComponentRegistry`, `AgentRegistry`). These rewards persist in the `mapUnitIncentives` mapping. The contract allows the owner to swap these registries via `changeRegistries()`. If the registries are changed, the new registry may issue the same `unitId` to a different owner. When `accountOwnerIncentives()` is called, it verifies ownership against the *current* registry (`registries[unitTypes[i]].ownerOf(unitIds[i])`). This allows a user who owns `unitId` 1 in the new registry to claim rewards accumulated by `unitId` 1 from the old registry, effectively stealing funds. This is a direct instance of the 'ExternalProtocolKeyCollision' pattern where mapping keys are not scoped to the provider source.

## Impact
Theft of all unclaimed rewards accumulated by units from the previous registry if IDs overlap.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Epoch 1: Unit 1 (owned by Alice in Registry A) accumulates 100 ETH rewards in `mapUnitIncentives`. Alice does not claim yet.
2. Admin calls `changeRegistries`, setting ComponentRegistry to Registry B.
3. Registry B has Unit 1 owned by Bob.
4. Bob calls `accountOwnerIncentives(Bob, [0], [1])`.
5. `Tokenomics` checks `RegistryB.ownerOf(1)` which returns Bob.
6. Bob passes the check and the contract transfers Alice's 100 ETH to Bob.

## Proof of Code
function testKeyCollision() public {
    // Setup: Registry A with Unit 1 owned by Alice
    mockRegistryA.setOwner(1, alice);
    tokenomics.changeRegistries(address(mockRegistryA), address(0), address(0));
    
    // Simulate rewards for Unit 1
    vm.prank(address(treasury));
    uint256[] memory ids = new uint256[](1); ids[0] = 1;
    uint256[] memory amts = new uint256[](1); amts[0] = 100 ether;
    tokenomics.trackServiceDonations(donator, serviceIds, amts, 100 ether);
    
    // Admin switches to Registry B where Unit 1 is owned by Bob
    mockRegistryB.setOwner(1, bob);
    vm.prank(owner);
    tokenomics.changeRegistries(address(mockRegistryB), address(0), address(0));
    
    // Bob claims Alice's rewards
    vm.prank(address(dispenser));
    (uint256 reward, ) = tokenomics.accountOwnerIncentives(bob, unitTypes, ids);
    assertEq(reward, 100 ether); // Bob stole the funds
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Include the registry address or a generation ID in the `mapUnitIncentives` key (e.g., `mapping(address => mapping(uint256 => IncentiveBalances))`), or forcefully settle/clear all pending incentives before allowing a registry change.
```

### M-183 / `wyY9IFelHr2w0yz44ZH9c`
- Finding title: Inconsistent Inflation Schedule Usage between Checkpoint and Update Functions
- Report lines: 14228-14277
```md
## [M-183]. Inconsistent Inflation Schedule Usage between Checkpoint and Update Functions

## id: wyY9IFelHr2w0yz44ZH9c

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Exploit Type
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Location
Tokenomics.checkpoint

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: checkpoint uses getInflationForYear(numYears) while updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions uses getActualInflationForYear(currentYear). In TokenomicsConstants, these differ materially (e.g., year 0: getInflationForYear(0)=3,159,000e18 while getActualInflationForYear(0)=0). Thus an owner call to updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions can abruptly change inflationPerSecond to a contradictory schedule value (potentially zero), altering maxBond/effectiveBond and emissions behavior. This is owner-only, so governance-controlled but still a real inconsistency that can break economic expectations.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `Tokenomics` contract uses two different functions to determine the inflation rate, which return contradictory values. `checkpoint()` calls `TokenomicsConstants.getInflationForYear(numYears)` to set `curInflationPerSecond` upon a year change. `updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions()` calls `TokenomicsConstants.getActualInflationForYear(currentYear)`. In `TokenomicsConstants.sol`, `getInflationForYear(0)` returns `~3.159e24` (3M OLAS), while `getActualInflationForYear(0)` returns `0`. This inconsistency means the inflation rate fluctuates significantly depending on whether it is updated automatically by a checkpoint or manually by the owner, leading to unpredictable token issuance.

## Impact
Unpredictable and inconsistent OLAS inflation. Calling `updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions` could unintendedly drop the inflation rate to zero (or a lower schedule) until the next year boundary, or vice versa, violating the economic design.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy Tokenomics. 2. Observe `inflationPerSecond` initialized using `getInflationForYear(0)` (High Value). 3. Call `updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions(...)`. 4. Observe `inflationPerSecond` update using `getActualInflationForYear(0)` (Zero/Low Value). 5. The inflation rate has drastically changed without a change in the underlying year.

## Proof of Code
contract InflationTest is Test {
    TokenomicsConstants tc;
    function setUp() public {
        tc = new TokenomicsConstantsMock(); // Inherits abstract
    }
    function test_InflationMismatch() public {
        uint256 high = tc.getInflationForYear(0);
        uint256 low = tc.getActualInflationForYear(0);
        assertTrue(high > low, 'Inflation schedules should match or be consistent');
    }
}
contract TokenomicsConstantsMock is TokenomicsConstants {}

## Suggested Mitigation
Standardize the usage of inflation lookup functions. Ensure both `checkpoint()` and `updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions()` use the same function (likely `getActualInflationForYear` if that represents the intended schedule, or `getInflationForYear` if the higher cap is intended).
```

### M-184 / `I03GutRGM72fRRxlk9i8H`
- Finding title: Permissionless storage corruption via delegatecall to stateful verifier in checkTransaction
- Report lines: 14278-14370
```md
## [M-184]. Permissionless storage corruption via delegatecall to stateful verifier in checkTransaction

## id: I03GutRGM72fRRxlk9i8H

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UntrustedDelegateCall

## Exploit Type
UntrustedDelegateCall

## Location
GuardCM.checkTransaction

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidGovernanceRisk
### Finding Status Justification: The permissionless trigger (`checkTransaction` is unrestricted) plus `delegatecall` is a design hazard: if a configured verifier ever writes to storage, *anyone* can trigger state mutation; that is not inherently a user “mistake” (it can arise from verifier implementation details/bugs).
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `checkTransaction` function is `external` and can be called by any user. When verifying a transaction that targets a bridge mediator, `GuardCM` executes `bridgeParams.verifierL2.delegatecall(processData)`. Since `delegatecall` runs in the context of `GuardCM`, any state writes performed by `verifierL2` will modify `GuardCM`'s storage. If the DAO configures a verifier that is stateful (e.g., uses `ReentrancyGuard` which writes to slot 0, or initializes variables), an attacker can call `checkTransaction` with crafted data to trigger these writes. This can overwrite critical storage slots such as `owner` (slot 0), `governor`, or the allowlist mappings, permanently compromising the guard.

## Impact
An attacker can overwrite the `owner` address or other critical state, leading to a permanent Denial of Service (locking the protocol) or allowing unauthorized transactions by corrupting the allowlist.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Governance configures a `verifierL2` that contains a state write (e.g., standard `ReentrancyGuard` or an initializer). 
2. Attacker calls `GuardCM.checkTransaction` passing `to` as the Timelock and `data` containing a schedule payload for the relevant bridge mediator. 
3. `GuardCM` extracts the payload and delegatecalls `verifierL2`. 
4. `verifierL2` writes to storage slot 0 (colliding with `owner`). 
5. `GuardCM.owner` is changed to a garbage value, preventing further configuration updates or unpausing.

## Proof of Code
contract MaliciousVerifier {
    // Slot 0 collision with GuardCM.owner
    uint256 public status;
    function processBridgeData(bytes memory, address, uint256) external {
        status = 2; // Write to storage
    }
}

contract GuardDelegateTest is Test {
    GuardCM guard;
    MaliciousVerifier verifier;
    address owner = address(this);
    address multisig = address(0x2);
    address governor = address(0x3);
    address mediator = address(0x4);

    function setUp() public {
        guard = new GuardCM(owner, multisig, governor);
        verifier = new MaliciousVerifier();
        
        address[] memory l1s = new address[](1);
        address[] memory verifiers = new address[](1);
        uint256[] memory chains = new uint256[](1);
        address[] memory l2s = new address[](1);
        
        l1s[0] = mediator;
        verifiers[0] = address(verifier);
        chains[0] = 1;
        l2s[0] = address(0);
        
        guard.setBridgeMediatorL1BridgeParams(l1s, verifiers, chains, l2s);
    }

    function testCorruption() public {
        // Construct schedule payload targeting the mediator
        bytes memory scheduleData = abi.encode(mediator, uint256(0), hex"112233", bytes32(0), bytes32(0), uint256(0));
        // Wrap in selector
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(guard.SCHEDULE(), scheduleData);

        // Call checkTransaction (permissionless)
        guard.checkTransaction(owner, 0, data, Enum.Operation.Call, 0, 0, 0, address(0), payable(0), hex"", address(0));

        // Check if owner is corrupted (Slot 0)
        // MaliciousVerifier wrote 2 to slot 0.
        assertEq(address(guard.owner()), address(2));
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use `call` or `staticcall` instead of `delegatecall` to execute verifier logic. This isolates the storage context. If `delegatecall` is strictly required for some reason, `checkTransaction` should be restricted or marked `view` (if supported by the Safe integration) to prevent state changes.





Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood
```

### M-185 / `5CYGtorHpzjecOuSXHiTi`
- Finding title: Protocol actions DoS due to ZeroValue check in _checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2
- Report lines: 14371-14428
```md
## [M-185]. Protocol actions DoS due to ZeroValue check in _checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2

## id: 5CYGtorHpzjecOuSXHiTi

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
LiquidityManagerOptimism._checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood
### Finding Status Justification: _checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2 reverts if getPoolTokens returns amounts[0]==0 || amounts[1]==0. This check is on pool balances, not on the exit results. While extreme, a pool can theoretically end up with one token balance at 0 (or deregistration/asset-manager edge states), in which case it may still be desirable to redeem the remaining token; the current code would hard-revert and block migration. This is a DoS edge case rather than an immediate theft vector.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The function `_checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2` in `LiquidityManagerOptimism.sol` performs a check on the token amounts returned from a Balancer pool: `if (amounts[0] == 0 || amounts[1] == 0) revert ZeroValue();`. This check enforces that the protocol's share of liquidity in the Balancer pool must result in non-zero amounts for *both* tokens.

However, it is possible for a Balancer pool to become imbalanced (one asset drained or price extreme) such that `exitPool` would return 0 for one of the assets. In such a scenario, the `LiquidityManager` should ideally still withdraw the remaining asset and the BPT should be redeemed. The current strict check causes the transaction to revert, permanently locking the BPTs and the non-zero asset balance within the `LiquidityManager` contract as long as the pool remains in that state.

## Impact
If a managed Balancer pool becomes extremely imbalanced (one token amount is 0), the `LiquidityManager` is unable to exit its position (`convertToV3` reverts). This leads to stuck funds (BPTs and the remaining token value) and prevents the DAO from migrating liquidity during potential emergency conditions.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. `LiquidityManager` holds BPT for a pool of TokenA and TokenB.
2. The Balancer pool state changes such that `exitPool` for the held BPT amount would return `1000 TokenA` and `0 TokenB` (due to extreme imbalance or rounding).
3. The DAO calls `convertToV3` to migrate liquidity.
4. `_checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2` is called.
5. It retrieves `amounts` from `getPoolTokens` (or calculated exit amounts). The logic checks `amounts[0] == 0 || amounts[1] == 0`.
6. The check fails and reverts `ZeroValue`.
7. The protocol cannot exit the position.

## Proof of Code
contract MockBalancer {
    function getPool(bytes32) external view returns (address, uint8) { return (address(this), 0); }
    function getPoolTokens(bytes32) external view returns (address[] memory t, uint256[] memory b, uint256) {
        t = new address[](2); t[0]=address(1); t[1]=address(2);
        b = new uint256[](2); b[0]=100; b[1]=0; // Imbalanced
        return (t, b, 0);
    }
    function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint256) { return 100; }
    function exitPool(bytes32, address, address,  tuple(address[],uint256[],bytes,bool)) external {}
}
// In a test importing LiquidityManagerOptimism and using this mock:
// calling _checkTokensAndRemoveLiquidityV2 would revert ZeroValue despite 100 TokenA being available.

## Suggested Mitigation
Remove the strict requirement that both amounts must be non-zero. Allow exiting even if one amount is zero to prevent fund lockup.
```

### H-186 / `qX92yQsD97rajkQC9uJtZ`
- Finding title: Missing Source Chain ID Verification in `_receiveMessage`
- Report lines: 14429-14498
```md
## [H-186]. Missing Source Chain ID Verification in `_receiveMessage`

## id: qX92yQsD97rajkQC9uJtZ

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
CrossChainMessageSpoofing

## Exploit Type
CrossChainMessageSpoofing

## Location
DefaultTargetDispenserL2._receiveMessage

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood
### Finding Status Justification: DefaultTargetDispenserL2 stores l1SourceChainId but _receiveMessage() never checks it; it only validates (messageRelayer == l2MessageRelayer) and (sourceProcessor == l1DepositProcessor). The missing check is real in the shown code path and there is even a WrongChainId error defined, suggesting chain/domain verification is an intended invariant. If the chosen bridge/relayer can deliver messages from multiple source chains while preserving the same “source address” (e.g., via CREATE2 address mirroring on another chain), this becomes message-spoofing that can trigger _processData and distribute OLAS to attacker-controlled targets. Exploitability depends on bridge properties, hence low likelihood, but impact is high if feasible.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_receiveMessage` function validates the `messageRelayer` and `sourceProcessor` addresses but fails to verify that the message originated from the expected L1 chain ID (`l1SourceChainId`). In cross-chain environments where the relayer (e.g., Wormhole, LayerZero) supports multiple source chains, an attacker can deploy a contract at the same address as the `l1DepositProcessor` on a different chain (e.g., via CREATE2) and send a malicious payload. The L2 dispenser would accept this spoofed message, allowing the attacker to trigger unauthorized distribution of OLAS tokens.

## Impact
An attacker can spoof legitimate L1 messages from a different chain, allowing them to drain the dispenser's OLAS balance by submitting fake staking targets.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker deploys a contract on a sidechain (Chain ID 999) at the same address as `l1DepositProcessor` (Address A). 2. Attacker uses a generic bridge (supported by `l2MessageRelayer`) to send a message from Chain 999/Address A to the L2 Dispenser. 3. `_receiveMessage` checks `sourceProcessor == Address A` (True). 4. It does not check the source chain ID. 5. The payload is processed, and tokens are dispensed to attacker-controlled targets.

## Proof of Code
contract SpoofTest is Test {
    MockDispenser dispenser;
    address relayer = address(2);
    address processor = address(3);
    // MockToken, MockFactory defined previously

    function setUp() public {
        dispenser = new MockDispenser(address(new MockToken()), address(new MockFactory()), relayer, processor, 1);
    }

    function testSpoofing() public {
        // Ideally, _receiveMessage should take sourceChainId and compare it to 1.
        // Since it doesn't, passing a message "from" chain 999 but correct address succeeds.
        
        // Simulate bridge wrapper passing data but NOT checking chain ID (as per contract abstract logic)
        // We assume the abstract contract implies the check is sufficient.
        
        // In a real test, we would show the wrapper calling _receiveMessage without chainId arg.
        // Here we demonstrate the logic inside _receiveMessage doesn't check l1SourceChainId.
        
        // 1. Prepare data
        address[] memory targets = new address[](1); targets[0] = address(0x123);
        uint256[] memory amounts = new uint256[](1); amounts[0] = 100;
        bytes memory data = abi.encode(targets, amounts, bytes32(uint256(1)));

        // 2. Call receiveMessage. 
        // Even if we assume the wrapper *knows* the chain ID is 999, it can't pass it to _receiveMessage to check against stored l1SourceChainId.
        vm.prank(relayer);
        MockDispenser(address(dispenser)).receiveMessageWrapper(relayer, processor, data);
        // Success implies vulnerability if relayer supports multi-chain.
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Modify `_receiveMessage` to accept a `sourceChainId` argument and enforce `require(sourceChainId == l1SourceChainId, "WrongChainId")`. Ensure the concrete implementation extracts the chain ID from the bridge payload and passes it securely.
```

### M-187 / `SCfdY55mMN213tR4nUljs`
- Finding title: Inflation cap violation due to effectiveBond reset in updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions
- Report lines: 14499-14560
```md
## [M-187]. Inflation cap violation due to effectiveBond reset in updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions

## id: SCfdY55mMN213tR4nUljs

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
GlobalParamMidFlowManipulation

## Exploit Type
GlobalParamMidFlowManipulation

## Location
Tokenomics.updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood
### Finding Status Justification: Owner-only gating makes it operationally gated, but the flaw can manifest even when the owner calls the function for its stated purpose (not necessarily an admin 'mistake'); it can reset/restore bond capacity mid-epoch and violate intended caps.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresAdminRole


## Description
The `updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions` function in `Tokenomics.sol` allows the owner to update inflation parameters mid-flow. When called, it recalculates `curMaxBond` based on the new parameters and incorrectly resets `effectiveBond` to `curMaxBond` (`effectiveBond = uint96(curMaxBond);`). `effectiveBond` is designed to track the *remaining* bond capacity for the current epoch and should account for capacity already consumed by `reserveAmountForBondProgram` in the current epoch. By resetting it to the full cap, the function ignores previously reserved amounts, allowing the Depository to reserve bonds significantly exceeding the epoch's intended inflation cap.

## Impact
Violation of the protocol's hard inflation cap for the epoch, allowing excess OLAS minting rights.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Epoch starts with `maxBond` = 1000. `effectiveBond` = 1000.
2. Depository calls `reserveAmountForBondProgram(1000)`. `effectiveBond` becomes 0.
3. Admin calls `updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions`. `curMaxBond` is calculated as 1000 (assuming similar params).
4. Function sets `effectiveBond = 1000` (resetting usage).
5. Depository calls `reserveAmountForBondProgram(1000)` again.
6. Total reserved is 2000, exceeding the 1000 cap.

## Proof of Code
function testEffectiveBondReset() public {
    // Assume epoch started, maxBond = 1000
    vm.prank(depository);
    tokenomics.reserveAmountForBondProgram(1000);
    assertEq(tokenomics.effectiveBond(), 0);
    
    // Admin updates inflation params
    vm.prank(owner);
    // Params chosen to keep maxBond roughly same
    tokenomics.updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions(50, 41, 9, 0);
    
    // Effective bond is incorrectly reset to full capacity
    assertGt(tokenomics.effectiveBond(), 0);
    
    // Depository can reserve again, breaking cap
    vm.prank(depository);
    bool success = tokenomics.reserveAmountForBondProgram(tokenomics.effectiveBond());
    assertTrue(success);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Adjust `effectiveBond` by the difference between the old and new max bond instead of overwriting it. E.g., `effectiveBond = effectiveBond + newMaxBond - oldMaxBond` (with underflow protection).
```

### M-188 / `QaIXRsTgON5X9mOYIdjRf`
- Finding title: Refunded staking incentives are permanently erased during inflation update
- Report lines: 14561-14614
```md
## [M-188]. Refunded staking incentives are permanently erased during inflation update

## id: QaIXRsTgON5X9mOYIdjRf

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Exploit Type
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Location
Tokenomics.updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood
### Finding Status Justification: Although owner-only, the state wipe (stakingIncentive = 0) can erase legitimately refunded staking amounts during the intended retain flow; this is a concrete accounting bug, not merely an admin misconfiguration.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresAdminRole


## Description
In `Tokenomics.sol`, the function `updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions` is designed to retrieve unused staking incentives from the Dispenser and update inflation parameters. The function iterates to call `dispenser.retain()`, which triggers a callback to `tokenomics.refundFromStaking(amount)`. This callback correctly adds the refunded amount to `mapEpochStakingPoints[curEpochCounter].stakingIncentive`. However, immediately after the loop, the function executes `mapEpochStakingPoints[curEpochCounter].stakingIncentive = 0;`. This unconditional assignment wipes out the refunded amounts that were just added, permanently removing them from the protocol's accounting. These funds are effectively lost and will not be included in the `checkpoint` calculation for the epoch (where they are meant to be added to `incentives[8]`), nor are they added to `effectiveBond`.

## Impact
Permanent loss of refunded staking incentives (OLAS tokens) from the accounting system, reducing the rewards available for future distribution or bonding.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Admin calls `Tokenomics.updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions`. 2. The function calls `dispenser.retain()`. 3. Dispenser calls `tokenomics.refundFromStaking(1000e18)`. 4. `mapEpochStakingPoints[epoch].stakingIncentive` increases by 1000e18. 5. `updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions` continues and executes `mapEpochStakingPoints[epoch].stakingIncentive = 0`. 6. The 1000e18 value is erased. 7. Subsequent `checkpoint` calls see 0 for `incentives[7]` (returned staking), effectively burning the accounting record of these tokens.

## Proof of Code
function testStakingIncentiveWipe() public {
    // Setup: simulate dispenser returning funds
    vm.prank(address(dispenser));
    tokenomics.refundFromStaking(1000e18);
    uint256 epoch = tokenomics.epochCounter();
    (uint96 incentive,,,) = tokenomics.mapEpochStakingPoints(epoch);
    assertEq(incentive, 1000e18);

    // Action: Admin updates inflation
    vm.prank(tokenomics.owner());
    tokenomics.updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions(10, 40, 40, 10);

    // Assert: Incentive is wiped
    (incentive,,,) = tokenomics.mapEpochStakingPoints(epoch);
    assertEq(incentive, 0);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Remove the line `mapEpochStakingPoints[curEpochCounter].stakingIncentive = 0;` in `updateInflationPerSecondAndFractions`. If the intention is to reset for a new logic, the existing value must first be preserved (e.g., added to `effectiveBond` or carried over).
```

### M-189 / `RTQnviiWvc8mNktnIAhWK`
- Finding title: Authorization bypass due to selector aliasing on short calldata
- Report lines: 14615-14689
```md
## [M-189]. Authorization bypass due to selector aliasing on short calldata

## id: RTQnviiWvc8mNktnIAhWK

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Zero-selector allowlist edge-case exploiter

## Exploit Type
AccessControl

## Location
VerifyData._verifyData

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood
### Finding Status Justification: The code path exists in VerifyData._verifyData: it computes `bytes4 targetSelector = bytes4(data);` without any `data.length` check. Per Solidity semantics, casting `bytes` shorter than 4 bytes to `bytes4` right-pads with zeros, so `data.length == 0..3` all collapse into selectors with trailing zeros (notably `0x00000000` for empty and 1–3 zero bytes). This creates an allowlist ambiguity: an allowlist entry for selector `0x00000000` cannot distinguish empty calldata (typical ETH transfer intent) from a non-empty calldata starting with 0s, potentially changing which entry point is hit on the target (`receive()` vs `fallback()` or a 0x00000000 function). There is no safeguard in this contract to separate these cases. Exploitability requires that governance has whitelisted selector 0 for a target and that the target treats empty vs non-empty differently in a security-relevant way; that’s not always true, hence Rare likelihood, but when true it can enable unintended state-changing behavior (Medium impact).
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_verifyData` function extracts the function selector using `bytes4(data)`. In Solidity, casting a bytes array shorter than 4 bytes to `bytes4` pads the result with zeros on the right. This causes empty calldata (`0x`) and calldata starting with `0x00` (e.g., calling a function with selector `0x00000000`) to result in the same derived selector `0x00000000`. 

This creates a semantic collision: if an admin allowlists `0x00000000` (intending to allow empty data/ETH transfers), they inadvertently allow calls to a function with selector `0x00000000` (or `fallback` with data). Conversely, if they allow a specific function `0x00000000`, they allow empty data calls. An attacker can exploit this ambiguity to execute unauthorized actions if the target contract treats empty data and zero-prefixed data differently (e.g., `receive()` vs `fallback()` vs `function dispatch`).

## Impact
Bypass of granular access control, allowing unintended function execution or fallback invocation on allowlisted targets.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Admin adds `(target, 0x00000000, chainId)` to the allowlist, intending to allow a specific function call.
2. Attacker calls the parent contract (e.g., GuardCM) with `data = ""` (empty).
3. `_verifyData` computes `bytes4("")` as `0x00000000`. The check passes.
4. The target receives a call with empty data, triggering `receive()` instead of the intended function.
5. Alternatively, if Admin allows empty data, Attacker calls function `0x00000000`.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "contracts/multisigs/VerifyData.sol";

contract Harness is VerifyData {
    function setAllowed(address t, bytes4 s, uint256 c, bool a) public {
        uint256 key = uint256(uint160(t));
        key |= uint256(uint32(s)) << 160;
        key |= c << 192;
        mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds[key] = a;
    }
    function verify(address t, bytes memory d, uint256 c) public {
        _verifyData(t, d, c);
    }
}

contract VerifyDataTest is Test {
    Harness harness;
    function setUp() public { harness = new Harness(); }
    function testSelectorAliasing() public {
        address target = address(0xCAFE);
        uint256 chainId = 1;
        // Admin authorizes selector 0x00000000
        harness.setAllowed(target, bytes4(0x00000000), chainId, true);
        
        // 1. Valid call passes
        harness.verify(target, abi.encodeWithSelector(bytes4(0x00000000)), chainId);
        
        // 2. Empty data (semantically different) also passes due to padding
        harness.verify(target, "", chainId);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Require `data.length >= 4` to extract a valid selector, or handle empty calldata explicitly (e.g., using a sentinel selector or separate allowlist logic). Example: `bytes4 selector = data.length < 4 ? bytes4(0xFFFFFFFF) : bytes4(data);` (if 0xFFFFFFFF is unused/reserved).
```

### M-190 / `XVE-QCBchEkHDeklN2Zw5`
- Finding title: Ambiguity in _verifyData treats empty data and selector 0x00000000 identically
- Report lines: 14690-14774
```md
## [M-190]. Ambiguity in _verifyData treats empty data and selector 0x00000000 identically

## id: XVE-QCBchEkHDeklN2Zw5

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Access Control / Semantic Mismatch

## Exploit Type
AccessControl

## Location
VerifyData._verifyData

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood
### Finding Status Justification: The vulnerability is present as described: `_verifyData` uses `bytes4(data)` directly, so `data.length == 0` yields `0x00000000`, and any calldata beginning with four zero bytes also yields `0x00000000`. There is no explicit differentiation between “empty calldata allowed” and “selector 0 allowed”, so a single allowlist entry for selector 0 covers both. This is not documented as intentional behavior in the provided code and is a semantic mismatch for an allowlist intended to be granular. Exploitation requires (i) selector `0x00000000` being allowlisted for a target and (ii) the target having a meaningful difference between empty and non-empty calls (e.g., `receive()` vs `fallback()` logic, or a proxy/dispatcher that processes calldata). When those conditions hold, a permissionless caller can pass verification with unexpected calldata shape and trigger unintended logic, which is a Medium-impact access control expansion. Likelihood is Rare because many targets’ fallback paths revert or are harmless, but the bypass mechanism is real and reproducible.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_verifyData` function extracts the function selector using `bytes4(data)`. If `data` is empty (length 0), the result of the cast `bytes4(data)` is `0x00000000`. If `data` is not empty but starts with four zero bytes (targeting a function with selector `0x00000000`), the result is also `0x00000000`.

This creates a semantic ambiguity in the allowlist. An admin intending to allow only ETH transfers (empty calldata) must allowlist selector `0x00000000`. However, this implicitly authorizes calls to any function on the target with selector `0x00000000`, or calls that trigger the target's fallback function with attached data (if the target's dispatcher forwards unknown selectors to fallback). This expands the attack surface beyond the intended "ETH transfer only" permission.

## Impact
Unintended authorization of function calls with selector `0x00000000` or fallback execution with data payload, when only ETH transfers (empty data) were intended.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Admin allows `(Target, 0x00000000, ChainID)` intending to allow ETH transfers.
2. Attacker calls `Target` with calldata `0x00000000...`.
3. `_verifyData` calculates selector `bytes4(data)` as `0x00000000`.
4. Verification passes.
5. Target executes call with data. If Target has a function `0x00000000()` or a fallback that handles data, it executes logic unintended by the admin.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/multisigs/VerifyData.sol";

contract HarnessVerifyData is VerifyData {
    function verify(address target, bytes memory data, uint256 chainId) external {
        _verifyData(target, data, chainId);
    }
    function setAllowed(address target, bytes4 selector, uint256 chainId, bool allowed) external {
        uint256 key = uint256(uint160(target));
        key |= uint256(uint32(selector)) << 160;
        key |= chainId << 192;
        mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds[key] = allowed;
    }
}

contract VerifyDataAmbiguityTest is Test {
    HarnessVerifyData verifier;
    function setUp() public { verifier = new HarnessVerifyData(); }
    
    function testAmbiguity() public {
        address target = address(0xABC);
        uint256 chainId = 1;
        // Allow "ETH Transfer" (Selector 0)
        verifier.setAllowed(target, bytes4(0), chainId, true);
        
        // Call with data for function 0x00000000
        bytes memory payload = abi.encodePacked(bytes4(0x00000000), uint256(123));
        
        // Should fail if we strictly wanted empty data, but passes
        verifier.verify(target, payload, chainId);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Differentiate between empty data and data with selector 0. For example, use a special sentinel value (e.g., `0xFFFFFFFF`) to represent empty data in the allowlist, and in `_verifyData`:

```solidity
bytes4 targetSelector;
if (data.length == 0) {
    targetSelector = 0xFFFFFFFF; // Sentinel for empty
} else {
    targetSelector = bytes4(data);
}
```
```

### M-191 / `FFvrBujSAMHcvsI0-KW8B`
