```md
## [M-191]. Zero-Selector Ambiguity Allows Payload Execution on Value-Transfer Allowlist

## id: FFvrBujSAMHcvsI0-KW8B

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AuthByPass

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
VerifyData._verifyData

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood
### Finding Status Justification: Same underlying issue: `bytes4(data)` in `_verifyData` collapses empty calldata and short/zero-prefixed calldata into the same selector `0x00000000`. If governance allowlists selector 0 for a target to enable value transfers, the allowlist cannot enforce “no payload”; an attacker can submit non-empty calldata that still maps to selector 0 and thus passes verification. Whether this leads to meaningful unauthorized execution depends on the target: a proxy/contract with a non-trivial payable fallback/dispatcher could treat empty vs non-empty differently and execute logic beyond simple ETH receipt. There is no built-in safeguard in VerifyData to require `data.length == 0` when selector 0 is allowlisted, nor to use a sentinel selector for empty calls. Exploitability is conditional but real; impact is Medium because it expands authorized behavior on a whitelisted target beyond the intended value-transfer-only surface, potentially enabling unintended state changes if the target’s fallback path is powerful.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_verifyData` function derives the function selector using `bytes4(data)`. If the payload `data` is empty (length 0), `bytes4(data)` evaluates to `0x00000000`. To authorize plain ETH transfers (which carry no data), governance must allowlist the selector `0x00000000`. 

However, `bytes4` casting of any bytes array shorter than 4 bytes pads with zeros. Consequently, a payload starting with zeros (e.g., `0x00...`) also results in a selector of `0x00000000`. This allows an attacker to send transactions with data to a target that was only intended to receive value (empty data). If the target is a proxy or has a fallback function that delegates execution based on data, this bypasses the allowlist's intent and can trigger arbitrary code execution on the implementation contract.

## Impact
An attacker can execute logic (via fallback or matching 0x00000000 selector) on a target contract that was only whitelisted for ETH transfers, potentially leading to unauthorized state changes or fund theft.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Governance allowlists `(target, 0x00000000, chainId)` to permit ETH transfers (empty data).
2. Target contract is a proxy or has a fallback that processes calldata.
3. Attacker calls the inheriting contract with `data = 0x00000000...` (malicious payload).
4. `_verifyData` computes `bytes4(data)` as `0x00000000`, matching the allowlist.
5. The call proceeds with the malicious payload, triggering the target's fallback/delegate logic instead of a simple value transfer.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/multisigs/VerifyData.sol";

contract VerifyDataHarness is VerifyData {
    function setAllowed(address target, bytes4 selector, uint256 chainId, bool allowed) public {
        uint256 key = uint256(uint160(target));
        key |= uint256(uint32(selector)) << 160;
        key |= chainId << 192;
        mapAllowedTargetSelectorChainIds[key] = allowed;
    }

    function verify(address target, bytes memory data, uint256 chainId) public {
        _verifyData(target, data, chainId);
    }
}

contract ZeroSelectorTest is Test {
    VerifyDataHarness verifier;
    address target = address(0xABC);
    uint256 chainId = 1;

    function setUp() public {
        verifier = new VerifyDataHarness();
    }

    function testZeroSelectorBypass() public {
        // 1. Governance authorizes "ETH Transfer" (empty data -> 0x00000000)
        verifier.setAllowed(target, 0x00000000, chainId, true);

        // 2. Attacker sends malicious payload starting with zeros
        bytes memory maliciousData = hex"00000000CAFEBABE";

        // 3. Verification passes, allowing data payload where only empty data was intended
        verifier.verify(target, maliciousData, chainId);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Differentiate between empty data and zero-selector data. If the intention is to allow ETH transfers, explicitly check `data.length == 0` or use a specific flag/sentinel value in the mapping rather than relying on `bytes4(data)`.
```

### M-192 / `eB9NPjiB-CU1BudV4pjah`
- Finding title: Permanent DoS of Tokenomics via checkpoint inactivity exceeding MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH
- Report lines: 14855-14918
```md
## [M-192]. Permanent DoS of Tokenomics via checkpoint inactivity exceeding MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH

## id: eB9NPjiB-CU1BudV4pjah

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
TimestampDependentLogic

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
Tokenomics.checkpoint

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood
### Finding Status Justification: This is a permissionless liveness failure caused by prolonged inactivity; it is not specifically an admin/privileged misuse scenario (anyone can call checkpoint, and the protocol can brick if nobody does for long enough).
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `checkpoint()` function in `Tokenomics.sol` enforces that the time elapsed since the last epoch (`diffNumSeconds`) must be less than or equal to `MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH` (defined as 1 year minus 1 day). 

```solidity
if (diffNumSeconds < curEpochLen || diffNumSeconds > MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH) {
    return false;
}
```

If the protocol remains inactive (no successful `checkpoint()` call) for longer than this duration—due to low activity, pausable conditions, or governance deadlock—`diffNumSeconds` will permanently exceed `MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH`. As a result, `checkpoint()` will always return `false`, making it impossible to advance to the next epoch, settle rewards, or update parameters. Since `prevEpochTime` can only be updated by a successful `checkpoint()`, the contract enters a permanently frozen state where no new epochs can ever be started.

## Impact
The protocol becomes permanently bricked. No new rewards, inflation, or bond/incentive updates can occur. Recovery would require a contract upgrade/redeployment.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy Tokenomics and initialize.
2. Warp time forward by `MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH + 1 second`.
3. Call `checkpoint()`.
4. Observe it returns `false` (does not revert, but fails to advance state).
5. No matter how many times `checkpoint()` is called or how much time passes, it will never succeed because `diffNumSeconds` will always be > MAX.

## Proof of Code
function test_PermanentDoS_AfterInactivity() public {
        // Assuming standard setup where tokenomics is initialized and epoch 1 started
        uint256 maxEpoch = tokenomics.MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH();
        vm.warp(block.timestamp + maxEpoch + 1 days);
        
        // Call checkpoint, expect false return
        bool success = tokenomics.checkpoint();
        assertEq(success, false, "Checkpoint should fail due to max epoch length exceeded");
        
        // Protocol is now bricked, further calls fail
        vm.warp(block.timestamp + 10 days);
        success = tokenomics.checkpoint();
        assertEq(success, false);
    }

## Suggested Mitigation
Modify the condition to allow `diffNumSeconds > MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH` but cap the inflation calculation to the max length, or provide a governance function to force-reset the epoch time if it becomes stale.
```

### M-193 / `p7Z_InxwuE8wN8_iUw5Mz`
- Finding title: Protocol Permanently Bricked if `checkpoint` is Delayed by > 1 Year
- Report lines: 14919-14984
```md
## [M-193]. Protocol Permanently Bricked if `checkpoint` is Delayed by > 1 Year

## id: p7Z_InxwuE8wN8_iUw5Mz

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
TimelockEdgeCase

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
Tokenomics.checkpoint

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood
### Finding Status Justification: Like finding #21, this is a protocol liveness/maintenance failure reachable without privileged misuse; labeling it purely as governance-risk (admin mistake) is not accurate.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `Tokenomics.checkpoint` function enforces a check that the time elapsed since the last epoch (`diffNumSeconds`) must be less than or equal to `MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH` (set to 1 year minus 1 day). 

```solidity
if (diffNumSeconds < curEpochLen || diffNumSeconds > MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH) {
    return false;
}
```

If the protocol experiences a period of inactivity (e.g., on a testnet, L2, or due to operational pause) lasting longer than 1 year, any subsequent call to `checkpoint` will fail (return false) because `diffNumSeconds` will exceed `MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH`. Since `checkpoint` is the only mechanism to advance the epoch and update `endTime`, the protocol will remain stuck in the old epoch indefinitely, preventing any future rewards distribution, inflation updates, or treasury rebalancing.

## Impact
Functionally permanent Denial of Service (DoS) of the Tokenomics system. No new epochs can ever be started, halting inflation and rewards.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy the protocol.
2. Advance block timestamp by `365 days + 1 second`.
3. Call `Tokenomics.checkpoint()`.
4. The function returns `false` due to `diffNumSeconds > MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH`.
5. The epoch counter does not increment, and `endTime` is not updated.
6. Any future calls will also have `diffNumSeconds` > 1 year, keeping the protocol bricked.

## Proof of Code
function testBrickTokenomics() public {
    // Move time forward by more than MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH (1 year)
    vm.warp(block.timestamp + 366 days);
    
    // Try to checkpoint
    bool success = tokenomics.checkpoint();
    
    // Verify failure
    assertEq(success, false);
    
    // Verify epoch didn't advance
    uint256 epoch = tokenomics.epochCounter();
    tokenomics.checkpoint();
    assertEq(tokenomics.epochCounter(), epoch);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Remove the upper bound check `diffNumSeconds > MAX_EPOCH_LENGTH` or cap the `inflationPerEpoch` calculation to 1 year instead of returning false.
```

### M-194 / `j2vl95Vwz13O03sjscqnI`
- Finding title: TWAP formula causes asymmetric price adaptation (Ratchet Effect)
- Report lines: 14985-15042
```md
## [M-194]. TWAP formula causes asymmetric price adaptation (Ratchet Effect)

## id: j2vl95Vwz13O03sjscqnI

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Oracle

## Exploit Type
Oracle

## Location
BalancerPriceOracle.updatePrice

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood
### Finding Status Justification: updatePrice derives an implied historical time weight as snapshot.cumulativePrice / snapshot.averagePrice and uses it in the denominator. Because averagePrice changes over time, this implied “time” is not a stable accumulator, creating asymmetric responsiveness depending on the direction of price moves. This is a real mathematical flaw (time-weighting should track time explicitly). Exploitability requires repeated controlled updates and influence over the pool spot price at update times, which is possible but typically costlier/rarer than simple one-shot manipulation.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The oracle uses a flawed formula to calculate the Time-Weighted Average Price (TWAP), where the weight of historical data is derived from the current price magnitude. 

```solidity
uint256 averagePrice = (snapshot.cumulativePrice + (currentPrice * elapsedTime)) /
    ((snapshot.cumulativePrice / snapshot.averagePrice) + elapsedTime);
```

The term `snapshot.cumulativePrice / snapshot.averagePrice` is intended to represent the total accumulated time, but it depends on `snapshot.averagePrice`. If `snapshot.averagePrice` increases, the effective 'historical time' decreases, making the oracle adapt *faster* to new prices. If `snapshot.averagePrice` decreases, the 'historical time' increases, making the oracle adapt *slower*. This asymmetry allows an attacker to ratchet the oracle value upwards by creating volatility, as pumps are recorded quickly while dumps are recorded slowly.

## Impact
Systematic bias in oracle pricing. An attacker can artificially inflate the TWAP over time, potentially leading to protocol overpaying for assets (buybacks) or overvaluing collateral.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Oracle initialized at Price=100.
2. Attacker pumps spot price to 200 and updates. The oracle math 'shrinks' historical time weight, causing the average to jump significantly towards 200.
3. Price returns to 100 and updates. The oracle math 'expands' historical time weight (since avg is now high), causing the average to drop slowly.
4. Repeated cycles result in a TWAP consistently higher than the true arithmetic mean.

## Proof of Code
function testMathBias() public { 
    // Setup oracle... 
    // Assume P0=100. TotalTime=1000. 
    // Update with P=200 for 100s. 
    // Check Average. 
    // Reset state. 
    // Update with P=50 for 100s. 
    // Assert Average_Pump > (Expected) AND Average_Dump > (Expected) 
    // Demonstrating the asymmetry requires tracing the `cumulative / average` term. 
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Use a standard TWAP accumulator. Store `totalAccumulatedTime` explicitly in the `PriceSnapshot` struct instead of deriving it from `cumulativePrice / averagePrice`.
```

### H-195 / `VCbG1vATG2v3-BcQfawIx`
- Finding title: Incorrect TWAP calculation reduces historical weight on price increases enabling manipulation
- Report lines: 15043-15164
```md
## [H-195]. Incorrect TWAP calculation reduces historical weight on price increases enabling manipulation

## id: VCbG1vATG2v3-BcQfawIx

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
PricePrecisionOrRoundingError

## Exploit Type
PricePrecision

## Location
BalancerPriceOracle.updatePrice

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood
### Finding Status Justification: The finding’s core claim matches code: historical “time” is inferred via cumulativePrice/averagePrice, which is only valid if averagePrice was constant historically. When averagePrice changes, the inferred time weight is distorted, effectively altering how much inertia past observations have. This can reduce resistance to upward moves relative to downward moves (bias), enabling faster-than-intended convergence under manipulation. No safeguard exists beyond maxSlippage, which is itself evaluated against the flawed computed average.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `updatePrice` function calculates the new time-weighted average price (TWAP) using a flawed formula for determining the total accumulated time weight. 

The contract derives the historical time weight using `snapshot.cumulativePrice / snapshot.averagePrice`. This derivation is only mathematically valid if the average price has been constant throughout the entire history. 

When the price rises, `snapshot.averagePrice` increases, causing the derived time weight (`cumulative / average`) to shrink significantly below the actual elapsed time. This effectively causes the oracle to 'forget' past observations, drastically reducing the inertia of the TWAP. Conversely, if the price drops, the derived time weight expands, making the oracle overly sluggish.

This asymmetry allows an attacker to manipulate the oracle price upwards with reducing resistance. By performing a series of updates with higher prices, the historical weight decays, allowing the TWAP to converge to the manipulated spot price much faster than intended, bypassing the security guarantees of the oracle mechanism.

## Impact
Manipulation of the oracle price becomes significantly cheaper and faster than intended, potentially leading to loss of funds in downstream contracts relying on this oracle for slippage protection or valuation.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deploy the oracle for a pool with Price = 100.
2. Initialize and wait 1000 seconds. Call `updatePrice`. The oracle correctly implies ~1000s of history (`Cumulative/Average` ~ 1000).
3. Manipulate the spot price to 200. Wait 1000 seconds. Call `updatePrice`.
4. Due to the formula `(Cum + P*dt) / (Cum/Avg + dt)`, the new Average rises to ~133 (overshooting the true linear average of 125).
5. The new implied history (`Cum / NewAvg`) drops significantly (e.g., from 2000s actual to ~1500s implied), reducing the weight of the stable history.
6. Repeat updates with high prices; the oracle 'inertia' degrades rapidly, allowing the price to be pinned to the high value.

## Proof of Code
contract BalancerPriceOraclePoC is Test {
    BalancerPriceOracle oracle;
    MockVault vault;
    address tokenA = address(0x1);
    address tokenB = address(0x2);
    bytes32 poolId = bytes32(uint256(1));

    function setUp() public {
        vault = new MockVault(tokenA, tokenB);
        oracle = new BalancerPriceOracle(
            address(0), // olas unused
            tokenB,
            100, // maxSlippage 100%
            0, // minUpdate
            address(vault),
            poolId
        );
    }

    function testTwapDecay() public {
        // 1. Init at Price 100
        vault.setBalances(1e18, 100e18);
        oracle.updatePrice();
        
        // 2. Advance 1000s, keep Price 100
        vm.warp(block.timestamp + 1000);
        oracle.updatePrice();
        
        (uint256 cum, , uint256 avg) = oracle.snapshotHistory();
        // Implied time = Cum / Avg = 100000 / 100 = 1000
        assertApproxEqAbs(cum / avg, 1000, 1);

        // 3. Advance 1000s, Spike Price to 200
        vm.warp(block.timestamp + 1000);
        vault.setBalances(1e18, 200e18);
        oracle.updatePrice();

        (cum, , avg) = oracle.snapshotHistory();
        
        // Real elapsed time is 2000s.
        // Implied time in oracle state:
        uint256 impliedTime = cum / avg;
        
        // With correct TWAP, implied time should be near 2000.
        // Due to bug, it shrinks significantly.
        emit log_named_uint("Implied Time", impliedTime);
        
        // Assert that the oracle thinks history is < 1600s (lost >20% of weight)
        assertLt(impliedTime, 1600);
    }
}

contract MockVault {
    address[] public tokens;
    uint256[] public balances;
    constructor(address t1, address t2) {
        tokens = new address[](2); tokens[0] = t1; tokens[1] = t2;
        balances = new uint256[](2); balances[0] = 1e18; balances[1] = 1e18;
    }
    function setBalances(uint256 b0, uint256 b1) external { balances[0] = b0; balances[1] = b1; }
    function getPoolTokens(bytes32) external view returns (address[] memory, uint256[] memory, uint256) {
        return (tokens, balances, 0);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Track the total accumulated time explicitly in a state variable `totalDuration` instead of deriving it from `cumulativePrice / averagePrice`. 

Formula:
`snapshot.cumulativePrice += currentPrice * elapsedTime;`
`snapshot.totalDuration += elapsedTime;`
`snapshot.averagePrice = snapshot.cumulativePrice / snapshot.totalDuration;`





Finding Status: InvalidUserErrorOrMistake
```

### H-196 / `Qvie14Z9NGfmWl-jaEJ3T`

- Report lines: 15165-15208
```md
## [H-196]. Unprotected `initializeTokenomics` allows front-running takeover

## id: Qvie14Z9NGfmWl-jaEJ3T

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
InitOrderOrUnintialized

## Exploit Type
UpgradeabilityInitializerSafety

## Location
Tokenomics.initializeTokenomics

## Finding Status: InvalidUserErrorOrMistake
### Finding Status Justification: Tokenomics.initializeTokenomics is external, sets `owner = msg.sender`, and only gates on `owner != address(0)` (AlreadyInitialized). There is no access restriction to a deployer/factory and no delegatecall-only guard, so a proxy left uninitialized in a separate transaction can be front-run and permanently hijacked (owner becomes attacker; critical addresses set). This is a standard initialization race. Exploitability depends on deployment practice (atomic init prevents it), hence “occasional” likelihood, but the code itself is vulnerable.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `initializeTokenomics` function in `Tokenomics.sol` is `external` and lacks any access control or state check other than `owner != address(0)`. Since the proxy contract delegates to this logic, an attacker can monitor the mempool for the deployment/initialization transaction and front-run it. By calling `initializeTokenomics` first, the attacker becomes the `owner` and can set all critical addresses (treasury, depository, registries, etc.) to malicious contracts, effectively hijacking the entire protocol deployment.

## Impact
Complete takeover of the protocol at deployment time. Attacker sets themselves as owner and controls all system parameters and linked contracts.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deployer deploys Tokenomics proxy and logic. 2. Attacker observes the transaction to call `initializeTokenomics`. 3. Attacker sends a transaction with higher gas price calling `initializeTokenomics` with their own address as owner and malicious contract addresses. 4. Attacker's tx executes first. 5. Attacker owns the protocol.

## Proof of Code
function testFrontRunInit() public {
    Tokenomics logic = new Tokenomics();
    // Attacker calls init immediately
    logic.initializeTokenomics(address(1), address(2), address(3), address(4), address(5), 10 days, address(6), address(7), address(8), address(9));
    assertEq(logic.owner(), address(this));
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Protect the `initializeTokenomics` function with a deployment-time check (e.g., restricted to a deployer address) or ensure the proxy deployment and initialization occur atomically in a constructor or a factory contract.
```

### H-197 / `rt8fvEM7S4oM5PsYxbiv8`
- Finding title: Front-runnable initialization allows contract takeover
- Report lines: 15209-15261
```md
## [H-197]. Front-runnable initialization allows contract takeover

## id: rt8fvEM7S4oM5PsYxbiv8

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UpgradeabilityInitializerSafety

## Exploit Type
UpgradeabilityInitializerSafety

## Location
BuyBackBurner.initialize

## Finding Status: InvalidUserErrorOrMistake
### Finding Status Justification: Code path exists: BuyBackBurner.initialize() is external and only checks owner != address(0) then sets owner = msg.sender. On a freshly deployed proxy/implementation storage, owner is 0, so any address can initialize first. If deployment and initialization are not atomic, a mempool attacker can front-run initialize and become owner, then call changeImplementation() (owner-only) to upgrade to malicious logic. No on-chain safeguard ties initialize to a deployer/factory nor uses OZ Initializable. This is a known, real upgradeable-proxy footgun; it requires an operational mistake (uninitialized window) but is still exploitable and catastrophic.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `initialize` function in `BuyBackBurner` is `external` and permissionless, solely guarded by the check `owner != address(0)`. When the contract (or its proxy) is deployed, the storage slot for `owner` is typically zero. If the deployment and initialization are not executed atomically (e.g., in the same transaction via a factory or constructor call), an attacker can front-run the `initialize` call. By calling `initialize` first, the attacker sets `owner` to their own address, granting them full control over the contract, including the ability to change the implementation, set oracles, and drain funds.

## Impact
Complete compromise of the contract. The attacker can upgrade the implementation to malicious logic, change the owner, or misconfigure oracles to drain funds.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deployer deploys the `BuyBackBurnerProxy` (pointing to `BuyBackBurner` implementation).
2. Attacker observes the deployment in the mempool.
3. Attacker sends a transaction to call `initialize(payload)` on the proxy address with a higher gas price.
4. Attacker's transaction executes first. `owner` is set to Attacker.
5. Deployer's `initialize` call reverts with `AlreadyInitialized`.
6. Attacker calls `changeImplementation` to point to a malicious contract and drains the treasury.

## Proof of Code
function testFrontRunInit() public {
    BuyBackBurner burner = new BuyBackBurnerImpl(); // deploying implementation
    // In reality this happens on the proxy
    vm.prank(attacker);
    burner.initialize("");
    assertEq(burner.owner(), attacker);
    vm.prank(deployer);
    vm.expectRevert(BuyBackBurner.AlreadyInitialized.selector);
    burner.initialize("");
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Restrict the `initialize` function so it can only be called by the deployer (e.g., check `msg.sender == deployer` set in constructor) or ensure that the proxy deployment and initialization happen atomically in a factory contract.
```

### M-198 / `CFiy9cFePq8vAWfRNh6EE`
- Finding title: DoS in create() if Service Owner is also an Agent Instance
- Report lines: 15262-15304
```md
## [M-198]. DoS in create() if Service Owner is also an Agent Instance

## id: CFiy9cFePq8vAWfRNh6EE

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
RecoveryModule.create

## Finding Status: InvalidUserErrorOrMistake
### Finding Status Justification: In RecoveryModule.create(), when the Safe currently has a single owner equal to serviceOwner, the code loops over multisigOwners and calls addOwnerWithThreshold() for each, then removes serviceOwner. If multisigOwners contains serviceOwner, the Safe will revert on adding an existing owner, causing deploy/redeploy to revert. ServiceRegistry does not prevent the service owner address from being registered as an agent instance (it only forbids operator == agentInstance), so this configuration is possible. This is avoidable by a simple pre-check/skip, but currently causes deployment DoS.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `create` function attempts to update a single-owner Safe to include the new agent instances. It iterates through `multisigOwners` (agents) and calls `addOwnerWithThreshold` for each. If the Service Owner (who is currently the sole owner of the Safe) is also listed as an Agent Instance for the service (which is allowed by the Registry), the `addOwnerWithThreshold` call will revert because Gnosis Safe prevents adding an existing owner. This causes the `create` function, and thus the `deploy` function, to revert.

## Impact
Services where the Service Owner is also an Agent Instance cannot be deployed using the `RecoveryModule`, causing a Denial of Service for this valid configuration.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. User U registers a Service. 2. User U registers Agent Instance A, where A == U. 3. User U owns the Safe (single owner). 4. User U calls `deploy`. 5. `RecoveryModule.create` tries to add A (which is U) to the Safe. 6. Safe reverts "Address is already an owner". 7. Deployment fails.

## Proof of Code
function testCreateDoSIfOwnerIsAgent() public { 
    // Setup Service where Agent == Owner 
    // Call deploy 
    // Expect revert 
 }

## Suggested Mitigation
In the update loop in `create`, check if `multisigOwners[i]` is already an owner of the Safe (or specifically if it equals `serviceOwner`) before calling `addOwnerWithThreshold`. If it is, skip the addition.
```

### M-199 / `0E4VE2almFaORjTYSzrGe`
- Finding title: DoS via Malformed Data Length in VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData
- Report lines: 15305-15384
```md
## [M-199]. DoS via Malformed Data Length in VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData

## id: 0E4VE2almFaORjTYSzrGe

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Dos

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData

## Finding Status: InvalidUserErrorOrMistake
### Finding Status Justification: After decoding payloadLength, _verifyBridgedData copies bytes using `payload[j] = data[i + j]` without checking that `i + payloadLength <= data.length`. If payloadLength is larger than the remaining bytes, the loop will revert with an out-of-bounds panic, failing the entire verification (and thus failing the surrounding operation). This is a real missing bounds check. Triggering it requires submitting malformed/incorrectly encoded packed data (likely by the actor constructing the bridged payload), so it is more likely to manifest as input error than as an external attack, but it still represents a DoS risk for any batch processing where one malformed entry causes full revert.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `_verifyBridgedData` function iterates over a packed data stream to extract and verify payloads. It reads a `payloadLength` (uint32) from the stream and subsequently enters a loop to copy `payloadLength` bytes into a new memory array: `for (uint256 j = 0; j < payloadLength; ++j) { payload[j] = data[i + j]; }`. Crucially, the code does not verify that `i + payloadLength` is within the bounds of `data.length` before starting the copy loop. If a malicious or malformed message specifies a `payloadLength` larger than the remaining data, the transaction will revert with a Solidity Panic (index out of bounds) when accessing `data[i + j]`. This causes the entire transaction to fail, effectively blocking the processing of any valid messages included in the same batch/transaction (DoS).

## Impact
Medium. A single malformed message can cause the reversion of a batch of cross-chain messages, causing denial of service for the bridge consumer.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker constructs a packed data blob containing multiple messages. 
2. One message has a header declaring `payloadLength = 100` but provides only 1 byte of payload. 
3. `_verifyBridgedData` reads the length 100. 
4. The copy loop iterates. When `j` exceeds the actual available bytes, `data[i + j]` triggers an OOB revert. 
5. The entire batch fails.

## Proof of Code
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;

import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import "../contracts/multisigs/bridge_verifier/VerifyBridgedData.sol";

contract VerifyBridgedDataHarness is VerifyBridgedData {
    function processBridgeData(bytes memory, address, uint256) external override {}
    function exposeVerifyBridgedData(bytes memory data, uint256 chainId) external {
        _verifyBridgedData(data, chainId);
    }
}

contract BridgedDataDoSTest is Test {
    VerifyBridgedDataHarness verifier;
    
    function setUp() public {
        verifier = new VerifyBridgedDataHarness();
    }
    
    function testDoSWithMalformedData() public {
        address target = address(0x1);
        uint96 value = 0;
        uint32 length = 100; // Claim 100 bytes
        
        // Create data with correct header but truncated payload (only 1 byte)
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(
            target,
            value,
            length,
            uint8(0xAA)
        );
        
        // The call should revert due to OOB access, confirming DoS vulnerability
        vm.expectRevert(); 
        verifier.exposeVerifyBridgedData(data, 1);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Validate that the declared length matches the available data before reading: `if (i + payloadLength > data.length) revert DataLengthIncorrect(payloadLength, data.length - i);`.
```

### H-200 / `ada3GmU2GHgF8dbrOOGIw`
- Finding title: Signature Replay in registerAgentsWithSignature allows forced bonding of operators
- Report lines: 15385-15484
```md
## [H-200]. Signature Replay in registerAgentsWithSignature allows forced bonding of operators

## id: ada3GmU2GHgF8dbrOOGIw

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
PermitOrSignatureReplay

## Exploit Type
SignatureReplay

## Location
ServiceManager.registerAgentsWithSignature

## Finding Status: InvalidUserErrorOrMistake
### Finding Status Justification: Nonces used for signature validation (`mapOperatorRegisterAgentsNonces`) are only incremented inside `registerAgentsWithSignature()`. A direct operator registration via `registerAgents()` does not consume/invalidate any previously issued signatures at the same nonce. Therefore, if an operator had shared a signature for nonce N but then registered directly (leaving nonce N unused), a service owner holding that signature can later execute it as long as the operator still has sufficient token allowance/balance, forcing another `registerAgentsTokenDeposit()` attempt. Operators can mitigate by revoking token allowance, but the replay property stems from the on-chain nonce lifecycle design.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:RequiresRole


## Description
The `ServiceManager` uses a nonce-based signature scheme (`OperatorSignedHashes`) to authenticate operator intent in `registerAgentsWithSignature`. The nonce associated with `(operator, serviceId)` is only incremented when the signature-based function is successfully executed. Critically, if an operator performs the action directly via `registerAgents` (without signature), the nonce is **not** incremented. 

This creates a replay vulnerability: An operator may sign a registration intent (Nonce N), but then choose to register directly (leaving Nonce N unused). If the operator later unbonds and leaves the service, the Service Owner can reuse the stale signature (still valid at Nonce N) to call `registerAgentsWithSignature`. This forces the operator to re-register and, significantly, triggers `ServiceRegistryTokenUtility.registerAgentsTokenDeposit` to transfer the bond amount (tokens) from the operator again without their current consent. A similar issue exists for `unbondWithSignature`.

## Impact
Service Owners can force operators to re-join a service and lock up bond funds (theft of liquidity) using stale signatures from previous sessions.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Operator approves TokenUtility. 
2. Operator signs a `registerAgents` message for Service S (Nonce 0). 
3. Operator calls `registerAgents` directly (Nonce 0 remains 0). 
4. Operator completes work and calls `unbond` to leave Service S. 
5. Malicious Service Owner calls `registerAgentsWithSignature` using the signature from step 2. 
6. The signature is valid (Nonce 0). The Operator is re-registered and their bond tokens are transferred again.

## Proof of Code
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
import {ServiceManager} from "../contracts/ServiceManager.sol";

contract ServiceManagerReplayTest is Test {
    ServiceManager sm;
    address registry = makeAddr("registry");
    address tokenUtility = makeAddr("tokenUtility");
    address operator = makeAddr("operator");
    address owner = makeAddr("owner");
    uint256 serviceId = 1;
    bytes32 DOMAIN_SEPARATOR;
    bytes32 REGISTER_TYPE_HASH;

    function setUp() public {
        sm = new ServiceManager(registry, tokenUtility);
        DOMAIN_SEPARATOR = sm.getDomainSeparator();
        REGISTER_TYPE_HASH = sm.REGISTER_AGENTS_TYPE_HASH();
    }

    function testReplayRegistration() public {
        // 1. Setup hashes
        uint256 nonce = sm.mapOperatorRegisterAgentsNonces(uint256(uint160(operator)) | (serviceId << 160));
        address[] memory instances = new address[](0);
        uint32[] memory ids = new uint32[](0);
        
        bytes32 structHash = keccak256(abi.encode(REGISTER_TYPE_HASH, operator, owner, serviceId, keccak256(abi.encode(instances, ids)), nonce));
        bytes32 digest = keccak256(abi.encodePacked("\x19\x01", DOMAIN_SEPARATOR, structHash));
        (uint8 v, bytes32 r, bytes32 s) = vm.sign(1, digest); // Operator key
        bytes memory signature = abi.encodePacked(r, s, v);

        // 2. Operator registers directly (mocking success)
        vm.prank(operator);
        vm.mockCall(registry, abi.encodeWithSelector(bytes4(keccak256("registerAgents(address,uint256,address[],uint32[])"))), abi.encode(true));
        vm.mockCall(tokenUtility, abi.encodeWithSelector(bytes4(keccak256("registerAgentsTokenDeposit(address,uint256,uint32[])"))), abi.encode(false));
        
        sm.registerAgents(serviceId, instances, ids);
        
        // Nonce should still be 0
        assertEq(sm.getOperatorRegisterAgentsNonce(operator, serviceId), 0);

        // 3. Operator Unbonds (simulated)
        
        // 4. Owner replays signature
        vm.prank(owner);
        vm.mockCall(registry, abi.encodeWithSelector(bytes4(keccak256("ownerOf(uint256)"))), abi.encode(owner));
        // Expect token pull from operator again
        vm.expectCall(tokenUtility, abi.encodeWithSelector(bytes4(keccak256("registerAgentsTokenDeposit(address,uint256,uint32[])"))));
        
        sm.registerAgentsWithSignature(operator, serviceId, instances, ids, signature);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Introduce a deadline parameter in the signed message to expire signatures. Additionally, consider providing a function for operators to manually increment/invalidate their nonces.





Finding Status: InvalidNotExploitable
```

### H-201 / `cCgsaq8gLbuwfN-PG3k8i`
- Finding title: Validation bypass in `_verifyBridgedData` due to dirty high bits in assembly reads
- Report lines: 15485-15559
```md
## [H-201]. Validation bypass in `_verifyBridgedData` due to dirty high bits in assembly reads

## id: cCgsaq8gLbuwfN-PG3k8i

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UnsafeAssembyTypeCasts

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
ProcessBridgedDataPolygon._verifyBridgedData

## Finding Status: InvalidNotExploitable
### Finding Status Justification: The missing masking after `mload` into `uint32 payloadLength`/`address target` is a real Solidity 0.8+ footgun: dirty upper bits can make `payloadLength` effectively enormous (>= 2^32) and cause `new bytes(payloadLength)` to revert/OOG. That is a potentially high-impact availability failure if a valid message ever yields non-zero upper bits in the loaded word, and it is not inherently “rare” for arbitrary packed bytes formats.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
In `VerifyBridgedData._verifyBridgedData`, the assembly block uses `mload` to read `target` and `payloadLength` from the packed `data`. `mload(add(data, i))` reads a full 32-byte word. Due to the packed structure, the read word for `target` includes the tail of the `data` length field in its high bits, and the read word for `payloadLength` includes bytes from `target`/`value` in its high bits. Since Solidity 0.8 does not strictly clean high bits of stack variables assigned in assembly before using them in comparisons, the check `if (target == address(0))` evaluates to `false` (bypassing the zero-address check) because `target` is non-zero (due to dirty high bits). Similarly, `if (payloadLength < SELECTOR_DATA_LENGTH)` fails to revert for small payloads (e.g., length 0) because the dirty high bits make the value appear large. This allows malformed or disallowed payloads (zero address, empty payload) to be passed to `_verifyData`.

## Impact
Bypasses critical sanity checks (`target != 0`, `length >= 4`). If `address(0)` or `bytes4(0)` (empty payload) are ever enabled in the allowlist (e.g., as wildcards or default entries), attackers can execute unauthorized actions. Violates protocol invariants.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Attacker constructs a bridged message with `target = address(0)` and a valid payload.
2. Calls `processBridgeData`.
3. The `mload` logic populates `target` with dirty high bits (from `data.length`).
4. `target == address(0)` check fails (evaluates to false).
5. `_verifyData(address(0), ...)` is called. If the allowlist contains an entry for `address(0)`, the check passes.

## Proof of Code
contract DirtyBitsPoC is Test {
    ProcessBridgedDataPolygon public verifier;
    function setUp() public {
        verifier = new ProcessBridgedDataPolygon();
    }
    function testBypassZeroAddressCheck() public {
        // Construct data: Selector + abi.encode(mediator, l2Message)
        // l2Message: [Target(0)][Value(0)][Len(4)][Payload]
        // Target is address(0). It should revert ZeroAddress, but will revert NotAuthorized (passed to _verifyData).
        
        bytes memory l2Message = abi.encodePacked(
            address(0), // Target
            uint96(0),  // Value
            uint32(4),  // PayloadLength
            bytes4(0x12345678) // Payload
        );
        
        bytes memory data = abi.encodePacked(
            verifier.SEND_MESSAGE_TO_CHILD(),
            abi.encode(address(this), l2Message)
        );

        // Expect NotAuthorized instead of ZeroAddress, proving the bypass
        vm.expectRevert(abi.encodeWithSelector(NotAuthorized.selector, address(0), bytes4(0x12345678), 137));
        verifier.processBridgeData(data, address(this), 137);
    }
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Clean the variables after assembly assignment using explicit type casting or masking:
`target := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF)`
`payloadLength := and(mload(add(data, i)), 0xFFFFFFFF)`





Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidUserErrorOrMistake
```

### M-202 / `lL0gC6M4OmEfeIGla7uqp`
- Finding title: Strict owner ordering in create() causes DoS for valid Gnosis Safes
- Report lines: 15560-15602
```md
## [M-202]. Strict owner ordering in create() causes DoS for valid Gnosis Safes

## id: lL0gC6M4OmEfeIGla7uqp

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
StandardViolation

## Exploit Type
Dos

## Location
RecoveryModule.create

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidUserErrorOrMistake
### Finding Status Justification: RecoveryModule.create() verifies owners by exact reverse-order equality: multisigOwners[i] must equal checkOwners[numOwners - i - 1]. This rejects Safes where the owner set matches but ordering differs (Gnosis Safe treats owners as an unordered set for authorization). If a Safe was created/modified via different tooling or owners were re-ordered, redeployment via this module reverts even though the Safe is otherwise valid. This is a liveness/UX DoS, typically requiring non-standard setup or user/owner actions that changed ordering, hence lower likelihood.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `create` function validates that the Gnosis Safe's owners match the Service Registry's agent instances. It enforces that the Safe's owner list (returned by `getOwners()`) is the exact reverse of the input `multisigOwners` array. While Gnosis Safe's default behavior (prepending new owners) typically results in this order, Gnosis Safe treats owners as a set, and valid Safes can have owners in any order (e.g., if created via different tooling or manual `addOwner` calls). If a Safe has correct owners but in a different order, `create` will revert with `WrongOwner`, causing a Denial of Service for deploying the service.

## Impact
Service owners are unable to deploy services using valid, existing Gnosis Safes if the internal owner order does not strictly match the module's expectations. This forces users to perform complex manual reordering of owners.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Setup a Gnosis Safe with owners [A, B] such that `getOwners()` returns [A, B] (e.g. by adding B then A). 2. Register a Service with agents [A, B]. 3. Call `ServiceRegistry.deploy` which calls `RecoveryModule.create`. 4. `create` expects `checkOwners` to be reverse of inputs ([B, A]). 5. `create` compares A vs B and reverts. Deployment fails.

## Proof of Code
function testCreateDoSOnWrongOrder() public { 
    // Setup Safe with owners [A, B] 
    // Call create with agents [A, B] 
    // Expect revert 
 }

## Suggested Mitigation
Relax the strict ordering check. Verify that the set of Safe owners matches the set of agent instances, irrespective of order (e.g., using a nested loop or sorting).
```

### H-203 / `ezEVch81DTQiaxIHHQ1Sx`
- Finding title: Unprotected `initialize` function allows ownership takeover
- Report lines: 15603-15651
```md
## [H-203]. Unprotected `initialize` function allows ownership takeover

## id: ezEVch81DTQiaxIHHQ1Sx

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
Unprotected initializer pattern

## Exploit Type
UpgradeabilityInitializerSafety

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.initialize

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidUserErrorOrMistake
### Finding Status Justification: initialize(uint16) is external and sets owner=msg.sender if owner is currently address(0). There is no restriction to a deployer/factory, no onlyProxy guard, and no constructor that disables initialization on the implementation. If a proxy is deployed and not initialized atomically in the same transaction, any attacker can front-run and become owner, gaining control over privileged functions (changeOwner, changeImplementation, transferToken, transferPositionId). The only safeguard is the one-time AlreadyInitialized check, which does not prevent the initial takeover.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `initialize` function in `LiquidityManagerCore` is external and permissionless. It sets `owner = msg.sender` and can only be called once. However, if the contract is deployed (e.g., as an implementation for a proxy or a standalone contract behind a proxy) and initialization is not performed atomically in the deployment transaction, an attacker can front-run the initialization call. This would grant the attacker the `owner` role, allowing them to control all privileged functions (e.g., `changeOwner`, `withdraw`, `changeImplementation`).

## Impact
High. Complete loss of protocol control and funds managed by the contract.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Developer deploys `LiquidityManagerProxy` pointing to `LiquidityManagerETH` (which inherits Core). 
2. Developer sends a separate transaction to call `initialize`. 
3. Attacker front-runs the initialization transaction, calling `initialize` first. 
4. Attacker becomes `owner` and can now drain funds or maliciously upgrade the contract.

## Proof of Code
function testFrontRunInitialize() public {
    // Deploy logic contract
    LiquidityManagerCore impl = new ConcreteLiquidityManager(...);
    // Attacker calls initialize before legitimate owner
    vm.prank(attacker);
    impl.initialize(100);
    assertEq(impl.owner(), attacker);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Ensure `initialize` is called atomically during deployment (e.g., using a constructor or `setup` payload in the proxy factory). Alternatively, restrict `initialize` to a specific deployer address.
```

### H-204 / `CAQkxuRZf3fTmVPDQI_WE`

## [H-204]. Front-runnable Initialization

## id: CAQkxuRZf3fTmVPDQI_WE

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
UpgradeabilityInitializerSafety

## Exploit Type
AuthByPass

## Location
LiquidityManagerCore.initialize

## Finding Status: LowSeverityDueToRareLikelihood + InvalidUserErrorOrMistake
### Finding Status Justification: Same root issue as other initialize reports: initialize is permissionless when owner==0 and sets owner to msg.sender. In any non-atomic deployment pattern (deploy proxy first, initialize later), an attacker can front-run the initialize transaction and take ownership. No deployer-only restriction exists, and the one-time guard only prevents re-initialization after takeover.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `initialize` function sets the `owner` to `msg.sender` and can be called by anyone if `owner` is currently `address(0)`. In a proxy deployment pattern, the proxy is deployed and then `initialize` is called. If these are not atomic (e.g., separate transactions), an attacker can front-run the initialization transaction, claim ownership, and gain full control over the contract, including the ability to drain tokens via `transferToken` or `transferPositionId`.

## Impact
Complete takeover of the contract and loss of all funds.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Deployer deploys the proxy pointing to the implementation. 
2. Attacker observes the deployment. 
3. Attacker calls `initialize(100)` on the proxy before the Deployer. 
4. Attacker becomes `owner`. 
5. Attacker can now control all privileged functions.

## Proof of Code
function testFrontRunInit() public {
    LiquidityManager manager = new LiquidityManager(...);
    // Attacker calls init first
    vm.prank(attacker);
    manager.initialize(100);
    assertEq(manager.owner(), attacker);
}

## Suggested Mitigation
Ensure initialization is atomic with deployment (using a Factory) or restrict `initialize` to a specific deployer address if possible. Alternatively, use a constructor to disable initializers on the implementation contract to prevent direct takeover, and use `ERC1967Proxy` with initialization data.





Finding Status: InvalidOutOfScope

### M-205 / `WjP6NDczfKF29K37gr7Ix`
- Finding title: Tokenomics.checkpoint fails to reduce effectiveBond when actual inflation is lower than estimated
- Report lines: 15705-15789
```md
## [M-205]. Tokenomics.checkpoint fails to reduce effectiveBond when actual inflation is lower than estimated

## id: WjP6NDczfKF29K37gr7Ix

## Derived From Pattern/Invariant
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Exploit Type
AccountingInvariantViolation

## Location
Tokenomics.checkpoint

## Finding Status: InvalidOutOfScope
### Finding Status Justification: Cross-year epoch boundary behavior is periodic (at least annually) and can plausibly occur depending on epoch timing; it is not inherently 'rare' even if operationally managed.
### Finding Complexity: 0
### PoC Test Status: ErrorRunningTests
## Minimim Privilege Required:Permissionless


## Description
The `effectiveBond` variable tracks the cumulative bonding capacity authorized by the protocol. It is updated in `checkpoint()` to reconcile the difference between the *estimated* bond cap (calculated at the start of the epoch) and the *actual* bond cap (calculated at the end, based on exact time passed). 

When an epoch crosses a year boundary where the inflation rate decreases (as defined in `TokenomicsConstants`), the actual allowable inflation (`incentives[4]`) may be lower than the estimated cap (`curMaxBond`) which was calculated using the higher rate of the previous year.

The current implementation only adjusts `effectiveBond` if `incentives[4] > curMaxBond` (adding the surplus). It lacks logic to handle the case where `incentives[4] < curMaxBond`. As a result, if the actual inflation allowance is lower than estimated, the excess capacity remains in `effectiveBond`, effectively permanently increasing the allowable minting cap beyond the protocol's inflation schedule.

Vulnerable snippet in `Tokenomics.checkpoint()`:
```solidity
        // incentives[4] > curMaxBond check exists
        if (incentives[4] > curMaxBond) {
            incentives[4] = effectiveBond + incentives[4] - curMaxBond;
            effectiveBond = uint96(incentives[4]);
        }
        // Missing else block for incentives[4] < curMaxBond
```

## Impact
Protocol permanently over-estimates bonding capacity, allowing minting of OLAS tokens exceeding the defined inflation schedule.

## Command to Run Test


## Proof of Concept
1. Configure Tokenomics such that Year N has high inflation and Year N+1 has low inflation.
2. Advance time to near the end of Year N and call `checkpoint()`. This sets `maxBond` (and increases `effectiveBond`) for the next epoch using Year N's high rate.
3. Advance time into Year N+1, crossing the year boundary within the epoch.
4. Call `checkpoint()`. `incentives[4]` (actual bond cap) is calculated using a weighted average of the high and low rates, resulting in a value lower than `curMaxBond` (which used only the high rate).
5. Observe that `effectiveBond` is not reduced by the difference, leaving 'phantom' bonding capacity that violates the inflation cap.

## Proof of Code
function testEffectiveBondInflationBug() public {
        // Assuming setup with high inflation in year 0 and lower in year 1
        // Advance to end of year 0
        vm.warp(tokenomics.timeLaunch() + 365 days - 10 days);
        tokenomics.checkpoint();
        
        // Store effectiveBond after checkpoint (includes credit for next epoch based on Year 0 rate)
        uint96 effectiveBondStart = tokenomics.effectiveBond();
        uint96 maxBondEstimated = tokenomics.maxBond();

        // Advance time into Year 1 (lower inflation)
        // Epoch length is 10 days, so we cross the boundary
        vm.warp(block.timestamp + 10 days);
        
        tokenomics.checkpoint();
        
        // Calculate expected maxBond for the elapsed period (should be lower due to rate drop)
        // But effectiveBond should have been reduced to reflect this.
        // In vulnerable code, effectiveBond remains high.
        uint96 effectiveBondEnd = tokenomics.effectiveBond();
        
        // Check that effectiveBond was NOT reduced (it should be equal to start + new_credit, without deducting the over-estimation)
        // Logic implies: effectiveBondEnd = effectiveBondStart + newMaxBond.
        // Correct logic implies: effectiveBondEnd = effectiveBondStart + newMaxBond - (maxBondEstimated - actualMaxBond);
        
        assertTrue(effectiveBondEnd > effectiveBondStart, "Effective bond should increase");
        // Detailed math assertion would show effectiveBondEnd is higher than protocol inflation allows
    }

## Suggested Mitigation
Update `Tokenomics.checkpoint` to handle the case where `incentives[4] < curMaxBond` by reducing `effectiveBond` by the difference, clamping to zero if underflow would occur (which implies capacity was already used).
```
